## REMERCIEMENTS

Après trois années d’aventures doctorales – enfin trois ans et quelques mois, mais on ne compte plus maintenant – je tiens tout d’abord à remercier mes directeurs de thèse pour m’avoir permis de réaliser cette thèse. Au-delà du projet scientifique, cette thèse m’a fait vivre une aventure sociale formidable au cours de laquelle j’ai pu rencontrer tant de belles personnes, que j’aimerais maintenant remercier. [SOURCE: Page_4]

Mes premiers remerciements vont à mon directeur de thèse, Jacques Labonne, qui m’a accordé sa confiance tout au long de ce projet. Il m’a laissé libre de mener mon projet de thèse, tout en m’apportant son aide et ses précieux conseils quand nécessaire. Il s’est toujours montré disponible et à l’écoute, et a su me rassurer dans les moments de doute, qui n’ont fait que grandir en fin de thèse. Je ressortais toujours un peu plus motivée de son bureau. Je tiens surtout à le remercier pour son aide, au-delà du professionnel, notamment suite à mon aquaplaning de début de thèse, pour les petits soucis personnels et sans oublier son secours sur un bord de route de la bergerie ;). Un grand merci pour tout. [SOURCE: Page_4]

J’aimerais également remercier mon co-directeur de thèse, Arnaud Caudron, pour son expertise, plus appliquées, ainsi que les échanges que nous avons eus sur le projet. Merci également pour l’accueil au sein de son entreprise, Scimabio Interface. Dommage que nous n’ayons pas pu échanger plus et mener à bout la collaboration avec les gestionnaires. [SOURCE: Page_4]

Pour m’avoir fait l’honneur de juger mon travail de thèse, un grand merci aux membres de mon jury : Annabel Porté, Lisa Jacquin, François Lefèvre et Nicolas Poulet (que je remercie également pour son suivi de ce projet). [SOURCE: Page_4]

ABSTRACT Contemporary patterns of diversity are often the result of allopatric evolution driven by either adaptive or non-adaptive processes. The admixture of previously isolated gene pools, through human intervention, often leads to gene flow and hybridization. Intraspecific diversity management is therefore a major issue as diversity represents a resilience mechanism for populations, but also because it holds economic and patrimonial values. A typical example is the admixture of the Atlantic (ATL) and the Mediterranean (MED) lineages of brown trout (Salmo trutta). In the French Mediterranean area, a century of intensive stocking from ATL origins led to various outcomes of hybridization with the MED lineage, going from MED resilience to total extirpation via full hybridization. Historical management practices aimed at restoring MED populations but failed to reach conservation targets as eco-evolutionary mechanisms (EEMs) driving hybridization were not accounted for. For instance, empirical studies evidenced two potential drivers of hybridization: female heterogamous preference and genotype-by-environment (GxE) interactions on embryonic survival – involving maternal genotype and water temperature. [SOURCE: Page_7]

My thesis aims at investigating how these EEMs interact with other ecological, genetic and demographic drivers to influence the evolution of intraspecific diversity, in a realistic spatiotemporal framework. To do so, I assembled all the knowledge available, both ecological and evolutionary, to create an agent-based demo-genetic modelling framework (DG-ABM): MEDITERRANEA. This DG-ABM allowed me to consider (i) the EEMs driving hybridization, (ii) their genetic bases, (iii) variations in ecological parameters, as well as (iv) management practices. In fine, my goal was to answer one question: do eco-evolutionary mechanisms matter for the management of intraspecific diversity? The short answer, in the present case, is yes. [SOURCE: Page_7]

In multiple case studies (real and fictive), I compared various scenarios, including or not EEMs, and considering several alternative management strategies. Heterogamous preference barely influenced the evolution of diversity. On the contrary, GxE interactions played a central role in the evolution of diversity. In cold environments, the implementation of this EEM led to selection against maternally inherited ATL alleles. The strength of that selection mainly changed with temperature variations, highlighting the importance of local conditions. In addition, simulated results were closer to empirical data when GxE interactions were considered. Finally, the implementation of GxE interactions modified the outcome of management strategies, and their efficiency when combined with temperature variations. Consequently, diversity management should always be context-specific and account for such mechanisms, as well as, their underlying ecological factors. Indeed, knowledge about EEMs could help in building more efficient and evolutionary-inspired management strategies. The MEDITERRANEA model, hence, represents an innovative decision-making tool for managers. For instance, it will allow to simultaneously address several objectives, such as conserving native diversity in some areas and maximizing evolutionary potential as a whole in others, in order to cope with future environmental variations. [SOURCE: Page_7]

RESUME Les patrons de diversité actuels résultent souvent d'une évolution allopatrique, menée par des processus adaptatifs ou non adaptatifs. Le mélange de pools génétiques précédemment isolés, dû à des actions anthropogéniques, mène souvent à des flux de gènes et à de l'hybridation. La gestion de la diversité intraspécifique est donc un enjeu majeur, dans la mesure où la diversité représente un mécanisme de résilience pour les populations, mais aussi car elle recèle des valeurs économiques et patrimoniales. Un exemple typique est le mélange des lignées atlantique (ATL) et méditerranéenne (MED) de la truite commune (Salmo trutta). En zone Méditerranéenne, un siècle de repeuplement intensif à partir de la lignée ATL a entraîné une introgression variable des populations MED par des gènes ATL, allant de la résilience des allèles MED à leur disparition, en passant par une hybridation complète. Les pratiques de gestion historiques visant à restaurer les populations MED n'ont pas atteint leurs objectifs de conservation car les mécanismes éco-évolutifs (MEEs) à l'origine de l'hybridation n'ont pas été pris en compte. En effet, des études empiriques ont mis en évidence deux moteurs potentiels de l'hybridation : une préférence hétérogame des femelles et une survie embryo-larvaire différentielle basée sur des interactions génotype x environnement (GxE) – impliquant le génotype maternel et la température de l’eau. [SOURCE: Page_8]

Ma thèse a pour but d’étudier comment ces MEEs interagissent avec d'autres facteurs écologiques, génétiques et démographiques pour influencer l'évolution de la diversité intraspécifique, dans un cadre spatio-temporel réaliste. Pour ce faire, j'ai rassemblé toutes les connaissances disponibles, tant écologiques qu'évolutives, pour créer un modèle démo-génétique individu-centré (DG-ABM) : MEDITERRANEA. Ce DG-ABM m'a permis de considérer (i) les MEEs de l'hybridation, (ii) leurs bases génétiques, (iii) des variations de paramètres écologiques, ainsi que (iv) des pratiques de gestion. In fine, mon objectif était de répondre à une question : les mécanismes éco-évolutifs sont-ils vraiment importants pour la gestion de la diversité intraspécifique ? Pour faire simple, dans le cas présent, oui. [SOURCE: Page_8]

En me basant sur de multiples cas d'étude (réels et fictifs), j'ai comparé différents scénarios, incluant ou non les MEEs, et considérant des stratégies de gestion alternatives. La préférence hétérogame influence à peine l'évolution de la diversité. A l'inverse, les interactions GxE jouent un rôle structurant dans l'évolution de la diversité. Dans les environnements froids, la mise en œuvre de ce MEE a conduit à une sélection contre les allèles ATL hérités maternellement. La force de cette sélection change principalement avec des variations de température, ce qui souligne l'importance des conditions locales. En outre, les résultats simulés sont plus proches des données empiriques lorsque les interactions GxE sont prises en compte. Enfin, la mise en œuvre des interactions GxE modifie le résultat des stratégies de gestion, ainsi que leur efficacité lorsqu'elles sont combinées à des variations de température. Par conséquent, la gestion de la diversité devrait toujours être spécifique à chaque contexte et tenir compte de ces mécanismes, ainsi que leurs facteurs écologiques sous-jacents. En effet, des connaissances sur les MEEs structurant la diversité pourraient permettre la construction de stratégies de gestion plus efficaces et inspirées par l'évolution. Le modèle MEDITERRANEA représente donc un outil innovant de soutien à la prise de décision pour les gestionnaires. Par exemple, il sera possible de répondre simultanément à plusieurs objectifs de gestion, tels que la conservation de la diversité native dans certaines zones, mais aussi la maximisation du potentiel évolutif dans son ensemble dans d'autres zones, afin de faire face aux variations environnementales futures. [SOURCE: Page_8]

## 1.1 BIOLOGICAL DIVERSITY AND ITS IMPORTANCE

Since ancient times, human populations have been aware of the natural diversity surrounding them [SOURCE: Page_13]

(Aristotle, 350 BC. J.-C.). Over time, they have been using, interacting with and benefiting from this [SOURCE: Page_13]

diversity in many ways. Nowadays, the values of natural diversity to human society are enormous in terms [SOURCE: Page_13]

of commercial (consumption and production) and ecological (ecosystem services) values, but also in terms [SOURCE: Page_13]

of aesthetic, social, cultural, ethical and scientific values. The concept of biodiversity – for biological [SOURCE: Page_13]

diversity – was only introduced towards the end of the 20th century, when the realisation was made that [SOURCE: Page_13]

a part of this diversity was endangered (Maris, 2016). The term has now achieved widespread use and [SOURCE: Page_13]

encompasses a large spectrum of concepts, including various scales of biological diversity. [SOURCE: Page_13]

Biodiversity represents the diversity of life on earth including genetic diversity, species diversity and [SOURCE: Page_13]

ecosystems diversity (Convention on biological diversity, 1992; Swingland, 2001). Ecological diversity is the [SOURCE: Page_13]

diversity arising from different ecosystems (ex: forest vs ocean). It is also termed ecosystem diversity and [SOURCE: Page_13]

it includes all the species, their interactions as well as the physical environment of a given ecosystem. [SOURCE: Page_13]

Species diversity also referred to as interspecific diversity, represents the different species found in a given [SOURCE: Page_13]

area. It is the most common type of diversity associated with the term “biodiversity”. Finally, genetic [SOURCE: Page_13]

diversity refers to the genetic variations – alleles or genes – arising within or among populations of a [SOURCE: Page_13]

particular species. It can lead to apparent morphological or functional diversity, making individuals of the [SOURCE: Page_13]

same species different. The term intraspecific diversity describes all forms of diversity arising within a [SOURCE: Page_13]

single species. [SOURCE: Page_13]

In the context of the biodiversity crisis, although a lot of attention has been devoted to interspecific [SOURCE: Page_13]

diversity – i.e. variations between species – and its importance for ecological functioning – community [SOURCE: Page_13]

2 [SOURCE: Page_13]

1.1 Biological diversity and its importance [SOURCE: Page_14]

and ecosystems (Downing & Leibold, 2002; Hillebrand & Matthiessen, 2009), intraspecific diversity has [SOURCE: Page_14]

been somewhat overlooked (Hoban et al., 2013; Laikre et al., 2010, 2020) despite its ecological and [SOURCE: Page_14]

evolutionary importance (A. Caballero & García-Dorado, 2013; Hughes et al., 2008). Recently, the loss of [SOURCE: Page_14]

intraspecific diversity has been referred to as a “hidden biodiversity crisis” (Des Roches et al., 2021) as its [SOURCE: Page_14]

rate is superior to that of species loss (Mimura et al., 2017). However, it is now receiving more and more [SOURCE: Page_14]

attention. [SOURCE: Page_14]

1.1.1    Intraspecific diversity [SOURCE: Page_14]

Intraspecific diversity is a key facet of biodiversity, accounting for a large part of the diversity measured [SOURCE: Page_14]

in plants and animals (de Bello et al., 2011; Fridley & Grime, 2010; Siefert et al., 2015). Intraspecific [SOURCE: Page_14]

diversity represents all the variations observed within and among populations from the same species, [SOURCE: Page_14]

including genetic, phenotypic (Schluter, 2000), and epigenetic variations (Rey et al., 2020). Genetic [SOURCE: Page_14]

variations can involve both neutral and functional loci (Mimura et al., 2017; Violle et al., 2007) and are [SOURCE: Page_14]

central to evolutionary processes, as it is both the material for and the product of evolutionary processes, [SOURCE: Page_14]

such as natural selection. Combined with environmental and epigenetic effects, functional genetic [SOURCE: Page_14]

variations will result in various types of phenotypic variations, such as morphological, physiological, [SOURCE: Page_14]

behavioural or functional variations – in the sense of an individual function in a population or ecosystem [SOURCE: Page_14]

(Figure 1 – 1). Functional genetic variations will affect the performance of individuals and may influence [SOURCE: Page_14]

population dynamics, species interactions as well as ecosystem functioning (Violle et al., 2007). [SOURCE: Page_14]

Phenotypic variations can therefore drive ecological dynamics, which in turn can change the available [SOURCE: Page_14]

genetic variations, by influencing the transmission to the next generation (Figure 1 – 1). Conversaly, [SOURCE: Page_14]

neutral genetic variations will not have any direct consequences on ecological dynamics. It is often used [SOURCE: Page_14]

as a proxy to differentiate individuals belonging to different lineages for example, or to inform on some [SOURCE: Page_14]

3 [SOURCE: Page_14]

Chapter 1. Introduction [SOURCE: Page_15]

important population parameters, such as effective population size (Ne) or gene flow (Mimura et al., [SOURCE: Page_15]

2017). [SOURCE: Page_15]

Figure 1—1: The different types of intraspecific diversity - including genetic and phenotypic variations - and their links through ecological and evolutionary dynamics. [SOURCE: Page_15]

It is therefore through changes in intraspecific diversity that an ecological process can lead to an [SOURCE: Page_15]

evolutionary outcome and vice-versa. This puts intraspecific diversity at the core of the fields of ecology [SOURCE: Page_15]

– which aims at understanding interactions between individuals and their environments – and evolution [SOURCE: Page_15]

– which studies the changes in genetic variations, i.e. the distribution of alleles over time (Figure 1 – 2). [SOURCE: Page_15]

4 [SOURCE: Page_15]

1.1 Biological diversity and its importance [SOURCE: Page_16]

1.1.2   Ecological and evolutionary importance of intraspecific diversity [SOURCE: Page_16]

The realisation that ecological and evolutionary responses of populations can occur on similar temporal [SOURCE: Page_16]

scales suggests that intraspecific diversity can have potential consequences on dynamics from genes to [SOURCE: Page_16]

ecosystems (Carroll et al., 2007). When environmental changes occur, it is the first level of diversity to be [SOURCE: Page_16]

altered (Spielman et al., 2004). Many studies now recognise the crucial role of intraspecific variations in [SOURCE: Page_16]

species survival and adaptation and in communities and ecosystems resilience, notably under increasing [SOURCE: Page_16]

threats imposed by human activities (Mimura et al., 2017). Indeed, genetic diversity within and among [SOURCE: Page_16]

populations increases effective population size and thereby decreases the risks of inbreeding depression [SOURCE: Page_16]

(Hoffman et al., 2014) and genetic drift (Charlesworth, 2009; J. Wang et al., 2016). When facing [SOURCE: Page_16]

environmental changes, it increases the evolutionary and adaptive potential of populations (Merilä & [SOURCE: Page_16]

Hendry, 2014) and hence temporal persistence and demographic stability through Portfolio effects (J. W. [SOURCE: Page_16]

Moore et al., 2014; Schindler et al., 2010, 2015). Moreover, intraspecific diversity may influence ecological [SOURCE: Page_16]

dynamics at higher levels than the population level, affecting the dynamics of communities and ecosystem [SOURCE: Page_16]

functioning (Blanchet et al., 2020; Hughes et al., 2008; Raffard et al., 2021; Violle et al., 2012). For example, [SOURCE: Page_16]

Crutsinger, (2016) reviewed how increasing genetic variations help in increasing ecosystem productivity [SOURCE: Page_16]

and species diversity, as well as community interactions; resulting in overall ecological stability (Genung [SOURCE: Page_16]

et al., 2010). [SOURCE: Page_16]

Figure 1—2: Intraspecific diversity is essential to the fields of ecology and evolution. [SOURCE: Page_16]

5 [SOURCE: Page_16]

## 1.2 ECO-EVOLUTIONARY MECHANISMS SHAPING BIODIVERSITY

From 1831 to 1836, during his voyage aboard the Beagle, Darwin observed distinct patterns of biological [SOURCE: Page_17]

diversity that could arise among closely related species but also within species. From his observations, [SOURCE: Page_17]

variations in biological diversity could arise in space (“species vary globally” and “species vary locally”) and [SOURCE: Page_17]

in time (“species vary over time”). For example, on the Galapagos Islands, he observed several types of [SOURCE: Page_17]

finches with beaks of different sizes related to food availability, which resembled a South American finch [SOURCE: Page_17]

species. He began to wonder whether these different yet similar finch species could have evolved from [SOURCE: Page_17]

the same South American finch species and concluded that natural processes influenced by ecological [SOURCE: Page_17]

variations could lead to changes within, and ultimately between species (Darwin, 1859). [SOURCE: Page_17]

Biodiversity stems from the interplay and feedback between ecological and evolutionary mechanisms. On [SOURCE: Page_17]

the one hand, evolutionary processes create intraspecific diversity within and among populations through [SOURCE: Page_17]

four main eco-evolutionary mechanisms (EEMs): mutation, selection, genetic drift and gene flow. On the [SOURCE: Page_17]

other hand, ecological processes determine the role, strength and direction of such evolutionary [SOURCE: Page_17]

processes, thereby also influencing intraspecific diversity (Figure 1 – 3). Recently, it has been more and [SOURCE: Page_17]

more acknowledged that ecological and evolutionary changes may be commensurate in time and interact [SOURCE: Page_17]

in a feedback loop (Carroll et al., 2007; N. G. J. Hairston et al., 2005; Pelletier et al., 2009; Reznick, Losos, [SOURCE: Page_17]

et al., 2019; Reznick, Bassar, et al., 2019). For example, inter-individual interactions, such as mating or [SOURCE: Page_17]

competition, within populations are one of the key drivers of selection, as they directly affect individual [SOURCE: Page_17]

fitness at the core of any evolutionary dynamics (Maynard Smith, 1974; Webber & Vander Wal, 2018). As [SOURCE: Page_17]

a result, they can drive evolution and adaptation, which in turn can influence the composition of the [SOURCE: Page_17]

population, which then can feedback to modify interactions and underlying ecological processes (Haloin [SOURCE: Page_17]

& Strauss, 2008; Mimura et al., 2017). The term “eco-evolutionary dynamics” (Hendry, 2017) has been [SOURCE: Page_17]

widely used to explicitly represent these feedback loops between responses at different levels, from genes [SOURCE: Page_17]

6 [SOURCE: Page_17]

1.2 Eco-evolutionary mechanisms shaping biodiversity [SOURCE: Page_18]

to ecosystem, at contemporary time scales (Govaert et al., 2019; Hendry, 2017; Pelletier et al., 2009). [SOURCE: Page_18]

These feedback loops acknowledge that (1) genetic diversity and its architecture determine the [SOURCE: Page_18]

demographic structure and population dynamics through phenotypic expression, (2) demographic [SOURCE: Page_18]

structure and population dynamics determine EEMs, i.e., genetic drift, selection and gene flow, which in [SOURCE: Page_18]

turn (3) determine genetic diversity (Figure 1 – 3). [SOURCE: Page_18]

Figure 1—3: Representation of the eco-evolutionary dynamics and the feedback loop between evolutionary and population dynamics. [SOURCE: Page_18]

1.2.1    Description of the eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_18]

Mutations.       Mutations are changes in the DNA sequence of genes. Although mutation rate for most [SOURCE: Page_18]

organisms is low, it is the ultimate source of genetic variations – creation of new alleles in a population [SOURCE: Page_18]

(Figure 1 – 4). The effect of mutations on evolution is usually not large, except when other EEMs, such as [SOURCE: Page_18]

natural selection, come into play. In regards to selection, mutations can be neutral, deleterious or [SOURCE: Page_18]

advantageous – i.e. having respectively no effect, a negative or a positive effect on individual fitness. While [SOURCE: Page_18]

neutral mutations will not influence evolution directly, deleterious or advantageous mutations will lead [SOURCE: Page_18]

7 [SOURCE: Page_18]

Chapter 1. Introduction [SOURCE: Page_19]

to changes in allele frequencies. Deleterious mutations are usually selected against and purged from the [SOURCE: Page_19]

population but can be found in low frequencies – equal to that of mutation rate. Advantageous mutations [SOURCE: Page_19]

are selected for and spread through the population, potentially until fixation when selective pressures are [SOURCE: Page_19]

high enough or continuous in time. [SOURCE: Page_19]

Figure 1—4: Occurrence of a random mutation, which involves the creation of a new allele “A” in a population exclusively made of “a”. When this allele is passed on to the next generation, it will lead to the creation of a new genotype “Aa” in the population and potentially a new phenotype – here “Aa” genotype lead to a blue phenotype whereas the population was only made of yellow phenotypes. [SOURCE: Page_19]

Gene flow.      Gene flow represents another important evolutionary force affecting diversity [SOURCE: Page_19]

(Seehausen, 2004). The movement of individuals – emigration or immigration – or their gametes – [SOURCE: Page_19]

dispersal for plants – between populations can participate in the increase or decrease of diversity through [SOURCE: Page_19]

various mechanisms, as it involves the movement of certain alleles into or out of a population (Figure 1 – [SOURCE: Page_19]

5). For example, the immigration of new genetic diversity in a population can help increase diversity via [SOURCE: Page_19]

genetic rescue (Frankham, 2015; Whiteley et al., 2015). In other cases, gene flow can also lead to the loss [SOURCE: Page_19]

of intraspecific diversity through outbreeding depression (Templeton et al., 1986) and maladaptation [SOURCE: Page_19]

(Lenormand, 2002). [SOURCE: Page_19]

8 [SOURCE: Page_19]

1.2 Eco-evolutionary mechanisms shaping biodiversity [SOURCE: Page_20]

Figure 1—5: Two genetically – “aa” and “AA” genotypes – and phenotypically – yellow and blue phenotypes – distinct fish populations are located respectively upstream and downstream of a waterfall. The movement of some individuals from the upstream to the downstream population brings in a new genotype and phenotype in the downstream population. Successful reproduction of this new phenotype will result in effective gene flow, bringing a new allele and potentially new genotypes, “aa” and “Aa”, into the downstream population. [SOURCE: Page_20]

Genetic drift.          Genetic drift (Boecklen, 1986) is a random process that always happens in non- [SOURCE: Page_20]

infinite population size. It involves changes in allele frequency due to chance events – such as random [SOURCE: Page_20]

mortality or reproduction that will lead to the random transmission of certain alleles and not others to [SOURCE: Page_20]

the next generation (Figure 1 – 6). This mechanism can have a strong impact on small populations, as it [SOURCE: Page_20]

can cause the random fixation of alleles, thereby reducing available genetic diversity (Whitlock, 2000). [SOURCE: Page_20]

9 [SOURCE: Page_20]

Chapter 1. Introduction [SOURCE: Page_21]

Populations subject to the effects of genetic drift might be less adaptive and at risk of extinction (Vilas et [SOURCE: Page_21]

al., 2006; Whitlock, 2000). [SOURCE: Page_21]

Figure 1—6: Genetic drift is a common process occurring in all non-infinite populations, which leads to the random reproduction and survival of some individuals and can cause changes in the genetic composition of the population. In this example, the initial population is made of an equal proportion of “AA” and “aa” genotypes. Following genetic drift, only a few “aa” individuals managed to survive and reproduce, which caused the loss of the “A” allele, changing the genotypic composition of the next generation – only composed of “aa” genotypes and hence yellow phenotypes. [SOURCE: Page_21]

Natural selection.       Natural selection proposed by Charles Darwin is a mechanism for evolution, [SOURCE: Page_21]

caused by the inheritance of traits and the competition among different individuals. While intraspecific [SOURCE: Page_21]

variations among individuals can give some individuals a fitness advantage – to survive and/or reproduce; [SOURCE: Page_21]

due to inheritance, advantageous variations will be more likely to be transmitted to the next generation [SOURCE: Page_21]

(Figure 1 – 7). As long as this variation remains advantageous, it will increase in frequency in the [SOURCE: Page_21]

population, leading to genetic changes and hence evolution. Natural selection is the process through [SOURCE: Page_21]

which adaptation occurs. This process is highly dependent upon ecological variations, as selection can [SOURCE: Page_21]

differ among environments. Local adaptation (G. C. Williams, 1966) can happen when the forces of natural [SOURCE: Page_21]

selection vary in space – divergent selection, resulting in potential genotype-by-environment – later on [SOURCE: Page_21]

10 [SOURCE: Page_21]

1.2 Eco-evolutionary mechanisms shaping biodiversity [SOURCE: Page_22]

referred to as GxE – interactions (Kawecki & Ebert, 2004). Natural selection drives phenotypic response [SOURCE: Page_22]

to changing environmental conditions. [SOURCE: Page_22]

Figure 1—7: In the environment A, the allele “A” confers a fitness advantage to blue individuals. Through natural selection, blue individuals pass on more alleles in that environment. In the next generation, this leads to an increase in the percentage of blue phenotypes and a decrease in that of yellow ones. In this example, as “Aa” genotypes also codes for blue phenotypes through dominance effects, the allele “a” remains segregated in the population and some “aa” individuals may still be found. [SOURCE: Page_22]

Sexual selection.        Due to the mechanisms of sexual selection – male competition and female [SOURCE: Page_22]

preference – non-random mating may occur based on individual genotypes or phenotypes. For example, [SOURCE: Page_22]

preference might be directed toward either similar or dissimilar mates (Figure 1 – 8). While these [SOURCE: Page_22]

differences in mating interactions might not always directly change allele frequencies in the population, [SOURCE: Page_22]

it often changes phenotypes and genotypes frequencies, and hence the composition of the population, [SOURCE: Page_22]

which in turn can further affect mating interactions. Like natural selection, it represents an important [SOURCE: Page_22]

EEM, as it directly affects individual fitness, thereby driving evolution. [SOURCE: Page_22]

11 [SOURCE: Page_22]

Chapter 1. Introduction [SOURCE: Page_23]

Figure 1—8: In a population composed of different phenotypes, sexual selection mechanisms – male-male competition and female preference – may result in non-random mating. For example, when two phenotypes are available – blue and yellow – the outcome of homogamous versus heterogamous mating will be different. Strictly homogamous mating will lead to the creation of parental genotypes only – “aa” and “AA”, while heterogamous mating will result in the creation of more genotypes – parental one “aa” and “AA” but also a new one “Aa” – similarly to random mating, but in a different frequency. [SOURCE: Page_23]

1.2.2   Creating patterns of diversity: from intra to interspecific diversity [SOURCE: Page_23]

Contemporary patterns of diversity are often the result of divergent evolution (Harrison, 2012), driven by [SOURCE: Page_23]

either adaptive or non-adaptive processes (Hendry & Gonzalez, 2008; Mayr, 1970). The interplay between [SOURCE: Page_23]

evolutionary mechanisms and ecological processes (EEMs) creates and shapes patterns of diversity [SOURCE: Page_23]

through space and time. Understanding these EEMs and how they lead to various patterns of biodiversity [SOURCE: Page_23]

is crucial for ecological, evolutionary and conservation sciences (Blanchet et al., 2020; Chave, 2013). At [SOURCE: Page_23]

the intraspecific level and for complexes of species, the resulting patterns of diversity at large scales [SOURCE: Page_23]

underline the strength and interplay of these EEMs to drive divergent evolution. [SOURCE: Page_23]

12 [SOURCE: Page_23]

1.2 Eco-evolutionary mechanisms shaping biodiversity [SOURCE: Page_24]

Gene pool divergence.            Divergence usually follows a separation in the initial gene pool (Figure 1 [SOURCE: Page_24]

– 9), which can result from the existence of a physical barrier (allopatric evolution), the colonisation of a [SOURCE: Page_24]

new niche (peri/parapatric evolution) or the rise of genetic polymorphism (sympatric evolution) within a [SOURCE: Page_24]

population. This separation is usually associated with limited gene flow and can lead to two segregated [SOURCE: Page_24]

gene pools, which will go through their own eco-evolutionary dynamics independently as described in [SOURCE: Page_24]

Figure 1 – 3. This may lead to further divergence, as all EEMs will interplay to shape – increasing or [SOURCE: Page_24]

decreasing – intraspecific diversity within each gene pool, but also among gene pools – i.e. increasing or [SOURCE: Page_24]

decreasing divergence. For example, gene flow between differentiated gene pools will generally lead to a [SOURCE: Page_24]

decrease in genetic variation among gene pools and an increase within gene pools (Garant et al., 2007). [SOURCE: Page_24]

The accumulation of genetic differences among gene pools often results in the evolution of reproductive [SOURCE: Page_24]

barriers, causing partial, sometimes even complete, reproductive isolation between gene pools and [SOURCE: Page_24]

eventually leading to speciation (Mallet, 2007). By reducing divergence, gene flow generally retards [SOURCE: Page_24]

speciation, as it may break the linkage between genes for reproductive isolation and those for adaptation [SOURCE: Page_24]

(Coyne & Orr, 2004). [SOURCE: Page_24]

Scales of divergence.            The process of divergence between gene pools can happen over [SOURCE: Page_24]

relatively short and long time scales (Carroll et al., 2007), and be observed over macro and [SOURCE: Page_24]

microgeographic scales (there is recent and mounting evidence for microgeographic adaptation, reviewed [SOURCE: Page_24]

in Richardson et al., 2014). It can lead populations down the path to speciation, on a time scale of century [SOURCE: Page_24]

to decades when natural selection is involved. Indeed, strong selection can cause substantial [SOURCE: Page_24]

differentiation among gene pools on observable time scales, in some instances representing the early [SOURCE: Page_24]

stages of speciation (Hendry et al., 2000, 2007; M. K. Schwartz et al., 2004). [SOURCE: Page_24]

13 [SOURCE: Page_24]

Chapter 1. Introduction [SOURCE: Page_25]

Figure 1—9: Gene pool divergence, driven by adaptive and non-adaptive processes, from intra to interspecific diversity.1 [SOURCE: Page_25]

1 In the present PhD, I will particularly target a situation where divergence has previously occurred through long period of geographic vicariance, generating incomplete reproductive isolation between gene pools. [SOURCE: Page_25]

14 [SOURCE: Page_25]

1.2 Eco-evolutionary mechanisms shaping biodiversity [SOURCE: Page_26]

Patterns of diversity.            Patterns of intraspecific diversity generated by isolation by distance are [SOURCE: Page_26]

among the most commonly reported spatial patterns of diversity (Sexton et al., 2014; Wright, 1943), but [SOURCE: Page_26]

patterns of isolation by adaptation have also been described (Nosil, 2009; Sexton et al., 2014). Following [SOURCE: Page_26]

new niche colonisation, patterns of diversity usually involve a decrease in genetic diversity at boundary [SOURCE: Page_26]

ranges and along colonisation routes (Eckert et al., 2008; Kirkpatrick & Barton, 1997; Liggins et al., 2015). [SOURCE: Page_26]

Hybridization.            Isolated gene pools may eventually come back into secondary contact naturally [SOURCE: Page_26]

and lead to a mixed population. This is known as natural hybridization (Genovart, 2009). Hybridization is [SOURCE: Page_26]

defined as the interbreeding of individuals originating from distinct populations or gene pools (Allendorf [SOURCE: Page_26]

et al., 2001), may it be intra or interspecific. Natural hybridization mainly happens between otherwise [SOURCE: Page_26]

allopatric taxa, which have been genetically isolated but not completely reproductively isolated [SOURCE: Page_26]

(Genovart, 2009). Natural hybridization is a common evolutionary process in plants and animals (Barton, [SOURCE: Page_26]

2001; Grabenstein & Taylor, 2018; Olden et al., 2004; Twyford & Ennos, 2012), which may play an [SOURCE: Page_26]

important role in shaping their evolutionary trajectory (Rieseberg et al., 2003; Seehausen, 2004). Natural [SOURCE: Page_26]

interspecific hybridization involves at least, respectively 25% and 10% of recent plant and animal species [SOURCE: Page_26]

respectively (Mallet, 2005). Hybridization can have various impacts on diversity (Epifanio & Nielsen, 2001; [SOURCE: Page_26]

Genovart, 2009; Todesco et al., 2016) and can potentially lead to a number of different situations (Figure [SOURCE: Page_26]

1 – 10): [SOURCE: Page_26]

 A. The maintenance of both parental gene pools (Situation A) without any hybrids. This may [SOURCE: Page_26]

happen when natural selection is high and gene flow is low. [SOURCE: Page_26]

 B. The maintenance of only one parental gene pool and the disappearance of the other one [SOURCE: Page_26]

(Situation B) that could result from natural selection mechanisms and be environment dependent [SOURCE: Page_26]

– one gene pool might be at an advantage in one environment compared to the other. [SOURCE: Page_26]

15 [SOURCE: Page_26]

Chapter 1. Introduction [SOURCE: Page_27]

 C. The disappearance of both parental gene pools resulting from a high level of genetic exchange [SOURCE: Page_27]

between gene pools that will reduce divergence and ultimately lead to a fully hybridized gene [SOURCE: Page_27]

pool (Situation C). This may occur when gene flow is high, and none or very few natural selection [SOURCE: Page_27]

forces are at work. It can result in the appearance of a new species through hybrid speciation [SOURCE: Page_27]

(Mallet, 2007). [SOURCE: Page_27]

 D. The maintenance of all diversity, with the establishment of a stable hybrid zone while the [SOURCE: Page_27]

integrity of each parental gene pool is still maintained (Situation D). For example, each gene pool [SOURCE: Page_27]

might be maintained in their favourable environment with hybrids found in intermediate [SOURCE: Page_27]

environments or at boundaries. [SOURCE: Page_27]

In some cases, hybridization can be seen as problematic as it may cause populations to fully homogenise [SOURCE: Page_27]

and lead to the extinction of some populations (Rhymer & Simberloff, 1996; Todesco et al., 2016), thereby [SOURCE: Page_27]

decreasing diversity and potentially adaptive divergence (Garant et al., 2007). Conversely, hybridization [SOURCE: Page_27]

may increase biodiversity by allowing for the creation of new gene complexes or species (E. Anderson & [SOURCE: Page_27]

Stebbins, 1954; Grant & Grant, 2002; Seehausen, 2004). With the escalating effects of global warming on [SOURCE: Page_27]

biodiversity, it may represent a resilience mechanism against climate change (Chan et al., 2019), as it may [SOURCE: Page_27]

increase adaptive potential (Garant et al., 2007). [SOURCE: Page_27]

Hybridization can have a dual impact on gene pools, as it may promote further evolutionary divergence, [SOURCE: Page_27]

through reinforcement for example (Hoskin et al., 2005; Servedio, 2004) but also prevent it (Coyne & Orr, [SOURCE: Page_27]

2004; Mayr, 1963). The rate and effectiveness of gene flow in interaction with adaptive processes will [SOURCE: Page_27]

control for the integrity of gene pools (Genovart, 2009) and the rate of adaptive divergence (Garant et al., [SOURCE: Page_27]

2007). Hence, the outcome of hybridization will depend on various interacting EEMs, thereby making each [SOURCE: Page_27]

situation unique (Genovart, 2009). The concept of reproductive isolation mechanism is used to describe [SOURCE: Page_27]

all the mechanisms or reproductive barriers that will regulate gene flow between gene pools (Dobzhansky, [SOURCE: Page_27]

1937). [SOURCE: Page_27]

16 [SOURCE: Page_27]

1.2 Eco-evolutionary mechanisms shaping biodiversity [SOURCE: Page_28]

Figure 1—10: During a secondary contact, hybridization may lead to various situations from widespread hybridization to the maintenance of parental populations (“types of hybridization” Allendorf et al. 2001), depending on many ecological factors and related EEMs. [SOURCE: Page_28]

17 [SOURCE: Page_28]

Chapter 1. Introduction [SOURCE: Page_29]

1.2.3    Drivers of hybridization [SOURCE: Page_29]

EEMs play an important and structuring role in the evolution of intraspecific diversity, as they may [SOURCE: Page_29]

enhance gene pool divergence or favour hybridization, through various mechanisms. Indeed, on the one [SOURCE: Page_29]

hand, these EEMs can lead to the evolution of reproductive isolation barriers between populations [SOURCE: Page_29]

(Schluter, 2000). These barriers may limit gene flow, and hence hybridization yet not always fully prevent [SOURCE: Page_29]

it (Bettles et al., 2005; Mallet, 2005). Reproductive isolation barriers can act before or after fertilisation, [SOURCE: Page_29]

at each step of reproduction and development (Coyne & Orr, 2004). It is generally divided into two types [SOURCE: Page_29]

of barriers: pre-zygotic barriers and post-zygotic barriers. On the other hand, the same EEMs can also [SOURCE: Page_29]

enhance hybridization by favouring effective gene flow, through enhanced mating between gene pools [SOURCE: Page_29]

and/or increased hybrid fitness. Finally, it is also possible that through fitness differences, these [SOURCE: Page_29]

mechanisms could favour one gene pool over the other, potentially leading to the extinction of one gene [SOURCE: Page_29]

pool. [SOURCE: Page_29]

Pre-zygotic drivers of hybridization.       Pre-zygotic drivers may prevent/enhance mating or fertilisation [SOURCE: Page_29]

between two populations (Harrison, 2012) by preventing/allowing potential mates from meeting or [SOURCE: Page_29]

successfully mating. [SOURCE: Page_29]

 Ecological isolation.        Hybridization is impossible when two populations are ecologically [SOURCE: Page_29]

segregated (Rundle & Nosil, 2005). Habitat and/or specific biotic interactions – plant-pollinator [SOURCE: Page_29]

interactions – can prevent individuals from meeting and hence hybridizing. For example, [SOURCE: Page_29]

ecological isolation has been playing an important role in assortative mating observed between [SOURCE: Page_29]

two populations of gall wasp that actively select for different species of oak (Egan et al., 2012). It [SOURCE: Page_29]

is important to note that habitat isolation is dictated by an active choice of habitat and not [SOURCE: Page_29]

geographic vicariance. For example, in some populations, hybridization between native steelhead [SOURCE: Page_29]

and coastal cutthroat trout is limited due to the choice of different spawning sites (Buehrens et [SOURCE: Page_29]

18 [SOURCE: Page_29]

1.2 Eco-evolutionary mechanisms shaping biodiversity [SOURCE: Page_30]

al., 2013). However, hybridization occurs to some extent when spawning sites overlap, as hybrid [SOURCE: Page_30]

individuals can in some contexts be found. [SOURCE: Page_30]

 Temporal isolation.     The timing of reproduction also controls hybridization, as two populations [SOURCE: Page_30]

can only mate if their reproductive timing is synchronous. Temporal isolation has been evidenced [SOURCE: Page_30]

in populations of salmonids (Hendry et al., 2004; Limborg et al., 2014; Quinn et al., 2000). For [SOURCE: Page_30]

example, in sockeye salmon, individuals reproducing early are genetically differentiated from [SOURCE: Page_30]

those reproducing late and display different life-history traits (Hendry et al., 2004). In sympatry, [SOURCE: Page_30]

temporal isolation due to shifts in flowering time led to the speciation of two mountain roses [SOURCE: Page_30]

species (Osborne et al., 2020). The mechanisms involved in temporal isolation play an important [SOURCE: Page_30]

role in preventing hybridization when populations share the same ecological niche. Changes in [SOURCE: Page_30]

these mechanisms can promote hybridization. For example, in Australian Banksia, habitat [SOURCE: Page_30]

disturbance led to an increase in the flowering season of both species and hence to the [SOURCE: Page_30]

disappearance of temporal isolation. This resulted in hybridization (Lamont et al., 2003). [SOURCE: Page_30]

 Behavioural isolation.     Behavioural isolation is an important reproductive barrier in animal [SOURCE: Page_30]

species and it often results from sexual selection mechanisms. Every signal involved in sexual [SOURCE: Page_30]

selection might play a role in behavioural isolation, especially between species, as they need to [SOURCE: Page_30]

recognise conspecific. For example, differences in bird songs might lead to premating isolation [SOURCE: Page_30]

between species (Balakrishnan & Sorenson, 2006; Grant & Grant, 1997). Male competition and [SOURCE: Page_30]

female preference can both play an important role in hybridization (Lipshutz, 2018) by either [SOURCE: Page_30]

driving behavioural reproductive isolation (Southcott & Kronforst, 2018; Tinghitella et al., 2018, [SOURCE: Page_30]

2020) or conversaly enhancing hybridization (heterogamous mating: Gil et al., 2015; absence of [SOURCE: Page_30]

discrimination and increased random mating: Aubier et al., 2019; Maroja et al., 2009; Wyman et [SOURCE: Page_30]

19 [SOURCE: Page_30]

Chapter 1. Introduction [SOURCE: Page_31]

al., 2011). Assortative and heterogamous mating have been suggested to play an important role [SOURCE: Page_31]

in the evolution of hybrid zones (Gil et al., 2015; Schumer et al., 2017). Therefore, different [SOURCE: Page_31]

behaviours involved in sexual selection are important to consider while studying hybridization, [SOURCE: Page_31]

and even more so since mating systems can drive evolutionary dynamics (Maan & Seehausen, [SOURCE: Page_31]

2011). [SOURCE: Page_31]

Post-zygotic drivers of hybridization.     Post-zygotic drivers involve hybrid sterility and viability (Harrison, [SOURCE: Page_31]

2012). It is based on the relative fitness of individuals – hybrids compared to parental gene pools. The [SOURCE: Page_31]

consequences of hybridization on individual fitness can be observed directly in the first generation (Araki [SOURCE: Page_31]

et al., 2007) or in further generations when backcrosses are involved. [SOURCE: Page_31]

 Hybrid sterility and non-viability.        Negative epistasis interactions may lead to the non-viability [SOURCE: Page_31]

of F$_1$ hybrids and their sterility. These negative effects will obviously have consequences for [SOURCE: Page_31]

hybrids fitness, but also for the fitness of parental populations, which will be investing in wasteful [SOURCE: Page_31]

reproduction (Allendorf et al., 2001). In some cases, only one gene pool might be negatively [SOURCE: Page_31]

affected, with for example only hybrids resulting from one-way crossing being sterile (Konishi & [SOURCE: Page_31]

Takata, 2004); leading to asymmetric hybridization and potentially the extinction of that gene [SOURCE: Page_31]

pool. [SOURCE: Page_31]

 Outbreeding depression.            Hybrids might be less fit or even non-adapted compared to parental [SOURCE: Page_31]

gene pools. The decrease in fitness for hybrid individuals is often due to the exchange of [SOURCE: Page_31]

maladaptive genes (Garant et al., 2007) and often happens when gene pools are highly genetically [SOURCE: Page_31]

differentiated (Aitken & Whitlock, 2013). For example, the loss of local adaptation has been [SOURCE: Page_31]

documented in cases where non-native and/or domesticated individuals have been introduced in [SOURCE: Page_31]

wild native populations (Verhoeven et al., 2011). Outbreeding depression might always affect F$_1$ [SOURCE: Page_31]

20 [SOURCE: Page_31]

1.2 Eco-evolutionary mechanisms shaping biodiversity [SOURCE: Page_32]

hybrids, but may be detected in F$_2$ or further generations (Goldberg et al., 2005). Genetic [SOURCE: Page_32]

incompatibilities, such as negative epistasis interactions may also cause a form of outbreeding [SOURCE: Page_32]

depression (Turelli & Orr, 2000). [SOURCE: Page_32]

 Heterosis.    In some cases, hybrid fitness might be superior to that of parental gene pools. This [SOURCE: Page_32]

fitness advantage might be associated with an increased growth rate and better survival and/or [SOURCE: Page_32]

fertility compared to parental gene pools (Chen, 2010). It is referred to as heterosis (Allendorf & [SOURCE: Page_32]

Luikart, 2007) – i.e. when heterozygotes have better fitness than homozygotes in a population – [SOURCE: Page_32]

and might be explained by the masking of deleterious alleles. Heterosis might represent a very [SOURCE: Page_32]

important mechanism for very small and potentially inbred populations (Chen, 2010; Lippman & [SOURCE: Page_32]

Zamir, 2007). [SOURCE: Page_32]

 Fitness differences between gene pools.          Fitness differences between gene pools – hybrid [SOURCE: Page_32]

included – might vary in different environments due to natural selection processes. For example, [SOURCE: Page_32]

a gene pool might be fitter than the other only in a specific environment (Folio et al., 2021). In [SOURCE: Page_32]

cases of natural hybridization, each gene pool might show better fitness in its own natural [SOURCE: Page_32]

environment, compared to the other (De La Torre et al., 2014), as they may be locally adapted. [SOURCE: Page_32]

Such fitness differences are likely to lead to different outcomes of hybridization in space and time [SOURCE: Page_32]

based on ecological variations. [SOURCE: Page_32]

Other types of isolation such as gametic incompatibilities (Lobov et al., 2019) or mechanical isolation – [SOURCE: Page_32]

incompatibility of sexual organs (Masly, 2011) – could also prevent hybridization between two [SOURCE: Page_32]

populations. These mechanisms are mainly involved in interspecific hybridization to maintain the integrity [SOURCE: Page_32]

of very divergent taxa. [SOURCE: Page_32]

21 [SOURCE: Page_32]

Chapter 1. Introduction [SOURCE: Page_33]

Overall, natural patterns of diversity are shaped in space and time by various EEMs playing in concert to [SOURCE: Page_33]

drive the fission – divergence – and the fusion – hybridization of gene pools. However, these patterns are [SOURCE: Page_33]

consistently altered by human-induced evolutionary changes (Hendry et al., 2017; Pelletier & Coltman, [SOURCE: Page_33]

2018; Smith & Bernatchez, 2008), which often lead to diversity loss (Ceballos et al., 2017; Mimura et al., [SOURCE: Page_33]

2017; Tilman et al., 2017). [SOURCE: Page_33]

## 1.3 ANTHROPOGENIC IMPACTS ON BIODIVERSITY

Anthropogenic actions are one of the major drivers of environmental changes (Steffen et al., 2011), [SOURCE: Page_33]

imposing strong selective pressures on natural populations. Human-induced selective pressures have [SOURCE: Page_33]

been shown to cause rapid phenotypic changes in populations (Darimont et al., 2009; Hendry et al., 2008; [SOURCE: Page_33]

Sullivan et al., 2017) that would often translate into genetic changes (Allendorf et al., 2008; Coltman et [SOURCE: Page_33]

al., 2003; Uusi-Heikkilä et al., 2015), thereby triggering rapid – contemporary – evolution (Carroll et al., [SOURCE: Page_33]

2007; Schoener, 2011). One of the most famous examples of rapid evolutionary changes that occurred at [SOURCE: Page_33]

the very beginning of industrialisation is the case of the peppered moths (Biston betularia). In England, [SOURCE: Page_33]

before industrialisation, moth populations were mainly composed of a light-coloured morph, better [SOURCE: Page_33]

adapted to the environment, as they would blend in lichen-covered trees. Following industrialisation, [SOURCE: Page_33]

environmental changes caused by coal pollution caused trees to turn dark and led to a rapid change in [SOURCE: Page_33]

moths phenotypes frequency (Tutt, 1896) – the dark morph went from about 0% before 1948 to 98% in [SOURCE: Page_33]

1895 (Kettlewell, 1973). The dark morph that would now camouflage better was favoured compared to [SOURCE: Page_33]

the light morph. This shift resulted from strong selection from predators (L. M. Cook et al., 2012). It [SOURCE: Page_33]

happened around manufacturing areas where pollution was high, creating patterns of local adaptation [SOURCE: Page_33]

across short distances – microgeographic adaptation (Richardson et al., 2014). This example is one among [SOURCE: Page_33]

22 [SOURCE: Page_33]

1.3 Anthropogenic impacts on biodiversity [SOURCE: Page_34]

many (Carroll et al., 2007; Pelletier et al., 2009; Reznick, Losos, et al., 2019) indicating that rapid evolution [SOURCE: Page_34]

is particularly common following changes in selection and population structure following anthropogenic [SOURCE: Page_34]

disturbances. [SOURCE: Page_34]

1.3.1    Main human threats to biodiversity [SOURCE: Page_34]

Nowadays, the main anthropogenic disturbances faced by natural populations are habitat fragmentation [SOURCE: Page_34]

and modification, over-exploitation, pollution, invasive species and climate change (Pelletier & Coltman, [SOURCE: Page_34]

2018). Different taxa are influenced at different rates by each of these disturbances but often, several of [SOURCE: Page_34]

these threats may affect them simultaneously (Pelletier & Coltman, 2018), amplifying their negative [SOURCE: Page_34]

impact on biodiversity (Brook et al., 2008) and thereby affecting natural ecological but also evolutionary [SOURCE: Page_34]

dynamics. For example, not only does overexploitation affect population dynamics, it often also acts as a [SOURCE: Page_34]

selective agent on populations life history traits – such as smaller body size in fish (Heino et al., 2015; [SOURCE: Page_34]

Swain et al., 2007; Uusi-Heikkilä et al., 2015). This is often detrimental to the value of resources (Reznick, [SOURCE: Page_34]

Losos, et al., 2019), thereby imposing a feedback on exploitation itself – for example, optimal fishing [SOURCE: Page_34]

patterns. It is therefore important to understand and evaluate the effect of human-induced changes on [SOURCE: Page_34]

intraspecific diversity (Mimura et al., 2017), for both the persistence of species but also for natural [SOURCE: Page_34]

resources exploitation, management and conservation (Pelletier & Coltman, 2018). [SOURCE: Page_34]

1.3.2    Anthropogenic hybridization [SOURCE: Page_34]

Anthropogenic hybridization is considered a major threat to biodiversity (Crispo et al., 2011; Rhymer & [SOURCE: Page_34]

Simberloff, 1996), and results from the admixture of naturally isolated gene pools through human [SOURCE: Page_34]

intervention. It is caused by a deliberate or accidental introduction of individuals from other taxa – [SOURCE: Page_34]

23 [SOURCE: Page_34]

Chapter 1. Introduction [SOURCE: Page_35]

species, lineages or non-autochthonous populations – that may have been domesticated. A recent review [SOURCE: Page_35]

(Todesco et al., 2016) reported that the most common causes of anthropogenic hybridization were [SOURCE: Page_35]

agriculture (55% of studies), invasive species (54%) and habitat disturbance (36%). Indeed, many [SOURCE: Page_35]

ecological interactions can be observed between wild and domesticated individuals – originating from [SOURCE: Page_35]

agriculture and aquaculture (Ryman et al., 1995; Utter, 2004) – that escaped or were willingly introduced [SOURCE: Page_35]

in natural populations (Drinan et al., 2015; Huvet et al., 2004; Kiang et al., 1979; McGinnity et al., 2003; [SOURCE: Page_35]

Reed, Prodöhl, et al., 2015). In salmonids, for example, repeated introductions of domesticated species, [SOURCE: Page_35]

such as brown trout and rainbow trout (Gozlan et al., 2010) led to extensive hybridization with wild [SOURCE: Page_35]

individuals, at both intra and interspecific levels (Bolstad et al., 2021; Drinan et al., 2015; Hitt et al., 2003; [SOURCE: Page_35]

McGinnity et al., 2003), sometimes threatening natural populations of extinction (McGinnity et al., 2003). [SOURCE: Page_35]

Hybridization between wild and domesticated rice almost led to the extinction of wild rice (Kiang et al., [SOURCE: Page_35]

1979). Overall, hybridization can have various impacts on genetic diversity and population demography [SOURCE: Page_35]

(Epifanio & Nielsen, 2001; Genovart, 2009; Todesco et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_35]

1.3.3    The consequences of hybridization on wild populations [SOURCE: Page_35]

Hybridization can result in both positive and negative effects on genetic diversity of wild populations. In [SOURCE: Page_35]

some cases, hybridization may increase genetic diversity via evolutionary rescue (Stelkens et al., 2014; [SOURCE: Page_35]

Todesco et al., 2016) and allow individuals to adapt to (Rieseberg et al., 2003) and colonise new [SOURCE: Page_35]

environments via adaptive introgression (De La Torre et al., 2014; Whitney et al., 2015). Indeed, [SOURCE: Page_35]

recombination can sometimes combine the most selectively advantageous genes of the parents or create [SOURCE: Page_35]

new advantageous recombinant genotypes (Latta et al., 2007; Lippman & Zamir, 2007; Sain et al., 2002). [SOURCE: Page_35]

However, in most cases, hybridization has negative impacts on native populations demography and [SOURCE: Page_35]

diversity (Rhymer & Simberloff, 1996; Todesco et al., 2016). Indeed, demographic, pathogenic, and [SOURCE: Page_35]

24 [SOURCE: Page_35]

1.3 Anthropogenic impacts on biodiversity [SOURCE: Page_36]

genetic consequences reducing the fitness of native populations are often observed following admixture [SOURCE: Page_36]

(Budy et al., 2013; McGinnity et al., 2003; Závorka et al., 2018 but see Stephens et al., 2020). Hybridization [SOURCE: Page_36]

can cause the loss of unique genotypes or advantageous combinations of genes, potentially leading to the [SOURCE: Page_36]

loss of local adaptation (Verhoeven et al., 2011) and decreasing population adaptive potential (Allendorf [SOURCE: Page_36]

et al., 2001; Bohling, 2016; Garant et al., 2007). For example, hybrid individuals can be more sensitive to [SOURCE: Page_36]

pathogens (Goldberg et al., 2005). In the worst case, extinction by hybridization and introgression can [SOURCE: Page_36]

occur (Rhymer & Simberloff, 1996; Seehausen, 2006), through two main mechanisms related to hybrids [SOURCE: Page_36]

fitness (Allendorf et al., 2001). On the one hand, lower fitness of hybrids individuals can cause [SOURCE: Page_36]

demographic swamping, where the wasteful production of hybrids leads to a decrease in population size, [SOURCE: Page_36]

and ultimately the disappearance of one or both parental populations (Allendorf et al., 2001; Konishi & [SOURCE: Page_36]

Takata, 2004; Wolf et al., 2001). On the other hand, higher fitness of hybrid individuals could lead to [SOURCE: Page_36]

genetic swamping, where the population becomes fully hybridized (“Hybrid swarms” Allendorf et al., [SOURCE: Page_36]

2001; Rhymer et al., 1994; Rhymer & Simberloff, 1996). Nevertheless, in other instances, the genetic [SOURCE: Page_36]

integrity of wild populations might be maintained despite recurrent hybridization, as it is the case in the [SOURCE: Page_36]

context of wolves x dogs hybridization (Pacheco et al., 2017; Pilot et al., 2018). [SOURCE: Page_36]

Anthropogenic hybridization is a conservation issue for managers, it is therefore important to understand [SOURCE: Page_36]

if it poses an actual threat to the viability and genetic integrity of wild populations before engaging in any [SOURCE: Page_36]

actions (Bohling, 2016). To do so, evaluating the type of hybridization occurring (Figure 1 – 10) and its [SOURCE: Page_36]

extent – prevalence of hybrid individuals – in a specific context is fundamental (Allendorf et al., 2001; [SOURCE: Page_36]

Bohling, 2016), as each case of hybridization is unique (Genovart, 2009). The latter also requires an [SOURCE: Page_36]

accurate identification of hybrid individuals, without which estimates might be highly biased. [SOURCE: Page_36]

25 [SOURCE: Page_36]

Chapter 1. Introduction [SOURCE: Page_37]

## 1.4 MANAGING HYBRIDIZATION

When hybridization poses a threat to native diversity (Crispo et al., 2011; Rhymer & Simberloff, 1996; [SOURCE: Page_37]

Seehausen et al., 2008; Todesco et al., 2016), identifying effective management strategies to avoid or [SOURCE: Page_37]

reduce introgression is of paramount importance (Allendorf et al., 2001). Various traditional management [SOURCE: Page_37]

practices are often implemented to avoid introgression and conserve native populations. These include [SOURCE: Page_37]

hybrids and/or non-native individuals’ removal and/or sterilisation, the creation of genetic refuge or the [SOURCE: Page_37]

transfer of native individuals, as well as the management of human disturbance that may affect [SOURCE: Page_37]

hybridization (Allendorf et al., 2001; Bohling, 2016; Gil et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_37]

1.4.1    Traditional assumptions underlying management practices [SOURCE: Page_37]

Commonly applied management practices, such as the implementation of genetic refuge, native [SOURCE: Page_37]

individual translocation or restocking (Caudron et al., 2006, 2011; Caudron & Champigneulle, 2011; [SOURCE: Page_37]

Grobler et al., 2011), or any practices involving admixture, often rely on one assumption: some [SOURCE: Page_37]

mechanisms, such as local adaptation or outbreeding depression, will act as a purifying force that could [SOURCE: Page_37]

favour the conservation of native— supposedly adaptive—genetic variation (Broadhurst et al., 2008; [SOURCE: Page_37]

Hansen et al., 2009; Kronenberger et al., 2018; Ruzzante et al., 2004). However, outbreeding depression [SOURCE: Page_37]

can also lead to demographic swamping (Allendorf et al., 2001; Wolf et al., 2001) and be associated with [SOURCE: Page_37]

the genetic swamping of adapted genes (Allendorf et al., 2001; Rutherford et al., 2019), which could [SOURCE: Page_37]

counter-effect these management practices. An alternative conservation measure is to remove “non- [SOURCE: Page_37]

native individuals” (Bohling, 2016; Caudron & Champigneulle, 2011; Guay et al., 2014; Muñoz-Fuentes et [SOURCE: Page_37]

al., 2007), but this can only be viable when hybridization is restricted in space or not frequent (Genovart, [SOURCE: Page_37]

2009). Finally, when hybridization leads to widespread introgression and, potentially the genetic rescue [SOURCE: Page_37]

of populations (Fitzpatrick et al., 2019), management options to conserve native variation might be limited [SOURCE: Page_37]

26 [SOURCE: Page_37]

1.4 Managing hybridization [SOURCE: Page_38]

(Genovart, 2009). When pure native populations still exist, one solution might be to isolate them to [SOURCE: Page_38]

prevent any further gene flow (Bohling, 2016; Guay et al., 2014). [SOURCE: Page_38]

1.4.2    The importance of considering EEMs driving hybridization [SOURCE: Page_38]

Mounting evidence indicates that these management practices generally fail at conserving native diversity [SOURCE: Page_38]

after admixture had already occurred (Gil et al., 2016; Muñoz-Fuentes et al., 2007; Vincenzi et al., 2012). [SOURCE: Page_38]

This is possibly due to the lack of knowledge and consideration regarding reproductive isolation [SOURCE: Page_38]

mechanisms (Bajec et al., 2015; Berrebi, Poteaux, et al., 2000; Iacolina et al., 2019; Taillebois et al., 2020), [SOURCE: Page_38]

which could help to build more efficient and evolutionary-inspired management strategies. Indeed, [SOURCE: Page_38]

effective management of anthropogenic hybridization requires a better understanding of all the EEMs [SOURCE: Page_38]

influencing the outcomes of hybridization (Allendorf et al., 2001; Hoffmann & Sgrò, 2011; Seehausen et [SOURCE: Page_38]

al., 2008), such as adaptive processes at play and effective gene flow between populations. As Genovart [SOURCE: Page_38]

(2009) stated, “if any management strategies are to be useful, then the original cause of the hybridization [SOURCE: Page_38]

should first be resolved” and “the more we know about the hybridization process and the ecological [SOURCE: Page_38]

factors involved, the more able we will be to analyze each situation” in order to understand the potential [SOURCE: Page_38]

consequences of any management practices on diversity dynamics. Indeed, EEMs, demographic [SOURCE: Page_38]

characteristics, environmental conditions and management practices will collectively influence [SOURCE: Page_38]

hybridization and diversity dynamics. It is therefore important to consider the interactive effects of these [SOURCE: Page_38]

factors on the initiation, persistence, and outcomes of hybridization. [SOURCE: Page_38]

Therefore, the identification and consideration of all EEMs involved in reproductive isolation should be a [SOURCE: Page_38]

prerequisite to any management decisions in order to avoid counter-productive results. Indeed, [SOURCE: Page_38]

considering differential social interactions (e.g. mating preference) and genetic performances (e.g. fitness [SOURCE: Page_38]

27 [SOURCE: Page_38]

Chapter 1. Introduction [SOURCE: Page_39]

differences) between gene pools (Castellani et al., 2015; Nathan et al., 2019) is essential to predict the [SOURCE: Page_39]

evolution of intraspecific diversity. For example, knowledge about the fitness of hybrids individuals [SOURCE: Page_39]

compared to parents could inform on the expected rate of adaptive introgression (Barrett & Schluter, [SOURCE: Page_39]

2008; Hedrick, 2013) and the stability of hybrid zones (Buggs, 2007; Taylor et al., 2015). Additional [SOURCE: Page_39]

information on the ecological factors contributing to such differences could also help in understanding [SOURCE: Page_39]

and predicting the outcomes of hybridization in specific environments. Such knowledge could then be [SOURCE: Page_39]

used to implement new management practices (Epifanio & Nielsen, 2001; Genovart, 2009; Todesco et al., [SOURCE: Page_39]

2016) to either avoid admixture (if applied soon enough), lesser the extent of introgression, and favour [SOURCE: Page_39]

“native” genes conservation when admixture has already occurred (Caudron, Champigneulle, et al., 2012), [SOURCE: Page_39]

or to favour diversity when it enhances the adaptive potential of the population (Chan et al., 2019). [SOURCE: Page_39]

## 1.5 THE RISE OF ECO-EVOLUTIONARY MANAGEMENT IN CONSERVATION SCIENCE

Since they realised the magnitude of their influence on biodiversity loss, human societies have had the [SOURCE: Page_39]

desire to conserve biodiversity (Maris, 2016). The notion of “conservation biology” was introduced in 1985 [SOURCE: Page_39]

by Soulé as a scientific field focusing on the biology and ecology of species impacted directly or indirectly [SOURCE: Page_39]

by anthropic activity. Since recently, management and conservation practices had been traditionally [SOURCE: Page_39]

based on ecological solutions (Reznick, Losos, et al., 2019), as they would happen on what was considered [SOURCE: Page_39]

an ecological time scale. Management was only thought to influence ecological dynamics – such as [SOURCE: Page_39]

population size – whereas evolutionary dynamics were mainly ignored since evolution was seen as a [SOURCE: Page_39]

historical process acting on long-time scales (Carroll et al., 2007). [SOURCE: Page_39]

Mounting evidence has now pushed scientists to acknowledge that evolution is a contemporary process [SOURCE: Page_39]

that happens on a similar time scale than ecological dynamics (Reznick, Losos, et al., 2019) and in a wide [SOURCE: Page_39]

28 [SOURCE: Page_39]

1.5 The rise of eco-evolutionary management in conservation science [SOURCE: Page_40]

diversity of ecological contexts (Carroll et al., 2007). Evolutionary changes have been shown to have [SOURCE: Page_40]

substantial effects on ecological dynamics – sometimes more than changes in important abiotic factors [SOURCE: Page_40]

(N. G. J. Hairston et al., 2005). Ignoring contemporary evolution and its potential feedback loops with [SOURCE: Page_40]

ecological processes will likely imperil the success of any management practices, even more so in the [SOURCE: Page_40]

context of climate change. Indeed, the realisation was made that populations could adapt rapidly to [SOURCE: Page_40]

changing conditions threatening them, especially in response to induced anthropogenic changes (Carroll [SOURCE: Page_40]

et al., 2005; Hendry et al., 2006; K. J. Rice & Emery, 2003; Strauss et al., 2006). [SOURCE: Page_40]

Considering the underlying eco-evolutionary dynamics when planning conservation decisions can change [SOURCE: Page_40]

the recommendations for management actions. For example, Walsworth et al. (2019) showed that ideal [SOURCE: Page_40]

management strategies were different based on the inclusion of adaptive evolution. When adaptive [SOURCE: Page_40]

responses of populations were absent, the ideal management strategy was to focus on thermal refugia, [SOURCE: Page_40]

in order to conserve the ideal habitat of populations. However, when adaptive responses were [SOURCE: Page_40]

considered, the ideal management strategy was the prioritisation of traits and habitat diversity in order [SOURCE: Page_40]

to conserve the evolutionary potential of the population and decrease the risks of trait-habitat [SOURCE: Page_40]

mismatches. Indeed, maximizing beneficial genetic and phenotypic variation should represent a resilience [SOURCE: Page_40]

mechanism against environmental change (C. N. Cook & Sgrò, 2018; Jump et al., 2009; López-Pujol et al., [SOURCE: Page_40]

2012; Violle et al., 2012). Overall, it is important to consider evolutionary processes and the adaptive [SOURCE: Page_40]

potential of populations to determine what will be the conditions maximizing the chances of populations [SOURCE: Page_40]

adapting to changes, in order to provide adequate prescriptions to managers (Carlson et al., 2014; [SOURCE: Page_40]

Whiteley et al., 2015). [SOURCE: Page_40]

The idea of including eco-evolutionary dynamics in the management of intraspecific diversity is part of a [SOURCE: Page_40]

nascent field termed “evolutionary conservation management”. It can be achieved through modelling, [SOURCE: Page_40]

29 [SOURCE: Page_40]

Chapter 1. Introduction [SOURCE: Page_41]

using DG-ABMs (Lamarins, Fririon, et al., 2022). Indeed, by allowing for the consideration of complex [SOURCE: Page_41]

ecological and evolutionary processes (Nathan et al., 2019), as well as management strategies – and their [SOURCE: Page_41]

impact on individuals and their interactions, simulations represent an efficient prospective tool to inform [SOURCE: Page_41]

decision-making (Gervasi & Ciucci, 2018; Walsworth et al., 2019). [SOURCE: Page_41]
