## 1.6 DG-ABMS: A SIMULATION FRAMEWORK TO MODEL ECO-EVOLUTIONARY DYNAMICS$_2$

The consequences of complex eco-evolutionary relationships on the evolution of diversity may be best [SOURCE: Page_41]

explored through simulations using eco-evolutionary models. Indeed, at the very core of these models is [SOURCE: Page_41]

the need of specifying the genetically variable and heritable traits, their impact on the focal organism’s [SOURCE: Page_41]

life history, and the ecological embedding that determines how life-history traits affect and are affected [SOURCE: Page_41]

by environmental conditions and the demographic context (Dieckmann & Ferrière, 2004). This can be [SOURCE: Page_41]

achieved by various modelling approaches, often based on either analytical models or Demo-Genetic [SOURCE: Page_41]

Agent-Based Models (DG-ABMs), which vary in assumptions (Figure 1 – 11). DG-ABMs can be defined as [SOURCE: Page_41]

individual-based (meta) population dynamics models with heritable trait variation and explicit phenotype- [SOURCE: Page_41]

dependent interactions between individuals. [SOURCE: Page_41]

DG-ABMs employ a specific bottom-up approach to fully integrate individual interactions and their [SOURCE: Page_41]

outcome over time and space within a population, the result of which will dictate the strength and [SOURCE: Page_41]

direction of evolutionary processes at the population level (DeAngelis & Mooij, 2005; Huston et al., 1988). [SOURCE: Page_41]

These models acknowledge that individuals by nature have non-uniform responses to their environment. [SOURCE: Page_41]

Accordingly, these approaches explicitly depict the interactions between individuals and their effects on [SOURCE: Page_41]

2 In this section, I extracted parts of a manuscript written with several co-authors aiming at defining the use and potential of DG-ABMs (Lamarins, Fririon, et al. 2022). [SOURCE: Page_41]

30 [SOURCE: Page_41]

1.6 DG-ABMs: a simulation framework to model eco-evolutionary dynamics [SOURCE: Page_42]

individual fitness, accounting for the social context, and observe the resulting dynamics in terms of [SOURCE: Page_42]

distributions of heritable traits and demography (Figure 1 – 12). A key feature of DG-ABMs is that fitness [SOURCE: Page_42]

variation emerges mechanically from interactions between individuals (as opposed to assuming an a priori [SOURCE: Page_42]

fitness function) and gives rise to the evolution of patterns structuring the population diversity and its [SOURCE: Page_42]

dynamics (e.g., genetic architecture, spatial genetic structure). This is radically different from assuming a [SOURCE: Page_42]

prescribed relationship between traits and fitness, as done in analytical models and some ABMs (Cotto et [SOURCE: Page_42]

al., 2020). This bottom-up construction of fitness in DG-ABMs allows for specific approaches to [SOURCE: Page_42]

fundamental and applied questions in ecology and evolution. [SOURCE: Page_42]

Figure 1—11: Different approaches to model eco-evolutionary feedback loops. This scheme summarises the main differences between two major modelling approaches used to investigate eco-evolutionary dynamics: analytical models on the left and Demo-Genetic Agent-Based Models (DG-ABMs) on the right. Their main difference is that analytical models consider evolutionary and/or ecological processes to be homogeneous within groups of individuals (the population or life-stages), whereas DG-ABMs can account for phenotypic and genotypic variation in groups of individuals, its effects on individual decisions/traits linked to local and instant conditions, and their outcome at the group level (i.e., emerging effects). In particular, some (although not all) DG-ABMs explicitly model inter-individual interactions, and their effects on individual fitness, which emerge in part from these interactions (from Lamarins, Fririon, et al. 2022). [SOURCE: Page_42]

31 [SOURCE: Page_42]

Chapter 1. Introduction [SOURCE: Page_43]

32 [SOURCE: Page_43]

1.6 DG-ABMs: a simulation framework to model eco-evolutionary dynamics [SOURCE: Page_44]

Figure 1—12: A conceptual scheme of demo-genetic agent-based models (DG-ABMs). Individuals (or agents) are characterised by their phenotypic traits, determined by their genotype, the environment, and interactions between them (G, E and GE respectively). The agents together define the population, hence determining its diversity and structure, where inter-individual interactions are explicit and shape the social environment. This social environment influences population dynamics, which ultimately drives evolutionary processes (drift, selection, gene flow). Fitness variations (e.g., survival, fecundity variation) emerge from different outcomes of inter-individual interactions (e.g., mating, competition, cooperation, and information exchange) and give rise to trait evolution via the trans-generational response to selection. This framework, highlighting the feedback loop central to eco-evolutionary approaches, is the core part of DG-ABMs and is identified by solid (units) / dashed (units properties) line boxes and bold arrows. DG-ABMs also offer the opportunity to study eco-evolutionary dynamics at multiple levels of organisations and spatiotemporal scales. At the population scale, habitat structuring and variation in the abiotic environment can be included to account for selection, stochastic events and subdivision of the social environment. These models also allow simulations of several populations’ dynamics connected through dispersal with potential gene flow, such as in a meta-population case. At a higher level, community dynamics can be modelled through interspecific interactions between individuals from directly or indirectly interacting species (from Lamarins, Fririon, et al. 2022). [SOURCE: Page_44]

1.6.1    DG-ABMs to better understand eco-evolutionary loops [SOURCE: Page_44]

Explicitly accounting for inter-individual interactions in a bottom-up approach allows DG-ABM to better [SOURCE: Page_44]

investigate the building up of fitness resulting from several complex EEMs and the interactions between [SOURCE: Page_44]

them. Accounting for the stochastic and context-dependent outcome of, for example, competitive or [SOURCE: Page_44]

mating interactions can change the predicted evolution of life-history traits under a prescribed [SOURCE: Page_44]

relationship between trait and fitness. [SOURCE: Page_44]

For instance, Fielding (2004) investigated competitive interactions in grasshoppers using an ABM and [SOURCE: Page_44]

showed that contrasted optimal values of life history traits can emerge from different types of localised [SOURCE: Page_44]

inter-individual interactions, i.e. exploitative or size-based competition. In a perch species, the eco-genetic [SOURCE: Page_44]

33 [SOURCE: Page_44]

Chapter 1. Introduction [SOURCE: Page_45]

model of Ivan & Höök (2015) was able to show variable patterns of energy allocation along individual [SOURCE: Page_45]

ontogeny, resulting from the interplay between plastic and adaptive responses to selection and density- [SOURCE: Page_45]

dependent competition for food. In their DG-ABM of trout population, Ayllón, Railsback, et al. (2018) [SOURCE: Page_45]

observed the emergence of different eco-evolutionary outcomes due to explicit competitive interactions [SOURCE: Page_45]

for food resources in a changing environment. Finally, with a DG-ABM representing indirect competition [SOURCE: Page_45]

among individuals choosing different life-history tactics, Piou & Prévost (2012) showed that climate [SOURCE: Page_45]

change may modify salmon population structure through plastic responses. [SOURCE: Page_45]

Reproductive interactions are also known to drive evolutionary dynamics (Maan & Seehausen, 2011). [SOURCE: Page_45]

Mate choice strongly depends on the population structure, making the outcome challenging to predict [SOURCE: Page_45]

yet rarely random (Klug & Stone, 2021). Explicit representation of sexual interactions is important as [SOURCE: Page_45]

sexual selection can sometimes oppose natural selection (Labonne & Hendry, 2010). DG-ABMs, by [SOURCE: Page_45]

allowing to observe the outcome of inter-individual interactions, are particularly adapted to explore the [SOURCE: Page_45]

evolution of traits considering the dynamic aspects of mating systems such as when sexual preference [SOURCE: Page_45]

and competition over mating partners occur, while still accounting for natural selection (Chevalier et al., [SOURCE: Page_45]

2020). In the same line, DG-ABMs also allow to investigate the origin of sexual dimorphism, such as Höök [SOURCE: Page_45]

et al. (2021) who stressed the evidence of sex-specific plasticity potentials by looking at perch evolutionary [SOURCE: Page_45]

response to the environment. DG-ABMs are also a relevant tool to explore how mating behaviour and [SOURCE: Page_45]

population size jointly affect fitness components or population growth rate through Allee effects. For [SOURCE: Page_45]

instance, (Berec et al., 2018) considered the rate of mate search as evolvable and found different optimal [SOURCE: Page_45]

values of search rates for populations at different densities, resulting in lower Allee thresholds in [SOURCE: Page_45]

populations kept at lower densities. [SOURCE: Page_45]

34 [SOURCE: Page_45]

1.6 DG-ABMs: a simulation framework to model eco-evolutionary dynamics [SOURCE: Page_46]

1.6.2    Extending in space, time and levels of organisation [SOURCE: Page_46]

In most examples detailed above, eco-evolutionary dynamics are modelled within a non-spatially explicit [SOURCE: Page_46]

population. However, the spatial arrangement of habitats shapes animal movements or gametes [SOURCE: Page_46]

propagation and therefore shapes social interactions and sexual networks (He et al., 2019). Since they [SOURCE: Page_46]

allow for fine-scale explicit representation of habitats as well as individual movements, DG-ABMs are well [SOURCE: Page_46]

suited to represent spatial evolutionary dynamics. Using an individual-based model of dispersal in [SOURCE: Page_46]

dendritic networks; Fronhofer & Altermatt (2017) showed eco-evolutionary feedbacks with consequences [SOURCE: Page_46]

on meta-population and genetic structure. Hrycik et al. (2019) explored the importance of environmental [SOURCE: Page_46]

cues in perch vertical movement and illustrated the role of DG-ABMs in determining appropriate [SOURCE: Page_46]

movement rules in spatially explicit ecological modelling. Travis et al. (2010) used a mechanical DG-ABM [SOURCE: Page_46]

approach to model the evolution of seed dispersal in plant populations, accounting for likely trade-offs [SOURCE: Page_46]

between traits in a patchy landscape. Additionally, sexual selection can determine the reproductive [SOURCE: Page_46]

success of immigrants in populations and thus the strength and direction of demo-genetic consequences [SOURCE: Page_46]

of dispersal (e.g., demographic rescue, evolutionary rescue versus gene swamping…). For instance, [SOURCE: Page_46]

Soularue & Kremer (2014) highlighted the major importance of gene flow and assortative mating in [SOURCE: Page_46]

shaping the genetic differentiation between populations in a heterogeneous environment. [SOURCE: Page_46]

At the higher level of organisation (e.g., community level), inter-individual interactions between species [SOURCE: Page_46]

may also ultimately change the evolutionary outcomes expected from single species systems (terHorst et [SOURCE: Page_46]

al., 2018; Weber et al., 2017). DG-ABMs can include multiple species once the interspecific interactions [SOURCE: Page_46]

are established; they are thus especially suited to represent both intra and interspecific complexity and [SOURCE: Page_46]

may help investigate macro-evolutionary consequences of interspecific interactions, such as speciation [SOURCE: Page_46]

(Gavrilets et al., 2007; Weber et al., 2017). [SOURCE: Page_46]

35 [SOURCE: Page_46]

Chapter 1. Introduction [SOURCE: Page_47]

In any case, it appears that DG-ABMs have a large potential to address fundamental eco-evolutionary [SOURCE: Page_47]

questions accounting for multiple drivers of fitness, and are increasingly used in an integrative way, [SOURCE: Page_47]

allowing effects to flow up and down between organisation levels. [SOURCE: Page_47]

1.6.3   DG-ABMs to assist management strategies [SOURCE: Page_47]

Another key feature of DG-ABMs is their capacity to model the effects of human-induced changes (e.g. [SOURCE: Page_47]

selective exploitation, conservation practices) on individuals and their interactions, and investigate the [SOURCE: Page_47]

eco-evolutionary consequences. Hence, by allowing emerging effects, DG-ABMs can also be efficient [SOURCE: Page_47]

prospective tools to elaborate and assess management strategies. When management consists of [SOURCE: Page_47]

demographic control of populations, in particular through individual phenotype-based choices, it can [SOURCE: Page_47]

deeply impact all demographic processes and population genetic composition, and therefore the intensity [SOURCE: Page_47]

and direction of the evolutionary processes (Lefèvre et al., 2014). For example, selective fishing (or [SOURCE: Page_47]

harvesting) directly affects competition among surviving fishes (or trees), while genetic composition [SOURCE: Page_47]

determines optimal fishing (or harvesting) patterns. In particular, different DG-ABMs were used to [SOURCE: Page_47]

understand how selective fisheries can affect the demography and evolution of fish populations (fisheries- [SOURCE: Page_47]

induced evolution), through cascading and sometimes counterintuitive effects on population [SOURCE: Page_47]

demographic structure, growth and maturation thresholds (Ayllón, Railsback, et al., 2018; Piou et al., [SOURCE: Page_47]

2015; H.-Y. Wang et al., 2017; H.-Y. Wang & Höök, 2009). By modelling simultaneously the plastic and [SOURCE: Page_47]

genetic responses of individuals, DG-ABMs also allow to disentangle the role of selective fisheries and [SOURCE: Page_47]

environment in the observed and predicted declining population and in phenotypic changes (Piou et al., [SOURCE: Page_47]

2015). [SOURCE: Page_47]

36 [SOURCE: Page_47]

1.6 DG-ABMs: a simulation framework to model eco-evolutionary dynamics [SOURCE: Page_48]

When evolutionary dynamics and land use planning decisions are linked, DG-ABMs also represent valuable [SOURCE: Page_48]

decision support tools. For example, Papaïx et al. (2018) and Rimbaud et al. (2018) used a spatially explicit [SOURCE: Page_48]

demo-genetic model to assess the joint effect of crop cultivar deployment strategies in space and time [SOURCE: Page_48]

and key pathogen life-history traits on epidemiological dynamics, resistance durability and long-term [SOURCE: Page_48]

evolutionary control. Using a DG-ABM, Mims et al. (2019) found strong effects of spatial connectivity on [SOURCE: Page_48]

demo-genetic outcomes in reintroduced bull trout populations, and allowed identification of watershed [SOURCE: Page_48]

areas with higher persistence probabilities. [SOURCE: Page_48]

In the case of hybridization between native/wild and introduced/domesticated gene pools, DG-ABMs [SOURCE: Page_48]

allow to study the impact of management on the dynamics of crossing within and between gene pools, [SOURCE: Page_48]

which depends on differential social interactions (e.g., mating preference) and genetic performances (e.g., [SOURCE: Page_48]

local adaptation) between gene pools (Castellani et al., 2015; Nathan et al., 2019). In this context, DG- [SOURCE: Page_48]

ABMs offer an efficient approach to develop genetic enrichment strategies in a prospective approach [SOURCE: Page_48]

(which genetic resources and which deployment modalities for which risks?), and conversely to evaluate [SOURCE: Page_48]

strategies to preserve the local gene pool from unwanted introgression. In a similar approach for native [SOURCE: Page_48]

versus transgenic gene pools, Kuparinen & Schurr (2007) and Difazio et al. (2012) studied transgene [SOURCE: Page_48]

dispersal capacity in a forestry landscape by using spatially explicit IBMs with differential demographic [SOURCE: Page_48]

rates for transgenic and natural trees. [SOURCE: Page_48]

In these different case studies, DG-ABMs offer a relevant framework to evaluate the short- and long-term [SOURCE: Page_48]

evolutionary costs and benefits of management actions and to compromise them. For example, it can be [SOURCE: Page_48]

used to deal with questions of exploiting a population or a meta-population (e.g., fishing, wood [SOURCE: Page_48]

production) while preserving its genetic value and diversity or to determine how to minimise the risks of [SOURCE: Page_48]

37 [SOURCE: Page_48]

Chapter 1. Introduction [SOURCE: Page_49]

demo-genetic collapses of populations facing climate change. Furthermore, by controlling the social [SOURCE: Page_49]

context of populations, management drives the overall ecological processes, and thus acts on biotic and [SOURCE: Page_49]

abiotic stressors, as well as on the susceptibility of populations to these stressors, and on selection [SOURCE: Page_49]

intensity (Jactel et al., 2009). [SOURCE: Page_49]

## 1.7 A RELEVANT CASE STUDY IN SALMONIDS

1.7.1    Background on salmonids [SOURCE: Page_49]

Salmonids embody the above-mentioned situation of diversity since they mostly evolved in allopatry [SOURCE: Page_49]

during the Pleistocene glaciations, where geographical reproductive isolation allowed potential divergent [SOURCE: Page_49]

evolution to operate (Bernatchez, 2001). They display a high genetic diversity as well as a large diversity [SOURCE: Page_49]

of life-history traits. For instance, some species are semelparous while others are iteroparous (Stearns & [SOURCE: Page_49]

Hendry, 2003); some might migrate at sea (anadromous) while others remain in freshwater for their entire [SOURCE: Page_49]

life cycle (Alexandrou et al., 2013). This diversification might have resulted from variations in [SOURCE: Page_49]

environmental and physiological factors during their evolutionary history, notably during the glaciation [SOURCE: Page_49]

periods (Macqueen & Johnston, 2014). [SOURCE: Page_49]

Salmonids are the target of a lot of attention throughout the world and represent one of the most [SOURCE: Page_49]

managed fish family (Antunes et al., 1999; Davidson et al., 2010; Fraser, 2008; Laikre et al., 1999). This is [SOURCE: Page_49]

due to their important socio-economic status, notably through aquaculture and recreational fishing [SOURCE: Page_49]

(Davidson et al., 2010; Hindar et al., 2006; Laikre et al., 1999). Many wild populations of salmonids are [SOURCE: Page_49]

threatened by human-induced disturbances, including overharvesting, habitat degradation and [SOURCE: Page_49]

destruction, domestication and various stocking practices often using domesticated individuals (Fraser, [SOURCE: Page_49]

2008; Glover et al., 2017; Harbicht et al., 2014; Skaala et al., 2014; Waples, 1991). [SOURCE: Page_49]

38 [SOURCE: Page_49]

1.7 A relevant case study in salmonids [SOURCE: Page_50]

For instance, each year thousands of domesticated fish escape from fish farms into the wild (Gausen & [SOURCE: Page_50]

Moen, 1991; Morris et al., 2008; Reed, Prodöhl, et al., 2015), and the resulting gene flow often has large [SOURCE: Page_50]

consequences on diversity. Similarly, stocking practices – aimed at increasing population size to sustain [SOURCE: Page_50]

recreational fishing (Champagnon et al., 2012; Laikre et al., 1999; Waples, 1991) – often lead to similar [SOURCE: Page_50]

negative consequences on wild populations (Araki et al., 2007; Araki & Schmid, 2010; Fraser, 2008; [SOURCE: Page_50]

Leitwein et al., 2016; Utter, 2004). As a consequence, hybridization between wild/native and [SOURCE: Page_50]

domesticated/introduced individuals and subsequent introgression represents a huge issue for fisheries [SOURCE: Page_50]

managers (Araki et al., 2007; Araki & Schmid, 2010; Bolstad et al., 2017, 2021; Fraser, 2008; Reed, Prodöhl, [SOURCE: Page_50]

et al., 2015). For example, introgression by domesticated alleles in salmon led to changes in age and size [SOURCE: Page_50]

at maturity (Bolstad et al., 2017), an important life-history trait influencing population dynamics (Ayllón [SOURCE: Page_50]

et al., 2016; Carlson & Seamons, 2008). [SOURCE: Page_50]

Since such gene flow may threaten the genetic integrity of wild/native populations, conservation [SOURCE: Page_50]

programs usually aim at reducing the impact of domesticated and/or introduced fish into wild populations [SOURCE: Page_50]

(Fraser, 2008; Glover et al., 2017; Harbicht et al., 2014; Perrier et al., 2013; Skaala et al., 2014; Waples, [SOURCE: Page_50]

1991). Therefore, many studies have already focused on the genetic consequences of hybridization and [SOURCE: Page_50]

gene flow from domesticated strains on native wild populations (Fernández-Cebrián et al., 2014; Laikre et [SOURCE: Page_50]

al., 2010; Lamaze et al., 2012; McGinnity et al., 2003, 2009; Muhlfeld et al., 2009; L. Sundt-Hansen et al., [SOURCE: Page_50]

2015; Waples, 1991). [SOURCE: Page_50]

The conservation of salmonids' inter and intraspecific diversity has now become a challenge for scientists [SOURCE: Page_50]

and fisheries managers (Waples & Hendry, 2008). For decades, many management practices, such as the [SOURCE: Page_50]

translocation of native individuals or stocking with native fry, have been implemented for that purpose. [SOURCE: Page_50]

However, mounting evidence indicates that management practices usually fail at conserving native [SOURCE: Page_50]

39 [SOURCE: Page_50]

Chapter 1. Introduction [SOURCE: Page_51]

diversity following such gene flow (Gil et al., 2016; Vincenzi et al., 2012) and may even result in larger [SOURCE: Page_51]

genetic consequences (Fernández-Cebrián et al., 2014; Leitwein et al., 2016). Nevertheless, none of them [SOURCE: Page_51]

has so far tried to apply evolutionary enlightened management practices by studying and using the [SOURCE: Page_51]

available knowledge on potential drivers of hybridization. [SOURCE: Page_51]

Furthermore, salmonids have also been shown to be particularly good models to study eco-evolutionary [SOURCE: Page_51]

dynamics in modelling frameworks. For instance, a growing number of DG-ABMs have been developed [SOURCE: Page_51]

for salmonids to study adaptation to climate change, hybridization, but also the impact and efficiency of [SOURCE: Page_51]

management practices (Ayllón et al., 2016, 2021; Ayllón, Grimm, et al., 2018; Ayllón, Nicola, et al., 2019; [SOURCE: Page_51]

Ayllón, Railsback, et al., 2018, 2019; Frank & Baret, 2013; Lamarins, Hugon, et al., 2022; Nathan et al., [SOURCE: Page_51]

2019; Piou & Prévost, 2012; Theriault et al., 2008; H.-Y. Wang & Höök, 2009). [SOURCE: Page_51]

1.7.2   The MED/ATL hybridization complex [SOURCE: Page_51]

Among Salmonids, the brown trout species (Salmo trutta L.) displays a high genetic diversity. The species [SOURCE: Page_51]

holds at least five genetically and phenotypically distinct lineages (Bernatchez, 2001) that evolved in [SOURCE: Page_51]

allopatry for 0.5 to 2 million years during the glacial era, where separated populations colonised different [SOURCE: Page_51]

areas across Europe (J.-L. García-Marín et al., 1999), although hybridization may have occurred at some [SOURCE: Page_51]

points (Hashemzadeh Segherloo et al., 2021). In France, two of those lineages: the Atlantic (ATL) and the [SOURCE: Page_51]

Mediterranean (MED) lineages are naturally found in allopatry. Due to their divergent evolution, they [SOURCE: Page_51]

display an important phenotypic divergence. However, until recently (Gil, 2015), there was no information [SOURCE: Page_51]

regarding the adaptive aspect of this divergence. [SOURCE: Page_51]

40 [SOURCE: Page_51]

1.7 A relevant case study in salmonids [SOURCE: Page_52]

Since the discovery of artificial reproduction at the end of the 19th century, brown trout became the most [SOURCE: Page_52]

introduced fish species in the world (Lowe et al., 2000). As one of the consequences, the distinct lineages [SOURCE: Page_52]

have been forced back in sympatry. The ATL lineage, in particular, has been intensively used for fish [SOURCE: Page_52]

farming and intensive river stocking – using homogeneous domesticated strains – to sustain recreational [SOURCE: Page_52]

fishing for more than a century (Beaudou et al., 1994; Berrebi, Povz, et al., 2000; Caudron & [SOURCE: Page_52]

Champigneulle, 2007; Krieg & Guyomard, 1985; Largiadèr et al., 1996; Launey et al., 2003; Presa et al., [SOURCE: Page_52]

1994). In the Mediterranean area, where the MED lineage has evolved, the resulting situation displays a [SOURCE: Page_52]

mosaic of outcomes regarding intraspecific diversity, with many cases of introgression of native MED [SOURCE: Page_52]

alleles by non-native ATL alleles. The mapping of genetic diversity indicates that introgression is [SOURCE: Page_52]

heterogeneous in space: going from the total extirpation of the MED lineage to various degrees of [SOURCE: Page_52]

hybridization, with some rare patches of pure MED gene pools remaining (Caudron, Champigneulle, et al., [SOURCE: Page_52]

2012). The reason for this spatial heterogeneity and diverse levels of intraspecific diversity cannot be [SOURCE: Page_52]

solely explained by management practices (i.e., intensity of stocking using the ATL lineage, Gil et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_52]

To counter the observed loss of native biodiversity, many management practices have been applied during [SOURCE: Page_52]

the last 20 years in order to restore MED native diversity: the interruption of ATL stocking, the creation of [SOURCE: Page_52]

a genetic refuge for MED populations and the reintroduction or transfer of MED trout (Gil et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_52]

However, mounting evidence suggested that they failed at conserving native diversity. Indeed, while the [SOURCE: Page_52]

proportion of non-native ATL trout did decline, hybrids increased in frequency (Gil et al., 2016) and often [SOURCE: Page_52]

represent the majority of populations. [SOURCE: Page_52]

Recent studies have pointed out the importance of EEMs involved in reproductive isolation. Indeed, pre- [SOURCE: Page_52]

zygotic mating behaviours – female preference (Gil et al., 2015) – and post-zygotic mechanisms – [SOURCE: Page_52]

embryonic survival based on GxE interactions (Folio et al., 2021) – may control for the evolution of [SOURCE: Page_52]

41 [SOURCE: Page_52]

Chapter 1. Introduction [SOURCE: Page_53]

diversity dynamics in space and time. These mechanisms could explain the observed heterogeneous [SOURCE: Page_53]

spatial patterns of introgression as well as the failure of management practices to restore the MED lineage [SOURCE: Page_53]

in some contexts. So far, such mechanisms have not been integrated into the design of management [SOURCE: Page_53]

practices for conserving the diversity of native populations and managing hybridization. [SOURCE: Page_53]

Today, despite the will of fishery managers to restore native intraspecific diversity in the Mediterranean [SOURCE: Page_53]

area, they lack scientific knowledge and an appropriate tool to guide their management practices. [SOURCE: Page_53]

The overarching goal of my PhD is to investigate the potential of eco-evolutionary concepts to improve [SOURCE: Page_53]

the management of intraspecific diversity. To do so, my first aim was to develop a DG-ABM to understand [SOURCE: Page_53]

the evolution of diversity dynamics in brown trout while considering the empirically demonstrated EEMs [SOURCE: Page_53]

involved in reproductive isolation. The model aims at simulating the population dynamics of brown trout [SOURCE: Page_53]

under management interventions for some EEMs. In doing so I will assess the potential role of these EEMs [SOURCE: Page_53]

in the structuration of diversity dynamics. Finally, this thesis should fill an identified gap between [SOURCE: Page_53]

management and evolution, by creating a decision-making tool that will allow the testing of new [SOURCE: Page_53]

management strategies in an eco-evolutionary, objective scientific framework. [SOURCE: Page_53]

1.7.3   Chapters’ specific objectives [SOURCE: Page_53]

My PhD will be composed of five other chapters. The second chapter will provide some basic knowledge [SOURCE: Page_53]

on the biology and ecology of Brown trout, and then proceed to detail the available knowledge on the [SOURCE: Page_53]

MED/ATL hybridization complex. More specifically, it will detail the EEMs empirically identified and [SOURCE: Page_53]

potentially involved in the evolution of diversity dynamics. The end of this first chapter will then describe [SOURCE: Page_53]

specifically how these mechanisms could be influencing the introgression dynamics, and therefore why [SOURCE: Page_53]

42 [SOURCE: Page_53]

1.7 A relevant case study in salmonids [SOURCE: Page_54]

they should be implemented in a modelling approach to measure the extent of this influence. The third [SOURCE: Page_54]

chapter will describe in detail the demo genetic individual-based model developed, as well as its user- [SOURCE: Page_54]

friendly interface destined to be used in interaction with managers. This model will allow us to actually [SOURCE: Page_54]

simulate the qualitative and quantitative impacts of EEMs in an integrative way, on population genetic, [SOURCE: Page_54]

phenotypic and demographic structure. The fourth chapter will make a first use of the model to evaluate [SOURCE: Page_54]

the actual role of two EEMs – female heterogamous preference and GxE interactions on survival – in [SOURCE: Page_54]

driving the evolution of intraspecific diversity. This will first be done prospectively in a very simple case [SOURCE: Page_54]

(i.e., a simplified environment) where the two lineages are brought back in secondary contact. Then, we [SOURCE: Page_54]

will also use the model in actual case studies applied to real rivers where documented management [SOURCE: Page_54]

practices have interacted with EEMs and specific environmental conditions to influence current diversity [SOURCE: Page_54]

patterns. Finally, in chapter 5, the model will be used in a more prospective and generic way, so to [SOURCE: Page_54]

hopefully produce more general guidelines. This will be done by building simulations carried out in [SOURCE: Page_54]

fictitious hydrographic networks whose characteristics (connectivity, initial spatial location of lineages) [SOURCE: Page_54]

represent situations typically encountered by managers in the field. The sixth and last chapter will [SOURCE: Page_54]

synthesise all the new knowledge produced, and discuss the relevance of integrating elements of [SOURCE: Page_54]

evolutionary ecology into the management of intraspecific biodiversity, in the particular case of the [SOURCE: Page_54]

MED/ATL complex, but also in a more general context in relation to the major issues of global change and [SOURCE: Page_54]

native biodiversity management. [SOURCE: Page_54]

## 2.1 BROWN TROUT ECOLOGY AND EVOLUTION

2.1.1   Brown trout life cycle [SOURCE: Page_57]

The brown trout species, Salmo trutta, are part of the Salmonids family. Brown trout is a facultative [SOURCE: Page_57]

anadromous fish. Hence, within the species, individuals can be distinguished based on alternative [SOURCE: Page_57]

migratory tactics. Resident trout do not migrate and will mature in freshwater, while migrant trout will [SOURCE: Page_57]

migrate to sea (anadromous trout) – or sometimes to lakes (lake trout) – for growth and maturation [SOURCE: Page_57]

before returning to freshwater for reproduction (P. Caballero et al., 2013). Here, I will only focus on [SOURCE: Page_57]

resident trout that spend their entire life cycle in freshwater. For the ideal development and growth of [SOURCE: Page_57]

brown trout, high water quality – a neutral pH and good oxygenation – as well as a moderate to high flow [SOURCE: Page_57]

stream are required (Maisse & Baglinière, 1999). For reproduction, the species also requires a specific [SOURCE: Page_57]

spawning ground with fine substratum – 2 millimetres to 5 centimetres (Champigneulle et al., 2003; [SOURCE: Page_57]

Maisse & Baglinière, 1999). [SOURCE: Page_57]

At the beginning of the brown trout life cycle (Figure 2 – 1), fertilised eggs develop under gravel during an [SOURCE: Page_57]

incubation period up to hatching, which happens around 400 degree-days (Killeen et al., 1999). Following [SOURCE: Page_57]

hatching, vesicled-fry continue growing under gravel until the resorption of their vesicle. Emergence then [SOURCE: Page_57]

happens – juveniles emerge from the substrate – as individuals need to feed and conquer territories, [SOURCE: Page_57]

marking the beginning of inter-individual competition (Lahti et al., 2001). A huge variability can be [SOURCE: Page_57]

observed in emergence timing, as there are advantages to early, as well as, late emergence (Lelong, 2006). [SOURCE: Page_57]

Juveniles then usually move downstream with the water flow. Brown trout will avoid temperatures above [SOURCE: Page_57]

20°C and optimal temperatures for growth can vary between 13 to 16°C depending on their food regime [SOURCE: Page_57]

46 [SOURCE: Page_57]

Chapter 2. The specific case of brown trout: the MED/ATL hybridization complex [SOURCE: Page_58]

(Elliott et al., 1995; Forseth et al., 2009; Forseth & Jonsson, 1994). Sexual maturation can occur as soon as [SOURCE: Page_58]

two years old. The reproductive season starts around November and can last for up to three months. Most [SOURCE: Page_58]

mature individuals will migrate to their spawning ground performing “homing” to their birth site or simply [SOURCE: Page_58]

migrating upstream towards suitable spawning habitat. Females select the spawning habitat and dig the [SOURCE: Page_58]

nest. Mate choice proceeds – potentially guided by the “agents” of sexual selection: male competition [SOURCE: Page_58]

and female preference – followed by spawning and fertilisation. After mating, females will bury the [SOURCE: Page_58]

fertilised eggs in the substratum (Haury et al., 1999). Females’ fecundity is positively correlated to female [SOURCE: Page_58]

body size (Lobon‐Cervia et al., 1997). [SOURCE: Page_58]

Figure 2—1: Simple representation of the brown trout life cycle. The under-gravel development phase includes hatching and emergence. Growth and survival happen during the entire life cycle. Reproduction happens following maturation and involves migration, spawning habitat selection as well as mate choice performed by females and the spawning act. [SOURCE: Page_58]

47 [SOURCE: Page_58]

2.1 Brown trout ecology and evolution [SOURCE: Page_59]

2.1.2   The evolution of brown trout diversity in Europe [SOURCE: Page_59]

The brown trout species shows a high level of intraspecific diversity (Bernatchez, 2001), with many genetic [SOURCE: Page_59]

and phenotypic variations observed between populations within the species natural expansion range: [SOURCE: Page_59]

Europe. These populations evolved in allopatric conditions for 0.5 to 2 million years during the Pleistocene [SOURCE: Page_59]

glaciations, where independent genetic evolution arose through vicariance with separated populations [SOURCE: Page_59]

colonising different areas across Europe (J.-L. García-Marín et al., 1999). Five distinct genetic lineages [SOURCE: Page_59]

(Figure 2 – 2) – i.e. groups of individuals sharing the same phenotypic traits and genotypic variations – [SOURCE: Page_59]

were then established using mitochondrial DNA (Bernatchez, 2001). [SOURCE: Page_59]

Figure 2—2: Spatial localisation of the five distinct genetic lineages identified by Bernatchez (2001) within brown trout natural expansion range. The figure is extracted from Caudron (2008). [SOURCE: Page_59]

48 [SOURCE: Page_59]

Chapter 2. The specific case of brown trout: the MED/ATL hybridization complex [SOURCE: Page_60]

Phylogeography indicates that division happened in two steps. Initially, hydrographic networks of three [SOURCE: Page_60]

main brown trout river basins – the Atlantic, the Danubian and the Mediterranean Basin – were [SOURCE: Page_60]

segregated due to important climatic events supposed to have happened approximately 700 000 years [SOURCE: Page_60]

ago (Webb & Bartlein, 1992). This led to the creation of three main lineages: the Atlantic, the Danubian [SOURCE: Page_60]

and the Mediterranean lineages. Then, the MED lineage subdivided into three lineages: The [SOURCE: Page_60]

Mediterranean, the Adriatic and the Marmoratus lineages, following divergent evolution that may have [SOURCE: Page_60]

occurred by geographical isolation (Keith, 1998). Despite long periods of allopatric evolution, those [SOURCE: Page_60]

lineages appeared to have not been fully segregated through space and time, as indicated by some natural [SOURCE: Page_60]

secondary contact zone located at the edges of each geographical range (Aurelle et al., 2002; Bouza et al., [SOURCE: Page_60]

2008; Sanz et al., 2006). Recent studies have confirmed that multiple contacts may have happened [SOURCE: Page_60]

between lineages (Hashemzadeh Segherloo et al., 2021), possibly due to topological modifications [SOURCE: Page_60]

occurring during glacial episodes. [SOURCE: Page_60]

2.1.3   Brown trout diversity in France [SOURCE: Page_60]

In France, there are two lineages of brown trout: the ATL and the MED lineages. They have segregated [SOURCE: Page_60]

geographical ranges as mountainous terrains separate the Atlantic and the Mediterranean Basin (Figure [SOURCE: Page_60]

2 – 3). MED populations of brown trout are considered to be native to the Mediterranean Basin [SOURCE: Page_60]

(Guyomard, 1989; Launey et al., 2003). Many genetic differences have been established between both [SOURCE: Page_60]

lineages, at mitochondrial and nuclear genes (Bernatchez, 2001; Guyomard, 1989; Launey et al., 2003). [SOURCE: Page_60]

Some microsatellites (str^541 and str^591) and single nucleotides polymorphism (OMM1144, OMM1105, [SOURCE: Page_60]

OMM1154, OMM117) loci have been shown to perfectly differentiate the ATL lineage from the MED [SOURCE: Page_60]

lineage (Gharbi et al., 2006; Guyomard & Caudron, 2008). Managers and scientists have been using these [SOURCE: Page_60]

markers to assign individuals to one of the three following genotypic categories: pure non-native ATL trout [SOURCE: Page_60]

49 [SOURCE: Page_60]

2.1 Brown trout ecology and evolution [SOURCE: Page_61]

(all alleles are from the ATL lineage), pure native MED trout (all alleles are from the MED lineage) or hybrid [SOURCE: Page_61]

(HYB) trout (combination of alleles from both lineages). [SOURCE: Page_61]

Figure 2—3: (A) Geographical map of France representing the main rivers and mountain ranges. (B) Map of French rivers showing the geographical range of the Atlantic and the Mediterranean lineages with a drawn delimitation line between both watersheds. [SOURCE: Page_61]

These different genotypic categories (i.e. genotypes: ATL, HYB, MED) can to some extent be phenotypically [SOURCE: Page_61]

differentiated based on robe criteria. Indeed, the ATL and the MED lineages present different phenotypes [SOURCE: Page_61]

(Figure 2 – 4) and many robe criteria (Table 2 – 1), specific to one or the other lineage, have been identified [SOURCE: Page_61]

(Aparicio et al., 2005; Largiadèr & Scholl, 1996; Lascaux, 1996). HYB individuals show intermediate [SOURCE: Page_61]

phenotypes with a mixture of robe criteria from both lineages. For instance, MED trout are characterised [SOURCE: Page_61]

by a high number of small spots, mainly black, located on its robe and opercular area while ATL trout has [SOURCE: Page_61]

a very low number of those. Instead, they are characterised by larger red spots with a white aureole. The [SOURCE: Page_61]

lateral line is absent for MED trout but visible on ATL trout. The presence of black lateral stripes on the [SOURCE: Page_61]

flank of MED trout is also a distinctive trait for that lineage. Finally, the opercular macrostigma is a [SOURCE: Page_61]

50 [SOURCE: Page_61]

Chapter 2. The specific case of brown trout: the MED/ATL hybridization complex [SOURCE: Page_62]

compulsory, but not an exclusive criterion for MED trout, as it is also observed in ATL trout. This trait is [SOURCE: Page_62]

often used to validate the attribution of an individual to the MED lineage. For juveniles, some differences [SOURCE: Page_62]

in the number and shapes of parr robe marks have also been observed (Mezzera et al., 1997). Saint-Pé et [SOURCE: Page_62]

al. (2019) provided an in-depth genomic analysis of the genetic bases of these robe criteria, which have [SOURCE: Page_62]

already been used as a proxy of lineage characterisation for field observations (Folio et al., 2021; Gil et al., [SOURCE: Page_62]

2015) in Haute-Savoie. However, in other trout populations of the Mediterranean basin, some studies [SOURCE: Page_62]

failed to find a consistent correlation between pigmentation patterns and genome-wide admixture [SOURCE: Page_62]

(Valette et al., 2020). This suggests that phenotype-genotype correlation might highly depend on the [SOURCE: Page_62]

population considered as well as the number and types of markers used. [SOURCE: Page_62]

Figure 2—4: Robe criteria characteristic of the MED and the ATL lineages of brown trout. Image modified from Aparicio et al. (2005). [SOURCE: Page_62]

51 [SOURCE: Page_62]

2.2 History on management practices and their consequences on diversity [SOURCE: Page_63]

Table 2-1: Robe criteria used in lineage characterisation. For each possible expected genotype, it is noted whether robe criteria are present, possible, partial, or absent. The number of opercular spots is ranged from high to low. Lateral stripes, spots aureole, lateral line, and the number of opercular spots were used to calculate an expected genotypic score as described in Gil et al. (2015). Opercular macrostigma was used to validate the attribution to the MED lineage, as it is a compulsory criterion in MED individuals. These criteria were used in two main studies for the determination of phenotypic score (Gil et al. 2015) and expected genotypic score (Folio et al. 2021). [SOURCE: Page_63]

Robe criteria                            MED                  HYB                   ATL [SOURCE: Page_63]

Lateral stripes                          Present              Possible              Absent [SOURCE: Page_63]

Spots aureole                            Absent               Possible              Present [SOURCE: Page_63]

Lateral line                             Absent               Partial               Present [SOURCE: Page_63]

Opercular macrostigma                    Present              Possible              Possible [SOURCE: Page_63]

Number of small spots                    High                 Medium                Low [SOURCE: Page_63]

The MED lineage appears to show a higher genetic diversity compared to the other lineages (Suárez et al., [SOURCE: Page_63]

2001). MED populations were less impacted by glaciation events compared to central and northern [SOURCE: Page_63]

Europe populations – ATL included – that experienced multiple glacial refuges (J.-L. García-Marín et al., [SOURCE: Page_63]

1999; Hamilton et al., 1989), possibly causing repeated bottleneck events and ultimately leading to a [SOURCE: Page_63]

decrease in genetic diversity (Suárez et al., 2001). However, it appears that different MED populations – [SOURCE: Page_63]

of the Pyrenees and Haute-Savoie – still present similar distinctive traits. [SOURCE: Page_63]

## 2.2 HISTORY ON MANAGEMENT PRACTICES AND THEIR CONSEQUENCES ON DIVERSITY

2.2.1      The rise of stocking [SOURCE: Page_63]

For more than a century, the main management practice for brown trout populations was river stocking [SOURCE: Page_63]

(Baglinière & Maisse, 1991) in order to counter-balance the perceived negative effects exerted by fishing [SOURCE: Page_63]

52 [SOURCE: Page_63]

Chapter 2. The specific case of brown trout: the MED/ATL hybridization complex [SOURCE: Page_64]

and anthropic pressures on wild populations. It started at the end of the 19th century when they mastered [SOURCE: Page_64]

the artificial reproduction of brown trout (Baglinière & Maisse, 1991). Stocking can be defined here as the [SOURCE: Page_64]

introduction of a large number of domesticated trout in rivers. Intensification of this practice followed [SOURCE: Page_64]

rapidly and brown trout consequently became the most introduced fish species in the world (Lowe et al., [SOURCE: Page_64]

2000). [SOURCE: Page_64]

Stocking, using domesticated strains belonging to the ATL lineage, (Almodovar et al., 2001) has been [SOURCE: Page_64]

reported in several streams of the Mediterranean Basin in France (Barbat-Leterrier et al., 1989; Beaudou [SOURCE: Page_64]

et al., 1994; Berrebi, Poteaux, et al., 2000; Guyomard, 1989; Poteaux, Beaudou, et al., 1998), in Spain [SOURCE: Page_64]

(Almodovar et al., 2006; Aparicio et al., 2005) and also Italy (Caputo Barucchi et al., 2004; Lucentini et al., [SOURCE: Page_64]

2006). These strains originating from central and northern Europe (Almodovar et al., 2001) were the [SOURCE: Page_64]

results of long periods of domestication (Guyomard, 1989); probably up to a century since the beginning [SOURCE: Page_64]

of fish farming. Stocking started in this area at the end of the 19th century as recreational fishing had [SOURCE: Page_64]

exerted strong pressure on natural populations and; it progressively became an intensive practice during [SOURCE: Page_64]

the 20th century in order to sustain fishing (Caudron & Champigneulle, 2007). In Haute-Savoie (Figure 2 – [SOURCE: Page_64]

5), 15 to 20 million trouts were poured each year in the rivers. Between 800,000 and 1,000,000 fry were [SOURCE: Page_64]

annually stocked in the Dranse system during the 80s and 90s (Caudron & Champigneulle, 2007). In the [SOURCE: Page_64]

Borne River alone, the number of fry stocked reached the million in 1989 (Caudron et al., 2009). [SOURCE: Page_64]

Stocking practices at that time were quite diverse and hard to quantify as information on precise parts of [SOURCE: Page_64]

the river stocked was missing, preventing the calculation of densities introduced (Almodovar et al., 2006). [SOURCE: Page_64]

Various information highlighted heterogeneity in stocking practices regarding both the amount of fish [SOURCE: Page_64]

stocked and the stages used for stocking (Poteaux, Beaudou, et al., 1998). Eggs, fry, juveniles, sub-adults [SOURCE: Page_64]

and adults have been introduced, with densities going from 0.05 to 2.4 kg.ha-1 for sub-adults and adults [SOURCE: Page_64]

and from 10 to 103 ind.m-2 for eggs and fry cumulated. [SOURCE: Page_64]

53 [SOURCE: Page_64]

2.2 History on management practices and their consequences on diversity [SOURCE: Page_65]

Figure 2—5: Localisation of some study sites on the Haute-Savoie hydro-geographic network among which, the two sites used in this manuscript – the Chevenne Creek and the Borne River. Sampling sites are marked. Figure adapted from Gil et al. 2016. [SOURCE: Page_65]

2.2.2   A shift in management practices [SOURCE: Page_65]

At the end of the 20th century, a shift was observed in management practices, toward the conservation of [SOURCE: Page_65]

genetic diversity, as advances in molecular genetics evidenced high levels of intraspecific diversity in [SOURCE: Page_65]

salmonids species (Allendorf & Leary, 1988; Ryman et al., 1995; Utter, 1981, 2004; Waples, 1991). Since [SOURCE: Page_65]

then fishery managers became progressively more aware of intraspecific genetic diversity and the [SOURCE: Page_65]

negative impact that domesticated individuals could have on wild native populations of salmonids when [SOURCE: Page_65]

admixture occurs (Ryman et al., 1995; Utter, 2004). In Laikre et al. (1999), geneticists highlighted the [SOURCE: Page_65]

54 [SOURCE: Page_65]

Chapter 2. The specific case of brown trout: the MED/ATL hybridization complex [SOURCE: Page_66]

ecological importance of diverse native lineages for the conservation of brown trout intraspecific diversity. [SOURCE: Page_66]

Hence, at the beginning of the 21st century in the Mediterranean area, the conservation issues of native [SOURCE: Page_66]

trout (Caudron, Vigier, et al., 2012) became the core objective of managers, since the introduction of the [SOURCE: Page_66]

ATL lineage in the Mediterranean area caused lineages admixture and resulted in anthropogenic [SOURCE: Page_66]

hybridization, threatening native diversity. Stocking practices were, therefore, massively reduced. In [SOURCE: Page_66]

Haute-Savoie, for example, stocking densities went down from year 2000 to year 2012 and the total [SOURCE: Page_66]

stocked area was reduced – more than twofold. The last decades have seen stocking practices using ATL [SOURCE: Page_66]

strains replaced by various practices aiming to conserve and restore native MED populations. [SOURCE: Page_66]

2.2.3   Consequences on diversity [SOURCE: Page_66]

In the Mediterranean area, hybridization led to the introgression of native MED alleles by non-native ATL [SOURCE: Page_66]

alleles at the individual and population levels. Introgression is the process where genetic information from [SOURCE: Page_66]

one species or lineages is integrated into the genome of another one and partially or fully take over. [SOURCE: Page_66]

Introgression can influence intraspecific diversity and affect the conservation of populations at the genetic [SOURCE: Page_66]

scale (Laikre et al., 1999). This has many consequences regarding management practices, as managers are [SOURCE: Page_66]

currently interested in the conservation of native MED diversity. However, ATL stocking did not lead to [SOURCE: Page_66]

homogenous patterns of hybridization in the MED area, despite homogenous implementation. Indeed, [SOURCE: Page_66]

genetic studies indicated that the consequences of hybridization were quite diverse with highly [SOURCE: Page_66]

heterogeneous introgression rates observed at a micro-geographical scale within a hydrographic network [SOURCE: Page_66]

(Figure 2 – 6). However, some very frequent situations are found among hydrographic networks. In a [SOURCE: Page_66]

majority of cases, hybridization resulted in full admixture (Figure 2 – 6; 2 – 7: situation A; 2 – 8) – [SOURCE: Page_66]

populations displaying ~ 50% of introgression, i.e. an equal proportion of MED and ATL alleles within the [SOURCE: Page_66]

population. [SOURCE: Page_66]

55 [SOURCE: Page_66]

2.2 History on management practices and their consequences on diversity [SOURCE: Page_67]

Figure 2—6: Cartographies of (A) the introgression of native MED alleles by non-native ATL alleles (in red: native populations, orange: lowly introgressed populations, light green: hybrid populations, dark green: highly introgressed populations and yellow: non-native populations); (B) lowly introgressed native populations which have been identified as conservation priorities; in some departments of the Mediterranean area: Rhône, Loire, Isère and Savoie. Figures modified from Scimabio Interface. [SOURCE: Page_67]

56 [SOURCE: Page_67]

Chapter 2. The specific case of brown trout: the MED/ATL hybridization complex [SOURCE: Page_68]

In some rare cases, both lineages are found in sympatry but without any HYB (Figure 2 – 7: situation B). [SOURCE: Page_68]

Finally, it is common to find both lineages, with or without HYB individuals along the same river. For [SOURCE: Page_68]

example, a gradient of introgression can be observed along the river with ATL individuals upstream, HYB [SOURCE: Page_68]

in the intermediate parts of the river and MED individuals downstream in the confluence area (Figure 2 – [SOURCE: Page_68]

7: situation C; The Overan Creek: Folio et al., 2021). Both lineages can also be found along the same river [SOURCE: Page_68]

segregated by an obstacle, with (Figure 2 – 7: situation E; The Chevenne Creek: Caudron & Champigneulle, [SOURCE: Page_68]

2011) or without (Figure 2 – 7: situation D; The Borne River: Caudron et al., 2009) the presence of HYB. [SOURCE: Page_68]

Pure non-native populations appear to be located where no native population had settled before, often [SOURCE: Page_68]

upstream, potentially due to impassable barriers. [SOURCE: Page_68]

Figure 2—7: Common diversity patterns found within hydrographic networks of the Mediterranean area. ATL non-native populations are often located upstream while MED native populations are found downstream. [SOURCE: Page_68]

57 [SOURCE: Page_68]

2.2 History on management practices and their consequences on diversity [SOURCE: Page_69]

In the Haute-Savoie area (Figure 2 – 8), an INTERREG III program on “autochthonous trout” aimed at [SOURCE: Page_69]

identifying and localising persistent MED populations from 2005 to 2008. It indicates that only a few pure [SOURCE: Page_69]

native populations and some lowly introgressed populations (<25%) – usually targeted for conservation [SOURCE: Page_69]

effort (Laikre et al., 1999) – remain. The intensity of stocking was proposed as an explanation for this [SOURCE: Page_69]

spatial heterogeneous introgression. However, historical data on stocking in Haute-Savoie are not enough [SOURCE: Page_69]

to explain the observed distribution of introgression (Scimabio Interface, Com. Pers.). [SOURCE: Page_69]

Figure 2—8: Cartography of the admixture rate observed in Haute-Savoie (in red/orange: pure or nearly pure MED populations, in green: fully hybridized populations, in blue: pure non-native populations). Some nearly pure native populations still remain and have been targeted for conservation efforts. [SOURCE: Page_69]

58 [SOURCE: Page_69]

## 2.3 TOWARD THE CONSERVATION OF THE NATIVE MED LINEAGE

2.3.1   Native conservation strategies [SOURCE: Page_70]

Since the last decades, some specific management practices had been implemented to try to conserve [SOURCE: Page_70]

and restore native MED populations in the Mediterranean area, notably in the Haute-Savoie. Four [SOURCE: Page_70]

management practices were implemented: [SOURCE: Page_70]

 The genetic refuge (GR) strategy is a passive strategy that simply bans ATL stocking. This is usually [SOURCE: Page_70]

the first strategy implemented in order to stop further introduction of non-native alleles [SOURCE: Page_70]

(Caudron, Champigneulle, et al., 2012; Caudron et al., 2011; Caudron & Champigneulle, 2011). [SOURCE: Page_70]

When implemented alone, the assumption is that native populations will be purged from non- [SOURCE: Page_70]

native individuals via natural selection. [SOURCE: Page_70]

The other strategies are active interventions as they result in the modification of population demography. [SOURCE: Page_70]

 ATL removal is the removal of all non-native individuals – ATL but also HYB – from an area where [SOURCE: Page_70]

the introgression rate is high, through electric fishing. This strategy is often conducted before the [SOURCE: Page_70]

introduction of MED individuals in order to replace a functional non-native population with a [SOURCE: Page_70]

native one (Caudron & Champigneulle, 2011). It is often conducted where both lineages are in [SOURCE: Page_70]

allopatry so that all individuals may be removed without having to distinguish between pure [SOURCE: Page_70]

native and non-native individuals. [SOURCE: Page_70]

 Direct translocation (DT) of MED individuals is the transfer of individuals from a pure or almost [SOURCE: Page_70]

pure MED population to another portion of the river to be colonised (Caudron, Champigneulle, et [SOURCE: Page_70]

al., 2012; Caudron et al., 2011). This strategy can be based on the translocation of either adult [SOURCE: Page_70]

wild fish only (genitors) or of populations (often from 1+ sub-adults due to catchability). [SOURCE: Page_70]

59 [SOURCE: Page_70]

2.3 Toward the conservation of the native med lineage [SOURCE: Page_71]

 Finally stocking with native fry (SNF) is the introduction of a certain amount of summer-fed fry [SOURCE: Page_71]

(2-4 centimetres), around June, produced in hatcheries, but originating from MED wild genitors [SOURCE: Page_71]

in a section of the river to be colonised (Caudron et al., 2006, 2011). [SOURCE: Page_71]

DT and SNF, implemented alone, are often used by managers to replace non-native populations, [SOURCE: Page_71]

presenting a high introgression and very low densities (< 10 ind.m-2, often non-functional), by a self- [SOURCE: Page_71]

maintaining native population (Caudron, Champigneulle, et al., 2012). [SOURCE: Page_71]

2.3.2   Unsuccessful outcomes [SOURCE: Page_71]

In a previous study, Gil et al. (2016) investigated the impact of some of these management strategies on [SOURCE: Page_71]

wild population intraspecific diversity by analysing the evolution of introgression and the genotypic [SOURCE: Page_71]

composition of the population. They considered three of the above-described strategies, implemented by [SOURCE: Page_71]

scientists and managers in 19 sites for more than 15 years: GR strategy, SNF and DT (see published case [SOURCE: Page_71]

studies: Caudron, Champigneulle, et al., 2012; Caudron et al., 2006, 2011). All implemented strategies [SOURCE: Page_71]

proved to be unsuccessful regarding the initial management goal of recovering pure or almost pure native [SOURCE: Page_71]

MED populations. Indeed, despite a general decrease in the percentage of ATL alleles (introgression rates, [SOURCE: Page_71]

Figure 2 – 9) and the successful removal of ATL individuals, the number of HYB individuals increased in the [SOURCE: Page_71]

populations over a short period (Genotypic composition of the population, Figure 2 – 10). However, the [SOURCE: Page_71]

magnitude and speed of these changes varied between strategies and among sites. The active strategies [SOURCE: Page_71]

showed a higher and faster decrease in ATL alleles and individuals compared to the GR strategy, where [SOURCE: Page_71]

the decrease was observed over a long time (Figure 2 – 9, 2 – 10). The increase of HYB individuals varied [SOURCE: Page_71]

among strategies but more so among sites for a given strategy with some sites where high rates of HYB [SOURCE: Page_71]

were observed compared to others. DT appeared to generate less HYB than any other strategies and more [SOURCE: Page_71]

MED individuals. For the GR strategy, a few sites even showed counterproductive results, as the [SOURCE: Page_71]

60 [SOURCE: Page_71]

Chapter 2. The specific case of brown trout: the MED/ATL hybridization complex [SOURCE: Page_72]

percentage of MED individuals in the population was decreasing (Figure 2 – 10), and the introgression rate [SOURCE: Page_72]

increasing with the rise of HYB individuals (Figure 2 – 9). [SOURCE: Page_72]

Figure 2—9: “Evolution of the introgression rate (calculated as the percentage of ATL alleles in the population) for each site according to the management strategy implemented.” Figure and legend from Gil et al. 2016. [SOURCE: Page_72]

These results suggest that management practices can quickly influence intraspecific diversity dynamics [SOURCE: Page_72]

and that these systematic responses might be driven by a combination of evolutionary processes, likely [SOURCE: Page_72]

environment-dependent. [SOURCE: Page_72]

61 [SOURCE: Page_72]

2.3 Toward the conservation of the native med lineage [SOURCE: Page_73]

Figure 2—10: “Evolution of the percentage of individual genotypes. Three genotypes possible: ATL (pure Atlantic alien), HYB (hybrids) and MED (pure native Mediterranean), according to all the observed number of hatchery specific alleles at the two diagnostic loci.” Figure and legend from Gil et al. 2016. [SOURCE: Page_73]

62 [SOURCE: Page_73]

Chapter 2. The specific case of brown trout: the MED/ATL hybridization complex [SOURCE: Page_74]

Indeed, following the refuge strategy, populations did not purge themselves from ATL alleles, as initially [SOURCE: Page_74]

expected. On the one hand, the presence and increase of hybrids indicate a lack of pre-zygotic isolation [SOURCE: Page_74]

between the lineages in our study sites, as recently demonstrated in the Dranse system in Haute-Savoie, [SOURCE: Page_74]

where patterns of heterogamous mating were observed (Gil et al., 2015, & Section 2.4.1). It also suggests [SOURCE: Page_74]

that selection against HYB might be low or inexistent. However, in other environments, reproductive [SOURCE: Page_74]

isolation barriers, may they be pre- or post-zygotic, may exist as some pure native MED populations are [SOURCE: Page_74]

still found in some river stretches. On the other hand, the collapse of pure ATL individuals, but not pure [SOURCE: Page_74]

MED individuals following hybridization, suggests that some fitness differences among parental lineages [SOURCE: Page_74]

exist. An analysis of post-zygotic survival (Folio et al., 2021, & Section 2.4.2), using offspring from all [SOURCE: Page_74]

possible crosses between MED, HYB and ATL individuals, indicates a role of maternal genotype-by- [SOURCE: Page_74]

temperature interaction on embryonic survival. It appears that the outcome of stocking with ATL [SOURCE: Page_74]

individuals has led to a mosaic of situations in terms of observed intraspecific diversity. Environmental, [SOURCE: Page_74]

behavioural and possibly selective factors, in addition to the implementation of various management [SOURCE: Page_74]

practices, are likely to have generated such diversified outcomes. [SOURCE: Page_74]

## 2.4 THE ECO-EVOLUTIONARY MECHANISMS DRIVING HYBRIDIZATION

Since observed patterns of diversity are often the result of allopatric evolution (Harrison, 2012), many [SOURCE: Page_74]

reproductive isolation mechanisms could have evolved between the previously isolated ATL and MED [SOURCE: Page_74]

populations, potentially involving eco-evolutionary mechanisms (Schluter, 2000). In that case, the [SOURCE: Page_74]

evolutionary outcome of hybridization and intraspecific diversity dynamics could be driven by a [SOURCE: Page_74]

combination of factors differentiating both lineages. For the MED-ATL hybridization complex, many [SOURCE: Page_74]

factors have been pointed out, and some experimentally investigated to explain the observed patterns of [SOURCE: Page_74]

intraspecific diversity. These studies have focused on two reproductive isolation mechanisms thought to [SOURCE: Page_74]

63 [SOURCE: Page_74]

2.4 The eco-evolutionary mechanisms driving hybridization [SOURCE: Page_75]

be the most influential on diversity dynamics. Gil et al. (2015) focused on pre-zygotic isolation mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_75]

and investigated if some behavioural differences existed between both lineages regarding sexual selection [SOURCE: Page_75]

– female preference and male competition. Folio et al. (2021) focused on post-zygotic isolation and [SOURCE: Page_75]

investigated patterns of offspring survival at egg stage based on parental origins (MED, HYB, ATL) and river [SOURCE: Page_75]

temperatures to detect potential genotype-by-environment (GxE) interactions. This section will describe [SOURCE: Page_75]

in detail these two reproductive isolation mechanisms, as well as the other factors that have not yet been [SOURCE: Page_75]

investigated but may also contribute to diversity dynamics. [SOURCE: Page_75]

2.4.1   Mating preference [SOURCE: Page_75]

Reproductive behaviour, such as female preference, is a potential candidate for pre-zygotic reproductive [SOURCE: Page_75]

isolation. Usually, female preference for locally adapted phenotypes (Fisher, 1930; G. C. Williams, 1966) [SOURCE: Page_75]

is expected to reinforce reproductive isolation mechanisms, since native individuals might be preferred [SOURCE: Page_75]

as mates when they present a fitness advantage compared to non-native individuals “good gene [SOURCE: Page_75]

hypothesis (Kirkpatrick, 1996)”. Largiadèr & Scholl (1996) investigated introgression patterns in a northern [SOURCE: Page_75]

population of brown trout (Doubs River) and suggested that the mating system was biased toward [SOURCE: Page_75]

homogamy in the MED-ATL complex due to some observed linkages disequilibrium and the absence of [SOURCE: Page_75]

intermediate phenotypes in this river. However, in other environments, the manifest presence of hybrids [SOURCE: Page_75]

suggests that pre-zygotic reproductive isolation is incomplete between the lineages. Using genetic studies, [SOURCE: Page_75]

other authors argued that the mating system between both lineages was random since genotypic [SOURCE: Page_75]

frequencies observed in some populations matched random pairing (Barbat-Leterrier et al., 1989). [SOURCE: Page_75]

Yet, both these assumptions are based on studies considering the diversity of individuals found in given [SOURCE: Page_75]

environments and do not portray the full range of mating behaviour that may occur. Indeed, this diversity [SOURCE: Page_75]

is likely to be the result of both pre and post-zygotic selection and the net effect then depends on [SOURCE: Page_75]

64 [SOURCE: Page_75]

Chapter 2. The specific case of brown trout: the MED/ATL hybridization complex [SOURCE: Page_76]

environmental conditions (Godineau et al., 2021). Because sexual selection can sometimes oppose natural [SOURCE: Page_76]

selection (Labonne & Hendry, 2010), it is compulsory to study mating behaviours in natural environments. [SOURCE: Page_76]

Gil et al. (2015) investigated two potential pre-zygotic isolation barriers potentially involved in sexual [SOURCE: Page_76]

selection – female preference and male competition – by directly observing mating behaviours in natural [SOURCE: Page_76]

environments. Behavioural patterns were studied based on an individual phenotypic score in order to [SOURCE: Page_76]

differentiate lineages. This score is calculated using the presence or absence of several robe criteria, which [SOURCE: Page_76]

are genetically contrasted between lineages (Table 2 – 1; Aparicio et al., 2005). Subaquatic cameras were [SOURCE: Page_76]

placed in a few Haute-Savoie Rivers: the Dranse d’Abondance, the Chevenne, the Serve and the Borne [SOURCE: Page_76]

during reproductive season. Videos were then analysed and specific behaviours recorded. Detailed [SOURCE: Page_76]

experimental settings are available in Gil et al. (2015). [SOURCE: Page_76]

This section describe results produced exclusively by Jordi Gil (Gil, 2015) that will be useful in the [SOURCE: Page_76]

understanding of my PhD and its hypothesis. [SOURCE: Page_76]

2.4.1.1    Male-male competition [SOURCE: Page_76]

Intrasexual competition can condition access to females during reproduction and antagonistic behaviours [SOURCE: Page_76]

have already been described in salmonids (Esteve, 2005; Greeley, 1932; J. W. Jones & Ball, 1954; Tentelier [SOURCE: Page_76]

et al., 2011), where size is usually the factor involved in competition (M. W. Jones & Hutchings, 2001). In [SOURCE: Page_76]

the MED-ATL complex, if one lineage happened to be more aggressive compared to the other one, access [SOURCE: Page_76]

to females could be variable based on males genotype, and such biased access could tip the scales toward [SOURCE: Page_76]

a biased mating and reproductive success, a potential source for partial reproductive isolation. In his PhD, [SOURCE: Page_76]

Gil also aimed at evaluating if male aggressiveness was lineage dependent and targeted. [SOURCE: Page_76]

65 [SOURCE: Page_76]

2.4 The eco-evolutionary mechanisms driving hybridization [SOURCE: Page_77]

In this experiment, aggressive behaviours were counted and included lateral threat, chasing and biting. [SOURCE: Page_77]

Three models were then tested to evaluate if a male phenotype influenced his probability of (i) attacking, [SOURCE: Page_77]

(ii) being attacked or if (iii) aggressiveness was oriented based on both male phenotypes – targeting [SOURCE: Page_77]

similarity or dissimilarity. For the three models, results indicated that male competition was not affected [SOURCE: Page_77]

by phenotypes – and hence lineages. This experiment only confirmed the importance of body size in male- [SOURCE: Page_77]

to-male competition. [SOURCE: Page_77]

2.4.1.2   Female preference [SOURCE: Page_77]

Intersexual female preference is widely recognised in salmonids and many phenotypic traits are under [SOURCE: Page_77]

sexual selection (such as fin size (Quinn & Foote, 1994), size (Petersson et al., 1999), male/female size [SOURCE: Page_77]

ratio (Labonne et al., 2009). Colour and patterning also play an important role in sexual selection (Auld et [SOURCE: Page_77]

al., 2019; Cuthill et al., 2017) and melanin colouration appears to reflect honest signals to conspecifics in [SOURCE: Page_77]

salmonids (Jacquin et al., 2017; Marie-Orleach et al., 2014; Wedekind et al., 2008) and could hence [SOURCE: Page_77]

potentially be under female preferences. In the MED-ATL complex, potential female preference based on [SOURCE: Page_77]

phenotypic differences among lineages (Table 2 – 1) could either drive or counter pre-zygotic isolation. [SOURCE: Page_77]

Gil et al. (2015) aimed at uncovering patterns of female preference based on male robe criteria defined [SOURCE: Page_77]

in Table 1. A behavioural model considering a succession of possible behaviours was used (based on a [SOURCE: Page_77]

model developed by Labonne et al., 2009). After a male arrived, the female could either leave or stay, and [SOURCE: Page_77]

if the latter, be inactive or active, i.e. start digging. Female preference was considered as the probability [SOURCE: Page_77]

of staying and being active. Multiple preference models were then tested. A null model only considered [SOURCE: Page_77]

the effects of male-female size ratio and operational sex ratio (OSR, Labonne et al., 2009). It was the base [SOURCE: Page_77]

of all other models. A dissimilarity model implemented a value of dissimilarity between male and female [SOURCE: Page_77]

66 [SOURCE: Page_77]

Chapter 2. The specific case of brown trout: the MED/ATL hybridization complex [SOURCE: Page_78]

phenotypic scores, where 0 accounted for similar partners while 1 corresponded to an encounter between [SOURCE: Page_78]

ATL and MED phenotypes. This model aimed at distinguishing between homogamous, heterogamous or [SOURCE: Page_78]

non-phenotypic preferences. The last model was lineage dependent and aimed at testing if preference [SOURCE: Page_78]

was dependent upon female phenotype, male phenotype and the interaction between both. The aim here [SOURCE: Page_78]

was to be able to test if local females or maybe all females (universal preference) tended to prefer local [SOURCE: Page_78]

and potentially adapted males (G. C. Williams, 1966), or the opposite. Model selection was performed [SOURCE: Page_78]

based on Deviance Information Criterion (DIC, Spiegelhalter et al., 2002). [SOURCE: Page_78]

Figure 2—11: Predictions for general female preference (s x a) for an average female, when (a) OSR = 2, BSR = 1; (b) OSR = 2, BSR = 1.4; (c) OSR = 8, BSR = 1; (d) OSR = 8, BSR = 1.4. Figure extracted from Gil et al 2015. [SOURCE: Page_78]

67 [SOURCE: Page_78]

2.4 The eco-evolutionary mechanisms driving hybridization [SOURCE: Page_79]

The authors found that the model selected for female preference was the dissimilarity model, which [SOURCE: Page_79]

indicated that female preference was positively correlated to the dissimilarity score. Therefore, the more [SOURCE: Page_79]

dissimilar a male is to a female, the more she will stay on the nest and start digging (Figure 2 – 11). On the [SOURCE: Page_79]

extreme, this would mean that each lineage will tend to prefer the other one – a MED female will more [SOURCE: Page_79]

likely stay and dig for an ATL male rather than for a MED male (more than fold 2) and vice versa. However, [SOURCE: Page_79]

within observed data, encounters between individuals of opposite pure lineages were scarce (see Gil et [SOURCE: Page_79]

al., 2015) and incertitude increased passed a dissimilarity value of 0.6. In addition, the higher the size ratio [SOURCE: Page_79]

was the lower the impact of dissimilarity was on female preference and the more likely female will have [SOURCE: Page_79]

stayed and dug, which confirmed the importance of body size in female preference (Labonne et al., 2009; [SOURCE: Page_79]

Petersson et al., 1999). Overall, females probability to stay and dig was around 50% no matter males [SOURCE: Page_79]

phenotype. Probability for a female to stay and dig increase with the increased in OSR. [SOURCE: Page_79]

These findings indicate that mating is mainly determined based on a size-ratio and on a phenotypic [SOURCE: Page_79]

dissimilarity-based female preference as well as a size-based male-to-male competition. In the MED-ATL [SOURCE: Page_79]

context, since both lineages appear to have similar growth rates (Data from Scimabio Interface), size – for [SOURCE: Page_79]

individuals of the same age – should not be a factor that would advantage one or the other lineage when [SOURCE: Page_79]

growth happened in the same environment. However, in cases where growth would have happened in [SOURCE: Page_79]

very different environments – for example when individuals are translocated – size could represent an [SOURCE: Page_79]

advantaging factor for one lineage since the growth environment would have conditioned access to [SOURCE: Page_79]

resources and density-dependent growth (Lobon-Cervia, 2007; Lorenzen & Enberg, 2002). Similarly, if [SOURCE: Page_79]

mortality rates are different (Gil, 2015), then body size distribution could vary among lineages and hereby [SOURCE: Page_79]

influence mating. [SOURCE: Page_79]

68 [SOURCE: Page_79]

Chapter 2. The specific case of brown trout: the MED/ATL hybridization complex [SOURCE: Page_80]

Focusing on lineage differences, female preference indicates that the mating system in those [SOURCE: Page_80]

environments is biased toward heterogamy, with females favouring males partially or entirely dissimilar [SOURCE: Page_80]

to their own phenotypes. In the MED-ATL complex, heterogamous mating could catalyse hybridization [SOURCE: Page_80]

and accelerate the decrease of extreme “pure” ATL and MED phenotypes, potentially affecting the [SOURCE: Page_80]

distribution of pure genotypes. These results are in accordance with some patterns of diversity observed [SOURCE: Page_80]

in Haute-Savoie where populations are now fully hybridized despite the implementation of various [SOURCE: Page_80]

management practices to conserve MED diversity (Gil et al., 2016). These findings oppose some expected [SOURCE: Page_80]

assumptions that local males are preferred as mates (Fisher, 1930; G. C. Williams, 1966) and counter the [SOURCE: Page_80]

genotypic observations made by Largiadèr & Scholl (1996) and Barbat-Leterrier et al. (1989), which [SOURCE: Page_80]

respectively suggested that mating system between MED and ATL trouts could be biased toward [SOURCE: Page_80]

homogamy or simply be random. However, even if heterogamous mating is more likely in the populations [SOURCE: Page_80]

studied; the actual mate choice will be environment-dependent. Mating systems could therefore be very [SOURCE: Page_80]

different in other environments and observations made by Largiadèr & Scholl (1996) and Barbat-Leterrier [SOURCE: Page_80]

et al. (1989) be true elsewhere. Nevertheless, mating systems cannot be solely inferred from a posteriori [SOURCE: Page_80]

genotypic analyses as usually done (Metcalf et al., 2008; E. Rubidge et al., 2005) since selection could have [SOURCE: Page_80]

already wiped out a part of the genetic variation. [SOURCE: Page_80]

2.4.2   GxE interactions [SOURCE: Page_80]

In his thesis manuscript (Gil, 2015), J. Gil investigated patterns of offspring survival at egg stage in Salmo [SOURCE: Page_80]

trutta with respect to parental genotypes – using markers related to the original MED and ATL lineages – [SOURCE: Page_80]

in interaction with river temperature to detect potential GxE interactions. During my own PhD, I used Gil’s [SOURCE: Page_80]

data and analyses to write a publication in order to make these results accessible to the scientific [SOURCE: Page_80]

69 [SOURCE: Page_80]

2.4 The eco-evolutionary mechanisms driving hybridization [SOURCE: Page_81]

community (Folio et al., 2021). I here included parts of this publication, because these results are central [SOURCE: Page_81]

to model the effects of selection on the product of hybridization. [SOURCE: Page_81]

Selection can act very strongly during early stages of development in salmonid species, notably as a [SOURCE: Page_81]

function of temperature (Huuskonen et al., 2003; Ojanguren & Braña, 2003; Régnier et al., 2013), a factor [SOURCE: Page_81]

that can show contrast at small scales (Brown & Hannah, 2008; Daigle et al., 2016). Offspring from the ATL [SOURCE: Page_81]

lineage were expected to be adapted to relatively warm temperatures (8-10°C) for pre-hatching survival [SOURCE: Page_81]

as already demonstrated (Jungwirth & Winkler, 1984; Ojanguren & Braña, 2003; Régnier et al., 2013), a [SOURCE: Page_81]

thermal range often encountered in their distribution area. For the MED lineage, which evolved [SOURCE: Page_81]

experiencing a wider range of contrasting temperatures (Daigle et al., 2016), we hypothesised that their [SOURCE: Page_81]

offspring should at least outperform ATL offspring in cold water rate (6 to 4°C) since ATL offspring display [SOURCE: Page_81]

very low survival at such temperatures (Ojanguren & Braña, 2003; Régnier et al., 2013). To assess the real [SOURCE: Page_81]

importance of temperature on post-zygotic selection, our experiment was performed directly in natural [SOURCE: Page_81]

environments where substantial temperature contrasts are observed during winter (Burt et al., 2011). In [SOURCE: Page_81]

that way, other uncontrolled known and unknown factors can interactively affect survival, and any [SOURCE: Page_81]

significant effect of temperature variation on offspring survival can therefore be deemed as an important [SOURCE: Page_81]

driver of natural evolution (J. T. Anderson et al., 2014; Genovart, 2009). [SOURCE: Page_81]

2.4.2.1    Materials and methods [SOURCE: Page_81]

Genitors sampling and study sites.        This experiment was performed in the Haute-Savoie region, [SOURCE: Page_81]

France, part of the Rhone river basin, originally occupied by the MED lineage of Salmo trutta. In this region, [SOURCE: Page_81]

a century of ATL Salmo trutta introduction has generally led to the presence of ATL individuals located [SOURCE: Page_81]

upstream, HYB individuals located in the intermediate parts of the rivers, and MED individuals located [SOURCE: Page_81]

downstream in the confluence areas (Figure 2 – 7). Adult Salmo trutta were sampled from the Overan [SOURCE: Page_82]

Creek, a four kilometres long tributary of the Borne River (46.55’57.68N’’;6°24’07.40E’’, Figure 2 – 12 A, [SOURCE: Page_82]

B) that harbours sufficient genetic variation required for the present study. Adults were selected on robe [SOURCE: Page_82]

criteria, which can be used to some extent as proxies of genetic lineages characterisation (Table 2 – 1). [SOURCE: Page_82]

environments. For more details on the protocol see (Folio et al., 2021). [SOURCE: Page_82]
