For each of the nine females, three of each expected genotype (MED, HYB, and ATL), clutches were divided into four batches. 72 [SOURCE: Page_83]

Chapter 2. The specific case of brown trout: the MED/ATL hybridization complex [SOURCE: Page_84]

Genotypic studies.       To assess parental genotype, genitors pelvic fins were clipped and analyzed at six [SOURCE: Page_84]

diagnostic markers (four single nucleotide polymorphism loci: OMM1144, OMM1105, OMM1154 and [SOURCE: Page_84]

OMM117 and two microsatellites: str^541 and str^591) perfectly differentiating the ATL and the MED [SOURCE: Page_84]

lineages in Salmo trutta (Gharbi et al., 2006; Guyomard & Caudron, 2008). Offspring genotype was not [SOURCE: Page_84]

assessed. A genotypic score was attributed to each parent and determined based on the allele number [SOURCE: Page_84]

of each lineage on all loci. An individual presenting 12 MED alleles was given a genotypic score of 1, [SOURCE: Page_84]

deemed as a pure MED genotype, whereas an individual with 12 ATL alleles was given a genotypic score [SOURCE: Page_84]

of 0, hence deemed as a pure ATL genotype. All other intermediate scores were referred to as HYB [SOURCE: Page_84]

genotypes. Although genotypic scores were obtained posterior to fertilisation, they were indeed strongly [SOURCE: Page_84]

correlated to robe criteria, ensuring that our fertilisation protocol allowed us to cross parents of the [SOURCE: Page_84]

desired genotypic scores (Folio et al., 2021; Supplementary Material 4). [SOURCE: Page_84]

Statistical analyses.    We analysed offspring survival probability 𝑝 (ratio of the surviving fry number [SOURCE: Page_84]

conditional on the initial egg number placed in each incubation box) as a function of parental GxE [SOURCE: Page_84]

interactions using mixed-effects logistic regression. To do so, we tested for linear effects of maternal (𝑀𝐺) [SOURCE: Page_84]

and paternal (𝑃𝐺 ) genotypes – i.e. genotypic score – in interaction with a linear effect of average [SOURCE: Page_84]

temperature during the incubation period (𝑇°) for the incubation box 𝑙. Since rivers presented contrasted [SOURCE: Page_84]

temperatures, the required time to reach 400 degree-days varied significantly among incubation boxes. [SOURCE: Page_84]

Therefore, we decided to control for the number of days spent in the incubation box before hatching [SOURCE: Page_84]

(𝐷𝑎𝑦𝑠). We also considered egg size (𝐸), as well as the occurrence of Saprolegnia (𝑆), to respectively [SOURCE: Page_84]

control for maternal investment and disease-related effects on offspring survival. We tested for other [SOURCE: Page_84]

targeted interactions of interest between both parental genotypes and temperature, and between egg [SOURCE: Page_84]

size, maternal genotype, and temperature. Pseudo replication related to the identity of males (𝛼𝑖 ) and [SOURCE: Page_84]

females (𝛽𝑗 ) was treated using random effects. We also included a random effect for the three rivers (𝛾𝑘 ) [SOURCE: Page_84]

73 [SOURCE: Page_84]

2.4 The eco-evolutionary mechanisms driving hybridization [SOURCE: Page_85]

to effectively disentangle pure temperature effects from other unknown environmental factors. Statistical [SOURCE: Page_85]

inference was conducted in the Bayesian framework using Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) techniques [SOURCE: Page_85]

as implemented in JAGS software (Plummer, 2003). For more details, see Folio et al. (2021). [SOURCE: Page_85]

2.4.2.2   Results [SOURCE: Page_85]

Most of the factors of interest and most of their interactions were significantly different from zero, [SOURCE: Page_85]

pointing at complex patterns of variation in offspring survival probability. It is noteworthy that the only [SOURCE: Page_85]

non-significant interactions were those including both maternal and paternal genotypes. All other [SOURCE: Page_85]

interactions including maternal genotype and temperature were significant, indicating that GxE [SOURCE: Page_85]

interactions occurred (see Table 1 in Folio et al., 2021). Figure 2 – 14 describes the predicted distribution [SOURCE: Page_85]

of survival probability of offspring, as a function of parental genotypes, temperature, and egg size, [SOURCE: Page_85]

allowing us to envision the shape and strength of the GxE interactions. In general, offspring survival was [SOURCE: Page_85]

higher at warm temperatures than at cold temperatures. For the largest eggs (5.25 mm), at cold (4.5°C) [SOURCE: Page_85]

temperature, the survival probability for offspring of MED females was at least three times higher than [SOURCE: Page_85]

that of offspring of ATL females, but this difference nearly disappeared for the smallest eggs (3.75 mm). [SOURCE: Page_85]

At intermediate temperature (6°C), offspring from MED females still had a slightly higher survival [SOURCE: Page_85]

probability than offspring from ATL females, whatever the egg size. At warm temperatures (7.5°C), the [SOURCE: Page_85]

relationship between maternal genotype and survival disappeared, as well as the effect of egg size. The [SOURCE: Page_85]

paternal genotype effect, though weak, was mainly expressed at high and intermediate temperatures, [SOURCE: Page_85]

favouring offspring sired by ATL males, hinting at another potential GxE interaction. The addition of the [SOURCE: Page_85]

female and male GxE interactions implied that offspring survival was often the highest for hybrid [SOURCE: Page_85]

descendants sired by ATL males and MED females, whereas other hybrids (different crosses) displayed [SOURCE: Page_85]

intermediate survival in most cases. [SOURCE: Page_85]

74 [SOURCE: Page_85]

Chapter 2. The specific case of brown trout: the MED/ATL hybridization complex [SOURCE: Page_86]

75 [SOURCE: Page_86]

2.4 The eco-evolutionary mechanisms driving hybridization [SOURCE: Page_87]

Figure 2—14: Predictions of offspring survival probability at low (4.5°C), medium (6°C), and high (7.5°C) temperatures as a function of maternal genotypic score, for ATL (0), HYB (0.5), and MED (1) paternal genotypes. Values for the smallest (3.5 cm) and largest (5.25 cm) eggs found in our sample are represented separately to account for egg size variation. Solid lines are posterior means while dotted lines are 95% credible intervals. Predictions were performed using male ID 23, female ID 9, and the Dranse River as random effects. The effect of Saprolegnia was discarded from the predictions. [SOURCE: Page_87]

2.4.2.3   Discussion [SOURCE: Page_87]

Evidence of gene-by-environment (GxE) interactions.               As hypothesised, temperature contrasts [SOURCE: Page_87]

between rivers substantially affected offspring survival in our experiment. Offspring generally had higher [SOURCE: Page_87]

survival at the highest temperature studied, which falls within the range of optimal temperatures for the [SOURCE: Page_87]

survival of Salmo trutta during the pre-hatching stage (Jungwirth & Winkler, 1984; Ojanguren & Braña, [SOURCE: Page_87]

2003). Ojanguren & Braña, (2003) reported an average 70% decrease in ATL offspring survival (at egg [SOURCE: Page_87]

stage) for a decrease of temperature from 7.5 to 4.5°C, very much in line with our findings for ATL females. [SOURCE: Page_87]

In particular, for the ATL lineage, offspring from larger eggs performed worse than those from smaller [SOURCE: Page_87]

eggs at low temperatures, confirming previous experimental results in captivity (Régnier et al., 2013). [SOURCE: Page_87]

However, for the same egg size, offspring from MED females had a three times higher survival probability [SOURCE: Page_87]

than offspring from ATL females at cold temperatures. This constitutes a major selective advantage in cold [SOURCE: Page_87]

environments for carriers of MED maternal genes. It is also noteworthy that, generally, egg size is [SOURCE: Page_87]

positively correlated to female body size in salmonids, as is fecundity (Lobon‐Cervia et al., 1997): large [SOURCE: Page_87]

females lay large and numerous eggs, and therefore can massively contribute to population growth. To [SOURCE: Page_87]

our knowledge, there are no documented differences between lineages regarding this size-dependent [SOURCE: Page_87]

allocation strategy. Thus, the present GxE interaction, mediated by maternal investment, we here [SOURCE: Page_87]

uncovered may fundamentally alter the genetic structure of populations through boosted population [SOURCE: Page_87]

growth by strongly favouring MED maternal ascendency in cold environments. [SOURCE: Page_87]

76 [SOURCE: Page_87]

Chapter 2. The specific case of brown trout: the MED/ATL hybridization complex [SOURCE: Page_88]

Focusing on the effects of paternal genotype now, we also found a GxE interaction involving temperature [SOURCE: Page_88]

contrast. Genes from ATL males tended to slightly improve offspring survival, but more so at warm [SOURCE: Page_88]

temperatures. The strength of this effect however was much lesser than the above-mentioned female [SOURCE: Page_88]

GxE interaction, a somewhat logical outcome since maternal effects are often stronger than paternal [SOURCE: Page_88]

effects during earlier phases of development (R. S. Burton et al., 2006; T. Burton et al., 2020; Huuskonen [SOURCE: Page_88]

et al., 2003; Régnier, Bolliet, et al., 2012). The fact that we did not find any significant interactive effect [SOURCE: Page_88]

between paternal and maternal genotypes seems to preclude any assumption regarding the possible [SOURCE: Page_88]

benefits of increased heterozygosity (Dahl et al., 2006; Edmands, 2007; Fraser et al., 2008; Meldgaard et [SOURCE: Page_88]

al., 2007; Weatherhead, 1999; Wells et al., 2019), such as higher diversity at functional loci like MHC (Jacob [SOURCE: Page_88]

et al., 2010; Landry et al., 2001; Turner et al., 2009 but see Labonne et al., 2016; Tentelier et al., 2017). [SOURCE: Page_88]

Therefore, ATL males may carry good genes – for some environments - that MED males do not have. One [SOURCE: Page_88]

possible speculation to explain this is to turn to the SEX locus in salmonids (Yano et al., 2012, 2013), which [SOURCE: Page_88]

is only present in males: some beneficial genes could be segregated with this locus only in the ATL lineage. [SOURCE: Page_88]

Potential consequences on diversity and reproductive isolation.             It is noteworthy that even if our [SOURCE: Page_88]

experiment was realised on a small spatial scale, we may have found footprints of adaptation to [SOURCE: Page_88]

temperature wherein some genotypes performed better in cold environments than others; where other [SOURCE: Page_88]

studies (also focusing on the embryonic stage) involving a single lineage failed to do so (Clark et al., 2013; [SOURCE: Page_88]

Stelkens et al., 2012). While evidence for adaptation to cold conditions in high altitudes was demonstrated [SOURCE: Page_88]

at the species level, for Salmo trutta (Jungwirth & Winkler, 1984) or Salvelinus alpinus (Arctic char, [SOURCE: Page_88]

(Huuskonen et al., 2003)), lineage-related adaptation to temperature is to our knowledge not [SOURCE: Page_88]

documented. Low temperatures are often reached in alpine systems, such as the Dranse d’Abondance, [SOURCE: Page_88]

where the MED lineage evolved since the post-glacial period (Bernatchez, 2001). This potential lineage- [SOURCE: Page_88]

related adaptation to cold conditions indicates in our case that despite several generations in sympatry, [SOURCE: Page_88]

77 [SOURCE: Page_88]

2.4 The eco-evolutionary mechanisms driving hybridization [SOURCE: Page_89]

gene flow has not erased the link between the set of 6 diagnostic markers that were previously designed [SOURCE: Page_89]

on the two separate lineages and genes under potential selection (de Lafontaine et al., 2015; Fitzpatrick [SOURCE: Page_89]

et al., 2020; Lamaze et al., 2012). Of particular interest is the fact that robe criteria were well correlated [SOURCE: Page_89]

to our genotypic score, whereas recent results indicate that such an approach may still miss a large part [SOURCE: Page_89]

of the recent genetic admixture (Saint-Pé et al., 2019). This indicates that additional investigations of [SOURCE: Page_89]

lineage differences regarding adaptation to cold (MED) or warm (ATL) temperatures could produce even [SOURCE: Page_89]

more insightful data to further our understanding of intraspecific diversity dynamics in hybrid zones, and [SOURCE: Page_89]

to understand how thermal environment could control for post-zygotic reproductive isolation (Leitwein [SOURCE: Page_89]

et al., 2016). Given the recent progress in the taxonomic status of Brown trout (Hashemzadeh Segherloo [SOURCE: Page_89]

et al., 2021), it is also likely that such hybridization events, through their consequences on fitness [SOURCE: Page_89]

variation, are central to the evolution of the whole species (or species complex). [SOURCE: Page_89]

2.4.3   Other potential drivers [SOURCE: Page_89]

Other mechanisms have been hypothesised to contribute to the evolution of diversity dynamics in the [SOURCE: Page_89]

MED-ATL complex. Some of these mechanisms have been ruled out while others remain unexplored. [SOURCE: Page_89]

 Fitness differences.                More fitness differences between both lineages might exist later [SOURCE: Page_89]

on in life. In his PhD work (Gil, 2015), using angler catches, Gil indicates that captured MED [SOURCE: Page_89]

individuals are generally older than captured ATL individuals suggesting differential survival at [SOURCE: Page_89]

adult stage. MED individuals may live longer and hence have a higher lifetime reproductive [SOURCE: Page_89]

success, potentially contributing more to the population than ATL individuals do. Other fitness [SOURCE: Page_89]

differences may arise from local adaptation, as it is possible in Salmonids (Fraser et al., 2011). The [SOURCE: Page_89]

MED lineage, native to the Mediterranean area, could be locally adapted to specific mountainous [SOURCE: Page_89]

78 [SOURCE: Page_89]

Chapter 2. The specific case of brown trout: the MED/ATL hybridization complex [SOURCE: Page_90]

environments. They may have been at an advantage compared to domesticated introduced ATL [SOURCE: Page_90]

individuals during stocking. In addition, it has been reported that after their introduction [SOURCE: Page_90]

domesticated ATL individuals were less likely to survive compared to MED native individuals [SOURCE: Page_90]

(Caudron & Champigneulle, 2002). [SOURCE: Page_90]

 Sensitivity to fishing.            Some studies (J. L. García-Marín et al., 1998; Mezzera & [SOURCE: Page_90]

Largiadèr, 2001) indicate that domesticated individuals, as well as hybrids, are more represented [SOURCE: Page_90]

within an angler catch, suggesting different catchabilities of individuals based on their origin. For [SOURCE: Page_90]

example, in cutthroat trout, catchability is positively correlated with the level of domestication [SOURCE: Page_90]

(Dwyer, 1990). In the MED/ATL hybridization complex, some studies have pointed toward a higher [SOURCE: Page_90]

catchability of ATL individuals compared to MED ones (Caudron, Champigneulle, et al., 2012). This [SOURCE: Page_90]

could influence the relative abundance of each lineage and cause variable gene flow between [SOURCE: Page_90]

populations. When stocking happened, this could have favoured native MED individuals over [SOURCE: Page_90]

introduced ATL ones. [SOURCE: Page_90]

However, after many years in sympatry, these observations might not hold since individuals from both [SOURCE: Page_90]

lineages would have undergone many generations of selection in the same environment. [SOURCE: Page_90]

 Female choice for spawning habitat.                 In some typical fast-flowing and steep streams, [SOURCE: Page_90]

such as the Chevenne Creek, it has been observed that MED females use a high diversity of [SOURCE: Page_90]

spawning habitats with five main types (Champigneulle et al., 2003). For instance, MED females [SOURCE: Page_90]

as often select unusual spawning habitats – such as “side pools” – as classical riffle habitats. In [SOURCE: Page_90]

addition, a high percentage of nests (65%) are built in protected areas where water velocity is [SOURCE: Page_90]

low. Sheltered spawning substrates were usually preferred compared to unsheltered ones. A [SOURCE: Page_90]

flood destroyed a part of the nests indicating that unusual and sheltered habitats were less [SOURCE: Page_90]

79 [SOURCE: Page_90]

2.5 The importance of eco-evolutionary mechanisms in the evolution of diversity [SOURCE: Page_91]

destroyed than other more classical habitats. Genotypic analyses on various nest sites also [SOURCE: Page_91]

indicated that ATL females would more generally select classical spawning habitats for [SOURCE: Page_91]

reproduction (Scimabio Interface, Com. Pers.), suggesting that habitat could constitute an [SOURCE: Page_91]

important post-zygotic reproductive isolation between the two lineages. Indeed, the MED habitat [SOURCE: Page_91]

diversification strategy might be advantageous as it might lower the chances of nest scouring and [SOURCE: Page_91]

give a higher survival chance to offspring from the MED lineage, which will be recruited in the [SOURCE: Page_91]

next generation. This could explain the persistence of native MED populations in some rivers. [SOURCE: Page_91]

Almodovar et al. (2006) also evidenced a correlation between introgression and factors related to [SOURCE: Page_91]

habitats such as flow, conductivity, bicarbonates concentration and pH, with stable flow and [SOURCE: Page_91]

“fertile” water being favourable to ATL individuals. [SOURCE: Page_91]

## 2.5 THE IMPORTANCE OF ECO-EVOLUTIONARY MECHANISMS IN THE EVOLUTION OF DIVERSITY

In the Mediterranean area, intensive stocking from non-native origins led to various outcomes of [SOURCE: Page_91]

hybridization with the native MED lineage, going from MED resilience to full hybridization, and in some [SOURCE: Page_91]

cases total extirpation. In addition, the implementation of various native conservation practices also led [SOURCE: Page_91]

to many unexpected outcomes regarding intraspecific diversity. Results suggested that many EEMs – [SOURCE: Page_91]

behavioural and selective factors – could have interacted with management practices to drive the [SOURCE: Page_91]

evolution of intraspecific diversity in the MED-ATL context. Indeed, in natural environments, behavioural [SOURCE: Page_91]

and selective factors are some substantial driving forces. [SOURCE: Page_91]

On the one hand, reproductive interactions are known to drive evolutionary dynamics (Maan & [SOURCE: Page_91]

Seehausen, 2011). Assuming random, homogamous or heterogamous mating might completely change [SOURCE: Page_91]

the outcome of diversity as it may, for example, play a role in shaping genetic differentiation between [SOURCE: Page_91]

80 [SOURCE: Page_91]

Chapter 2. The specific case of brown trout: the MED/ATL hybridization complex [SOURCE: Page_92]

populations (Soularue & Kremer, 2014). Moreover, mate choice strongly depends on population structure [SOURCE: Page_92]

and hence available intraspecific diversity, making the outcome challenging to predict anyway, yet rarely [SOURCE: Page_92]

random (Klug & Stone, 2021). Therefore, knowing the existence of heterogamous female preference (Gil [SOURCE: Page_92]

et al., 2015), it is essential to investigate the evolution of diversity dynamics considering the dynamic [SOURCE: Page_92]

aspect of mating systems in order to grasp the actual role of mating preference in structuring diversity [SOURCE: Page_92]

dynamics. [SOURCE: Page_92]

On the other hand, genotype-by-environment interactions could drive post-zygotic reproductive isolation [SOURCE: Page_92]

mechanisms, and result in a mosaic of intraspecific diversity patterns, depending on the local environment [SOURCE: Page_92]

(Folio et al., 2021). As natural selection is an important evolutionary mechanism shaping patterns of [SOURCE: Page_92]

diversity (G. C. Williams, 1966), understanding GxE interactions is paramount to predict the evolution of [SOURCE: Page_92]

diversity dynamics in a specific context. Indeed, information on the relative fitness of each category of [SOURCE: Page_92]

individuals (native, hybrids, non-native) under various environmental conditions could help to predict the [SOURCE: Page_92]

expected rate and extent of adaptive introgression (Barrett & Schluter, 2008; Hedrick, 2013). It is [SOURCE: Page_92]

therefore important to grasp the role of GxE interactions and environmental conditions – i.e. selective [SOURCE: Page_92]

pressures – in structuring diversity dynamics. [SOURCE: Page_92]

In our case, depending on selective pressure experienced in a given environment – may they be biotic for [SOURCE: Page_92]

inter-individual interactions or abiotic for environmental variables – reproductive isolation between the [SOURCE: Page_92]

MED and the ATL lineages may fluctuate in space and time. Consequently, it is clear that the underlying [SOURCE: Page_92]

EEMs cannot be discarded when implementing management practices, as not accounting for such driving [SOURCE: Page_92]

forces could jeopardise management efficiency. In addition, considering such specific knowledge could [SOURCE: Page_92]

help build more efficient and evolutionary-inspired management strategies, which could be context- [SOURCE: Page_92]

dependent. [SOURCE: Page_92]

81 [SOURCE: Page_92]

2.5 The importance of eco-evolutionary mechanisms in the evolution of diversity [SOURCE: Page_93]

However, acknowledging the existence of such mechanisms is insufficient to actually measure their [SOURCE: Page_93]

impact on hybridization and diversity dynamics, with or without considering interventions of fisheries [SOURCE: Page_93]

managers. To grasp the actual role of these EEMs, one has to actually project and consider how they will [SOURCE: Page_93]

interact with the other ecological, genetic, and demographic drivers, among which management [SOURCE: Page_93]

practices. Therefore, the aim of my PhD is to use a DG-ABM approach to integrate all the knowledge [SOURCE: Page_93]

available, including these EEMs, so to in fine be able to answer one question: do eco-evolutionary [SOURCE: Page_93]

mechanisms really matter for the management of intraspecific diversity? To answer this question, I will [SOURCE: Page_93]

use a DG-ABM model I developed during my PhD to evaluate whether these mechanisms really impact [SOURCE: Page_93]

the evolution of diversity on short time scales, as we expect them to, considering the contemporary and [SOURCE: Page_93]

rapid interplay between ecology and evolution (Carroll et al., 2007; N. G. J. Hairston et al., 2005; M. T. [SOURCE: Page_93]

Kinnison & Hairston Jr, 2007; Pelletier et al., 2009; Reznick, Losos, et al., 2019; Reznick, Bassar, et al., [SOURCE: Page_93]

2019). [SOURCE: Page_93]

The next chapter focuses on the construction of this DG-ABM model: MEDITERRANEA, which allows for [SOURCE: Page_93]

the consideration of known EEMs – and their genetic basis – as well as management practices and specific [SOURCE: Page_93]

environmental variations. More specifically, this chapter will describe the model's purpose, assumptions [SOURCE: Page_93]

and specific processes, as well as how to use it via its user-friendly graphical interface. [SOURCE: Page_93]

82 [SOURCE: Page_93]

## 3 MEDITERRANEA: A DG-ABM TO SIMULATE THE EVOLUTION OF INTRASPECIFIC DIVERSITY IN BROWN TROUT

My first goal was to develop a demo genetic agent-based model to understand and predict the evolution [SOURCE: Page_96]

of intraspecific diversity dynamics between two genetically distinct lineages of Salmo trutta: the [SOURCE: Page_96]

Mediterranean (MED) and the Atlantic (ATL) lineages. While another modelisation framework has been [SOURCE: Page_96]

developed for brown trout in the Mediterranean area (in Spain, Ayllón et al., 2016), their specific [SOURCE: Page_96]

objectives do not match managers’ nor ours. In addition, the degree of precision of both models varies, [SOURCE: Page_96]

and managers may not be able to inform certain parameters owing to Ayllón et al., (2016) model. [SOURCE: Page_96]

Furthermore, their model does not implement different lineages, which is the essence of this work. For [SOURCE: Page_96]

instance, the core idea of this approach is to include eco-evolutionary concepts in the management of [SOURCE: Page_96]

intraspecific diversity in order to account for previously demonstrated lineage-based behavioural and [SOURCE: Page_96]

fitness differences since they will interact with management practices and may play an important role in [SOURCE: Page_96]

hybridization dynamics. This modelling framework allows to consider inter-individual variability in life- [SOURCE: Page_96]

history traits and behaviour, as well as their genetic basis, and to implement different eco-evolutionary [SOURCE: Page_96]

scenarios under various environmental conditions and management practices. This second chapter will [SOURCE: Page_96]

first give an overview of our modelling approach, and will then focus on a detailed description of our [SOURCE: Page_96]

model (based on ODD, Grimm et al., 2006, 2010), with its specific processes and possible management [SOURCE: Page_96]

practices simulations. Finally, it will describe the user-friendly interface and model initialisation. [SOURCE: Page_96]

## 3.1 AN OBJECT-ORIENTED MODELLING FRAMEWORK

MEDITERRANEA uses an object-oriented modelling framework to simulate the evolution of brown trout [SOURCE: Page_96]

intraspecific diversity on a discrete time step. Objects, also called classes, are specific modelling entities [SOURCE: Page_96]

85 [SOURCE: Page_96]

3.1 An object-oriented modelling framework [SOURCE: Page_97]

characterised by state variables and methods adapted to their internal structure. They are useful for the [SOURCE: Page_97]

simulation of ABM as they allow for a simple – realistic or conceptualise – representation of agents with [SOURCE: Page_97]

their characteristics, as well as processes. Objects are created using an initial set of state variables but are [SOURCE: Page_97]

dynamic in time. They can be accessed using a unique object reference (ID) and modified through specific [SOURCE: Page_97]

methods. Their state can be visualised at each time step throughout the simulation. [SOURCE: Page_97]

Figure 3—1: Representation of the main objects used in MEDITERRANEA to simulate its virtual environment. [SOURCE: Page_97]

MEDITERRANEA uses a wide collection of objects to describe and manipulate the components of its virtual [SOURCE: Page_97]

environment – habitat, individual, genome… (Figure 3 – 1). For example, habitat is described using two [SOURCE: Page_97]

types of topological "node" objects – reaches and weirs –, which store information regarding spatial [SOURCE: Page_97]

coordinates, and neighbouring node objects. Individuals are modelled as fish objects, which state [SOURCE: Page_97]

variables can be a unique ID, life history traits values, or even a genotype. Individual processes, such as [SOURCE: Page_97]

survival or movement, are methods of fish objects, called individually for each fish object. Hatch objects [SOURCE: Page_97]

86 [SOURCE: Page_97]

Chapter 3. MEDITERRANEA: A DG-ABM to simulate the evolution of intraspecific diversity in brown trout [SOURCE: Page_98]

are used to model groups of individuals sharing similar characteristics (time of creation, habitat…). They [SOURCE: Page_98]

are created to simulate cohorts of juveniles in a specific location, as they can be excessively numerous. [SOURCE: Page_98]

This represents a way to generate a lower number of objects to accelerate simulation time and save [SOURCE: Page_98]

memory. Finally, species objects are used to define the genome structure of a fish object and are [SOURCE: Page_98]

characterised by a genetic map, allelic diversity and effect as well as kinship (when multiple species are [SOURCE: Page_98]

defined). All individuals belonging to the same species share the same genetic architecture. There is only [SOURCE: Page_98]

one species object in MEDITERRANEA as the model only implements one species. [SOURCE: Page_98]

MEDITERRANEA runs under CAPSIS-4, a simulation platform initially used for forestry models that uses a [SOURCE: Page_98]

Java language. CAPSIS-4 has been developed by the AMAP laboratory since 1999. It is a user-friendly tool [SOURCE: Page_98]

(graphical interface) for scientists and managers. Various types of models can be implemented in CAPSIS- [SOURCE: Page_98]

4 and simulations can be run in an interactive (graphical user interface) or script mode. In the graphical [SOURCE: Page_98]

user interface, all or a subset of time steps can be visualised, and all objects and variables of interest are [SOURCE: Page_98]

observable through different graphical options (Section 3.6.2). In the script mode, the user can only record [SOURCE: Page_98]

a set of individual or population level variables of his choice over time. Since 2004, CAPSIS-4 has also been [SOURCE: Page_98]

used to model fish populations' eco-evolutionary dynamics (Labonne et al., 2008; Labonne & Hendry, [SOURCE: Page_98]

2010), from which the MEDITERRANEA model is derived. A software package for MEDITERRANEA is [SOURCE: Page_98]

available under the CAPSIS-4 simulation platform and as an executable file, which only contains the [SOURCE: Page_98]

CAPSIS-4 simulation platform and MEDITERRANEA (model, graphical interface and If required original [SOURCE: Page_98]

codes), allowing users to install and interact with the model interface. Java and Java Runtime Environment [SOURCE: Page_98]

(JRE) have to be installed. CAPSIS-4 offers a detailed installation guide, as well as general documentation [SOURCE: Page_98]

on the software (http://capsis.cirad.fr/capsis/documentation). Online documentation is also available for [SOURCE: Page_98]

the          MEDITERRANEA              module            at          the          following          address [SOURCE: Page_98]

(http://capsis.cirad.fr/capsis/help_en/mediterranea). [SOURCE: Page_98]

87 [SOURCE: Page_98]

## 3.2 MODEL OVERVIEW

MEDITERRANEA is a simulation tool that aims at investigating the evolution of brown trout intraspecific [SOURCE: Page_99]

diversity while considering the effect of (i) EEMs, (ii) environmental conditions and (iii) management [SOURCE: Page_99]

practices on hybridization dynamics. It represents explicitly the entire life cycle of brown trout (Figure 2 – [SOURCE: Page_99]

1) with individual life-history traits from birth to death, reproduction and traits transmission between [SOURCE: Page_99]

generations, in a spatially explicit environment. Individual processes, such as survival and growth, are [SOURCE: Page_99]

explicitly modelled and calibrated based on general knowledge available for brown trout, as well as [SOURCE: Page_99]

specific data originating from MED and HYB populations of the Mediterranean area (Scimabio Interface [SOURCE: Page_99]

and the Fishing Federation FDPPMA 74). [SOURCE: Page_99]

3.2.1    Scales of the model [SOURCE: Page_99]

Organisational scales.       This model is multi-scale and three levels of complexity are considered: (i) the [SOURCE: Page_99]

gene level, (ii) the individual level and (iii) the population level. At the gene level, seven bi-allelic neutral [SOURCE: Page_99]

loci are implemented, each allele belonging to a specific lineage in order to track the evolution of genetic [SOURCE: Page_99]

diversity dynamic. Information at the gene level codes for a trait related to lineages and hence a genotypic [SOURCE: Page_99]

category (in our case, we defined: MED, HYB or ATL), which will influence reproductive behaviour and [SOURCE: Page_99]

fitness at the individual level. Most processes – survival, growth, reproduction… – happens at the [SOURCE: Page_99]

individual level. Therefore, the performance of different individuals – in terms of reproduction and [SOURCE: Page_99]

survival – will vary based on the biotic and abiotic environment they experience. This will in turn influence [SOURCE: Page_99]

the population level, referred to as a group of individuals at a given time step, as it will dictate changes in [SOURCE: Page_99]

the frequency distributions of alleles and genotypes in the population. Changes at the population level [SOURCE: Page_99]

will then influence the biotic and abiotic environment experienced by individuals and consequently affect [SOURCE: Page_99]

both the individual and the gene level. Over generations, natural and sexual selection will act together to [SOURCE: Page_99]

88 [SOURCE: Page_99]

Chapter 3. MEDITERRANEA: A DG-ABM to simulate the evolution of intraspecific diversity in brown trout [SOURCE: Page_100]

select advantageous genetic variations. The population level can represent the entire population of the [SOURCE: Page_100]

hydrographic network or that of one reach or a sub-group of reaches. [SOURCE: Page_100]

Time scale. Model dynamics are based on simulating the life cycle of brown trout. Individual processes [SOURCE: Page_100]

happen on a discrete temporal scale with continuous processes – survival, growth, ageing and migration [SOURCE: Page_100]

– happening on a monthly time step and discrete events – mate choice and reproduction – taking place [SOURCE: Page_100]

at given time steps during the reproductive period. [SOURCE: Page_100]

3.2.2   Model architecture [SOURCE: Page_100]

To model its virtual environment, MEDITERRANEA explicitly represents habitat, individuals, groups of [SOURCE: Page_100]

individuals and genome structure. [SOURCE: Page_100]

3.2.2.1   Modelling space and habitat [SOURCE: Page_100]

Space is implemented as a spatially explicit hydrographic network composed of two types of habitat [SOURCE: Page_100]

elements: reaches and weirs (Figure 3 – 2). Reaches are assumed to be units of river habitat, large enough [SOURCE: Page_100]

to represent an individual home range. Habitat is homogeneous within each reach but can differ between [SOURCE: Page_100]

reaches. Weirs are positioned between reaches and can be virtual connection points or represent real [SOURCE: Page_100]

barriers. While water discharge is not modelled, altitude is accounted for and will influence temperature [SOURCE: Page_100]

dynamics. The hydrographic network is oriented: it has one single root, which can be considered as the [SOURCE: Page_100]

mouth of the river, or simply an outlet if only a part of a watershed is envisioned. [SOURCE: Page_100]

Modelling reaches.     Each reach is characterised by several habitat variables informing on its surface, [SOURCE: Page_100]

altitude, quality and fishing regulations (Appendix A: Table 1). Therefore, spatial environmental variation [SOURCE: Page_100]

arises between reaches. Reach surface is calculated using reach length and mean width. Combined with [SOURCE: Page_100]

89 [SOURCE: Page_100]

3.2 Model overview [SOURCE: Page_101]

a habitat quality variable (labelled carrying factor), it determines each reach carrying capacity. Habitat [SOURCE: Page_101]

quality for reproduction affects individual decisions for reproductive movement as well as female [SOURCE: Page_101]

fecundity. Altitude is used to implement temperatures, with higher altitudes being associated with lower [SOURCE: Page_101]

temperatures. Fishing regulations are based on various variables such as regulation in force – standard [SOURCE: Page_101]

fishing or reserve mode for example – or catch size limits, which will determine the proportion and [SOURCE: Page_101]

category of individuals that may be fished. [SOURCE: Page_101]

Figure 3—2: Spatial structure of a hydrographic network in MEDITERRANEA, represented by the interconnection of two objects: Reaches and Weirs, respectively representing river sections and real barriers or virtual connection points. Each object has a father object always located downstream, which is a Reach for Weirs and a Weir for Reaches. The characterisation of a network always starts from downstream and the starting point (first object) is always a Weir. [SOURCE: Page_101]

90 [SOURCE: Page_101]

Chapter 3. MEDITERRANEA: A DG-ABM to simulate the evolution of intraspecific diversity in brown trout [SOURCE: Page_102]

Modelling weirs. A weir is placed between each pair of reach. Each weir is characterised by the presence [SOURCE: Page_102]

or absence of a fish pass and by its passability (Appendix A: Table 2) – difficulty for an individual to pass [SOURCE: Page_102]

upstream or downstream of a weir to join an adjacent reach. When the passability is total in both [SOURCE: Page_102]

directions, the weir is “virtual”, in the sense that it does not affect the simulation. [SOURCE: Page_102]

3.2.2.2   Modelling groups of individuals [SOURCE: Page_102]

As explained before, groups of individuals can be created based on a single object, for the sake of memory [SOURCE: Page_102]

consumption. This is especially useful just after reproduction, thus avoiding the creation of hundreds of [SOURCE: Page_102]

thousands of objects, as hundreds of thousands of eggs and fry can be produced. We named these groups [SOURCE: Page_102]

“hatches”, for they represent the progeny of two parents within a nest. Hatches can be used to pool [SOURCE: Page_102]

multiple individuals from egg stage to early juvenile stage, up to 8 months. After 8 months, individualised [SOURCE: Page_102]

fish are created using hatches information via a process called individualisation (Figure 3 – 3). [SOURCE: Page_102]

Figure 3—3: Individual life stages from egg stage up to juvenile stage and corresponding objects. Individuals are within a Hatch object for 8 months. Then they are individualised into fish objects. The timing and windows for hatching and emergence are indicated in yellow. [SOURCE: Page_102]

91 [SOURCE: Page_102]

3.2 Model overview [SOURCE: Page_103]

A hatch clusters the number of eggs created in a given reach at a given time step. One line represents one [SOURCE: Page_103]

mating event – i.e. the mating between one male and one female – and informs on the maternal and [SOURCE: Page_103]

paternal ID (Figure 3 – 4). Within hatches, individuals are sharing similar characteristics, such as age and [SOURCE: Page_103]

average body size (Appendix A: Table 3). They undergo the following processes: survival, growth, ageing [SOURCE: Page_103]

and ultimately individualisation. Maternal gene-by-environment selection applied at hatching is [SOURCE: Page_103]

implemented using maternal ID. As long as individuals are in hatches they cannot move between reaches [SOURCE: Page_103]

and do not have any genotypic information. At individualisation (Section 3.3.8), the number of juveniles [SOURCE: Page_103]

left in the hatch will be turned into individualised fish and parental ID used to create their genome. [SOURCE: Page_103]

Figure 3—4: Representation of a hatch object in MEDITERRANEA – representing groups of individuals – with its main state variables and methods. [SOURCE: Page_103]

3.2.2.3   Modelling individuals [SOURCE: Page_103]

Individualised fish (Figure 3 – 5) each have their own characteristics (such as size, age and genotype [SOURCE: Page_103]

Appendix A: Table 4). They undergo the following individual processes: survival, growth, movement and [SOURCE: Page_103]

reproduction. Individuals are located in a specific reach at a given time step but they can move from one [SOURCE: Page_103]

92 [SOURCE: Page_103]

Chapter 3. MEDITERRANEA: A DG-ABM to simulate the evolution of intraspecific diversity in brown trout [SOURCE: Page_104]

reach to another when a passable weir connects them (movement process). They can interact with each [SOURCE: Page_104]

other directly, via reproductive processes – including mate choice and potential male competition – or [SOURCE: Page_104]

indirectly via a density-dependent mechanism effect on growth and survival mimicking competition. [SOURCE: Page_104]

Individuals are meant to perceive environmental and phenotypic clues when making their decisions. [SOURCE: Page_104]

Individual interactions and their related processes always happen within a reach. [SOURCE: Page_104]

Figure 3—5: Representation of a fish object in MEDITERRANEA – representing an individual – with its main state variables and methods. [SOURCE: Page_104]

3.2.2.4   Modelling the genome structure [SOURCE: Page_104]

In MEDITERRANEA, there is only one species: Salmo trutta fario, hence, all individuals have the same [SOURCE: Page_104]

genome structure. Individuals are diploids and possess a nuclear genome made of seven bi-allelic unlinked [SOURCE: Page_104]

neutral loci for which each allele is related to a specific lineage. In the model, the genetic structure to [SOURCE: Page_104]

differentiate alleles from each lineage is coded in a binary way with ATL alleles valued at 1 and MED ones [SOURCE: Page_104]

valued at 0 (Figure 3 – 6). This is the lineage information transmitted to offspring, which inherit two alleles [SOURCE: Page_104]

93 [SOURCE: Page_104]

3.2 Model overview [SOURCE: Page_105]

for each locus: one maternally and one paternally. In the genetic structure, the first allele in a pair is [SOURCE: Page_105]

inherited maternally and the second one paternally. [SOURCE: Page_105]

Figure 3—6: Representation of the genome structure of Salmo trutta and individual genotype with examples of corresponding allelic combinations. The genome structure is diploid and made of seven unlinked loci, here represented on different chromosomes. For each locus, individuals inherit an allele maternally and one paternally. For all loci, alleles are simply coded in a binary way: 0 to represent the MED lineage and 1 to represent the ATL lineage. [SOURCE: Page_105]

94 [SOURCE: Page_105]

Chapter 3. MEDITERRANEA: A DG-ABM to simulate the evolution of intraspecific diversity in brown trout [SOURCE: Page_106]

These 14 alleles constitute our genetic map and are used to calculate individual introgression. Individual [SOURCE: Page_106]

introgression refers to the introgression – or takeover – of MED alleles by ATL alleles within an individual [SOURCE: Page_106]

genome. This choice of genetic structure is based on the use by managers of seven known single [SOURCE: Page_106]

nucleotide polymorphism (SNP) and microsatellites loci perfectly differentiating the ATL lineage from the [SOURCE: Page_106]

MED lineage (Gharbi et al., 2006; Guyomard & Caudron, 2008). Allelic effects are equal and additive within [SOURCE: Page_106]

and between loci, i.e. dominance effects are not considered. Recombination is maximal and loci are [SOURCE: Page_106]

hypothesised to belong to separate chromosomes. Therefore the value of the resulting trait, here [SOURCE: Page_106]

individual introgression, corresponds to the mean of allelic values (1 or 0) at all loci. The value of this trait [SOURCE: Page_106]

can be used to infer a genotypic category - what I will sometimes refer to as individual genotype: MED [SOURCE: Page_106]

when individual introgression is 0, i.e. all 14 allelic values are 0 (MED allele); ATL when individual [SOURCE: Page_106]

introgression is 1, i.e. all 14 allelic values are 1 (ATL allele); HYB for all other intermediate scores of [SOURCE: Page_106]

individual introgression, i.e. presence of both MED and ATL alleles (Figure 3 – 6). This simplification is [SOURCE: Page_106]

meant to compare most of our results to results obtained by managers. [SOURCE: Page_106]

3.2.3    Process overview and scheduling (Figure 3 – 7) [SOURCE: Page_106]

During population dynamics, processes affecting habitat, individuals and groups of individuals are called. [SOURCE: Page_106]

Habitat and time are first updated. The population dynamics then start. Individuals and groups of [SOURCE: Page_106]

individuals undergo development, ageing and survival, which rates vary depending on life stages (egg, [SOURCE: Page_106]

larvae, juveniles, sub-adults and adults). Development and ageing make individuals go from one life stage [SOURCE: Page_106]

to another (Figure 3 – 8). During the early phases of life, development is temperature-dependent (Killeen [SOURCE: Page_106]

et al., 1999) and involves hatching (eggs to larvae) and emergence (larvae to juveniles). To account for [SOURCE: Page_106]

reproductive isolation barriers, a post-zygotic survival rate may be applied at hatching to implement [SOURCE: Page_106]

95 [SOURCE: Page_106]

3.2 Model overview [SOURCE: Page_107]

maternal genotype-by-temperature (referred to as GxE) interactions on embryonic survival (Folio et al., [SOURCE: Page_107]

2021). Processes happening from egg to juvenile stages are affecting hatches. [SOURCE: Page_107]

Figure 3—7: Process overview and scheduling. Population dynamics is split into two parts: the individual phase and the group phase. Orders and interactions between processes, as well as potential genetic or phenotypic and temperature effects, are represented. [SOURCE: Page_107]

After 8 months, the individualisation process happens and individuals are created using hatches [SOURCE: Page_107]

information. This process happens only once per hatch, and then the hatch disappears. After survival is [SOURCE: Page_107]

applied to individualised fish, the movement process happens for the growth period. Individuals decide [SOURCE: Page_107]

to move from one reach to another, or not, based on experienced density. Following the movement [SOURCE: Page_107]

process, density-dependence growth and ageing happen. Individual body size and age are updated. In [SOURCE: Page_107]

96 [SOURCE: Page_107]

Chapter 3. MEDITERRANEA: A DG-ABM to simulate the evolution of intraspecific diversity in brown trout [SOURCE: Page_108]

November, the maturation process takes place. Following maturation, individuals engage in the [SOURCE: Page_108]

movement process for reproduction, which happens in two main steps. First, individuals decide to move [SOURCE: Page_108]

or stay in their current reach for reproduction. Then, those who decided to move choose between [SOURCE: Page_108]

upstream movement – a decision that can be made based on habitat quality for reproduction – and [SOURCE: Page_108]

“homing”, i.e. movement toward their birth reach. From December to February – i.e. during mating [SOURCE: Page_108]

season – individuals go through reproductive processes, involving individual sampling, sexual selection [SOURCE: Page_108]

and fertilisation after a female has accepted to mate with a male. To consider potential pre-zygotic [SOURCE: Page_108]

reproductive barriers (Gil et al., 2015), multiple options of mate choice, such as phenotypic-based female [SOURCE: Page_108]

preference or male competition can be chosen from during model initialisation. When reproduction is [SOURCE: Page_108]

over, individuals who moved try to return to their last known growth reach. Following reproduction, new [SOURCE: Page_108]

hatches are created. At the end, individuals will go through a fishing process, but not individually. The [SOURCE: Page_108]

fishing method will randomly draw out a given proportion of individuals that fit fishing regulation input. [SOURCE: Page_108]

3.2.4   Model stochasticity [SOURCE: Page_108]

Since it is an agent-based model, most processes in MEDITERRANEA are inherently stochastic. Indeed, [SOURCE: Page_108]

processes such as survival or movement for example involve a part of randomness as they depend upon [SOURCE: Page_108]

the realisation of a Bernoulli random draw with a given probability. Random inter-individual interactions [SOURCE: Page_108]

are also a source of stochasticity that will influence reproduction. Indeed males are randomly sampled to [SOURCE: Page_108]

constitute a female available mating pool. Mate choice is then also a probabilistic function based on [SOURCE: Page_108]

females acceptance to mate with a given male through her expressed preference. From its stochastic [SOURCE: Page_108]

nature, the model will generate variability among simulations. [SOURCE: Page_108]

97 [SOURCE: Page_108]

3.3 Sub-models: detailed processes [SOURCE: Page_109]

## 3.3 SUB-MODELS: DETAILED PROCESSES

3.3.1   Development and ageing [SOURCE: Page_109]

Individuals or groups of individuals will develop and age, going from one life stage to the other based on [SOURCE: Page_109]

temperature or age thresholds (Figure 3 – 8). Age is counted in months since the model runs on a monthly [SOURCE: Page_109]

time step. Eggs become larvae after hatching and larvae become juveniles following emergence, based on [SOURCE: Page_109]

temperature. At 12 months, juveniles become sub-adults, and at 24 months, sub-adults become adults. [SOURCE: Page_109]

Survival and growth processes will vary based on individual stages. [SOURCE: Page_109]

Figure 3—8: Development and ageing threshold to go from one life stage to another. Under-gravel development is based on temperature and post-emergence development on age in months. Hatches are represented in light orange and individuals in light blue. [SOURCE: Page_109]

Embryonic development.           Hatches, created after reproduction, are initially at egg stage. They will [SOURCE: Page_109]

then undergo hatching and emergence processes, to become larvae and then juveniles, based on an [SOURCE: Page_109]

environmental clue: accumulated temperature. Accumulated temperature is calculated at each time step [SOURCE: Page_109]

since the creation date (i.e. birth) of each hatch and corresponds to the sum of monthly accumulated [SOURCE: Page_109]

98 [SOURCE: Page_109]

Chapter 3. MEDITERRANEA: A DG-ABM to simulate the evolution of intraspecific diversity in brown trout [SOURCE: Page_110]

temperature (in degree-days) experienced within a reach over a given number of months, up until the [SOURCE: Page_110]

current time step (Figure 3 – 9). [SOURCE: Page_110]

Figure 3—9: Accumulated winter temperature (in degree-day) and resulting hatching and emergence time for a given hatch created in December (t = %3). Monthly cumulated temperatures are indicated and summed up until emergence has happened. [SOURCE: Page_110]

The accumulated temperature required for eggs to hatch is approximately 400 degree days and [SOURCE: Page_110]

emergence usually happens after around 720 accumulated degree days (Killeen et al., 1999). As processes [SOURCE: Page_110]

happen monthly in the model, hatching and emergence will only happen when monthly and not daily [SOURCE: Page_110]

accumulated temperature will be above this threshold. This means that some individuals, meant to hatch [SOURCE: Page_110]

midway through a month, will only hatch the following month. Therefore, as we assume that all individuals [SOURCE: Page_110]

will be hatching at the beginning of a given month, we set our threshold slightly lower at respectively 380 [SOURCE: Page_110]

and 700 degree-days. [SOURCE: Page_110]

3.3.2   Survival [SOURCE: Page_110]

Survival happens monthly and is stage-dependent. The survival rate is calculated as follows for each stage: [SOURCE: Page_110]

𝐶𝑜𝑒𝑓𝑆𝑡𝑎𝑔𝑒 𝑒 𝑆𝑢𝑟𝑣𝑖𝑣𝑎𝑙 𝑟𝑎𝑡𝑒𝑠𝑡𝑎𝑔𝑒 =            𝐶𝑜𝑒𝑓𝑆𝑡𝑎𝑔𝑒     ∗ 𝐷𝐸𝑠𝑡𝑎𝑔𝑒   (Equation 3 – 1) (1+ 𝑒             ) [SOURCE: Page_110]

99 [SOURCE: Page_110]

3.3 Sub-models: detailed processes [SOURCE: Page_111]

Survival rate is the individual survival applied monthly for each stage. It is based on a stage-dependent [SOURCE: Page_111]

survival coefficient (Coefstage), which has been parameterised based on survival data on brown trout [SOURCE: Page_111]

populations located in the Mediterranean area (Data from Scimabio Interface). The survival rate is [SOURCE: Page_111]

counter-balanced by density-dependent (DE) mortality (Elliott, 1994) to mimic competition and control [SOURCE: Page_111]

for maximum carrying capacity. DE is stage-dependent, as we hypothesised that competition mainly [SOURCE: Page_111]

happens between individuals of the same stage and not among stages, as they are often found in different [SOURCE: Page_111]

types of habitats (Kaspersson et al., 2012). [SOURCE: Page_111]

For under-gravel survival, i.e. egg and larvae stage, DE values 1 as we hypothesised that survival at those [SOURCE: Page_111]

stages is not impacted by competition and resulting density-dependent mortality. [SOURCE: Page_111]

For post-emergence stages, i.e. juveniles, sub-adults and adults, DE is expressed as follows: [SOURCE: Page_111]

1 𝐷𝐸𝑠𝑡𝑎𝑔𝑒,𝑟𝑒𝑎𝑐ℎ =       𝐷𝑒𝑛𝑠𝑖𝑡𝑦𝑠𝑡𝑎𝑔𝑒,𝑟𝑒𝑎𝑐ℎ                                    (Equation 3 – 2) (1+                   ⁄ 𝐾𝑚𝑎𝑥𝐸 𝑥 𝑐𝑎𝑟𝑟𝑦𝑖𝑛𝑔 𝑓𝑎𝑐𝑡𝑜𝑟𝑟𝑒𝑎𝑐ℎ ) [SOURCE: Page_111]

For each stage, depending on the density experienced within a reach, DE will act as a more or less strong [SOURCE: Page_111]

brake on survival. Density is calculated at each time step, per reach and stage, and is expressed in [SOURCE: Page_111]

individuals per metre square (ind.m-2). KmaxE is a survival parameter that allows the user to manage [SOURCE: Page_111]

maximum carrying capacity within the hydrographic network. The default parameter is 2, which equals [SOURCE: Page_111]

approximately 3500 ind.ha-1. It has to be informed during model initialisation and bespoke to each [SOURCE: Page_111]

population, using the user interface. Indeed, exact densities matching this parameter will be highly [SOURCE: Page_111]

network-dependent as individual movement – which itself depends on weirs – can influence densities. [SOURCE: Page_111]

The following figure (3 – 10) indicates densities reached at demographic equilibrium as a function of [SOURCE: Page_111]

KMaxE in a simple hydrographic network made of only one reach. [SOURCE: Page_111]

100 [SOURCE: Page_111]

Chapter 3. MEDITERRANEA: A DG-ABM to simulate the evolution of intraspecific diversity in brown trout [SOURCE: Page_112]

Figure 3—10: Observed densities at demographic equilibrium in a simple hydrographic network (a single reach of a hectare) for various values of KmaxE. [SOURCE: Page_112]

Note that this density-dependent effect can be different between reaches. Indeed, carrying factor is a [SOURCE: Page_112]

reach variable that indicates habitat quality for resources. By multiplying KmaxE, it allows to set a specific [SOURCE: Page_112]

maximum carrying capacity for each reach. Carrying factor can be modulated using a scale parameter, [SOURCE: Page_112]

wherein 0 implies, a bad habitat hence null carrying capacity, and 1 implies a very good habitat hence [SOURCE: Page_112]

maximum carrying capacity. A value of 0.5 means that density is that reach will only reach half of the [SOURCE: Page_112]

maximum carrying capacity allowed in the hydrographic network. [SOURCE: Page_112]

Post-zygotic isolation.             In addition to the above-described mortality process, extra mortality can [SOURCE: Page_112]

be simulated to recreate known post-zygotic isolation mechanisms, as I described in the previous chapter [SOURCE: Page_112]

(Section 2.4.2) and a published manuscript (Folio et al., 2021). This recreates the interactive effect of [SOURCE: Page_112]

maternal genotype x temperature on embryonic survival, i.e. egg stage survival from birth to hatching. It [SOURCE: Page_112]

is applied once to hatches at hatching time – when its accumulated temperature is above 380 degree- [SOURCE: Page_112]

days. It is expressed as follows: [SOURCE: Page_112]

101 [SOURCE: Page_112]

3.3 Sub-models: detailed processes [SOURCE: Page_113]

𝑒 𝑃𝑜𝑠𝑡𝑍𝑦𝑔𝑜𝑡𝑖𝑐 𝐶𝑜𝑒𝑓 𝑃𝑜𝑠𝑡 − 𝑧𝑦𝑔𝑜𝑡𝑖𝑐 𝑠𝑢𝑟𝑣𝑖𝑣𝑎𝑙 𝑟𝑎𝑡𝑒 = (1+(𝑒 𝑃𝑜𝑠𝑡𝑍𝑦𝑔𝑜𝑡𝑖𝑐 𝐶𝑜𝑒𝑓                   (Equation 3 – 3) )) [SOURCE: Page_113]

𝑃𝑜𝑠𝑡𝑍𝑦𝑔𝑜𝑡𝑖𝑐 𝐶𝑜𝑒𝑓 = 𝛽 + 𝛼 ∗ 𝑤𝑖𝑛𝑡𝑒𝑟𝑇° + (𝑚𝑎𝑡𝑒𝑟𝑛𝑎𝑙 𝐼𝑛𝑡𝑟𝑜𝑔𝑟𝑒𝑠𝑠𝑖𝑜𝑛 − 0.5) ∗ (𝛿 + 𝛾 ∗ 𝑤𝑖𝑛𝑡𝑒𝑟𝑇°) (Equation 3 – 4) [SOURCE: Page_113]

Where 𝛽, 𝛼, 𝛿 and 𝛾 are the equation parameters informed in a logit scale. In the user interface, a pre- [SOURCE: Page_113]

set button exists to directly set the values found in Folio et al., 2021 for post-zygotic isolation. The [SOURCE: Page_113]

following figures show the effect that each parameter has on offspring survival rate. [SOURCE: Page_113]

𝛽 is the slope intercept. Its default value is that of egg stage survival coefficient. [SOURCE: Page_113]

Figure 3—11: Effect of slope on post-zygotic embryonic survival. [SOURCE: Page_113]

𝑎 is the parameter used to adjust the effect of mean winter temperature (winterT°) on survival. Positive [SOURCE: Page_113]

values will make this survival coefficient increase with temperature. [SOURCE: Page_113]

102 [SOURCE: Page_113]

Chapter 3. MEDITERRANEA: A DG-ABM to simulate the evolution of intraspecific diversity in brown trout [SOURCE: Page_114]

Figure 3—12: Mean winter temperature effect on post-zygotic embryonic survival. Positive or negative values of α and β can be used to determine the relationship (positive or negative) between temperature and embryonic survival. [SOURCE: Page_114]

𝛿 is the parameter used to set the effect of maternal genotype, in order to favour offspring survival of one [SOURCE: Page_114]

or the other maternal lineage. [SOURCE: Page_114]

Figure 3—13: Maternal introgression (genotype) effect on post-zygotic embryonic survival. Negative values give an advantage to the MED lineage while positive values favour the ATL lineage. [SOURCE: Page_114]

103 [SOURCE: Page_114]

3.3 Sub-models: detailed processes [SOURCE: Page_115]

𝛾 is the interaction parameter between maternal genotype and mean winter temperature. It can be [SOURCE: Page_115]

adjusted to favour one lineage in some given temperature conditions and the other in different ones. [SOURCE: Page_115]

Figure 3—14: Effect of a maternal gene by temperature interaction on embryonic survival. This combination of values allows the user to give an advantage to the MED lineage in colder winter temperatures and to the ATL lineage in warmer winter temperatures. [SOURCE: Page_115]

3.3.3   Growth [SOURCE: Page_115]

From emergence, individuals go through the growth process to update their body size, i.e. defined as the [SOURCE: Page_115]

fork length of a fish. Individual growth is based on a modified von Bertalanffy function (von Bertalanffy, [SOURCE: Page_115]

1938). In the model, the growth parameter 𝑘 dictates the size-at-age relationship based on various [SOURCE: Page_115]

factors. Indeed, this parameter depends on experienced temperature, density and maturation since [SOURCE: Page_115]

temperature influences individual growth (Elliott et al., 1995; Forseth et al., 2009; Forseth & Jonsson, [SOURCE: Page_115]

1994; Richard et al., 2015) and in order to account for the trade-off between density-dependence and [SOURCE: Page_115]

growth (Lobon-Cervia, 2007; Lorenzen & Enberg, 2002; Richard et al., 2015), as well as that between [SOURCE: Page_115]

reproduction and growth (Berg & Fleming, 2017). Each month, individuals’ body size is updated as [SOURCE: Page_115]

follows: [SOURCE: Page_115]

104 [SOURCE: Page_115]

Chapter 3. MEDITERRANEA: A DG-ABM to simulate the evolution of intraspecific diversity in brown trout [SOURCE: Page_116]

𝑏𝑜𝑑𝑦 𝑠𝑖𝑧𝑒 𝐵𝑜𝑑𝑦 𝑠𝑖𝑧𝑒 = 𝑏𝑜𝑑𝑦 𝑠𝑖𝑧𝑒 + 𝑏𝑜𝑑𝑦 𝑠𝑖𝑧𝑒 ∗ 𝑘 ∗ (1 −                          )    (Equation 3 – 5) 𝑚𝑎𝑥𝑖𝑚𝑢𝑚 𝑏𝑜𝑑𝑦 𝑠𝑖𝑧𝑒 [SOURCE: Page_116]

Maximum body size is set by the user and represents the size towards which individual body size will tend. [SOURCE: Page_116]

The growth parameter k is expressed as follows: [SOURCE: Page_116]

(𝑜𝑝𝑡𝑖𝑚𝑎𝑙𝑇𝑒𝑚𝑝−𝑡𝑒𝑚𝑝)2 − 𝑘 = 𝑏𝑎𝑠𝑒 ∗ 𝑔𝑟𝑜𝑤𝑡ℎ𝐷𝐸 ∗ 𝑀𝐸 ∗ 𝑒             2∗𝑡𝑒𝑚𝑝𝐴𝑚𝑝𝐸2       (Equation 3 – 6) [SOURCE: Page_116]

Where, base is the equation slope, a very sensitive parameter that allows accelerating or slowing down [SOURCE: Page_116]

growth. This parameter should be set within [0.22-0.25] to have a classic and realistic growth. The higher [SOURCE: Page_116]

the parameter, the faster the growth. [SOURCE: Page_116]

To account for density-dependent growth, growthDE is expressed as a negative exponential function that [SOURCE: Page_116]

takes a density effect parameter (a) multiplied by current density. GrowthDE is stage-dependent. [SOURCE: Page_116]

𝑔𝑟𝑜𝑤𝑡ℎ𝐷𝐸𝑠𝑡𝑎𝑔𝑒 = 𝑒 (−𝑎𝑠𝑡𝑎𝑔𝑒 ∗ 𝑑𝑒𝑛𝑠𝑖𝑡𝑦𝑠𝑡𝑎𝑔𝑒 )       (Equation 3 – 7) [SOURCE: Page_116]

The density effect parameter (a) was calibrated based on observed patterns of body size distribution as a [SOURCE: Page_116]

function of density obtained by electric fishing (Data from Scimabio Interface). The higher a is the stronger [SOURCE: Page_116]

the effect of density on growth will be. Its variation range is [0-2] with 2 being a high value that may not [SOURCE: Page_116]

be biologically realistic. No density-dependent effect is accounted for when a = 0, hence growth is [SOURCE: Page_116]

maximal. When a increases, growth decreases. For a given density of 1 ind.m-2, individuals achieve [SOURCE: Page_116]

approximately 10% of their maximal growth when a = 2 (growthDE = 0.13); 50% when a = 0.7 (growthDE [SOURCE: Page_116]

= 0.5) and 64% when a = 0.45. [SOURCE: Page_116]

A maturation effect parameter (ME) is used to model the trade-off between growth and reproduction. It [SOURCE: Page_116]

is a rate [0-1] that simply decreases the growth of mature individuals as they are now investing in [SOURCE: Page_116]

reproduction. For non-mature individuals, this parameter is set at 1. For mature individuals, this [SOURCE: Page_116]

105 [SOURCE: Page_116]

3.3 Sub-models: detailed processes [SOURCE: Page_117]

parameter was calibrated so that the growth curve fit the observed difference of size-at-age between 1+ [SOURCE: Page_117]

and 2+ individuals (Data Scimabio Interface). Its default value is 0.7, hence I hypothesised that individuals [SOURCE: Page_117]

now invest 30% of their resources in reproduction. In the model, I also consider that this energy allocation [SOURCE: Page_117]

is similar between individuals – more specifically, it does not vary based on sex or individual genotype [SOURCE: Page_117]

since there is no evidence of different allocation strategies. [SOURCE: Page_117]

The temperature effect is calculated based on experienced water temperature (temp), a parameter [SOURCE: Page_117]

indicating optimal temperature for growth (optimalTemp) – estimated around 15°C for brown trout in [SOURCE: Page_117]

summer (Elliott et al., 1995; Forseth et al., 2009; Forseth & Jonsson, 1994) and a curve amplitude [SOURCE: Page_117]

parameter (tempAmpE). Growth will progressively decrease symmetrically around the optimum when [SOURCE: Page_117]

temperature increases or decreases based on the curve amplitude. [SOURCE: Page_117]

The evolution of size-at-age (Figure 3 – 15) can be visualised in the user interface when setting growth [SOURCE: Page_117]

parameters, as a function of altitude – and hence temperature – and density. [SOURCE: Page_117]

[SOURCE: Page_117]

3.3.4   Movement [SOURCE: Page_118]

Movement during the growth period.                 During the growth phase, individuals can move among [SOURCE: Page_118]

reaches for growth purposes. Movement decision is based on a movement threshold probability θ, [SOURCE: Page_118]

calculated using a given movement probability and two environmental cues: experienced density and [SOURCE: Page_118]

reach maximum carrying capacity. [SOURCE: Page_118]

𝐷𝑒𝑛𝑠𝑖𝑡𝑦𝑠𝑡𝑎𝑔𝑒,𝑟𝑒𝑎𝑐ℎ ∗ 𝑀𝑜𝑣𝑒𝑚𝑒𝑛𝑡 𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑏𝑎 𝜃𝑠𝑡𝑎𝑔𝑒,𝑟𝑒𝑎𝑐ℎ =                                            (Equation 3 – 8) 𝑀𝑎𝑥𝑖𝑚𝑢𝑚 𝑑𝑒𝑛𝑠𝑖𝑡𝑦∗ 𝑐𝑎𝑟𝑟𝑦𝑖𝑛𝑔 𝑓𝑎𝑐𝑡𝑜𝑟𝑟𝑒𝑎𝑐ℎ [SOURCE: Page_118]

Movement probability is the maximum value θ can take. It is low in our settings (0.2) as reaches are big [SOURCE: Page_118]

enough to cover individuals’ home range. Since θ is density-dependent, it varies among individuals of [SOURCE: Page_118]

different stages (juveniles, sub-adults, adults) and belonging to different reaches. Reach maximum [SOURCE: Page_118]

carrying capacity is determined using the maximum density allowed in the river – informed by the user – [SOURCE: Page_118]

multiplied by the reach carrying factor, a variable that informs on habitat quality for resources. Density, [SOURCE: Page_118]

per stage and reach, is calculated by the model at each time step. [SOURCE: Page_118]

Individuals are more likely to decide on moving if density, and hence competition, is too high in their [SOURCE: Page_118]

current reach. Individuals who decided to move then have a choice between upstream or downstream [SOURCE: Page_118]

movement. This choice is made based on a given probability to move downstream, informed during [SOURCE: Page_118]

initialisation. Individuals then try to move in their given direction. Movement can only be done one reach [SOURCE: Page_118]

at a time. Chances of success depend on habitat variables. If no reach is available, movement fails. If a [SOURCE: Page_118]

reach is available, then weir passability is assessed. If it is fully passable, movement succeeds and the [SOURCE: Page_118]

individual changes reach. If the weir is partially passable, movement success is proportional to weir [SOURCE: Page_118]

passability. Therefore, some individuals may pass while others will not. If an unpassable barrier exists, [SOURCE: Page_118]

movement fails (Figure 3 – 16). [SOURCE: Page_118]

107 [SOURCE: Page_118]

3.3 Sub-models: detailed processes [SOURCE: Page_119]

Figure 3—16: Diagram of individuals' movement decisions during the growth period. To determine movement, individuals undergo two sequential choices: moving or not, and movement direction (up or down). Their chances of moving are then determined by habitat variables: availability of a reach and passability of the connecting weir. [SOURCE: Page_119]

Movement during the reproductive period.        In November (time step = 2), just before the reproductive [SOURCE: Page_119]

season, individuals' movement for reproduction happens for individuals whose age is > 15 months, as 16 [SOURCE: Page_119]

months is the minimum age for early maturation. Movement happens in two main steps. First, the [SOURCE: Page_119]

decision for movement is made based on a certain movement probability threshold, specific to the [SOURCE: Page_119]

spawning season. Individuals who decide to move will choose between "homing" and upward migration [SOURCE: Page_119]

based on a given probability of "homing" (Figure 3 – 17). [SOURCE: Page_119]

 “Homing”.         Individuals attempting homing try to go back to their reach of birth for [SOURCE: Page_119]

reproduction. They first assess the most direct path from their current reach to their reach of [SOURCE: Page_119]

birth. Then they try to move toward their birth reach following that path. A method checking if [SOURCE: Page_119]

108 [SOURCE: Page_119]

Chapter 3. MEDITERRANEA: A DG-ABM to simulate the evolution of intraspecific diversity in brown trout [SOURCE: Page_120]

the individual can pass weirs or not will determine where the individual final destination for [SOURCE: Page_120]

reproduction is: his reach of birth in case of success or another reach in case of failure on one [SOURCE: Page_120]

weir. [SOURCE: Page_120]

Figure 3—17: Diagram representing movement choices faced by individuals during reproduction. To determine if movement happens, individuals undergo two sequential choices: moving or not and the type of movement (upward or homing). Based on their choice their chances of moving are then subject to other conditions. [SOURCE: Page_120]

 Upward Migration.         Individuals who do not perform homing try to move upstream. The [SOURCE: Page_120]

process is a recurrent function assessing movement feasibility sequentially and one reach at a [SOURCE: Page_120]

time. Movement stops when individuals have managed to migrate their maximum allowed [SOURCE: Page_120]

109 [SOURCE: Page_120]

3.3 Sub-models: detailed processes [SOURCE: Page_121]

distance – set by the user, reached the most upward part of the river (no reach available to go [SOURCE: Page_121]

to), drawn a probability to stay (10% chances), or failed to pass a weir. When an individual [SOURCE: Page_121]

manages to pass a weir, he will update his maximum distance left (-1) and the loop continues. [SOURCE: Page_121]

Upward migration can also depend on habitat quality for reproduction – user setting – as fish may [SOURCE: Page_121]

choose their spawning ground, based on substrate type and availability for example (Heggenes, [SOURCE: Page_121]

1988). If so, individuals decide to stay in their current reach for reproduction when habitat quality [SOURCE: Page_121]

for reproduction is above a certain threshold. Otherwise, he continues moving. [SOURCE: Page_121]

Returning.       Individuals, who migrated, try to return to their last known growth reach after [SOURCE: Page_121]

reproduction. For that, they will move one reach at a time and undergo the same movement process [SOURCE: Page_121]

described in upward migration. Depending on weirs passability they may or may not reach their target [SOURCE: Page_121]

reach. [SOURCE: Page_121]

3.3.5   Maturation [SOURCE: Page_121]

In our model, individuals may undergo sexual maturation, meaning that they will be able to participate in [SOURCE: Page_121]

reproduction. More precisely, their probability to mature is age and sex-dependent (Table 3 – 1). Older [SOURCE: Page_121]

individuals have higher chances to mature. Males have higher chances to mature at an early age compared [SOURCE: Page_121]

to females. These rules were established to recreate observed patterns in maturation for Mediterranean [SOURCE: Page_121]

populations of brown trout (Scimabio Interface, Com. Pers.). In the model, this process happens in [SOURCE: Page_121]

November, just before the beginning of reproduction. Note that once an individual has been deemed [SOURCE: Page_121]

eligible for maturation, he will mature every year. [SOURCE: Page_121]

110 [SOURCE: Page_121]

3.3.6    Reproduction [SOURCE: Page_122]

The reproductive season starts in December and is spread over 3 months (Gil et al., 2015; Labonne et al., [SOURCE: Page_122]

2009). Reproduction happens between individuals within the same reach. A mating pool is created within [SOURCE: Page_122]

each reach and constituted by available mature males and females – i.e. which still have mating [SOURCE: Page_122]

opportunities left. In the model, males can mate up to 10 times per season, whereas females can only [SOURCE: Page_122]

mate up to twice. Reproductive processes happen as follows: individuals are sampled from the reach [SOURCE: Page_122]

mating pool, male competition and female preference will guide mate choice, and ultimately fertilisation [SOURCE: Page_122]

happens (Figure 3 – 18). This represents one mating event in the model. It will happen each month during [SOURCE: Page_122]

the reproductive season up to twice if mature females are still available. [SOURCE: Page_122]

Individuals sampling.    All available and mature females undergo mating event processes, but not all [SOURCE: Page_122]

males do. Indeed, each female is presented successively with a few males randomly sampled from the [SOURCE: Page_122]

reach mating pool. The number of males sampled per female is drawn from a Poisson distribution with a [SOURCE: Page_122]

mean rate determined by an OSR value informed by the user. This value is set at four, meaning that a [SOURCE: Page_122]

female mating pool is on average formed by four males. Females can only mate with one male during one [SOURCE: Page_122]

mating event; hence, multiple mating is not simulated. Therefore, when a female accepts to mate with a [SOURCE: Page_122]

3.3 Sub-models: detailed processes [SOURCE: Page_123]

Figure 3—18: Representation of the reproductive processes implemented in MEDITERRANEA, including individual sampling, sexual selection, fertilisation and hatch creation. [SOURCE: Page_123]

Sexual selection.    Male competition determines the order in which males are displayed to females. [SOURCE: Page_123]

Female preference guides females’ decision to mate with a given male. [SOURCE: Page_123]

112 [SOURCE: Page_123]

Chapter 3. MEDITERRANEA: A DG-ABM to simulate the evolution of intraspecific diversity in brown trout [SOURCE: Page_124]

 Male Competition.           During the male competition process, males within a female mating pool [SOURCE: Page_124]

are ranked based on the competition mechanisms enforced in the model, which can favour [SOURCE: Page_124]

individuals based on their individual introgression. The process simply consists in presenting [SOURCE: Page_124]

males in a given order of introgression to females, so that more competitive ones try to mate first. [SOURCE: Page_124]

If no competition is enforced, then the setting “equal” is used and the ranking process is random. [SOURCE: Page_124]

Male competition can either favour lower (MED) or higher (ATL) introgressed individuals. [SOURCE: Page_124]

 Female Preference.          This process allows for the implementation of a demonstrated lineage- [SOURCE: Page_124]

based behavioural difference involved in pre-zygotic reproductive isolation: female preference [SOURCE: Page_124]

for phenotypic dissimilarity (Gil et al., 2015). In this process, multiple systems can be [SOURCE: Page_124]

implemented, with female preference expressed based on male phenotype and female [SOURCE: Page_124]

genotype^3. [SOURCE: Page_124]

Female preference can be absolute or relative. Absolute preference is when the female preference [SOURCE: Page_124]

function is scaled to the maximum possible range of variation for male phenotype, whatever the male [SOURCE: Page_124]

phenotypic variation she has access to. Relative preference is when the female preference function is [SOURCE: Page_124]

scaled on the accessible range of male phenotypic variation for the female. In that case, the range is [SOURCE: Page_124]

calculated using the phenotypic variation sampled during the above-mentioned process to reflect the [SOURCE: Page_124]

actual OSR available to females. Note that the first option implies that female preference is context- [SOURCE: Page_124]

independent, and the female will not discriminate strongly between two close phenotypes. The second [SOURCE: Page_124]

option is extremely context-dependent: if a female meets only two males and they have a very close [SOURCE: Page_124]

phenotype, she will nevertheless discriminate among them very efficiently. [SOURCE: Page_124]

3 We implemented a relation based on the genotype of the female. In reality, the relation is between both male and female phenotypes. [SOURCE: Page_124]

113 [SOURCE: Page_124]

3.3 Sub-models: detailed processes [SOURCE: Page_125]

For absolute preference, male phenotype is equal to males’ individual introgression while for relative [SOURCE: Page_125]

preference, male phenotype depends on the minimum and maximum introgression value (Min and Max, [SOURCE: Page_125]

Equation 3 – 9) observed in a female mating pool. [SOURCE: Page_125]

𝑀𝑎𝑙𝑒 𝐼𝑛𝑑𝑖𝑣𝑖𝑑𝑢𝑎𝑙 𝐼𝑛𝑡𝑟𝑜𝑔𝑟𝑒𝑠𝑠𝑖𝑜𝑛−𝑀𝑖𝑛 𝑀𝑎𝑙𝑒 𝑝ℎ𝑒𝑛𝑜𝑡𝑦𝑝𝑒 =                 𝑀𝑎𝑥−𝑀𝑖𝑛 (Equation 3 – 9) [SOURCE: Page_125]

Three preference models are proposed in MEDITERRANEA: [SOURCE: Page_125]

 Non-genetic preference (Figure 3 – 19, Equation 3 – 10): Preference is universal when all females [SOURCE: Page_125]

express the same preference, independent of their own genotype. It is directed toward either [SOURCE: Page_125]

MED or ATL male phenotype. [SOURCE: Page_125]

𝑁𝑜𝑛 − 𝑔𝑒𝑛𝑒𝑡𝑖𝑐 𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑓𝑒𝑟𝑒𝑛𝑐𝑒 = 𝐼𝑛𝑡𝑒𝑟𝑐𝑒𝑝𝑡 + 𝑆𝑙𝑜𝑝𝑒 ∗ 𝑀𝑎𝑙𝑒 𝑝ℎ𝑒𝑛𝑜𝑡𝑦𝑝𝑒                       (Equation 3 – 10) [SOURCE: Page_125]

Figure 3—19: Representation of non-genetic preference with all females expressing a preference for either MED (left) or ATL (right) males. [SOURCE: Page_125]

114 [SOURCE: Page_125]

Chapter 3. MEDITERRANEA: A DG-ABM to simulate the evolution of intraspecific diversity in brown trout [SOURCE: Page_126]

 Genetic dependent preference (Figure 3 – 20, Equation 3 – 11): Preference is expressed as a [SOURCE: Page_126]

function of female individual introgression. It can be set in two ways: (i) preference is positively [SOURCE: Page_126]

correlated with female introgression – the ATL lineage is, therefore, more selective – or (ii) [SOURCE: Page_126]

preference is negatively correlated with female introgression – the MED lineage is, therefore, [SOURCE: Page_126]

more selective. In any case, HYB females express an intermediate preference. Preference is then [SOURCE: Page_126]

directed toward MED or ATL male phenotype. [SOURCE: Page_126]

𝐺𝑒𝑛𝑒𝑡𝑖𝑐 𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑓𝑒𝑟𝑒𝑛𝑐𝑒 = 𝐼𝑛𝑡𝑒𝑟𝑐𝑒𝑝𝑡 + 𝑆𝑙𝑜𝑝𝑒 ∗ 𝐹𝑒𝑚𝑎𝑙𝑒 𝑔𝑒𝑛𝑜𝑡𝑦𝑝𝑒 ∗ 𝑀𝑎𝑙𝑒 𝑝ℎ𝑒𝑛𝑜𝑡𝑦𝑝𝑒 (Equation 3 – 11) [SOURCE: Page_126]

Female genotype is expressed differently based on which lineage is more selective. [SOURCE: Page_126]

𝐹𝑒𝑚𝑎𝑙𝑒 𝑔𝑒𝑛𝑜𝑡𝑦𝑝𝑒 = 1 − 𝐹𝑒𝑚𝑎𝑙𝑒 𝐼𝑛𝑑𝑖𝑣𝑖𝑑𝑢𝑎𝑙 𝐼𝑛𝑡𝑟𝑜𝑔𝑟𝑒𝑠𝑠𝑖𝑜𝑛 when the MED lineage is selective. [SOURCE: Page_126]

𝐹𝑒𝑚𝑎𝑙𝑒 𝑔𝑒𝑛𝑜𝑡𝑦𝑝𝑒 = 𝐹𝑒𝑚𝑎𝑙𝑒 𝐼𝑛𝑑𝑖𝑣𝑖𝑑𝑢𝑎𝑙 𝐼𝑛𝑡𝑟𝑜𝑔𝑟𝑒𝑠𝑠𝑖𝑜𝑛                 when the ATL lineage is selective. [SOURCE: Page_126]

Figure 3—20: Representation of genetic dependent preference. On the left, ATL females express a preference for ATL males, while HYB females express an intermediate preference and MED females are not choosy. On the right, it is the opposite, MED females express a preference for ATL males. [SOURCE: Page_126]

3.3 Sub-models: detailed processes [SOURCE: Page_127]

 Homo/heterogamous preference (Figure 3 – 21, Equation 3 – 12): Preference is respectively [SOURCE: Page_127]

expressed for phenotypic similarity or dissimilarity. Hence, preference depends on both, female [SOURCE: Page_127]

genotype and male phenotype, and a dissimilarity value is calculated. [SOURCE: Page_127]

𝐻𝑜𝑚𝑜/ℎ𝑒𝑡𝑒𝑟𝑜𝑔𝑎𝑚𝑜𝑢𝑠 𝑝𝑟𝑒𝑓𝑒𝑟𝑒𝑛𝑐𝑒 = 𝐼𝑛𝑡𝑒𝑟𝑐𝑒𝑝𝑡 + 𝑆𝑙𝑜𝑝𝑒 ∗ 𝐷𝑖𝑠𝑠𝑖𝑚𝑖𝑙𝑎𝑟𝑖𝑡𝑦 𝑣𝑎𝑙𝑢𝑒 (Equation 3 – 12) [SOURCE: Page_127]

With [SOURCE: Page_127]

𝐷𝑖𝑠𝑠𝑖𝑚𝑖𝑙𝑎𝑟𝑖𝑡𝑦 𝑣𝑎𝑙𝑢𝑒 = |𝑀𝑎𝑙𝑒 𝐼𝑛𝑑𝑖𝑣𝑖𝑑𝑢𝑎𝑙 𝐼𝑛𝑡𝑟𝑜𝑔𝑟𝑒𝑠𝑠𝑖𝑜𝑛 − 𝐹𝑒𝑚𝑎𝑙𝑒 𝐼𝑛𝑑𝑖𝑣𝑖𝑑𝑢𝑎𝑙 𝐼𝑛𝑡𝑟𝑜𝑔𝑟𝑒𝑠𝑠𝑖𝑜𝑛| (Equation 3 – 13) [SOURCE: Page_127]

For relative preference, the minimum and maximum dissimilarity values observed in a female mating pool [SOURCE: Page_127]

are (Min and Max) used to calculate a relative dissimilarity value for each male: [SOURCE: Page_127]

|(𝑀𝑎𝑙𝑒 𝐼𝑛𝑑𝑖𝑣𝑖𝑑𝑢𝑎𝑙 𝐼𝑛𝑡𝑟𝑜𝑔𝑟𝑒𝑠𝑠𝑖𝑜𝑛−𝐹𝑒𝑚𝑎𝑙𝑒 𝐼𝑛𝑑𝑖𝑣𝑖𝑑𝑢𝑎𝑙 𝐼𝑛𝑡𝑟𝑜𝑔𝑟𝑒𝑠𝑠𝑖𝑜𝑛)−𝑀𝑖𝑛 | (𝑀𝑎𝑥 −𝑀𝑖𝑛 ) (Equation 3 – 14) [SOURCE: Page_127]
