116 [SOURCE: Page_127]

Chapter 3. MEDITERRANEA: A DG-ABM to simulate the evolution of intraspecific diversity in brown trout [SOURCE: Page_128]

In each model, female preference is always based on male phenotype. The slope of the curve represents [SOURCE: Page_128]

the strength of the preference while the intercept could be seen as female choosiness. [SOURCE: Page_128]

Genetic contribution to preference (GCP).              In the model, individuals are characterised by genotypic [SOURCE: Page_128]

information: individual introgression but phenotypes – based on robe criteria – are not implemented. [SOURCE: Page_128]

Hence to translate female preference, expressed on phenotype in nature, a coefficient (GCP) is used to [SOURCE: Page_128]

determine how much of the mating preference is based on individual introgression and will fit the [SOURCE: Page_128]

implemented preference curve (based on phenotype). The rest of the mating stays random, with a [SOURCE: Page_128]

probability q of the mating preference following uniform law probability. This is to account for errors while [SOURCE: Page_128]

assessing genotype based on phenotype. Indeed, there could be uncertainties from fish individuals and [SOURCE: Page_128]

from experimentations in natura on phenotypes assessment. In addition, correlations between [SOURCE: Page_128]

phenotype – determined by robe criteria (Section 2.1.3, Table 2 – 1) – and genotype are uncertain and [SOURCE: Page_128]

have been shown to vary based on samples used (Folio et al., 2021; Gil et al., 2015). GCP is therefore an [SOURCE: Page_128]

omnibus way to control the strength of genetically based preference. [SOURCE: Page_128]

Females choice to mate with a male based on her preference curve will then depends on a probabilistic [SOURCE: Page_128]

random draw with the following probability: [SOURCE: Page_128]

𝑃𝑟𝑒𝑓𝑒𝑟𝑒𝑛𝑐𝑒 𝑔𝑒𝑛𝑒𝑡𝑖𝑐 𝑐𝑜𝑛𝑡𝑟𝑖𝑏𝑢𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛∗(𝑒                ⁄                )+((100−𝑔𝑒𝑛𝑒𝑡𝑖𝑐 𝑐𝑜𝑛𝑡𝑟𝑖𝑏𝑢𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛)∗𝑞) 1+ 𝑒 𝑃𝑟𝑒𝑓𝑒𝑟𝑒𝑛𝑐𝑒 𝜌=                                                100 (Equation 3 – 15) [SOURCE: Page_128]

If the value drawn is below mating will happen, otherwise, it will not. [SOURCE: Page_128]

 Lastly, if no mating system is informed, all females will choose to mate with a given male [SOURCE: Page_128]

randomly, with a 50% chance to accept mating with any males, independently of their phenotype. [SOURCE: Page_128]

117 [SOURCE: Page_128]

3.3 Sub-models: detailed processes [SOURCE: Page_129]

Fertilisation.             Spawning and fertilisation happen when a female accepts to mate with a male. [SOURCE: Page_129]

Females produce a certain number of eggs. Egg number depends on females’ body size and fecundity [SOURCE: Page_129]

(coefficient and exponent) since bigger females tend to produce more eggs (Lobon‐Cervia et al., 1997). [SOURCE: Page_129]

The number of eggs laid depends however on the female’s spawning count (which is set to 2 by default). [SOURCE: Page_129]

Fertility will also be controlled by habitat quality for reproduction in the reach and predation – i.e. egg [SOURCE: Page_129]

cannibalism. Indeed, in the model, females have to split their available gametes between mating events [SOURCE: Page_129]

when they decide to mate multiple times (set up to twice a year). They also lay fewer eggs when habitat [SOURCE: Page_129]

quality for reproduction is low since spawning substrate availability and quality can influence female [SOURCE: Page_129]

investment in reproduction (Heggenes, 1988). Finally, fewer eggs are fertilised when egg cannibalism [SOURCE: Page_129]

occurs, as satellite males, females or unsuccessful mates may be present around the spawning site to [SOURCE: Page_129]

predate on eggs (Aymes et al., 2010). In the model, it is considered a random event, which effect is based [SOURCE: Page_129]

on a randomly drawn variable α ranging between 1 (no cannibalism) and 5 (maximum cannibalism [SOURCE: Page_129]

pressure). [SOURCE: Page_129]

𝑟𝑒𝑝𝑟𝑜𝑑𝑢𝑐𝑡𝑖𝑜𝑛 𝑓𝑎𝑐𝑡𝑜𝑟 𝑛𝐸𝑔𝑔𝑠 = 𝑓𝑒𝑐𝑢𝑛𝑑𝑖𝑡𝑦 𝑐𝑜𝑒𝑓𝑓𝑖𝑐𝑖𝑒𝑛𝑡 ∗ (10 ∗ 𝑏𝑜𝑑𝑦 𝑠𝑖𝑧𝑒) 𝑓𝑒𝑐𝑢𝑛𝑑𝑖𝑡𝑦 𝑒𝑥𝑝𝑜𝑛𝑒𝑛𝑡 ∗ (𝛼∗𝑠𝑝𝑎𝑤𝑛𝑖𝑛𝑔 𝑐𝑜𝑢𝑛𝑡) (Equation 3 – 16) [SOURCE: Page_129]

The number of eggs produced during fertilisation will be added to the hatch created during this time step [SOURCE: Page_129]

in that reach, along with maternal and paternal IDs involved in mating. [SOURCE: Page_129]

3.3.7    Fishing [SOURCE: Page_129]

The effect of recreational fishing can be simulated in the model. At initialisation, the user decides on the [SOURCE: Page_129]

settings of this fishing process (Figure 3 – 22, Appendix B: Table 5). It can step in at different months and [SOURCE: Page_129]

it is applied repeatedly every year on those selected months. Fishing pressure is defined as a monthly [SOURCE: Page_129]

118 [SOURCE: Page_129]

Chapter 3. MEDITERRANEA: A DG-ABM to simulate the evolution of intraspecific diversity in brown trout [SOURCE: Page_130]

additional mortality rate applied to the population (this is not an individual process). This mortality rate [SOURCE: Page_130]

varies based on fishing regulations in place in given areas – reaches in the model. Four types of different [SOURCE: Page_130]

fishing zones have been defined: [SOURCE: Page_130]

 “Standard” reach where fishing is allowed at its maximal rate within catch size(s) limits. [SOURCE: Page_130]

 “Active reserve” reach where fishing is allowed but limited to a certain amount of fish per angler. [SOURCE: Page_130]

For this modality, we assume that the fishing mortality will be twice lower than the mortality [SOURCE: Page_130]

applied in the standard areas. [SOURCE: Page_130]

 “No-kill” reach where fishing is allowed only on a catch and release mode. Here we assume a low [SOURCE: Page_130]

residual mortality rate, as catch-and-release is known to cause some mortality (Bartholomew & [SOURCE: Page_130]

Bohnsack, 2005). [SOURCE: Page_130]

 “Full reserve” reach where fishing is banned. [SOURCE: Page_130]

This mortality can then only be applied to individuals of a certain size as some size regulation can be [SOURCE: Page_130]

implemented. Upper and lower catch size limits are set per reach and the user specifies whether fishing [SOURCE: Page_130]

pressure is applied between, above or below these given size limits. Fishing regulation and catch size limits [SOURCE: Page_130]

are informed per reach in the initial data file. [SOURCE: Page_130]

During this fishing process, several individuals randomly sampled within size limit regulations will be [SOURCE: Page_130]

removed from the simulation every month when fishing is allowed. This number is the product of the [SOURCE: Page_130]

mortality rate applied by the number of individuals present in a given area at a given time step. [SOURCE: Page_130]

It is important to notice that these fishing settings will be applied throughout the entire simulation, as this [SOURCE: Page_130]

fishing process will happen repeatedly every year. Other options (Section 3.4.2) are available to simulate [SOURCE: Page_130]

punctual fishing pressure, such as electric fishing. [SOURCE: Page_130]

119 [SOURCE: Page_130]

3.3 Sub-models: detailed processes [SOURCE: Page_131]

Figure 3—22: Graphical user interface for classic fishing pressure. The user can decide on which month fishing will happen, catch size regulation and mortality rates related to fishing regulation. [SOURCE: Page_131]

3.3.8    Individualisation [SOURCE: Page_131]

The individualisation process is a modelling operation where fish grouped in a hatch are turned into [SOURCE: Page_131]

individuals – i.e. fish objects. As a reminder, hatch objects are built right after fertilisation and serve as a [SOURCE: Page_131]

collection object where the parental contribution and number of eggs produced are stored, for delayed [SOURCE: Page_131]

instantiation into individualised fish objects, so to reduce memory consumption in the simulations. In the [SOURCE: Page_131]

present study, this process will happen at a given age for hatches: 8 months, when fish are still juveniles. [SOURCE: Page_131]

120 [SOURCE: Page_131]

Chapter 3. MEDITERRANEA: A DG-ABM to simulate the evolution of intraspecific diversity in brown trout [SOURCE: Page_132]

The number of fish left in the Hatch at that age will be the number of individuals created in the model [SOURCE: Page_132]

(Figure 3 – 3). During that process, each individual will be given a genome, determined based on their [SOURCE: Page_132]

maternal and paternal genome – available through parental contribution. This is supported by genetic [SOURCE: Page_132]

libraries that will allow the random transmission of an allele from each parent at each locus (Figure 3 – 6). [SOURCE: Page_132]

Since there are no linkages between the seven loci, alleles for each locus are transmitted independently. [SOURCE: Page_132]

Finally, the trait value – individual introgression – will be calculated and attributed to all newly [SOURCE: Page_132]

individualised fish. Hatches characteristics – information on hatching and emergence, or age and body [SOURCE: Page_132]

size – are transferred to each individual created while other characteristics, specific to individuals, such as [SOURCE: Page_132]

sex or spawning age, are either randomly determined or set by default. [SOURCE: Page_132]

3.3.9    Temperature model [SOURCE: Page_132]

Temperature is a required environmental variable in MEDITERRANEA. Indeed, on the one hand, it affects [SOURCE: Page_132]

individual embryonic development (Section 3.3.1), as well as growth (Section 3.3.3). On the other hand, it [SOURCE: Page_132]

dictates genotype-by-environment interactions involved in post-zygotic isolation (Section 3.3.2). [SOURCE: Page_132]

Therefore, the model generates a first set of 12 temperatures, one per month, using either informed [SOURCE: Page_132]

empirical data – when water temperature data are available – or a temperature model. Monthly [SOURCE: Page_132]

temperatures will be updated based on the climatic model enforced. [SOURCE: Page_132]

The temperature model uses a periodic function to simulate temperature based on altitude since altitude [SOURCE: Page_132]

is among the best predictors of river temperature in the Mediterranean area (Daigle et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_132]

Managers can also easily inform this variable. In addition, preliminary analyses on river empirically [SOURCE: Page_132]

measured water temperatures in the Haute-Savoie area (Appendix C) indicate that temperatures vary [SOURCE: Page_132]

based on altitudes. This variation also appears to be season dependent. Overall, in winter, temperatures [SOURCE: Page_132]

vary less with altitudes than in summer. This temperature model also allows for the implementation of a [SOURCE: Page_132]

121 [SOURCE: Page_132]

3.3 Sub-models: detailed processes [SOURCE: Page_133]

climate effect to simulate climate change, such as a gradual increase in temperatures throughout the [SOURCE: Page_133]

years. Temperature is calculated for each reach based on their given altitude and on a daily time step, as [SOURCE: Page_133]

follows: [SOURCE: Page_133]

(𝑑𝑎𝑦𝑠−𝑝ℎ𝑎𝑠𝑒) 𝑡𝑒𝑚𝑝𝑒𝑟𝑎𝑡𝑢𝑟𝑒 = (𝐴0 + 𝐴1 ∗ 𝑎𝑙𝑡𝑖𝑡𝑢𝑑𝑒 + 𝐴2 ∗ 𝑑𝑎𝑦𝑠) + sin (2 ∗ 𝜋 ∗            365 )+ (𝐵0 + 𝐵1 ∗ 𝑎𝑙𝑡𝑖𝑡𝑢𝑑𝑒 + 𝐵2 ∗ 𝑑𝑎𝑦𝑠)         (Equation 3 – 17) [SOURCE: Page_133]

The sinusoidal function takes π, a phase corresponding to the first day of simulation – i.e. 1 September – st [SOURCE: Page_133]

that values 245 (Julian days) as the intercept value must be met there, and days, which is the current day [SOURCE: Page_133]

number the temperature is calculated for. Days represent a cumulative value, calculated over years to [SOURCE: Page_133]

allow for the implementation of a climate change effect. [SOURCE: Page_133]

The temperature model requires base value parameters (the base value (B$_0$) as well as altitude (B$_1$) and [SOURCE: Page_133]

climate (B$_2$) effect on that base value) to which an amplitude effect is added (amplitude parameters: sea [SOURCE: Page_133]

level amplitude (A$_0$) as well as altitude (A$_1$) and climate (A$_2$) effect on amplitude). Other additional and [SOURCE: Page_133]

optional climatic parameters, to calculate an overall noise on mean temperature and its variance, are [SOURCE: Page_133]

available but not considered here in this manuscript. [SOURCE: Page_133]

To avoid negative values and to fit observed patterns of water temperature, the sinusoidal model is [SOURCE: Page_133]

broken in winter. The temperature value initially calculated is modified so that water temperature [SOURCE: Page_133]

decreases slower in winter. This takes place when the temperature is below or equal to 4°C. [SOURCE: Page_133]

 𝑡𝑒𝑚𝑝𝑒𝑟𝑎𝑡𝑢𝑟𝑒 < 0        →     𝑡𝑒𝑚𝑝𝑒𝑟𝑎𝑡𝑢𝑟𝑒 = 0 [SOURCE: Page_133]

 𝑡𝑒𝑚𝑝𝑒𝑟𝑎𝑡𝑢𝑟𝑒 ≤ 4        →     𝑡𝑒𝑚𝑝𝑒𝑟𝑎𝑡𝑢𝑟𝑒 = 0.125 ∗ 𝑡𝑒𝑚𝑝𝑒𝑟𝑎𝑡𝑢𝑟𝑒 2 + 2 [SOURCE: Page_133]

Temperature value is not allowed to go below 2°C since it would delay development and causes [SOURCE: Page_133]

emergence to potentially happen after July, which would never happen in reality (Scimabio Interface, [SOURCE: Page_133]

Com. Pers.). [SOURCE: Page_133]

122 [SOURCE: Page_133]

Chapter 3. MEDITERRANEA: A DG-ABM to simulate the evolution of intraspecific diversity in brown trout [SOURCE: Page_134]

Based on daily temperatures, the model will then calculate monthly-cumulated temperatures in degree- [SOURCE: Page_134]

days and monthly-mean temperatures in degree-Celsius. The evolution of temperature – using monthly [SOURCE: Page_134]

mean values – based on altitudes can be visualised in the user interface (Figure 3 – 23). [SOURCE: Page_134]

Figure 3—23: Graphical user interface for the setting of the temperature model. The user can modify each parameter. Some pre-set buttons can be created to implement an already define temperature model. Yearly water temperatures can be visualised as a function of various altitudes (sea level, 500m, 1000m and 1500m). [SOURCE: Page_134]

## 3.4 SIMULATING MANAGEMENT STRATEGIES

The MEDITERRANEA model allows the implementation of “interventions” during any simulation to [SOURCE: Page_134]

simulate various impacts on the demo-genetic dynamics. These interventions are used to represent the [SOURCE: Page_134]

effect of management strategies or natural events on the evolution of demography and diversity. They [SOURCE: Page_134]

123 [SOURCE: Page_134]

3.4 Simulating management strategies [SOURCE: Page_135]

can be punctual or repeated throughout time and affect individuals – referred to as population [SOURCE: Page_135]

modifications – or habitats – referred to as habitat modifications. Punctual interventions will happen only [SOURCE: Page_135]

once, after being called in the simulation, while repeated interventions will be set to happen X times with [SOURCE: Page_135]

a certain frequency for a certain duration. An intervention can be called at any time step but the first one. [SOURCE: Page_135]

3.4.1   Habitat modifications [SOURCE: Page_135]

Connectivity management.         This intervention is used to represent a dam or barrier modification, [SOURCE: Page_135]

removal or construction. It consists in modifying upstream or downstream weir passability (Figure 3 – 24, [SOURCE: Page_135]

left). This modification can be done on one or multiple weirs at a time. [SOURCE: Page_135]

Habitat management: quality modification.         To mimic an improvement or degradation in habitat [SOURCE: Page_135]

quality for survival or reproduction, the user can change the value informing on reaches habitat quality, [SOURCE: Page_135]

respectively: carrying factor and reproduction factor (Figure 3 – 24, right). Modification in habitat quality [SOURCE: Page_135]

for survival will change the maximum carrying capacity allowed in a given reach. Modification in habitat [SOURCE: Page_135]

quality for reproduction will affect reproductive movement decisions as well as females' fecundity – the [SOURCE: Page_135]

number of eggs produced. This modification can be done on one or multiple reaches at a time. [SOURCE: Page_135]

Figure 3—24: Graphical user interface for managing connectivity (left) and habitat quality (right). The user simply specifies a new value for each weir or reach state variable. [SOURCE: Page_135]

124 [SOURCE: Page_135]

Chapter 3. MEDITERRANEA: A DG-ABM to simulate the evolution of intraspecific diversity in brown trout [SOURCE: Page_136]

3.4.2   Population modifications [SOURCE: Page_136]

Fishing intervention. Various fishing interventions can be carried out in the model: punctual selective [SOURCE: Page_136]

fishing or recurrent fishing to simulate the effect of electric fishing, or selective removal for example. [SOURCE: Page_136]

Selective fishing can be based on age, size, individual introgression or a combination of all factors. In the [SOURCE: Page_136]

graphical interface, the user can just interactively remove a certain amount of individuals knowing the [SOURCE: Page_136]

exact number of individuals in each category (Figure 3 – 25). Recurrent fishing resembles the classic fishing [SOURCE: Page_136]

process that mimics recreational fishing. It can be used to add additional, recurrent, but timed fishing [SOURCE: Page_136]

regulations. The number of times this intervention happens and its frequency in years has to be specified. [SOURCE: Page_136]

Figure 3—25: Graphical user interface for selective fishing based on individual introgression. The number of individuals available in each category of introgression can be visualised. The user can just move the cursor to decide how many individuals are to be removed in each category. [SOURCE: Page_136]

125 [SOURCE: Page_136]

3.4 Simulating management strategies [SOURCE: Page_137]

Fish translocation. Fish translocation is used to move individuals from one reach to another. The method [SOURCE: Page_137]

takes a minimum and maximum size as well as a minimum and maximum individual introgression value. [SOURCE: Page_137]

This allows transferring individuals based on specific characteristics to implement measures such as the [SOURCE: Page_137]

translocation of native individuals. Information on sampled reach and translocated reach are required, as [SOURCE: Page_137]

well as the number of individuals to be transferred. The user interface allows for multiple translocation [SOURCE: Page_137]

events to happen simultaneously – originating from different sampled reaches (Figure 3 – 26). [SOURCE: Page_137]

Figure 3—26: Graphical user interface for fish translocation. The user can see how many fish is available in each reach and indicate the number of fish to move and their destination reach. [SOURCE: Page_137]

Fish introduction. To mimic fish introduction, the interface allows the user to upload an extra data file [SOURCE: Page_137]

in the model, containing only individuals. Individuals from this new file will be added to the population, in [SOURCE: Page_137]

a reach determined based on their current reach ID – informed in the file. [SOURCE: Page_137]

126 [SOURCE: Page_137]

Chapter 3. MEDITERRANEA: A DG-ABM to simulate the evolution of intraspecific diversity in brown trout [SOURCE: Page_138]

Hatch addition or removal.      Hatch removal is used to represent full nest mortality, potentially due to [SOURCE: Page_138]

nest scouring. The intervention simply consists in applying a given mortality rate to hatches. [SOURCE: Page_138]

Hatch addition is used to implement fry stocking. It consists in introducing a large number of young [SOURCE: Page_138]

individuals (generally ~ 6 months) – grouped in hatches (up to 8 months) in the model. The user indicates [SOURCE: Page_138]

the density of individuals stocked per hectare. In addition, some information has to be specified to create [SOURCE: Page_138]

these hatches: age, body size, if hatching and emergence have happened yet, as well as average individual [SOURCE: Page_138]

introgression – usually 0 or 1 as it is done using native or non-native fry in the MED/ATL context. This [SOURCE: Page_138]

intervention can be done on one or multiple reaches at a time and one hatch is created per reach. [SOURCE: Page_138]

Figure 3—27: Graphical user interface for hatch removal and hatch addition. [SOURCE: Page_138]

127 [SOURCE: Page_138]

3.4 Simulating management strategies [SOURCE: Page_139]

3.4.3   Viability analyses [SOURCE: Page_139]

This intervention allows for simulating catastrophic mortality events, such as annual or decennial floods [SOURCE: Page_139]

and consists of applying a high mortality rate to the population (Figure 3 – 28). Months during which the [SOURCE: Page_139]

mortality rate should be applied can be specified, as well as its yearly frequency. Two different mortality [SOURCE: Page_139]

rates can be set, one for adults and sub-adults and one for juveniles as they may not be affected similarly [SOURCE: Page_139]

by such catastrophic events since they do not share the same habitats (Kaspersson et al., 2012). Studying [SOURCE: Page_139]

population viability usually represents managers’ first step in management decision-making. Scimabio [SOURCE: Page_139]

Interface has already used this functionality of the model in interaction with fishery managers for an [SOURCE: Page_139]

applied case study to test the viability of an ATL population in the Mechet River. [SOURCE: Page_139]

Figure 3—28: Graphical user interface for the viability protocol. The user can specify the month during which the catastrophic event happened and its frequency – by informing the number of years where it should be applied and the interval length between each event. [SOURCE: Page_139]

128 [SOURCE: Page_139]

Chapter 3. MEDITERRANEA: A DG-ABM to simulate the evolution of intraspecific diversity in brown trout [SOURCE: Page_140]

## 3.5 MODEL INITIALISATION AND EVOLUTION

To initialise a simulation, MEDITERRANEA uses an initial data file and a set of initial parameters – for each [SOURCE: Page_140]

of its processes – to create an initial step and allow model evolution (Figure 3 – 29). [SOURCE: Page_140]

Figure 3—29: Simple representation of the model initialisation and evolution in MEDITERRANEA. The model requires a set of initial parameters and an initial data file to create the initial step (t = 0). Multiple trajectories can be run from the same initial step (replications). Interventions can be implemented at any time step, on any replications. [SOURCE: Page_140]

129 [SOURCE: Page_140]

3.5 Model initialisation and evolution [SOURCE: Page_141]

3.5.1    Initial data file [SOURCE: Page_141]

MEDITERRANEA requires initial data on species, habitat – reaches and weirs – and individuals. These data [SOURCE: Page_141]

are stored in a single text file: the initial data file (Figure 3 – 30), where species data are informed first [SOURCE: Page_141]

followed by reach, weirs and finally individuals. Data has to be informed in a specific order so that the [SOURCE: Page_141]

model can read them. For species data, the model only deals with one species: Salmo trutta. Therefore, [SOURCE: Page_141]

no specific information is required (here: fario), as the model will directly inform them (genetic map and [SOURCE: Page_141]

various allelic effects). For reaches and weirs, initial data are compulsory to build the hydrographic [SOURCE: Page_141]

network. These spatial and topological information can be simply prepared using SIG, or manually for [SOURCE: Page_141]

small rivers. Reaches data must contain topological information (ID, father ID), physical (length, width, [SOURCE: Page_141]

slope, altitude), ecological (reproduction and carrying factor) and management (fishing regulation, catch [SOURCE: Page_141]

sizes, specificities) characteristics as well as geographical coordinates (used to visualise the river). For [SOURCE: Page_141]

weirs, topology, coordinates and passability characteristics (Fish Pass, Up pass, Down pass) have to be [SOURCE: Page_141]

provided. Finally, data for individuals – i.e. fish object – gather a specific ID and a variety of state variables [SOURCE: Page_141]

that describe phenotypic (sex: 1 is male / 2 is female, body size and age), behavioural (movement status) [SOURCE: Page_141]

and genetic (nuclear and cytoplasmic DNA) characteristics as well as parentage (maternal and paternal [SOURCE: Page_141]

ID) and birth information (creation date, birth reach ID). Parental IDs and creation date are set to -1 [SOURCE: Page_141]

(unknown). [SOURCE: Page_141]

3.5.2    Initial parameters [SOURCE: Page_141]

Initial parameters for each of the previously described processes – survival, growth, movement, [SOURCE: Page_141]

reproduction, fishing and temperature model – have to be informed to start a simulation. These [SOURCE: Page_141]

parameters will be used throughout the entire simulation and cannot be modified after initialisation. In [SOURCE: Page_141]

this manuscript, some of these parameters have been fixed – i.e. will not vary among scenarios – as they [SOURCE: Page_141]

130 [SOURCE: Page_141]

Chapter 3. MEDITERRANEA: A DG-ABM to simulate the evolution of intraspecific diversity in brown trout [SOURCE: Page_142]

have been calibrated using real data on the MED-ATL complex. Others will be variable among scenarios. [SOURCE: Page_142]

Initial parameters for each process are described in the section where the specific processes are explained [SOURCE: Page_142]

(Section 3.3). The appendix with default parameters also indicates if the parameter is considered fixed or [SOURCE: Page_142]

variable for the present study (Appendix B). [SOURCE: Page_142]

Figure 3—30: Example of an input data file with information on Species, Reaches, Weirs and Fish objects. Lines starting with ‘//’ are comments and are not considered by the model. [SOURCE: Page_142]

131 [SOURCE: Page_142]

3.6 Overview of the graphical user interface [SOURCE: Page_143]

3.5.3    Creation of the initial time step [SOURCE: Page_143]

An initial stand is created using the initial data file and initial parameters set by the user. The hydrographic [SOURCE: Page_143]

network is created using the information provided on reaches and weirs. Individuals are distributed [SOURCE: Page_143]

among reaches based on their reach of birth. Using parameters from the temperature model or informed [SOURCE: Page_143]

empirical data, the first set of 12 temperatures, one per month, is created for each reach. The genetic [SOURCE: Page_143]

map for the species is also created as described in genome structure. The initial step is September (t = 0). [SOURCE: Page_143]

3.5.4    Model evolution [SOURCE: Page_143]

After the creation of the initial step, the model is ready to run. The evolution of the model is set by the [SOURCE: Page_143]

user for a given number of time steps. Multiple simulations can be run from the same initial step. During [SOURCE: Page_143]

model evolution, interventions can be applied at each step. [SOURCE: Page_143]

## 3.6 OVERVIEW OF THE GRAPHICAL USER INTERFACE

The graphical user interface represents all the elements that the user has access to, to interact with the [SOURCE: Page_143]

model. To open a model, a new project has to be created and the model specify – here MEDITERRANEA. [SOURCE: Page_143]

This leads to a first window divided into three main parts (Figure 3 – 31). The first part allows the user to [SOURCE: Page_143]

save or load a given set of initial parameters. The second part is where the initial data file, including the [SOURCE: Page_143]

hydrographic network and individuals, is loaded. The third part gives access to the initial parameters of [SOURCE: Page_143]

the model – ordered by processes. [SOURCE: Page_143]

132 [SOURCE: Page_143]

Chapter 3. MEDITERRANEA: A DG-ABM to simulate the evolution of intraspecific diversity in brown trout [SOURCE: Page_144]

Figure 3—31: CAPSIS-4 graphical interface to initialise the model chosen and MEDITERRANEA first window to load an initial data file and set parameters. [SOURCE: Page_144]

3.6.1   Running simulations [SOURCE: Page_144]

To initialise a simulation, an initial data file has to be loaded. The user can then use default values for [SOURCE: Page_144]

initial parameter settings or set his own values. A new project can then start. Simulations always run on a [SOURCE: Page_144]

monthly time step but they can be visualised on a monthly or yearly time step. [SOURCE: Page_144]

In the interface an initial step for the project appears – September time step 0. The user can now start the [SOURCE: Page_144]

evolution of the model – click <Evolution> - for a given number of time steps. This results in the first [SOURCE: Page_144]

trajectory of the project. Each time step can be analyzed and contains every object within the simulation [SOURCE: Page_144]

at that given time. Multiple trajectories (or replications), from the same project, can be launched similarly, [SOURCE: Page_144]

either from the initial step or from any other intermediate step of the model. Figure 3 – 32 illustrate [SOURCE: Page_144]

The model interface allows the user to save multiple types of information, such as sets of parameters – [SOURCE: Page_145]

when creating a new project – and projects themselves. Each time a project is created, it can be saved [SOURCE: Page_145]

independently (Project > Save project) or as part of a session including multiple projects (Files > Save [SOURCE: Page_145]

session). [SOURCE: Page_145]

At every time step during a simulation, it is possible to call for an intervention that will affect the current [SOURCE: Page_145]

dynamic of the population. A right-click on a time step will lead to <Intervention>. The related dialogue [SOURCE: Page_145]

box will propose the different types of actions possible (Figure 3 – 33). Groupers can be used to carry out [SOURCE: Page_145]

interventions on a subset of reaches or weirs. Each intervention can be parameterised through a specific [SOURCE: Page_145]

3.6.2   Observing and analysing simulations [SOURCE: Page_146]

A set of extensions is included in the user interface of MEDITERRANEA. They allow visualisation and [SOURCE: Page_146]

analyses of data resulting from simulations. They are accessible on the left of the interface and are divided [SOURCE: Page_146]

into two main parts: a graphical part (Chart) and a stand visualisation part (Scene viewer). They can be [SOURCE: Page_146]

modified interactively (type of graph, time step …) and data filtered to only plot a part of the information [SOURCE: Page_146]

(some reaches only for example). They are called by time step to visualise a given data at this given time [SOURCE: Page_146]

or its temporal evolution up to that time step. [SOURCE: Page_146]

135 [SOURCE: Page_146]

3.6 Overview of the graphical user interface [SOURCE: Page_147]

Data extractors.         Data extractors are used to analyse graphically a simulation and hence gather [SOURCE: Page_147]

various types of data of interest memorised during the simulation. Some are used to visualise temporal [SOURCE: Page_147]

data (evolution of: population size, introgression rate, genotypic composition of the population…) while [SOURCE: Page_147]

others show the distribution of some variables (Individual age, body size or introgression, reach [SOURCE: Page_147]

characteristics …) at a given time or the relationship between two variables of interests (age and size [SOURCE: Page_147]

relationship). Multiple simulations or time steps can be visualised using the same data extractor (click on [SOURCE: Page_147]

the step to add and do <control + click>). [SOURCE: Page_147]

Among the most important data extractors, MEDITERRANEA has some on diversity dynamics, [SOURCE: Page_147]

demographic dynamics and population characteristics and others on habitat. To follow diversity dynamics, [SOURCE: Page_147]

the user can visualise the introgression rate and the genotypic composition of the population (numbers [SOURCE: Page_147]

of MED, HYB and ATL individuals) through time, the distribution of individual introgression at a given time [SOURCE: Page_147]

step as well as ATL allele frequency per locus (differentiating maternally and paternally inherited alleles). [SOURCE: Page_147]

To study demographic dynamics and various population characteristics the user can visualise the [SOURCE: Page_147]

evolution of population size per life stage, size-at-age relationship or the distribution of size and age at a [SOURCE: Page_147]

given time step. Regarding habitat – reaches and weirs, the user can visualise the evolution of [SOURCE: Page_147]

temperature per reaches and reaches information on slope, mean width, altitude-temperature [SOURCE: Page_147]

relationship… [SOURCE: Page_147]

Types of graphical representation.        CAPSIS-4 allows for four different graphical representations of [SOURCE: Page_147]

data: lines plot, scatter plot, bars plot and cumulative areas (Figure 3 – 34). All available data extractors [SOURCE: Page_147]

can use these different representations. Graphs can then be saved to JPG or PNG format. Data can also [SOURCE: Page_147]

be saved in a table. Right-clicking on graphs gives access to different options of data visualisation, as data [SOURCE: Page_147]

can be filtered by groups (of individuals, reaches or weirs) based on various grouping variables. Colours [SOURCE: Page_147]

can also be modified. [SOURCE: Page_147]

Figure 3—34: Example of various graphical representations provided by CAPSIS-4 software for MEDITERRANEA. [SOURCE: Page_148]

Scene viewer.            In the stand visualisation part, the hydrographic network and the information of [SOURCE: Page_148]

the different objects in the model – reach, weir, individual or hatch – can be visualised at each time step. [SOURCE: Page_148]

Mediterranea viewer offers a two-dimension representation of the hydrographic network where reaches [SOURCE: Page_148]

and weirs are delimited (Figure 3 – 35). Some of their characteristics can be shown on the river map. The [SOURCE: Page_148]

user can click on each object constituting the river (reaches and weirs) and access all its information (states [SOURCE: Page_148]

variables, as well as other related objects – such as individuals located in a particular reach – for which [SOURCE: Page_148]

Groupers.       They are often used when the user wants to analyze sub-parts of the data, using filters on [SOURCE: Page_149]

three main objects of the simulation: individuals, reaches and weirs. Those filters allow the user to choose [SOURCE: Page_149]

objects based on the value of one of its state variables, such as Boolean values (true or false), numerical [SOURCE: Page_149]

intervals or directly by selecting a specific object (ID). For example, the user can follow the dynamic of a [SOURCE: Page_149]

subset of reaches only (directly chosen by ID or based on a specificity such as fishing regulation) or that of [SOURCE: Page_149]

a certain type of individuals (females, juveniles…). He can also create a group of individuals within a group [SOURCE: Page_149]

of reaches. Groups can be “dynamic” or “static”. Dynamic groups can evolve through time, meaning that [SOURCE: Page_149]

the composition of the group can change through time as long as the filtering condition is matched. A [SOURCE: Page_149]

dynamic male group will hence show at each time step male individuals. Static groups will not change [SOURCE: Page_149]

through time, meaning that they will be made of the same objects – such as individuals – every time. A [SOURCE: Page_149]

static male group will only represent the males present in the population at a given time step. This type [SOURCE: Page_149]

of filter can be useful when following the evolution of a specific cohort. [SOURCE: Page_149]

## 4 MEDITERRANEA: A TOOL TO MEASURE THE ROLE OF ECO- EVOLUTIONARY MECHANISMS

In the MED-ATL hybridization complex, some eco-evolutionary mechanisms (EEMs) driving hybridization [SOURCE: Page_153]

have been explored in natural environments to understand observed heterogeneous patterns of diversity. [SOURCE: Page_153]

On the one hand, despite long allopatric isolation, no pre-zygotic barriers have evolved between the [SOURCE: Page_153]

lineages. Instead, (Gil et al., 2015) unravelled patterns of heterogamous preference with female [SOURCE: Page_153]

preference generally aimed at dissimilar male phenotypes. During secondary contact, such a mating [SOURCE: Page_153]

system could enhance hybridization between both lineages. On the other hand, even after more than a [SOURCE: Page_153]

century in sympatry, lineage-related differences in fitness can still be observed in some environmental [SOURCE: Page_153]

conditions. Indeed, maternal gene-by-temperature interaction appears to be one of the main drivers of [SOURCE: Page_153]

embryonic survival up to hatching (Folio et al., 2021). Temperature contrasts could therefore influence [SOURCE: Page_153]

the persistence of a post-zygotic reproductive barrier between the lineages. [SOURCE: Page_153]

Such EEMs are likely to have a strong influence on the evolution of diversity. However, acknowledging [SOURCE: Page_153]

their existence is insufficient to grasp their actual role in hybridization and diversity dynamics. One has to [SOURCE: Page_153]

project and calculate how much these mechanisms will interact with the other ecological, genetic, and [SOURCE: Page_153]

demographic drivers, including human interventions. This chapter will aim at evaluating the actual role [SOURCE: Page_153]

and relative importance of heterogamous preference and GxE interactions on embryonic survival in [SOURCE: Page_153]

driving the evolution of diversity and demography, with and without considering interventions of fisheries [SOURCE: Page_153]

managers. DG-ABM provides an operational framework to test the fate of these two gene pools under [SOURCE: Page_153]

different eco-evolutionary scenarios, as (i) it realistically simulates interactions between individuals, giving [SOURCE: Page_153]

rise to demographic and genetic processes, such as allelic transmission and (ii) it allows for the [SOURCE: Page_153]

implementation of complex eco-evolutionary relationships, such as mate preference behaviour and [SOURCE: Page_153]

142 [SOURCE: Page_153]

4.1 Objectives [SOURCE: Page_154]

lineage dependent survival, mediated by environment. Such models also enable the implementation of [SOURCE: Page_154]

management practices, which can interfere with EEMs and hence diversity dynamics. [SOURCE: Page_154]

## 4.1 OBJECTIVES

In this Chapter, MEDITERRANEA, which mimics the brown trout life cycle, will be used to compare [SOURCE: Page_154]

trajectories of diversity dynamics and demography in the MED-ATL hybridization complex when [SOURCE: Page_154]

implementing, or not, heterogamous preference and/or gene-by-temperature (referred to as GxE) [SOURCE: Page_154]

survival difference (Figure 4 – 1). Diversity dynamics will include the population introgression rate and the [SOURCE: Page_154]

evolution of population genotypic composition – i.e. proportion of MED, HYB and ATL individuals –, as [SOURCE: Page_154]

well as the distribution of individual introgression at specific time steps – to differentiate between hybrids. [SOURCE: Page_154]

Demography will mainly focus on the evolution of population size through time and the age class structure [SOURCE: Page_154]

– juveniles, sub-adults and adults. [SOURCE: Page_154]

Figure 4—1: Simplified representation of the eco-evolutionary mechanisms implemented in MEDITERRANEA and chapter 4’s main goals. [SOURCE: Page_154]

143 [SOURCE: Page_154]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_155]

 In the first section, I will investigate the evolution of diversity dynamics under various eco- [SOURCE: Page_155]

evolutionary scenarios, over long- and short-time scales (relative to management practices, so [SOURCE: Page_155]

respectively > 20 years and < 20 years), in a very simplified and theoretical case study of first [SOURCE: Page_155]

secondary contact (Figure 4 – 5, A). I will initially focus on the influence of (i) heterogamous [SOURCE: Page_155]

preference, (ii) GxE interactions and (iii) temperature contrast on the evolution of diversity [SOURCE: Page_155]

dynamics and demography. Then, as both EEMs may have opposite effects, one potentially [SOURCE: Page_155]

favouring hybridization and the other native diversity, I will also investigate the impact of [SOURCE: Page_155]

heterogamous preference on the potential selection mediated by GxE interactions. I will discuss [SOURCE: Page_155]

those results in regard to the model specificities and theoretical knowledge of these mechanisms. [SOURCE: Page_155]

 Then, I will turn to more complex case studies, based on real and documented situations, where [SOURCE: Page_155]

management practices have been carried out to restore brown trout populations and/or conserve [SOURCE: Page_155]

the MED lineage (Figure 4 – 5, B). On the one hand, I will evaluate the impact of management [SOURCE: Page_155]

practices on the evolution of diversity dynamics while considering the same eco-evolutionary [SOURCE: Page_155]

scenarios. I will investigate whether these mechanisms play a similar role in the evolution of [SOURCE: Page_155]

diversity when considering specific case studies – compared to the simple one. Indeed, as they [SOURCE: Page_155]

might interact with many ecological and environmental variations (landscape features, spatial [SOURCE: Page_155]

variation in temperature, management practices…) it is important to evaluate how they will act [SOURCE: Page_155]

in a specific context and whether some other parameters might influence their impact. On the [SOURCE: Page_155]

other hand, as punctual genetic and demographic data are available, before and after the [SOURCE: Page_155]

implementation of management practices in these case studies, I will compare available empirical [SOURCE: Page_155]

data to simulation data to determine whether the inclusion of evolutionary-based mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_155]

improves or degrades the ability of the model to predict the empirical findings. [SOURCE: Page_155]

This will help identify the relevant mechanisms – and related ecological contrasts – that influence the [SOURCE: Page_156]

most the evolution of intraspecific diversity and hybridization between the lineages. This should allow [SOURCE: Page_156]

## 4.2 DEFINITION OF THE ECO-EVOLUTIONARY SCENARIOS

4.2.1   Main eco-evolutionary scenarios implemented [SOURCE: Page_157]

The neutral scenario.           The neutral scenario assumes no mating preference or survival [SOURCE: Page_157]

differences between the MED and the ATL lineages. Default parameters are used for both lineages and all [SOURCE: Page_157]

individuals should undergo similar processes independently of their genotype. Random preference will be [SOURCE: Page_157]

applied with a 50% chance to mate with any males, no matter their phenotype. Embryonic survival will [SOURCE: Page_157]

not be dependent upon maternal genotype. [SOURCE: Page_157]

The heterogamous preference scenario.                    (Also referred to as heterogamy for more [SOURCE: Page_157]

simplicity on graphical representation). This scenario assumes heterogamous preference: female [SOURCE: Page_157]

preference is based on phenotypic dissimilarity, wherein females prefer males showing contrasted [SOURCE: Page_157]

phenotype in relation to their own phenotype, following (Gil et al., 2015) findings. Figure 4 – 3 displays [SOURCE: Page_157]

the female preference curve implemented in the model, as a function of male phenotypes, for each female [SOURCE: Page_157]

genotype. However, the phenotype is not explicitly represented in our model. Yet the correlation [SOURCE: Page_157]

coefficient between the genetic markers allowing distinguishing between lineages and phenotypic [SOURCE: Page_157]

variation is estimated to be around 0.35 to 0.7 depending on the sources (Aparicio et al., 2005; Folio et [SOURCE: Page_157]

al., 2021; Scimabio Interface additional data). We, therefore, assumed that 35 % of the decision of the [SOURCE: Page_157]

female was based on the male genotype, whereas the other 65% were random (following Equation 3 – [SOURCE: Page_157]

15). [SOURCE: Page_157]

146 [SOURCE: Page_157]

4.2 Definition of the eco-evolutionary scenarios [SOURCE: Page_158]

Figure 4—3: Female heterogamous and random preference curves implemented in the model. The heterogamous preference function is implemented with a GCP at 35% and shows a preference for dissimilarity as a function of male introgression. [SOURCE: Page_158]

The genotype-by-environment interactions scenario.               (Also referred to as GxE for more [SOURCE: Page_158]

simplicity on graphical representation). This scenario assumes a genotypic-based fitness difference, [SOURCE: Page_158]

wherein embryonic survival is depending on a maternal gene-by-temperature interaction. Post-zygotic [SOURCE: Page_158]

survival is hence implemented as a function of the maternal genotypic score and mean winter [SOURCE: Page_158]

temperature experienced up to hatching. Parameter values have been chosen so that the survival curve [SOURCE: Page_158]

of each genotype approximately fits the survival observed in natural environments (Folio et al., 2021). At [SOURCE: Page_158]

an 8°C temperature, there is no difference in survival between eggs with different maternal genotypes. [SOURCE: Page_158]

As water temperatures get colder, offspring with MED maternal genotype have an increasingly higher [SOURCE: Page_158]

survival compared to offspring with ATL maternal genotype. Survival curves in various temperature [SOURCE: Page_158]

conditions are represented in figure 4 – 4 as a function of maternal genotype. Paternal genotype effects [SOURCE: Page_158]

were not accounted for, since they appeared to be minor compared to maternal effects (Folio et al., 2021). [SOURCE: Page_158]

147 [SOURCE: Page_158]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_159]

Figure 4—4: Offspring survival curve implemented in the model at hatching as a function of maternal introgression for various modalities of temperature and scenarios considering G x E interactions. [SOURCE: Page_159]

The heterogamy + GxE scenario.                      This scenario assumes that both potential EEMs driving [SOURCE: Page_159]

hybridization apply: pre-zygotic (using the above-described heterogamous mating preference) and post- [SOURCE: Page_159]

zygotic (using the above-described maternal genotype-by-environment interaction). This scenario will be [SOURCE: Page_159]

used to investigate the relative role of the two mechanisms on intraspecific diversity dynamics. [SOURCE: Page_159]

4.2.2    Sensitivity analyses [SOURCE: Page_159]

Since these pre and post-zygotic isolation mechanisms have only been studied in a specific environment [SOURCE: Page_159]

(Folio et al., 2021; Gil et al., 2015), I will analyse the model sensitivity to variation in pre- and post-zygotic [SOURCE: Page_159]

isolation parameters. [SOURCE: Page_159]

Heterogamous preference.                   Sensitivity analyses were performed by changing the genetic [SOURCE: Page_159]

contribution to the preference (GCP, i.e. the percentage of the choice that was based on the genetic [SOURCE: Page_159]

148 [SOURCE: Page_159]

4.2 Definition of the eco-evolutionary scenarios [SOURCE: Page_160]

dissimilarity between the female and the male) while keeping the same shape for the preference curve [SOURCE: Page_160]

(i.e. same slope and intercept, Figure 4 – 3). Knowing that observed correlation between phenotype and [SOURCE: Page_160]

genotype may vary within samples and populations, we tested scenarios where 35% (correlation observed [SOURCE: Page_160]

in Aparicio et al., 2005 & Gil et al., 2015), 50% (correlation observed in general data on Haute-Savoie, [SOURCE: Page_160]

Scimabio Interface Expertise), 70% (correlation observed in Folio et al., 2021) and 100% of the choice [SOURCE: Page_160]

followed the heterogamous preference curve. [SOURCE: Page_160]

GxE interactions.                Model sensitivity analyses were performed by conducting a factorial [SOURCE: Page_160]

design simulation to investigate how variations in GxE strength combined with different winter [SOURCE: Page_160]

temperatures influence the evolution of intraspecific diversity. The strength of GxE interactions was either [SOURCE: Page_160]

low (Figure 4 – 5, A), medium (Figure 4 – 4) or high (Figure 4 – 5, B). [SOURCE: Page_160]
