## 4.3 EXPLORING VARIOUS ECO-EVOLUTIONARY SCENARIOS IN A BASIC CASE STUDY OF FIRST SECONDARY CONTACT

This first section will use MEDITERRANEA to explore the potential role of both EEMs implemented in the [SOURCE: Page_161]

model – i.e. mate preference behaviour and gene-by-temperature survival – on the evolution of [SOURCE: Page_161]

intraspecific diversity. To do so I will follow diversity dynamics in a simple case study of first contact [SOURCE: Page_161]

between the ATL and the MED lineages. Here, to avoid as many confounding ecological effects as possible, [SOURCE: Page_161]

the environment will be as simple as possible even if yet unrealistic. Therefore environmental variables [SOURCE: Page_161]

that may affect population dynamics – such as connectivity, temperature or fishing regulation – will be, [SOURCE: Page_161]

either fixed, discarded or removed. In this context, I will use my simulation framework to investigate the [SOURCE: Page_161]

role of (i) mate preference behaviour – with a focus on heterogamous preference, (ii) gene-by- [SOURCE: Page_161]

temperature survival applied to embryonic survival, as well as (iii) the relative effect of each factor on the [SOURCE: Page_161]

evolution of intraspecific diversity. Following first contact, one would expect that a heterogamous [SOURCE: Page_161]

preference would increase and/or speed up the rate of hybridization, as mating between dissimilar mates [SOURCE: Page_161]

– of each lineage – will be favoured. However, this is only expected to be true during the early time step [SOURCE: Page_161]

when pure individuals are still dominant in the population. Prediction for heterogamous preference when [SOURCE: Page_161]

a population mainly consists of a variety of HYB individuals is less straightforward. Regarding demography, [SOURCE: Page_161]

heterogamous preference might lead to a different outcome compared to random preference. Indeed, [SOURCE: Page_161]

heterogamous preference strength varies between 0.45 to 0.8 based on phenotypic dissimilarity whereas [SOURCE: Page_161]

random preference strength is always set at 0.5. Based on the level of dissimilarity involved in mating, this [SOURCE: Page_161]

may cause populations’ mating success to vary. As survival – a fitness component – is a strong determinant [SOURCE: Page_161]

of short and long-term population dynamics, gene-by-temperature selection should favour the increase [SOURCE: Page_161]

of the advantageous genetic variation – here MED alleles inherited maternally only. The higher the [SOURCE: Page_161]

selective pressure is – here lower temperature – the more native diversity should be favoured over time. [SOURCE: Page_161]

150 [SOURCE: Page_161]

4.3 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in a basic case study of first secondary contact [SOURCE: Page_162]

Knowing that embryonic survival is only dependent upon maternal genetic variation when both barriers [SOURCE: Page_162]

are considered hybrid individuals originating from the MED maternal lineage might be favoured. [SOURCE: Page_162]

4.3.1    Simulations settings [SOURCE: Page_162]

Initial ecological settings.             A very simple ecological setting will be used to describe the [SOURCE: Page_162]

environment so to avoid any confounding effects. Hence, the hydrographic network will only contain one [SOURCE: Page_162]

reach of 5000m^2 – 1000 metres in length and 5 metres in width – to discard the acknowledged role that [SOURCE: Page_162]

connectivity and movement have on population dynamics (Fronhofer & Altermatt, 2017; He et al., 2019; [SOURCE: Page_162]

Labonne et al., 2008). All quality parameters for carrying capacity and reproduction are set at the [SOURCE: Page_162]

maximum, and maximum density is set at approximately 40 ind.100m-2. Fishing regulation is set on full [SOURCE: Page_162]

reserve all along the simulation. Only settings for temperature and EEMs will vary among simulations [SOURCE: Page_162]

based on the scenario. All other parameters are set by default. [SOURCE: Page_162]

Initial population.             To simulate first contact between both lineages, the initial data file [SOURCE: Page_162]

contains an equal number of pure MED and pure ATL individuals – here 1000 individuals for each lineage [SOURCE: Page_162]

– with an equal and balanced sex ratio. All individuals are introduced as adults or sub-adults in the model [SOURCE: Page_162]

with age starting from 21 months as they are introduced in September – birth month is hypothesised to [SOURCE: Page_162]

be December. This will allow the majority of individuals to mature during the first mating season. [SOURCE: Page_162]

Temperature settings.           Temperatures are informed per month with only two values: a mean [SOURCE: Page_162]

winter temperature from November to April and a mean summer temperature from May to October. This [SOURCE: Page_162]

simplification will allow us to test the impact of gene-by-temperature post-zygotic isolation on embryonic [SOURCE: Page_162]

survival at hatching while knowing the exact winter temperature applied. Summer temperature will be [SOURCE: Page_162]

151 [SOURCE: Page_162]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_163]

fixed among simulations and is set at 12°C, while winter temperature will vary to test for the sensibility of [SOURCE: Page_163]

diversity to the selective pressure exerted by temperature. There will be three modalities for winter [SOURCE: Page_163]

temperatures: a low (2.5°C), a medium (5°C) and a high (8°C) modality. As shown in Figure 4 – 4, the low- [SOURCE: Page_163]

temperature modality is associated with a larger fitness difference between the lineages while the high- [SOURCE: Page_163]

temperature modality is not associated with any differences. [SOURCE: Page_163]

Simulation framework.            The same initial data file is used for all simulations and 20 simulations – [SOURCE: Page_163]

replicates – are run per scenario. Each simulation starts in September and is run over 100 years to track [SOURCE: Page_163]

the evolution of diversity dynamics and demography over a long period under the four eco-evolutionary [SOURCE: Page_163]

scenarios described above. [SOURCE: Page_163]

4.3.2   The role of heterogamous preference [SOURCE: Page_163]

4.3.2.1   Evolution of diversity dynamics under random vs heterogamous preference [SOURCE: Page_163]

Whatever the mating preference considered – random or heterogamous preference – diversity dynamics [SOURCE: Page_163]

evolved similarly over time. The population rate of introgression (Figure 4 – 6) was stable, and its mean [SOURCE: Page_163]

value was around the initial value of 0.5. The mean introgression rate seemed slightly lower (~ 0.47) for [SOURCE: Page_163]

simulations including heterogamous preference compared to random preference (~ 0.5). However, [SOURCE: Page_163]

confidence intervals overlapped more and more over time, suggesting that heterogamous preference [SOURCE: Page_163]

might not influence introgression dynamics. [SOURCE: Page_163]

152 [SOURCE: Page_163]

4.3 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in a basic case study of first secondary contact [SOURCE: Page_164]

Figure 4—6: Evolution of introgression rate over a hundred years considering random versus heterogamous preference (GCP = 35%). Solid lines represent mean values averaged over 20 simulations while polygons are 95% credible intervals. [SOURCE: Page_164]

Despite a stable introgression rate, the composition of the population (Figure 4 - 7) – MED, HYB and ATL [SOURCE: Page_164]

– changed rapidly over the first time steps. Indeed, a rapid decrease of pure genotypes – MED and ATL – [SOURCE: Page_164]

was observed within the first 10 years, up until their extinction (Figure 4 – 7, from 1000 to 0). Meanwhile, [SOURCE: Page_164]

HYB individuals were created. Therefore, random and heterogamous preferences led to the same [SOURCE: Page_164]

outcome at the same pace: a population exclusively made of HYB individuals. However, differences can [SOURCE: Page_164]

be observed between both scenarios when focusing on the distribution of individual introgression at early [SOURCE: Page_164]

time steps (Figure 4 – 8). [SOURCE: Page_164]

153 [SOURCE: Page_164]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_165]

Figure 4—7: Evolution of the genotypic composition of the population – proportions of MED (full lines), HYB (dashed line) and ATL (dotted line) genotypes – over a hundred years considering random versus heterogamous preference (GCP = 35%). Solid lines represent mean values averaged over 20 simulations while polygons are 95% credible intervals. [SOURCE: Page_165]

During the first time step (Figure 4 – 8, t = 1), heterogamous preference led to the creation of more HYB [SOURCE: Page_165]

individuals compared to random preference (a mean of 1075 vs 1000 individuals). Heterogamous mating [SOURCE: Page_165]

was, therefore, slightly more frequent initially. Along that, numbers of pure genotypes were also lower [SOURCE: Page_165]

when considering heterogamous preference (600 vs 670 individuals for MED, 580 vs 610 individuals for [SOURCE: Page_165]

ATL), suggesting that homogamous mating might be less frequent. However, differences between [SOURCE: Page_165]

scenarios remained small, and confidence intervals were sometimes overlapping. In addition, this slight [SOURCE: Page_165]

difference rapidly faded away with the creation of a wide range of HYB – less (< 0.5) and more (> 0.5) [SOURCE: Page_165]

highly introgressed. [SOURCE: Page_165]

154 [SOURCE: Page_165]

4.3 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in a basic case study of first secondary contact [SOURCE: Page_166]

Figure 4—8: Distribution of individual introgression during early time steps considering random versus heterogamous preference (GCP = 35%). Rectangular bars represent the mean number of individuals per class of individual introgression while error bars are 95% CI. [SOURCE: Page_166]

Overall, both scenarios homogenised very early with the distribution of individual introgression forming a [SOURCE: Page_166]

bell shape centred on 0.5 (Figure 4 – 8, t = 10). This distribution stayed stable over the long term for both [SOURCE: Page_166]

scenarios (Figure 4 - 9), with a final distribution still centred on 0.5. It is interesting to note that, for the [SOURCE: Page_166]

heterogamous scenarios, more MED individuals were found in time step 2 compared to ATL individuals [SOURCE: Page_166]

(Figure 4 – 8, 414 vs 327 individuals), which may explain the fact that the population introgression rate [SOURCE: Page_166]

was slightly lower than 0.5 initially (Figure 4 – 6). The initial composition of the population and random [SOURCE: Page_166]

155 [SOURCE: Page_166]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_167]

processes that affect genotype frequencies might therefore play an important role in the evolution of [SOURCE: Page_167]

diversity dynamics. [SOURCE: Page_167]

Figure 4—9: Distribution of individual introgression after 50 and 100 years of evolution considering random versus heterogamous preference (GCP = 35%). Rectangular bars represent the mean number of individuals per class of individual introgression while error bars are 95% CI. [SOURCE: Page_167]

4.3.2.2    Evolution of demography under random vs heterogamous preference [SOURCE: Page_167]

Demography – i.e. population size and age class structure – was stable over time (Figure 4 – 10). The [SOURCE: Page_167]

number of juveniles varied around 2000 individuals with small pics each year, indicating a small variability [SOURCE: Page_167]

in yearly reproductive success. Sub-adults and adults dynamics were smoother, as mortality applied at [SOURCE: Page_167]

each stage (stage-specific and density-dependent mortality) controlled for the number of individuals [SOURCE: Page_167]

recruited to the next life stage. The number of individuals recruited at sub-adult stage was more than four [SOURCE: Page_167]

times lower than the number of juveniles. Similarly, the number of adults was twice as low as the number [SOURCE: Page_167]

156 [SOURCE: Page_167]

4.3 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in a basic case study of first secondary contact [SOURCE: Page_168]

of sub-adults. Overall, demography was similar for both scenarios, suggesting that heterogamous [SOURCE: Page_168]

preference does not seem to affect the mating success of the population. [SOURCE: Page_168]

Figure 4—10: Evolution of demography structured per age class – juveniles (full lines), sub-adults (dashed line) and adults (dotted line) – over a hundred years of evolution considering random versus heterogamous preference (GCP = 35%). Lines represent mean values averaged over 20 simulations while polygons are 95% credible intervals. [SOURCE: Page_168]

Heterogamous preference, implemented with a GCP of 35%, appears to have very little impact on diversity [SOURCE: Page_168]

dynamics and demography over the long term. Heterogamous preference might slightly influence the [SOURCE: Page_168]

outcome of first secondary contact (1st time step only) – with more mating involving MED and ATL [SOURCE: Page_168]

individuals. However, its effect seems to fade away when HYB individuals are created, as heterogamous [SOURCE: Page_168]

preference might not matter anymore when the population is entirely composed of very similar HYB [SOURCE: Page_168]

individuals. [SOURCE: Page_168]

157 [SOURCE: Page_168]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_169]

4.3.2.3   Sensitivity analyses on the genetic contribution to preference (GCP) [SOURCE: Page_169]

Sensitivity analyses were performed on the value of GCP implemented in the model to account for [SOURCE: Page_169]

variability in the potential correlation between phenotype and genotype. They also allowed evaluating [SOURCE: Page_169]

whether the lack of effect was due to the low contribution of the heterogamous preference curve to the [SOURCE: Page_169]

mating decision (only 35% vs 65% random). [SOURCE: Page_169]

Figure 4—11: Evolution of introgression rate over a hundred years considering heterogamous preference with various values of GCP. Solid lines represent mean values averaged over 20 simulations while polygons are 95% credible intervals. [SOURCE: Page_169]

Looking at introgression and genotype proportion, variations in GCP did not influence significatively the [SOURCE: Page_169]

evolution of introgression rate (Figure 4 – 11: slight variations of mean values around 0.5 between [0.48, [SOURCE: Page_169]

0.53], and overlapping CI) nor that of the genotypic composition of the population (Figure 4 – 12). [SOURCE: Page_169]

158 [SOURCE: Page_169]

4.3 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in a basic case study of first secondary contact [SOURCE: Page_170]

Figure 4—12: Evolution of the genotypic composition of the population – proportions of MED (full lines), HYB (dashed line) and ATL (dotted line) genotypes – over a hundred years considering heterogamous preference with various values of GCP. Solid lines represent mean values averaged over 20 simulations while polygons are 95% credible intervals. [SOURCE: Page_170]

However, the distribution of individual introgression during first contact (Figure 4 – 13, t = 1) showed that [SOURCE: Page_170]

the higher the value of GCP is, the more HYB individuals are created during the first time step (going from [SOURCE: Page_170]

1140 to 1290 individuals), confirming that heterogamous mating might be more frequent. Following the [SOURCE: Page_170]

creation of HYB, this slight difference among scenarios started to fade away. Nevertheless, the final [SOURCE: Page_170]

distribution of individual introgression (Figure 4 – 13, t = 100) highlighted few differences in the bell shape [SOURCE: Page_170]

of the distribution, but no effects appeared to be significant as distributions all remained centred on 0.5 [SOURCE: Page_170]

with large and overlapping confidence intervals. Convergence for such patterns might not be reached with [SOURCE: Page_170]

20 replicates considering the amount of stochasticity that remains in the mating decision (Section 3.3.6). [SOURCE: Page_170]

159 [SOURCE: Page_170]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_171]

Figure 4—13: Distribution of individual introgression after 50 and 100 years of evolution considering heterogamous preference with various values of GCP. Rectangular bars represent the mean number of individuals per class of individual introgression while error bars are 95% CI. [SOURCE: Page_171]

4.3.2.4    Discussion [SOURCE: Page_171]

Effect on diversity.       These results suggest that heterogamous preference might play a very little role [SOURCE: Page_171]

in the evolution of intraspecific diversity. Indeed, despite the implementation of a higher genetic [SOURCE: Page_171]

contribution to preference (CPG) – up to 100%, the effect of mating preference on diversity dynamics over [SOURCE: Page_171]

the long term remained marginal. In fact, the predicted patterns were quite variable when testing [SOURCE: Page_171]

different values of GCP, suggesting that random processes might strongly influence the outcome of [SOURCE: Page_171]

diversity dynamics. [SOURCE: Page_171]

Heterogamous preference only appears to influence the outcome of the first contact by leading to the [SOURCE: Page_171]

creation of more HYB individuals initially. During a first contact, only pure genotypes – MED and ATL – [SOURCE: Page_171]

160 [SOURCE: Page_171]

4.3 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in a basic case study of first secondary contact [SOURCE: Page_172]

encounter each other. Since female preference is based on a scale of phenotypic dissimilarity, it is easier [SOURCE: Page_172]

to visualise the effect of heterogamous preference in such contexts, where only the two extreme values [SOURCE: Page_172]

of dissimilarity can be found - entirely dissimilar mates (MED-ATL) or entirely similar mates (MED-MED or [SOURCE: Page_172]

ATL-ATL). Indeed, when heterogamous preference is applied, the strength of female preference is almost [SOURCE: Page_172]

twice as high for entirely dissimilar mates (~ 0.8) compared to entirely similar mates (~ 0.45), meaning [SOURCE: Page_172]

that when GCP is considered at its maximum, heterogamous mating is twice as likely to occur compared [SOURCE: Page_172]

to homogamous mating. Whereas, when random preference is considered, heterogamous mating and [SOURCE: Page_172]

homogamous mating have equal chances to occur. [SOURCE: Page_172]

However, throughout time, with the creation of a wide range of hybrids and the disappearance of pure [SOURCE: Page_172]

genotypes, it becomes more and more difficult to envision and predict the effect of such preference. [SOURCE: Page_172]

Indeed, after only a few years of evolution, the population homogenises and is mainly composed of highly [SOURCE: Page_172]

similar HYB individuals. Considering that female preference is absolute (i.e., its strength is expressed [SOURCE: Page_172]

between the minimum and maximum theoretical dissimilarities [0 to 1]), heterogamous preference might [SOURCE: Page_172]

not be expressed as much in that context, as when extremely dissimilar phenotypes can be encountered [SOURCE: Page_172]

(Section 3.3.6: see absolute vs relative preference). [SOURCE: Page_172]

Mating stochasticity.            In our model, despite the implementation of a preference function, the [SOURCE: Page_172]

outcome of mating remains the result of a combination of stochastic factors including available genotypic [SOURCE: Page_172]

variation and mating decision (Section 3.3.6). On the one hand, mating can only occur within mature [SOURCE: Page_172]

individuals located in the same reach at a given time step, which will be determined by a variety of [SOURCE: Page_172]

processes, all partly stochastic (survival, movement …). Therefore, mate choice is likely to be variable in [SOURCE: Page_172]

space and time since population composition may vary. Moreover, a stochastic sampling routine in the [SOURCE: Page_172]

model – independent of phenotypes or genotypes – determines the mating pool available to a given [SOURCE: Page_172]

161 [SOURCE: Page_172]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_173]

female at a given time step, making initial encounters and pairing entirely random. In other terms, the [SOURCE: Page_173]

preference strength and GCP only increase or decrease the chances of certain types of mating being [SOURCE: Page_173]

successful, but they are entirely only conditioned to the fact that an encounter occurs. Additionally, the [SOURCE: Page_173]

ultimate mating decision results from a random draw in a binomial process, even if the value of GCP [SOURCE: Page_173]

governs the probability of this process. [SOURCE: Page_173]

All of this makes the outcome of mating at the population level challenging to predict, despite the [SOURCE: Page_173]

implementation of a specific mating preference, such as heterogamy. For instance, ultimate mating [SOURCE: Page_173]

success will strongly depend on stochastic events and the structure of the population (Klug & Stone, 2021). [SOURCE: Page_173]

Indeed, chance may play an important role in determining mating success and has been shown to [SOURCE: Page_173]

influence the strength of sexual selection mechanisms (Jennions et al., 2012; Sousa & Westneat, 2013; [SOURCE: Page_173]

Sutherland, 1985). However, the relative effect of chance may vary with other factors, such as OSR and [SOURCE: Page_173]

frequency of advantageous phenotypes (Klug & Stone, 2021). It is therefore difficult beforehand to predict [SOURCE: Page_173]

how various factors will influence mating processes (Jennions et al., 2012), even when there is evidence [SOURCE: Page_173]

of some sexual selection mechanisms (Hubbell & Johnson, 1987; Sutherland, 1985). [SOURCE: Page_173]

It also proves that knowing the existence of a mechanism - here heterogamous mating - and even having [SOURCE: Page_173]

quantified its strength in nature does not allow making intuitive predictions on the influence of such [SOURCE: Page_173]

mechanisms in the general eco-evolutionary trends of a population. The DG-ABM approach typically helps [SOURCE: Page_173]

us to project the effect of such mechanisms in the complexity of life cycles, space, inter-individual [SOURCE: Page_173]

interactions and heredity, and the outcome may somewhat differ from our initial expectations. [SOURCE: Page_173]

A very specific context.         The present simulations were tuned to envision a rather theoretical [SOURCE: Page_173]

population, with a large number of spawners and a specific initial population structure - an equal [SOURCE: Page_173]

162 [SOURCE: Page_173]

4.3 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in a basic case study of first secondary contact [SOURCE: Page_174]

proportion of MED and ATL individuals and an equal OSR. Considering this context, it is not entirely [SOURCE: Page_174]

surprising that random preference also led to extensive hybridization, since the initial chance of dissimilar [SOURCE: Page_174]

individuals encountering was high, and progressively became higher with time as parental genotypes [SOURCE: Page_174]

rarefied. In reality, many situations may occur where both OSR and phenotypic availability are contrasted. [SOURCE: Page_174]

In such situations, a specific mating preference may have a greater influence on diversity dynamics. [SOURCE: Page_174]

Indeed, the relative effect of chance on mating success may vary depending on OSR and frequency- [SOURCE: Page_174]

dependence effects, causing sexual selection to operate more or less in certain contexts (Jennions et al., [SOURCE: Page_174]

2012; Klug & Stone, 2021). In addition, the strength of sexual selection arising from behavioural processes [SOURCE: Page_174]

has been shown to change with the environmental and social context (Cotton et al., 2006; Gauthey, 2014). [SOURCE: Page_174]

For example, if one phenotype is more frequent, despite random preference, mating should be biased [SOURCE: Page_174]

toward that common phenotype, as it should be encountered more often due to chances. On the [SOURCE: Page_174]

opposite, heterogamous preference might advantage the rare phenotype, when encountered, due to [SOURCE: Page_174]

negative frequency dependence effects (Kokko et al., 2007; O’Donald & Majerus, 1988). Understanding [SOURCE: Page_174]

if, and when, stochastic events covary with biological factors of interest is important to determine in which [SOURCE: Page_174]

cases we might expect sexual selection to be relatively important in shaping evolution (Klug & Stone, [SOURCE: Page_174]

2021). [SOURCE: Page_174]

The importance of representing mating interactions.       As reproductive interactions are known to drive [SOURCE: Page_174]

evolutionary dynamics (Maan & Seehausen, 2011), explicit representation of sexual interactions remains [SOURCE: Page_174]

important in any case, despite the lack of apparent effects on diversity dynamics in this context. This is [SOURCE: Page_174]

particularly important when considering adaptive processes. Indeed, mate choice could have more effect [SOURCE: Page_174]

on diversity dynamics and demography in cases where the trait under sexual selection is also under [SOURCE: Page_174]

natural selection (Chevalier et al., 2020; Thibert-Plante & Gavrilets, 2013). It could also play an important [SOURCE: Page_174]

163 [SOURCE: Page_174]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_175]

role when sexual selection acts in a different direction compared to other factors of selection (Andersson, [SOURCE: Page_175]

1994), especially when it opposes natural selection (Ellers & Boggs, 2003; Labonne & Hendry, 2010; A. K. [SOURCE: Page_175]

Schwartz & Hendry, 2006). Mating is an important process creating the diversity available for selection to [SOURCE: Page_175]

occur on. In addition, considering the dynamic aspect of mating systems is important when studying [SOURCE: Page_175]

population demography. Indeed, in certain contexts, mating behaviour may play a crucial role in [SOURCE: Page_175]

determining the density threshold for population persistence as it may affect the overall fitness and [SOURCE: Page_175]

growth of the population (Berec et al., 2018). [SOURCE: Page_175]

Take home message:        When considering only heterogamous preference, the effect on diversity [SOURCE: Page_175]

dynamics and demography is close to nothing in our model and the conditions simulated. [SOURCE: Page_175]

4.3.3   The role of GxE interactions on survival [SOURCE: Page_175]

4.3.3.1    Evolution of diversity dynamics under neutral vs GxE survival [SOURCE: Page_175]

Now turning to a possible source of post-zygotic isolation mechanism – GxE interactions on embryonic [SOURCE: Page_175]

survival in relation to water temperature –, very clear and divergent results can be observed in our [SOURCE: Page_175]

simulations, conditional on the presence or absence of GxE interactions. When implementing GxE [SOURCE: Page_175]

interactions on embryonic survival, the introgression rate (Figure 4 – 14) did not stabilise and decreased [SOURCE: Page_175]

continuously over time, reaching approximately 0.1 after a hundred years of evolution. Conversely, it [SOURCE: Page_175]

remained stable and centred on 0.5 for the neutral survival scenario. The confidence interval was rather [SOURCE: Page_175]

low, which implies that the implementation of GxE interactions has a rather strong effect, making the [SOURCE: Page_175]

result more predictable. [SOURCE: Page_175]

164 [SOURCE: Page_175]

4.3 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in a basic case study of first secondary contact [SOURCE: Page_176]

Figure 4—14: Evolution of introgression rate over a hundred years considering neutral versus GxE interactions on embryonic survival (medium strength) applied at 5°C. Solid lines represent mean values averaged over 20 simulations while polygons are 95% credible intervals. [SOURCE: Page_176]

Focusing on genotype proportions (Figure 4 – 15), the composition of the population changed rapidly over [SOURCE: Page_176]

the first time steps for both scenarios, with the number of HYB going up fast while that of pure native [SOURCE: Page_176]

genotypes was going down. While both scenarios led to a population mainly composed of HYB individuals [SOURCE: Page_176]

after a hundred years, the implementation of GxE interactions allowed for the maintenance of some pure [SOURCE: Page_176]

MED genotypes. Moreover, the increase of HYB individuals in the population was lower compared to the [SOURCE: Page_176]

scenario implementing neutral survival. The numbers of HYB and MED individuals did not stabilise after [SOURCE: Page_176]

20 years. The number of HYB began to decrease slowly but continuously, whereas the number of MED [SOURCE: Page_176]

individuals rose at the same pace. [SOURCE: Page_176]

165 [SOURCE: Page_176]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_177]

Figure 4—15: Evolution of the genotypic composition of the population – proportions of MED (full lines), HYB (dashed line) and ATL (dotted line) genotypes – over a hundred years considering neutral versus GxE interactions on embryonic survival (medium strength) applied at 5°C. Solid lines represent mean values averaged over 20 simulations while polygons are 95% credible intervals. [SOURCE: Page_177]

The distribution of individual introgression in the population provided a deeper insight into this pattern [SOURCE: Page_177]

(Figures 4 – 16 & 4 – 17). Indeed, when first contact occurred (Figure 4 – 16, t = 1) a large number of F$_1$ [SOURCE: Page_177]

HYB were initially created for both scenarios. However, with the creation of a wide range of HYB [SOURCE: Page_177]

individuals, distributions diverged progressively over time (Figure 4 – 16, t = 10; neutral: centred on 0.5, [SOURCE: Page_177]

GxE: centred on 0.2). Scenarios implementing GxE interactions selected for low introgressed HYB [SOURCE: Page_177]

individuals (<0.5). In addition, compared to the neutral survival scenario, a higher number of pure MED [SOURCE: Page_177]

individuals was found at each time step while the number of ATL individuals diminished faster. [SOURCE: Page_177]

166 [SOURCE: Page_177]

4.3 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in a basic case study of first secondary contact [SOURCE: Page_178]

Figure 4—16: Distribution of individual introgression during early time steps considering neutral versus GxE interactions on embryonic survival (medium strength) applied at 5°C. Rectangular bars represent the mean number of individuals per class of individual introgression while error bars are 95% CI. [SOURCE: Page_178]

The distribution of individual introgression stabilised fast for the neutral survival scenario while it [SOURCE: Page_178]

continued shifting toward lower values of introgression (Figure 4 – 17, distribution centred on 0.28 at = [SOURCE: Page_178]

50 vs 0.14 at t = 100) for the GxE survival scenario. Alongside this shift the number of MED individuals [SOURCE: Page_178]

increased (Figure 4 – 17, ~ 125 at t = 50 vs ~ 370 at t = 100). Overall, diversity dynamics seem to stabilise [SOURCE: Page_178]

rapidly when survival is similar for all genotypes whereas it is still evolving after a hundred years when [SOURCE: Page_178]

GxE interactions are implemented. [SOURCE: Page_178]

167 [SOURCE: Page_178]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_179]

Figure 4—17: Distribution of individual introgression after 50 and 100 years of evolution considering neutral versus GxE interactions on embryonic survival (medium strength) applied at 5°C. Rectangular bars represent the mean number of individuals per class of individual introgression while error bars are 95% CI. [SOURCE: Page_179]

4.3.3.2   Evolution of demography under neutral vs GxE survival [SOURCE: Page_179]

Demography – i.e. population size and age class structure – was stable over time (Figure 4 – 18). However, [SOURCE: Page_179]

a slightly lower number of juveniles was observed for the scenario implementing GxE interactions (~ 2000 [SOURCE: Page_179]

vs 1900). Sub-adults and adults dynamics remained similar among scenarios, suggesting that GxE [SOURCE: Page_179]

interactions only affect juvenile dynamics but not recruitment to the other life stages. [SOURCE: Page_179]

168 [SOURCE: Page_179]

4.3 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in a basic case study of first secondary contact [SOURCE: Page_180]

Figure 4—18: Evolution of demography structured per age class – juveniles (full lines), sub-adults (dashed line) and adults (dotted line) – over a hundred years of evolution considering neutral versus GxE interactions on embryonic survival (medium strength) applied at 5°C. Lines represent mean values averaged over 20 simulations while polygons are 95% credible intervals. [SOURCE: Page_180]

G x E interactions, applied at 5°C, appear to impact diversity dynamics and demography over short and [SOURCE: Page_180]

long terms. Indeed, GxE interactions influenced the genotypes selected for the next time step by favouring [SOURCE: Page_180]

offspring originating from MED females. However, prior to selection, initial context (50% MED & 50% ATL) [SOURCE: Page_180]

and random mating initially led to the creation of a large amount of F$_1$ HYB individuals. Throughout time, [SOURCE: Page_180]

as selection favoured maternally inherited MED alleles, the distribution of individual introgression was [SOURCE: Page_180]

progressively biased toward lower values of introgression, leading to an important and continuous [SOURCE: Page_180]

decrease in the population introgression rate. The population only appeared to go through a transitory [SOURCE: Page_180]

state where HYB individuals were created. Indeed, the selection of MED alleles over time allowed for a [SOURCE: Page_180]

slow but progressive increase of MED individuals. GxE interactions also led to lower juvenile population [SOURCE: Page_180]

169 [SOURCE: Page_180]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_181]

size, because a part of the population – offspring from the ATL maternal lineage – have lower survival at [SOURCE: Page_181]

hatching. This difference was erased during recruitment to the next life stage. [SOURCE: Page_181]

4.3.3.3    Sensitivity analyses on the strength of GxE interactions and temperature [SOURCE: Page_181]

Sensitivity analyses were performed on the strength of GxE interactions (low, medium or high, Figure 4 – [SOURCE: Page_181]

4, section 4.1.1 & Figure 4 – 5, section 4.1.2) combined with various winter temperature modalities (2.5, [SOURCE: Page_181]

5 and 8°C) in order to account for possible variations in selective pressure experienced. This allowed [SOURCE: Page_181]

evaluating the relative importance of ecological variations, such as temperature, and GxE strength on [SOURCE: Page_181]

diversity dynamics and demography. [SOURCE: Page_181]

The strength of GxE interactions had a clear effect on diversity dynamics and demography. This effect was [SOURCE: Page_181]

highly controlled by the winter temperature. For the high temperature modality – 8°C – GxE strength did [SOURCE: Page_181]

not influence diversity dynamics nor demography, since differences in survival among genotypes are [SOURCE: Page_181]

almost null at that temperature (Figure 4 – 4, section 4.1.1 & Figure 4 – 5, section 4.1.2). For all three GxE [SOURCE: Page_181]

scenarios, HYB individuals were rapidly created and parental genotypes fully disappeared before 10 years [SOURCE: Page_181]

(Figure 4 – 20). Introgression rates were stable (Figure 4 – 19) and distributions of individual introgression [SOURCE: Page_181]

were centred on 0.5 (Figure 4 – 21). These scenarios are therefore equivalent to the neutral survival [SOURCE: Page_181]

scenario. [SOURCE: Page_181]

For the other temperature modalities, – 2.5 and 5°C – GxE strength had a strong impact on diversity [SOURCE: Page_181]

dynamics. For all scenarios, the introgression rate decreased over time (Figure 4 – 19), but the decrease [SOURCE: Page_181]

was steeper and faster when the temperature was lower – at 2.5°C compared to 5°C – and when the [SOURCE: Page_181]

strength of GxE interactions was higher. [SOURCE: Page_181]

170 [SOURCE: Page_181]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_183]

However, for the lower temperature, one can notice that introgression dynamics were similar for the [SOURCE: Page_183]

medium and the high modality of GxE interactions. Introgression rates reached almost 0 after a hundred [SOURCE: Page_183]

years in both cases, suggesting that selective pressures experienced through temperature were strong. [SOURCE: Page_183]

This is because differences in survival implemented at lower temperatures are the highest (Figure 4 – 5, [SOURCE: Page_183]

section 4.1.2). [SOURCE: Page_183]

The evolution of genotypes proportion (Figure 4 – 20) highlighted more differences among scenarios and [SOURCE: Page_183]

confirmed the combined effect of GxE interactions and temperatures on diversity dynamics. The shift [SOURCE: Page_183]

observed in MED and HYB dynamics happened faster when GxE strength was higher and even more so [SOURCE: Page_183]

when the temperature was lower. Indeed, comparing GxE scenarios at 2.5°C, the extent of the shift can [SOURCE: Page_183]

be visualised within a hundred years. MED individuals took over HYB individuals faster when the strength [SOURCE: Page_183]

of GxE interactions was higher – respectively at t = 55, t = 62 and t = 95 for the high, medium and low [SOURCE: Page_183]

modalities. Overall, the initial creation of HYB individuals was slightly slower and then decreased faster, [SOURCE: Page_183]

while conversely, the decrease in MED individuals was slower and then increased faster, especially when [SOURCE: Page_183]

high GxE interactions were implemented. At 5°C, the strength of GxE interactions also affected genotypes [SOURCE: Page_183]

proportion but differences appeared lower as the shift happened at a slower rate – because differences [SOURCE: Page_183]

in survival implemented are lower (Figure 4 – 5, section 4.1.2). Meanwhile, the disappearance of ATL [SOURCE: Page_183]

individuals happened at the same rate since the survival of ATL females' offspring is low in any case. [SOURCE: Page_183]

For all scenarios, even the fastest one, the genotypic composition of the population was unstable and still [SOURCE: Page_183]

evolved after a hundred years, suggesting that selective pressure enforced by GxE interactions might [SOURCE: Page_183]

ultimately lead to a MED population, in the simulated conditions. This pattern was confirmed by the [SOURCE: Page_183]

distribution of individual introgression (Figure 4 – 21), which shifted faster when GxE interactions were [SOURCE: Page_183]

stronger and even more so when temperatures were lower. For example, at 2.5°C the distribution was [SOURCE: Page_183]

already biased toward 0 at t = 50 years whereas it took longer at 5°C since the distribution was still not [SOURCE: Page_183]

centred on 0 – but 0.07 – at t = 100, and this for all GxE modalities. [SOURCE: Page_183]

175 [SOURCE: Page_186]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_187]

Temperature modality highly influenced demography (Figure 4 – 22). Indeed, population size was higher [SOURCE: Page_187]

for the lower temperature, and even more so for juveniles. In addition, at 2.5°C, the number of juveniles [SOURCE: Page_187]

was the highest for the highest strength of GxE interactions. This could be explained by the survival [SOURCE: Page_187]

differences implemented at different temperatures and for various strengths of GxE interactions. Indeed, [SOURCE: Page_187]

to increase the survival difference between both lineages, offspring survival is generally higher for MED [SOURCE: Page_187]

females when selective pressure is higher, while it is lower for ATL females (Figure 4 – 5, section 4.1.2). [SOURCE: Page_187]

This allows the optimal fitness of the population to be higher when selective pressures are higher. [SOURCE: Page_187]

4.3.3.4    Discussion [SOURCE: Page_187]

Effect on diversity.       The above-described results indicate that the implementation of GxE interactions [SOURCE: Page_187]

on embryonic survival – a fitness component – had a strong influence over the evolution of intraspecific [SOURCE: Page_187]

diversity on short but also long time scales. Indeed, the fitness differences implemented appear to select [SOURCE: Page_187]

against ATL alleles over time – and hence for MED alleles. [SOURCE: Page_187]

However, selection only acts on alleles transmitted maternally, as survival differences are implemented [SOURCE: Page_187]

based on maternal genotype (Section 3.3.2). Therefore, there might be some room for potentially [SOURCE: Page_187]

maladapted ATL genes to be transmitted via paternal inheritance. This may partially explain the shifting [SOURCE: Page_187]

patterns of genotype distribution observed, where HYB individuals were initially created following [SOURCE: Page_187]

secondary contact, before being purged out of the population very progressively. Despite the “random” [SOURCE: Page_187]

transmission of paternal alleles, the proportion of ATL alleles in the population kept decreasing – decrease [SOURCE: Page_187]

in introgression rate and continuous shift toward lowly introgressed HYB individuals – suggesting that [SOURCE: Page_187]

selection was strong enough to eliminate ATL alleles, over the long term at least. As offspring survival [SOURCE: Page_187]

depends on the proportion of ATL alleles carried by females, selection favours offspring from MED or lowly [SOURCE: Page_187]

introgressed females, thereby progressively increasing the rate of MED alleles in the population, but also [SOURCE: Page_187]

176 [SOURCE: Page_187]

4.3 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in a basic case study of first secondary contact [SOURCE: Page_188]

in males, which will ultimately accelerate the decrease of ATL alleles in the population. The creation of [SOURCE: Page_188]

hybrid individuals, therefore, appears to be a compulsory but only transitory stage, where a substantial [SOURCE: Page_188]

genetic load is created, since selection cannot act directly on paternally inherited alleles. This indicates [SOURCE: Page_188]

that it is important to follow evolution over the long term and not just over a few years. Over the long [SOURCE: Page_188]

term, this selection results in patterns of directional selection (Futuyma, 2009), since an extreme [SOURCE: Page_188]

phenotype is selected against (ATL) while the other extreme is selected for (MED), as indicated by the [SOURCE: Page_188]

shift in individual introgression toward one end of the spectrum. [SOURCE: Page_188]

Types of natural selection^4.          Wallace (1975) identified two types of natural selection, which can have [SOURCE: Page_188]

different effects on population dynamics: soft selection and hard selection (Bell et al., 2021). [SOURCE: Page_188]

 “Soft selection occurs when the absolute fitness of an individual depends on its trait value relative [SOURCE: Page_188]

to that of other conspecifics with which it interacts (i.e. the phenotypic composition of the [SOURCE: Page_188]

population).” Soft selection often occurs when competition over a limited resource occurs – such [SOURCE: Page_188]

as for territories or mating partners; it is, therefore, density and frequency-dependent. As a result, [SOURCE: Page_188]

it will influence the composition of the population but will not incur a demographic cost. [SOURCE: Page_188]

 “Hard selection occurs when the absolute fitness of an individual depends on its absolute trait [SOURCE: Page_188]

value and is independent of the phenotypic composition of the population.” Hard selection is [SOURCE: Page_188]

density and frequency independent and can incur a demographic cost on the life stage where [SOURCE: Page_188]

selection occurs, potentially impacting population dynamics. [SOURCE: Page_188]

4 Arguments are from Bell et al., 2021; and definitions are directly extracted from Box 1 Glossary. [SOURCE: Page_188]

177 [SOURCE: Page_188]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_189]

Our results point toward patterns of hard natural selection involving an abiotic factor – temperature. [SOURCE: Page_189]

Indeed, individuals’ fitness is here independent of the population genetic composition but only depends [SOURCE: Page_189]

on the fitness associated with the trait value – individual introgression – for a specific temperature. [SOURCE: Page_189]

Therefore, effects on demography and potentially population dynamics are to be expected. [SOURCE: Page_189]

Effects on demography.                    The implementation of GxE interactions had a slight impact on [SOURCE: Page_189]

population dynamics, as it slightly decreased the number of juveniles. However, this effect did not [SOURCE: Page_189]

translate into a decrease in population growth, as other life stages were not impacted. This is not [SOURCE: Page_189]

surprising, since hard selection often results in reduced survival or reproductive success in the life stage [SOURCE: Page_189]

where it is applied, but does not always influence population dynamics (Bell et al., 2021). This may have [SOURCE: Page_189]

multiple explanations. First, selection may be too weak to influence population growth significantly (Bell [SOURCE: Page_189]

et al., 2021). However, considering the impact of selection on diversity dynamics, this is unlikely to be the [SOURCE: Page_189]

case. Second, the life stage affected by selection may have little influence on population growth (Mills, [SOURCE: Page_189]

2013). This is also unlikely here since juveniles’ survival will affect recruitment in fish populations. [SOURCE: Page_189]

Alternatively, it could be explained by mechanisms of compensatory selection, where selective mortality [SOURCE: Page_189]

at one life stage can be compensated partially or entirely later on (Bell et al., 2021). Indeed, hard selection [SOURCE: Page_189]

can be expected to increase mean fitness in subsequent generations (Fisher, 1930), or to be compensated [SOURCE: Page_189]

for under the effects of density-dependence (Ratikainen et al., 2008; Reed, Gienapp, et al., 2015). For [SOURCE: Page_189]

example, focusing on selection happening early on in life, more juveniles are produced compared to what [SOURCE: Page_189]

can be recruited in the population (one of Darwin’s observations that led to natural selection). Therefore, [SOURCE: Page_189]

hard selection may only remove this excess from the population. However, by acting on a specific [SOURCE: Page_189]

genotype, hard selection reduces its proportion in the population, thereby changing the composition of [SOURCE: Page_189]

the population, but without specifically affecting demography, if sufficient diversity is available. [SOURCE: Page_189]

178 [SOURCE: Page_189]

4.3 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in a basic case study of first secondary contact [SOURCE: Page_190]

In our context, where both MED and ATL diversity was initially available, selection, by removing [SOURCE: Page_190]

deleterious ATL alleles, may have resulted in an increase in the mean fitness of the population over [SOURCE: Page_190]

generations. Indeed, as MED alleles were more transmitted over time and ATL alleles decreased in [SOURCE: Page_190]

frequency, the population genetic structure changed and was progressively made of more and more [SOURCE: Page_190]

advantageous phenotypes, thereby increasing the population fitness. Sometimes such mechanisms of [SOURCE: Page_190]

compensatory selection may even increase population growth (Abrams, 2009; McIntire & Juliano, 2018). [SOURCE: Page_190]

It appears to be the case here when higher selective pressures are implemented. Indeed, results of [SOURCE: Page_190]

sensitivity analyses indicated that higher selective pressure actually resulted in higher population size, as [SOURCE: Page_190]

stronger selection more rapidly selected for advantageous MED alleles, thereby leading to a higher mean [SOURCE: Page_190]

fitness of the population. [SOURCE: Page_190]

In a different context of diversity – where only ATL diversity will be available – such patterns of GxE [SOURCE: Page_190]

interactions could have had stronger and more visible consequences on population growth. Indeed, [SOURCE: Page_190]

increased mortality or reduced fertility may incur a demographic cost and thereby affect population [SOURCE: Page_190]

dynamics (sensu Haldane, 1957). For instance, in an ATL population, low juvenile survival might result in [SOURCE: Page_190]

reduced recruitment. As mean population fitness will remain low throughout generations, there might [SOURCE: Page_190]

not be room for compensatory mechanisms, thereby resulting in a decline in population size. [SOURCE: Page_190]

Evidence of an eco-evolutionary dynamic.          The implementation of GxE interactions on survival [SOURCE: Page_190]

created an eco-evolutionary loop, affecting both diversity (evolution) and population (ecology) dynamics. [SOURCE: Page_190]

However, the demographic influence of evolutionary responses to selection might not always be visible [SOURCE: Page_190]

and can lead to a type of cryptic eco-evolutionary dynamics (M. Kinnison et al., 2015). Indeed, in our case, [SOURCE: Page_190]

in response to hard selection, evolution eliminated alleles that may have caused reduced fitness, [SOURCE: Page_190]

179 [SOURCE: Page_190]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_191]

potentially maladaptation; which otherwise could have led to a population decline. Understanding the [SOURCE: Page_191]

links between natural selection and population dynamics is central to eco-evolutionary dynamics (Carroll [SOURCE: Page_191]

et al., 2007; Hendry, 2017) and responses to anthropogenic changes caused by disturbances, climate [SOURCE: Page_191]

change or management practices (Pelletier et Coltman, 2018). [SOURCE: Page_191]

Speed of selection.      The implementation of stronger selective pressures – through modification in GxE [SOURCE: Page_191]

strength or temperature – appeared to result in stronger and hence faster selection. Indeed, the higher [SOURCE: Page_191]

fitness differences were between the advantage (MED) and the disadvantage (ATL) phenotype in a specific [SOURCE: Page_191]

environment, the faster the elimination of ATL alleles and the directional selection was. The strength of [SOURCE: Page_191]

selection is highly related to differences in individuals’ fitness. In other words, considering Darwin’s idea [SOURCE: Page_191]

of natural selection, the more one genotype is advantaged over the other; the faster natural selection will [SOURCE: Page_191]

act in favour of that genotype, as it will transmit more alleles to the next generation (Darwin, 1859). It is [SOURCE: Page_191]

the case in our model where differences in fitness increase with an increase in the strength of GxE [SOURCE: Page_191]

interactions and/or a decrease in temperature (Figure 4 – 5, section 4.1.2). The consideration of the fitness [SOURCE: Page_191]

landscape associated with a trait undergoing such directional selection is important to understand the [SOURCE: Page_191]

immediate effects of selection on demography. Indeed, in a population, the mean fitness will decrease [SOURCE: Page_191]

progressively as the mean trait value gets further away from the optimal phenotype (Bell et al. 2021). [SOURCE: Page_191]

Temperature variations.          The selective pressure exerted by temperature greatly influenced the [SOURCE: Page_191]

evolution of diversity dynamics. Indeed, the lower temperature was, the shorter the transitory stage of [SOURCE: Page_191]

hybridization was and the faster the effects of directional selection could be seen, as the distribution of [SOURCE: Page_191]

the trait was more rapidly biased toward MED phenotypes. This suggests that considering variation in [SOURCE: Page_191]

ecological parameters that may drive natural selection is essential to predict the evolution of diversity [SOURCE: Page_191]

dynamics. [SOURCE: Page_191]

180 [SOURCE: Page_191]

4.3 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in a basic case study of first secondary contact [SOURCE: Page_192]

Ecological parameters, such as temperature, are often varying in space and time, thereby consistently [SOURCE: Page_192]

driving variation in selective environments, which could potentially be linked to adaptation (Carroll et al. [SOURCE: Page_192]

2007). With the increasing impact of climate change on natural environments (Pelletier & Coltman, 2018), [SOURCE: Page_192]

considering the ecological context in which natural selection occurs is crucial to understand its influence [SOURCE: Page_192]

on contemporary evolution and demography (Bell et al. 2021). Indeed, changes in environmental [SOURCE: Page_192]

conditions may consistently alter the balance between gene flow and natural selection, potentially [SOURCE: Page_192]

influencing adaptive divergence but also population dynamics (Garant et al., 2007; Hendry, 2017; M. T. [SOURCE: Page_192]

Kinnison & Hairston Jr, 2007). For example, despite potential hybridization, local adaptation may still occur [SOURCE: Page_192]

in environments where the forces of selection exceed the homogenizing effects of gene flow (Garant et [SOURCE: Page_192]

al. 2007), which appears to be the case in our context. [SOURCE: Page_192]

The direction of selection.      In this specific case study, patterns of selection were quite clear and [SOURCE: Page_192]

directional, as selective pressure and fitness differences were constant in time. However, changes in [SOURCE: Page_192]

selective pressure may change the strength and direction of selection, potentially generating different [SOURCE: Page_192]

and more fluctuating patterns of diversity over time. Indeed, in reality, selection and evolution fluctuate [SOURCE: Page_192]

in direction, as it is often tracking environmental conditions, making that small changes may rarely [SOURCE: Page_192]

accumulate in such a long directional trend (Ellner et al., 1999; Gibbs & Grant, 1987; Grant & Grant, 2002, [SOURCE: Page_192]

2006; N. G. Hairston & Dillon, 1990). For example, in our case selection against ATL alleles might be slowed [SOURCE: Page_192]

down, or even prevented if temperatures were to increase since fitness differences will be reduced. [SOURCE: Page_192]

Take home message:       The maternal genotype-by-temperature interaction over embryonic survival is [SOURCE: Page_192]

structuring diversity dynamics and demography in the conditions simulated in our model. [SOURCE: Page_192]

181 [SOURCE: Page_192]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_193]

4.3.4   The relative importance of heterogamous preference considering GxE interactions on survival [SOURCE: Page_193]

When implemented separately and compared to a neutral scenario (respectively random preference and [SOURCE: Page_193]

neutral survival), GxE interactions on survival seem to play a structuring role in the evolution of diversity, [SOURCE: Page_193]

over short and long time scales, while heterogamous preference barely has any influence. However, [SOURCE: Page_193]

evaluating the combined effect of both mechanisms remains important, especially considering that [SOURCE: Page_193]

heterogamous preference and GxE interactions on survival are opposite forces. While one favours [SOURCE: Page_193]

hybridization, the other selects MED diversity. Therefore, implementing heterogamous preference in [SOURCE: Page_193]

addition to GxE interactions could modify the rate of selection by influencing the genetic variance [SOURCE: Page_193]

available for selection. Given the role of heterogamous preference and GxE interactions implemented [SOURCE: Page_193]

alone, I suspect it might slow down selection. This section aims at investigating the impact of [SOURCE: Page_193]

heterogamous preference on the selection resulting from GxE interactions on survival. First, scenarios [SOURCE: Page_193]

only implementing GxE interactions were compared to scenarios implementing GxE interactions [SOURCE: Page_193]

combined with heterogamous preference – at 35% of GCP. Here the various strengths of GxE interactions [SOURCE: Page_193]

were considered – at 5°C only – to evaluate if stronger or lower selective pressures are more or less [SOURCE: Page_193]

affected by the implementation of heterogamous preference. Then a sensitivity analysis was conducted [SOURCE: Page_193]

on heterogamous preference for medium GxE interactions to evaluate if higher percentages of GCP have [SOURCE: Page_193]

a stronger influence on selection. [SOURCE: Page_193]

4.3.4.1    Does heterogamous preference affect the selection mediated by GxE interactions? [SOURCE: Page_193]

The implementation of heterogamous preference while considering GxE interactions influenced diversity [SOURCE: Page_193]

dynamics. Its effect slightly differed in intensity, based on the strength of GxE interactions. Indeed, when [SOURCE: Page_193]

considering heterogamous preference, the decrease in introgression rate was slower for all three [SOURCE: Page_193]

182 [SOURCE: Page_193]

4.3 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in a basic case study of first secondary contact [SOURCE: Page_194]

modalities of GxE interactions, but even more so for the lower and medium ones (Figure 4 – 23). The same [SOURCE: Page_194]

observation could be made regarding genotype proportion (Figure 4 – 24), where the decrease in HYB [SOURCE: Page_194]

individuals and the increase in MED individuals were slower. The distribution of individual introgression [SOURCE: Page_194]

(Figure 4 – 25) confirmed that heterogamous preference influences the outcome of first contact, with [SOURCE: Page_194]

more HYB individuals initially created and fewer MED individuals. After a hundred years of evolution, [SOURCE: Page_194]

when GxE interactions were strong, the impact of heterogamous preference on diversity dynamics faded [SOURCE: Page_194]

away whereas it was still visible for the medium and lower modalities. Indeed, the introgression rate was [SOURCE: Page_194]

still slightly higher (Figure 4 – 23: Low GxE ~ 0.24 vs ~ 0.2 and medium GxE ~ 0.17 vs ~ 0.14) and a shift in [SOURCE: Page_194]

the distribution of individual introgression could still be observed (Figure 4 – 25, t = 100). [SOURCE: Page_194]

Solid lines represent mean values averaged over 20 simulations while polygons are 95% credible intervals. [SOURCE: Page_194]

Solid lines represent mean values averaged over 20 simulations while polygons are 95% credible intervals. [SOURCE: Page_195]

These results suggest that heterogamous preference slows down selection, and even more so when [SOURCE: Page_196]

selective pressure is lower. In other words, stronger selection might be more resistant to the effect of [SOURCE: Page_196]

heterogamous preference. However, observed differences remained very low, possibly due to the low [SOURCE: Page_196]

value of GCP implemented. [SOURCE: Page_196]

4.3.4.2   Does higher genetic contribution to heterogamous preference have a stronger influence on selection? [SOURCE: Page_197]

When implementing higher values of GCP, a pattern was observed: the more the system was [SOURCE: Page_197]

heterogamous, the lower the decrease in introgression rate was (Figure 4 – 26) and the higher the number [SOURCE: Page_197]

of HYB individuals (Figure 4 – 27) was. Indeed, the distribution of individual introgression during first [SOURCE: Page_197]

contact confirmed that higher values of GCP lead to the creation of more and more HYB to the detriment [SOURCE: Page_197]

of MED individuals during the first time step (Figure 4 – 28: at 35% ~ 1000 HYB and ~ 760 MED vs 1150 [SOURCE: Page_197]

HYB and 620 MED at 100% of heterogamy). [SOURCE: Page_197]

Solid lines represent mean values averaged over 20 simulations while polygons are 95% credible intervals. [SOURCE: Page_198]

After a hundred years of evolution, a shift in the distribution of individual introgression could still be [SOURCE: Page_198]

observed (Figure 4 – 28: at 35% centred on 0.14 and at 100% centred on 0.21). Moreover, throughout the [SOURCE: Page_198]

years, differences in the genotypic composition of the population were progressively accentuated, with [SOURCE: Page_198]

the increase in MED individuals being faster for lower values of GCP (Figure 4 – 27). These results suggest [SOURCE: Page_198]

that heterogamous preference might slow down the selection of MED individuals, but does not prevent [SOURCE: Page_198]

the selection of MED alleles in the population, as observed differences in introgression rate remained very [SOURCE: Page_198]

small (Figure 4 – 26: ~ 0.18 à 100% vs ~ 0.14 à 35%). [SOURCE: Page_198]

4.3.4.3   Discussion [SOURCE: Page_199]

We have seen that while GxE interactions yielded tremendous effects on diversity dynamics, [SOURCE: Page_199]

heterogamous mating only had little influence; when both mechanisms were considered independently. [SOURCE: Page_199]

However, when considered together, the implementation of heterogamous preference in addition to GxE [SOURCE: Page_199]

interactions changed the rate and magnitude of selection. Indeed, the decrease in population [SOURCE: Page_199]

introgression rate – so in ATL alleles – and the shift in the distribution of individual introgression – and [SOURCE: Page_199]

hence in genotype – were slower. Since selection only acts on maternally inherited alleles, room for [SOURCE: Page_199]

maladapted alleles is possible through paternal inheritance. In this context, heterogamous preference [SOURCE: Page_199]

accentuates this effect, as the most advantaged females – MED females – will preferentially select for the [SOURCE: Page_199]

less adapted phenotype – ATL males; leading to the initial creation of more HYB individuals and a longer [SOURCE: Page_199]

188 [SOURCE: Page_199]

4.3 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in a basic case study of first secondary contact [SOURCE: Page_200]

transitory hybrid stage. Therefore, the implementation of heterogamous preference in addition to GxE [SOURCE: Page_200]

interactions participates in creating and maintaining more hybrid polymorphisms associated with a [SOURCE: Page_200]

genetic load. [SOURCE: Page_200]

A pleiotropic effect.             GxE interactions and female preference are both coded by the same [SOURCE: Page_200]

genetic variation, making our loci partially pleiotropic. Indeed, the seven loci implemented in the model [SOURCE: Page_200]

code for individual introgression. This trait is directly involved in GxE interactions and indirectly in female [SOURCE: Page_200]

preference, as it is respectively used as a direct measure of maternal genotype and an indirect measure [SOURCE: Page_200]

of phenotype. For the latter, the relationship between an individual genotype and its phenotype will [SOURCE: Page_200]

depend on the value of GCP. Consequently, ATL alleles that are disfavoured by natural selection, through [SOURCE: Page_200]

maternal genotype, might be favoured by sexual selection, as female preference increases the [SOURCE: Page_200]

reproductive fitness of ATL males; thereby countering the effect of natural selection. [SOURCE: Page_200]

Sensitivity analyses.             Natural selection mediated by GxE interactions can be more or less [SOURCE: Page_200]

slowed down by heterogamous preference, depending on the strength of selection and the genetic [SOURCE: Page_200]

contribution to preference implemented. [SOURCE: Page_200]

On the one hand, stronger selection – higher strength of GxE interactions – appears to be more resistant [SOURCE: Page_200]

to the antagonistic effect of heterogamous preference. Indeed, compared to the lower and medium [SOURCE: Page_200]

strength of GxE interactions, diversity dynamics were less affected by the implementation of [SOURCE: Page_200]

heterogamous preference. This could be explained by the fact that stronger natural selection acts faster [SOURCE: Page_200]

to shift the distribution of traits – individual introgression – leaving fewer phenotypes available for sexual [SOURCE: Page_200]

selection to act on. [SOURCE: Page_200]

189 [SOURCE: Page_200]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_201]

On the other hand, the implementation of higher values of GCP appeared to slow down selection more. [SOURCE: Page_201]

This is because the genetic variation coding for natural selection becomes more and more related to the [SOURCE: Page_201]

phenotypic variation coding for female preference, exerting the pleiotropic effect mentioned above. [SOURCE: Page_201]

Increasing links between trait variation and ecological interactions will increase the importance of [SOURCE: Page_201]

evolution to ecological interactions and vice-versa (N. G. J. Hairston et al., 2005). [SOURCE: Page_201]

Antagonism between natural and sexual selection.                    In some instances, sexual selection can, [SOURCE: Page_201]

indeed, be antagonistic to natural selection (Ellers & Boggs, 2003; Labonne & Hendry, 2010; A. K. Schwartz [SOURCE: Page_201]

& Hendry, 2006) and slow down or even prevent populations’ mean phenotype from reaching a fitness [SOURCE: Page_201]

optimum (Andersson, 1994), sometimes preventing adaptation to occur (Chenoweth et al., 2015). The [SOURCE: Page_201]

outcome of such interplay will likely vary based on the relative strength and direction of each selection [SOURCE: Page_201]

force (Kasimatis et al., 2019; Labonne & Hendry, 2010). For example, Labonne & Hendry (2010) also [SOURCE: Page_201]

showed that sexual selection through female choice could modify the effects of divergent natural [SOURCE: Page_201]

selection, but this is depending on the strength of natural selection and the direction of sexual selection. [SOURCE: Page_201]

Many authors have argued for the consideration of the role of sexual selection, in addition to natural [SOURCE: Page_201]

selection, in shaping the potential for evolution (Labonne & Hendry, 2010; A. K. Schwartz & Hendry, 2006; [SOURCE: Page_201]

Svensson & Gosden, 2007). [SOURCE: Page_201]

A form of sexual conflict.                In evolution, this antagonism could have the potential to create [SOURCE: Page_201]

a sexual conflict. Indeed, while inheritance from ATL males will have a negative effect on female [SOURCE: Page_201]

descendants, through reduced survival of their offspring; it will have a positive effect on male [SOURCE: Page_201]

descendants, through potential increased mating success. However, as the autosomal genome is shared [SOURCE: Page_201]

between the sexes, the fitness optimum of each sex cannot be reached. Indeed, even if selection can push [SOURCE: Page_201]

190 [SOURCE: Page_201]

4.3 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in a basic case study of first secondary contact [SOURCE: Page_202]

males and females in opposite directions initially (Bonduriansky & Chenoweth, 2009; W. R. Rice & Holland, [SOURCE: Page_202]

1997); after sexual reproduction chromosomal segregation re-arranges autosomal associations across the [SOURCE: Page_202]

sexes (Kasimatis et al., 2019). This type of sexual conflict could cause the maintenance of high genetic [SOURCE: Page_202]

polymorphisms with a substantial genetic load (Chenoweth et al., 2015; Kasimatis et al., 2019). [SOURCE: Page_202]

Role of natural selection.                However, in our context, the implementation of heterogamous [SOURCE: Page_202]

preference did not seem to result in patterns of sexual selection over the long term. Even when [SOURCE: Page_202]

considering the highest value of GCP implemented (100%), long-term effects on diversity dynamics [SOURCE: Page_202]

remained small. The genetic load created during the hybrid stage was still removed over the long term. [SOURCE: Page_202]

Therefore, natural selection is here a much stronger force, as it remains efficient at removing deleterious [SOURCE: Page_202]

alleles and still results in patterns of directional selection. In fact, heterogamous preference is indirectly [SOURCE: Page_202]

and negatively impacted by natural selection resulting from GxE interactions. Indeed, while it does not [SOURCE: Page_202]

affect her reproductive success directly, it reduces her female descendants' reproductive success. This [SOURCE: Page_202]

indirect selection explains both: why a transitory hybrid stage is initially possible, but also why selection [SOURCE: Page_202]

still acts in the same direction despite the implementation of heterogamous preference. [SOURCE: Page_202]

Role of female preference.                In addition, in our model, female preference is not associated [SOURCE: Page_202]

with any direct benefits for females, through direct natural selection (Kirkpatrick, 1996). The [SOURCE: Page_202]

implementation of a slight fitness advantage conferred to offspring from ATL males, as suggested in Folio [SOURCE: Page_202]

et al. (2021), could potentially result in heterogamous preference playing a more structuring role in the [SOURCE: Page_202]

evolution of diversity. Indeed, for sexual selection to shape evolution, some additional selective forces [SOURCE: Page_202]

operating directly on female preference might be required, as direct selection has been shown to be more [SOURCE: Page_202]

effective at driving the evolution of female preference (Hall et al., 2004; Qvarnström et al., 2006). [SOURCE: Page_202]

191 [SOURCE: Page_202]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_203]

The balance of the forces between natural and sexual selection is always important to consider when [SOURCE: Page_203]

studying the evolution of diversity, as both have the potential to drive evolution, potentially in an [SOURCE: Page_203]

antagonistic way. Particularly, in a management context, their consideration is crucial as they might [SOURCE: Page_203]

interact with management practices to drive evolution, sometimes in unpredictable ways. In addition, [SOURCE: Page_203]

each situation should be considered unique. Indeed, context could influence the relative roles of these [SOURCE: Page_203]

EEMs in driving diversity dynamics, as it will determine population structure and ecological interactions, [SOURCE: Page_203]

thereby potentially affecting eco-evolutionary dynamics differently. [SOURCE: Page_203]

192 [SOURCE: Page_203]

4.4 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in more complex case studies [SOURCE: Page_204]

## 4.4 EXPLORING VARIOUS ECO-EVOLUTIONARY SCENARIOS IN MORE COMPLEX CASE STUDIES

The previous part gave us a grasp of the potential role played by each of the eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_204]

(EEMs) – i.e. mate preference behaviour and GxE interactions on embryonic survival – implemented in [SOURCE: Page_204]

the model, independently and combined. Now, turning to more complex case studies, I will use [SOURCE: Page_204]

MEDITERRANEA to evaluate whether their role is similar when considering more complex hydrographic [SOURCE: Page_204]

networks, where various environmental parameters (temperature, population structure but also [SOURCE: Page_204]

landscape features such as connectivity) and management practices could interact to also influence [SOURCE: Page_204]

diversity dynamics and demography. To do so, I will use three real case studies, where some indicators of [SOURCE: Page_204]

diversity and demography had been monitored before and after the implementation of management [SOURCE: Page_204]

practices. I will compare the evolution of diversity dynamics and demography under the four main eco- [SOURCE: Page_204]

evolutionary scenarios defined in section 4.1.1. Sensitivity analyses will be presented for mechanisms of [SOURCE: Page_204]

interest only. [SOURCE: Page_204]

 My first aim will be to explore how the EEMs influence the outcome of management practices [SOURCE: Page_204]

and point out relevant parameters (ecological parameters, landscape features, and initial [SOURCE: Page_204]

population structure/composition) that may interact with these mechanisms to influence [SOURCE: Page_204]

diversity and demography in each context. [SOURCE: Page_204]

 My second objective will be to compare the available empirical data to our simulation data and [SOURCE: Page_204]

check whether the inclusion of these EEMs improves or degrades the ability of the model to [SOURCE: Page_204]

predict the empirical findings. [SOURCE: Page_204]

As winter temperature is influencing post-zygotic embryonic survival, we will simulate each scenario [SOURCE: Page_204]

under two temperature models, considering warmer versus colder winter temperatures, so to investigate [SOURCE: Page_204]

what could have been the role of such selective pressure in shaping patterns of diversity. [SOURCE: Page_204]

193 [SOURCE: Page_204]
