## CASE STUDY 1:

THE CHEVENNE CREEK [SOURCE: Page_205]

4.4 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in the Chevenne Creek [SOURCE: Page_206]

4.4.1   The Chevenne Creek [SOURCE: Page_206]

4.4.1.1   Study site [SOURCE: Page_206]

The Chevenne Creek, a tributary of the Dranse d'Abondance River, is a typical middle-altitude northern [SOURCE: Page_206]

French Alpine mountain stream. It is 2.5 kilometres long and ranges between 1 to 4 metres in width. [SOURCE: Page_206]

Elevation ranges from 1250 to 1000 metres with a mean slope of 10%. It presents a heterogeneous and [SOURCE: Page_206]

fragmented habitat characterised by riffles, cascades, and pools (Figure 4 – 29). This stream lies within [SOURCE: Page_206]

the geographical range of Mediterranean brown trout with its downstream and median parts harbouring [SOURCE: Page_206]

a nearly pure MED population (Largiadèr et al., 1996; Largiadèr & Scholl, 1996; Launey et al., 2003). [SOURCE: Page_206]

Figure 4—29: Representation and characteristics of the Chevenne Creek, an affluent of the Dranse d’Abondance River. Its unpassable barriers are represented. Figure modified from Caudron and Champigneulle (2011). [SOURCE: Page_206]

195 [SOURCE: Page_206]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_207]

4.4.1.2   Historical management [SOURCE: Page_207]

Stocking of brown trout had already begun in 1913 in the Mediterranean area. In the Chevenne Creek, it [SOURCE: Page_207]

led to allopatric spatial repartition with the presence of a non-native ATL population in the isolated upper [SOURCE: Page_207]

part of the river. The downstream native MED population was initially barely impacted by these practices, [SOURCE: Page_207]

but since it was located immediately downstream (Barnetta, 2005); it was threatened by an upstream ATL [SOURCE: Page_207]

gene flow. Indeed, downstream migration of ATL trout led to a gradient of hybridization in the [SOURCE: Page_207]

intermediate part of the river. As fisheries managers became more aware of the importance of conserving [SOURCE: Page_207]

native populations and intraspecific diversity they became interested in the conservation of remaining [SOURCE: Page_207]

native brown trout populations to protect well-adapted local populations. Therefore, several [SOURCE: Page_207]

management practices were carried out to preserve this native population (Figure 4 – 30). [SOURCE: Page_207]

Figure 4—30: Management history timeline for the Chevenne Creek. [SOURCE: Page_207]

A genetic refuge (GR) – where all stocking practices are stopped – was set in 1993 and angling was banned [SOURCE: Page_207]

in 1998. However, the upstream ATL population persisted despite years without stocking and genetic [SOURCE: Page_207]

analysis indicated that the admixture pattern between the MED and the ATL lineages had not changed [SOURCE: Page_207]

significantly (25 to 20%) within 10 years without stocking and angling (Caudron & Champigneulle, 2011). [SOURCE: Page_207]

Therefore, from 2006 to 2009, non-native ATL individuals were removed from the upper part of the river [SOURCE: Page_207]

196 [SOURCE: Page_207]

4.4 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in the Chevenne Creek [SOURCE: Page_208]

by electrofishing, in order to stop this gene flow. Each year two electrofishing samplings were carried out [SOURCE: Page_208]

and all trout that had been caught were removed from the river. The removal efficiency was estimated [SOURCE: Page_208]

per year based on the number of trout caught and estimated population size per stage: juveniles (or young [SOURCE: Page_208]

of the year), sub-adults and adults. Caudron & Champigneulle (2011) monitored the evolution of [SOURCE: Page_208]

demography and genetic diversity in the Chevenne Creek following these management strategies. [SOURCE: Page_208]

Following ATL removal, MED individuals sampled from the nearly pure native downstream population [SOURCE: Page_208]

(<1% introgression) were translocated upstream to establish a native population upstream and reduce [SOURCE: Page_208]

the intermediate gradient of hybridization (Caudron & Champigneulle, 2011). In total 105 MED brown [SOURCE: Page_208]

trout of variable size were transferred in the upper part of the river stretch (Figure 4 – 31). Dynamics of [SOURCE: Page_208]

colonisation and change in genetic diversity were then monitored in 2016 to evaluate the effectiveness [SOURCE: Page_208]

of these strategies in restoring a native MED population in the Chevenne Creek (Scimabio Interface data). [SOURCE: Page_208]

Figure 4—31: Spatial representation of management practices carried out on the Chevenne Creek from 1993 to 2009. [SOURCE: Page_208]

197 [SOURCE: Page_208]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_209]

4.4.1.3   Simulation protocol [SOURCE: Page_209]

Hydrographic network.            In MEDITERRANEA, the Chevene population is considered a close [SOURCE: Page_209]

population, as there is no connectivity represented with the downstream Dranse River. The hydrographic [SOURCE: Page_209]

network is explicitly represented by a succession of 5 reaches and 5 weirs defined based on the topology [SOURCE: Page_209]

of the Chevenne Creek (Figure 4 – 32). Four of the five weirs represent the Chevenne partially passable [SOURCE: Page_209]

waterfall barriers (Figure 4 – 29). Reaches and weirs state variables are informed by the Chevenne physical [SOURCE: Page_209]

characteristics (Appendix D: Table 1 & 2). [SOURCE: Page_209]

Figure 4—32: Representation of the Chevenne Creek in MEDITERRANEA. It is made of a succession of reaches and weirs. Reach and weir numbers are represented. [SOURCE: Page_209]

Parameters settings.             Each hydrographic network can be characterised by its own densities. [SOURCE: Page_209]

Therefore, parameters informing and controlling for maximum density are set based on observational [SOURCE: Page_209]

data (Scimabio Interface data). For the Chevenne Creek observed density is around 3000 individuals per [SOURCE: Page_209]

hectare (KmaxE = 1.8). The rest of the parameters used in the simulations are set by default. [SOURCE: Page_209]

198 [SOURCE: Page_209]

4.4 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in the Chevenne Creek [SOURCE: Page_210]

Temperature models.             As temperature influences GxE selection and potentially diversity [SOURCE: Page_210]

dynamics, two temperature models were used. The first temperature model was calibrated based on [SOURCE: Page_210]

temperature data informed by Scimabio interface. Yearly temperatures are overall more buffered with [SOURCE: Page_210]

warmer winter temperatures and lower summer temperatures (Figure 4 – 33). The second model is based [SOURCE: Page_210]

on data from Daigle et al. (2016), where a few temperatures were available for the Chevenne. Yearly [SOURCE: Page_210]

temperatures are overall more fluctuating with colder winter temperatures and warmer summer [SOURCE: Page_210]

temperatures (Figure 4 – 34). [SOURCE: Page_210]

Figure 4—33: Buffered temperature model (warmer modality) used for simulations carried out on the Chevenne Creek. One water temperature is calculated per reach each month. A zoom of winter temperatures shows the temperature from December to March. [SOURCE: Page_210]

199 [SOURCE: Page_210]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_211]

Figure 4—34: Fluctuating temperature model (colder modality) used for simulations carried out on the Chevenne Creek. One water temperature is calculated per reach each month. A zoom of winter temperatures shows the temperature from December to March. [SOURCE: Page_211]

Simulations.             All simulations – with the same initial parameters – are replicated 15 times. To [SOURCE: Page_211]

stabilise population dynamics and obtain a realistic initial population – population size stabilisation, fitting [SOURCE: Page_211]

distributions of individual state variables, creation of hatches and juveniles – the model is run for ten years [SOURCE: Page_211]

prior to starting the case study. The actual scenario then starts at t = 11, in 1995 when the first genetic [SOURCE: Page_211]

data are available, to 2016 when the last genetic data are available. Genotypic distribution for the initial [SOURCE: Page_211]

population is based on observed genotypic distribution per section, informed by Scimabio Interface. From [SOURCE: Page_211]

t = 11 to t = 21 – hence from 1995 to 2005 – the model simulates the GR strategy. Fishing is also banned [SOURCE: Page_211]

from the river. From t = 22 to t = 25 – hence from 2006 to 2009, ATL removal is simulated by simply [SOURCE: Page_211]

removing individuals from reach 4. The probability of being removed is stage-dependent – juvenile, sub- [SOURCE: Page_211]

200 [SOURCE: Page_211]

4.4 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in the Chevenne Creek [SOURCE: Page_212]

adult, adult – and defined per year (Table 4 – 1) to match the removal efficiency described in Caudron and [SOURCE: Page_212]

Champigneulle (2011). Finally, at t = 25 so in 2009, 105 individuals are transferred from reach 2 to reach [SOURCE: Page_212]

4, considering a variety of sizes and based on individual introgression. To match the sample of individuals [SOURCE: Page_212]

transferred, 70% of individuals were pure MED and 30% were allowed to be slightly introgressed – four [SOURCE: Page_212]

ATL alleles maximum. Diversity dynamics and demography were recorded for three sections of the river [SOURCE: Page_212]

(Figure 4 – 35) – the downstream zone, the intermediate zone and the upstream zone. Most results [SOURCE: Page_212]

represent the mean value of all the simulations as well as a 95% confidence interval. [SOURCE: Page_212]

Figure 4—35: Representation of the management strategies in MEDITERRANEA and delimitation of the three zones monitored – the downstream zone, the intermediate zone and the upstream zone. Weir passability is indicated between zones of interest. [SOURCE: Page_212]

4.4.2     Evolution of diversity dynamics and demography in the Chevenne Creek [SOURCE: Page_213]

Following the implementation of various management strategies – GR, ATL removal and MED [SOURCE: Page_213]

translocation – I monitored the evolution of diversity dynamics and demography in each zone of the [SOURCE: Page_213]

Chevenne Creek. As a reminder, diversity dynamics include the evolution of introgression rate and [SOURCE: Page_213]

genotypic composition as well as the distribution of individual introgression. Demography focuses on [SOURCE: Page_213]

population size and age class structure. As results are numerous, I will not go through all the results for all [SOURCE: Page_213]

zones but only target those of interest. [SOURCE: Page_213]

4.4.2.1   Evolution of introgression [SOURCE: Page_213]

In the downstream and intermediate zones, introgression rates were very low (Figure 4 – 36, < 0.2) and [SOURCE: Page_213]

mainly remained stable over time. For the colder temperature modality, introgression was lower for [SOURCE: Page_213]

scenarios implementing GxE interactions compared to neutral survival scenarios. This indicates that [SOURCE: Page_213]

selection mediated by GxE interactions always acts and that its effect can be seen even in cases where [SOURCE: Page_213]

ATL alleles are rare in the population. In the downstream zone, there was a very slight increase in [SOURCE: Page_213]

introgression for scenarios implementing neutral survival. Meanwhile, in the intermediate zone, there [SOURCE: Page_213]

was a small decrease in introgression for all scenarios during model stabilisation. This pattern could simply [SOURCE: Page_213]

4.4 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in the Chevenne Creek [SOURCE: Page_214]

be due to individual movement as the downstream zone might get some individuals from the intermediate [SOURCE: Page_214]

zone – likely to be introgressed and hence increase the introgression rate – and conversely, the [SOURCE: Page_214]

intermediate zone might get some individuals from the downstream zone – likely to be pure and hence [SOURCE: Page_214]

decrease introgression. In addition, the implementation of management practices did not seem to affect [SOURCE: Page_214]

introgression dynamics in these zones. This could be explained by the fact that, in the model, the [SOURCE: Page_214]

intermediate zone did not seem to be highly influenced by an upstream ATL gene flow, as it might have [SOURCE: Page_214]

been the case in reality. A possible explanation is to look at the value given to weir passability (Figure 4 – [SOURCE: Page_214]

35), which is very high (99/100) between the upstream and intermediate zone, and lower (60/100) [SOURCE: Page_214]

between the downstream and intermediate zone, allowing more gene flow to happen from downstream [SOURCE: Page_214]

than upstream. To obtain a more realistic gene flow – that would match that observed in reality – [SOURCE: Page_214]

sensitivity analyses on the values given to weir passability should be conducted. [SOURCE: Page_214]

In the upstream zone, following the implementation of the GR strategy (from year 11 to year 21), the [SOURCE: Page_214]

introgression rate remained very high for neutral survival scenarios while it decreased progressively for [SOURCE: Page_214]

scenarios considering GxE interactions (Figure 4 – 36). In these scenarios, despite low passability, MED [SOURCE: Page_214]

individuals – or lowly introgressed HYB – that managed to move upstream might have been selected. [SOURCE: Page_214]

During the ATL removal (from year 21 to year 25), introgression rates decreased rapidly over the years for [SOURCE: Page_214]

all scenarios – close to zero, as most individuals were removed from the river stretch – ATL as well as HYB [SOURCE: Page_214]

individuals. The introgression rate then rapidly stabilised at very low levels after MED translocation. With [SOURCE: Page_214]

the implementation of GxE interactions, the introgression rate decreased more rapidly and to slightly [SOURCE: Page_214]

lower rates. Overall, for all zones, at warmer temperatures, the implementation of EEMs did not appear [SOURCE: Page_214]

to impact much introgression dynamics, whereas it had a stronger influence at colder temperatures, as [SOURCE: Page_214]

observed differences in introgression rates were higher. [SOURCE: Page_214]

Sensitivity analyses on GxE interactions.                The strength of GxE interactions also influenced [SOURCE: Page_216]

the evolution of introgression in the Chevenne Creek (Figure 4 – 37). In the upstream zone, the higher the [SOURCE: Page_216]

strength of GxE interactions was, the higher and stronger the decrease in introgression rate was following [SOURCE: Page_216]

the implementation of the GR and ATL removal. The introgression rate reached approximately 0.9 for the [SOURCE: Page_216]

low, 0.75 for the medium and 0.62 for the high strength of GxE interactions just before ATL removal [SOURCE: Page_216]

205 [SOURCE: Page_216]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_217]

(Figure 4 – 37, t = 21). After full ATL removal and MED translocation, all scenarios ultimately led to the [SOURCE: Page_217]

same rate of introgression (close to 0), which was to be expected considering ATL removal. In the [SOURCE: Page_217]

downstream and intermediate zone, the introgression rate also decreased with higher GxE interactions, [SOURCE: Page_217]

but observed differences between scenarios were lower, as the introgression rate was initially very low [SOURCE: Page_217]

already. These results suggest that there is always room for selection to act, even slightly, when some ATL [SOURCE: Page_217]

alleles are still segregated in the population. [SOURCE: Page_217]

Management practices were implemented to protect the intermediate zone from an alien gene flow and [SOURCE: Page_217]

to replace the upstream ATL population with a native MED population. However, in the simulations, [SOURCE: Page_217]

results indicate that the intermediate part was not affected by an upstream gene flow, as it is the case, in [SOURCE: Page_217]

reality, no matter the eco-evolutionary scenario considered. On the contrary, the gene flow from [SOURCE: Page_217]

downstream appeared to influence the evolution of introgression in that zone. Consequently, the [SOURCE: Page_217]

management practices implemented did not affect the intermediate nor the downstream zone. [SOURCE: Page_217]

Therefore, the following results will only focus on diversity dynamics in the upstream zone where [SOURCE: Page_217]

management practices combined with various eco-evolutionary scenarios led to different outcomes. [SOURCE: Page_217]

4.4.2.2   Genotypic composition of the population in the upstream zone [SOURCE: Page_217]

In the upstream zone, the population was initially composed of ATL individuals with a lower population [SOURCE: Page_217]

size observed when considering GxE interactions (Figure 4 – 38). The implementation of the GR strategy [SOURCE: Page_217]

was associated with an increase in the number of HYB individuals while the number of ATL individuals [SOURCE: Page_217]

decreased. This pattern was very slight for neutral selection scenarios and very pronounced when GxE [SOURCE: Page_217]

interactions were implemented – steeper and faster. ATL removal led to a high decrease in population [SOURCE: Page_217]

size, as most individuals were removed from the ATL zone. [SOURCE: Page_217]

206 [SOURCE: Page_217]

4.4 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in the Chevenne Creek [SOURCE: Page_218]

Figure 4—38: Evolution of the genotypic composition of the population over 31 years in the upstream zone. Trajectories are represented for each eco-evolutionary scenario and temperature modality. The time step during which some management practices were implemented is indicated. [SOURCE: Page_218]

207 [SOURCE: Page_218]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_219]

Finally, following MED translocation, the numbers of MED and HYB individuals increased (Figure 4 – 38). [SOURCE: Page_219]

This increase was different based on the survival scenario implemented. When GxE interactions were [SOURCE: Page_219]

considered, more MED individuals and fewer HYB individuals were found in the population while it was [SOURCE: Page_219]

the other way around when considering neutral survival. Temperature modality affected the differences [SOURCE: Page_219]

observed between neutral and GxE survival scenarios, with more pronounced differences observed at [SOURCE: Page_219]

colder temperatures. [SOURCE: Page_219]

Figure 4—39: Evolution of the genotypic composition of the population over 31 years in the upstream zone. Trajectories are represented for various strengths of GxE interactions and the cold temperature modality. The time step during which some management practices were implemented is indicated. [SOURCE: Page_219]

208 [SOURCE: Page_219]

4.4 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in the Chevenne Creek [SOURCE: Page_220]

Sensitivity analyses on GxE interactions.                  The strength of GxE interactions had a significant [SOURCE: Page_220]

impact on the genotypic composition of the population. Population size at equilibrium in the upstream [SOURCE: Page_220]

zone stabilised at lower values when higher GxE interactions were implemented (Figure 4 – 39, at t = 10 [SOURCE: Page_220]

years, ~ 350 individuals for the high vs ~ 500 individuals for the medium vs ~ 650 individuals for the low [SOURCE: Page_220]

modality). The decrease in the proportion of ATL individuals, following the implementation of the GR [SOURCE: Page_220]

strategy then happened at the same speed. Meanwhile, the number of HYB individuals increased faster [SOURCE: Page_220]

for the higher modality of GxE interactions before ATL removal. Then following MED translocation, higher [SOURCE: Page_220]

GxE strength led to a higher increase in the number of MED individuals, while the increase in HYB was [SOURCE: Page_220]

slightly lower. [SOURCE: Page_220]

4.4.2.3   Distribution of individual introgression in the upstream zone [SOURCE: Page_220]

Ten years after the implementation of the GR strategy (t=21), a wide range of highly introgressed HYB [SOURCE: Page_220]

were created when GxE interactions were implemented, whereas a large number of ATL individuals were [SOURCE: Page_220]

still found in the population otherwise (Figure 4 – 40). Even fewer ATL individuals were found considering [SOURCE: Page_220]

GxE interactions and the colder temperature modality. Ten years after MED translocation (t=31), the [SOURCE: Page_220]

distribution of individual introgression switched toward MED individuals as ATL individuals had been [SOURCE: Page_220]

previously removed. More HYB individuals were found when considering neutral survival scenarios, while [SOURCE: Page_220]

GxE interaction scenarios favoured MED individuals. Again, this pattern was more pronounced at colder [SOURCE: Page_220]

temperatures. [SOURCE: Page_220]

209 [SOURCE: Page_220]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_221]

Figure 4—40: Distribution of individual introgression just before the implementation of the GR strategy (t = 11), 10 years after and before ATL removal (t = 21) and at the end of the simulation, after ATL removal and MED translocation (t = 31). Distributions are represented for each eco-evolutionary scenario and temperature modality. [SOURCE: Page_221]

4.4.2.4   Evolution of demography: total population size [SOURCE: Page_221]

In the downstream and the intermediate zone, population size mainly remained stable over time except [SOURCE: Page_221]

at t = 25, where a slight decrease in population size in the downstream zone was observed due to MED [SOURCE: Page_221]

sampling for translocation (Figure 4 – 41). [SOURCE: Page_221]

210 [SOURCE: Page_221]

4.4 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in the Chevenne Creek [SOURCE: Page_222]

Figure 4—41: Evolution of population size over 31 years in each zone of the Chevenne Creek. Trajectories are represented for each eco-evolutionary scenario and temperature modality. The time step during which some management practices were implemented is indicated. [SOURCE: Page_222]

211 [SOURCE: Page_222]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_223]

Management practices and eco-evolutionary scenarios led to variation in demography mainly in the [SOURCE: Page_223]

upstream zone (Figure 4 – 41). Population size was initially lower when GxE interactions were [SOURCE: Page_223]

implemented, and even more so at colder temperatures. With the implementation of the GR strategy, [SOURCE: Page_223]

population size increased progressively, but only at colder temperatures. At that temperature, higher [SOURCE: Page_223]

selective pressure might cause selection to be stronger, favouring the gene flow from the intermediate [SOURCE: Page_223]

part. The resulting progressive increase in MED alleles might then allow the population mean fitness to [SOURCE: Page_223]

increase. Following ATL removal, population size decreased for all scenarios. It then increased back up [SOURCE: Page_223]

with the translocation of MED individuals. This increase was stronger for GxE interaction scenarios, as [SOURCE: Page_223]

population size was higher after MED translocation compared to before ATL removal. In addition, the [SOURCE: Page_223]

population size was slightly higher at colder temperatures for all scenarios. This could potentially result [SOURCE: Page_223]

from a model bias on survival, as survival is initially higher for lower stages of life (eggs vs larvae vs [SOURCE: Page_223]

juveniles), which last longer at colder temperatures (Section 3.3.1 & 3.3.2). [SOURCE: Page_223]

4.4.2.5   Evolution of population size per age class in the upstream zone [SOURCE: Page_223]

Focusing on the evolution of population size per stage in the upstream zone (Figure 4 – 42): before ATL [SOURCE: Page_223]

removal and translocation, differences in population size between scenarios considering neutral versus [SOURCE: Page_223]

GxE interactions survival were strong for all three life stages, even more so for juveniles and at colder [SOURCE: Page_223]

temperatures. For all scenarios, translocation led to an increase in the number of juveniles, sub-adults [SOURCE: Page_223]

and adults and the population size for each stage became similar among scenarios. At colder [SOURCE: Page_223]

temperatures, this increase was initially faster when GxE interactions were implemented. [SOURCE: Page_223]

212 [SOURCE: Page_223]

4.4 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in the Chevenne Creek [SOURCE: Page_224]

Figure 4—42: Evolution of population size per age class over 31 years in the upstream zone. Trajectories are represented for each eco-evolutionary scenario and temperature modality. The time step during which some management practices were implemented is indicated. [SOURCE: Page_224]

213 [SOURCE: Page_224]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_225]

Figure 4—43: Evolution of population size per age class over 31 years in the upstream zone. Trajectories are represented for various strengths of GxE interactions and the cold temperature modality. The time step during which some management practices were implemented is indicated. [SOURCE: Page_225]

Sensitivity analyses on GxE interactions.                The strength of GxE interactions had a significant [SOURCE: Page_225]

impact on demography in the upstream zone before ATL removal (Figure 4 – 43). For all life stages, [SOURCE: Page_225]

population size after stabilisation was initially smaller for the higher modality of GxE interactions. [SOURCE: Page_225]

Following the implementation of the GR strategy, population size increased faster for the highest modality [SOURCE: Page_225]

of GxE interactions, leading to the same population at t = 21, just before ATL removal. Following MED [SOURCE: Page_225]

translocation, population size became the highest for the higher modality of GxE interactions. This is [SOURCE: Page_225]

214 [SOURCE: Page_225]

4.4 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in the Chevenne Creek [SOURCE: Page_226]

because the mean fitness of a MED population is higher when selective pressures are higher (either [SOURCE: Page_226]

through temperature or GxE strength), as MED offspring survival increases while ATL offspring survival [SOURCE: Page_226]

decreases (Section 4.2). [SOURCE: Page_226]

## CASE STUDY 2 & 3:

THE BORNE RIVER [SOURCE: Page_227]

4.4 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in the Borne River [SOURCE: Page_228]

4.4.3   The Borne River [SOURCE: Page_228]

4.4.3.1   Study site [SOURCE: Page_228]

Located in the Rhone River Basin, the Borne River is a mountainous fast-flowing water stream that [SOURCE: Page_228]

expends 32 kilometres long and ranges from 2750 to 400 metres in elevation in a 158 kilometres square [SOURCE: Page_228]

catchment area. This typical Northern French Alpine stream is characterised by a strong slope (mean [SOURCE: Page_228]

value: 3.3%) and lies within the geographical range of Mediterranean brown trout, native to the area [SOURCE: Page_228]

(Bernatchez et al., 1992; Guyomard, 1989; Launey et al., 2003). Multiple artificial and natural impassable [SOURCE: Page_228]

barriers fragment this river, causing isolated populations of brown trout that cannot disperse. Three [SOURCE: Page_228]

artificial ones are located in the mainstream. Barriers one and three (Figure 4 – 44) are old watermill dams [SOURCE: Page_228]

with small impassable waterfalls (2-3 metres high) and barrier two is a small hydroelectric dam (9 metres [SOURCE: Page_228]

high). Two natural impassable waterfalls (4 & 5) are located in the tributaries of the Borne River. [SOURCE: Page_228]

Figure 4—44: Representation and characteristics of the Borne River with the mainstream and its tributaries. Main fragmentations that represent impassable barriers are represented. [SOURCE: Page_228]

217 [SOURCE: Page_228]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_229]

In the Borne River, habitat quality is considered good for both reproduction – i.e. spawning ground – and [SOURCE: Page_229]

carrying capacity of brown trout, even though some upstream areas limits population settlement due to [SOURCE: Page_229]

upstream pollution (skiing tourism and cattle wintering). However, such steep and fast-flowing streams [SOURCE: Page_229]

are characterised by harsh conditions, such as flash floods, which can have a direct impact on habitat [SOURCE: Page_229]

structure and harshly affect brown trout populations. Flash floods happen periodically (decennial, [SOURCE: Page_229]

centennial …) and can be of variable magnitude. In the Borne, discharge can range from two m^3.s-1 (annual [SOURCE: Page_229]

average flow) to more than 200 m^3.s-1, with a decennial flood value of 75 m^3.s-1. In July 1987, the Borne [SOURCE: Page_229]

River experienced a major flooding event (the "Grand Bornand disaster"), expected to occur every 400 [SOURCE: Page_229]

years. A violent storm caused the river to flood in the upper part of the catchment area with discharge [SOURCE: Page_229]

values superior to 200 m^3.s-1. This flood scoured the entire upstream area and wiped out all brown trout [SOURCE: Page_229]

populations located upstream of dam 3 (Figure 4 – 44). Downstream populations were also severely [SOURCE: Page_229]

affected with all redd scoured and only a small proportion of the population remaining. To restore a viable [SOURCE: Page_229]

population along the Borne River, stocking was highly intensified following this flood. [SOURCE: Page_229]

4.4.3.2   Historical management [SOURCE: Page_229]

Stocking of brown trout had already begun in 1913 (Figure 4 – 45) when a hatchery was created nearby [SOURCE: Page_229]

(Vizille, Isère), with 20 000 fry yearly stocked. This number gradually increased and peaked after the flood [SOURCE: Page_229]

in 1989, reaching 1 000 000 introduced fry. To help restore brown trout populations and counter the [SOURCE: Page_229]

damage caused by the catastrophic flood, stocking was at its highest in 1989, 1990 and 1991. Genotypic [SOURCE: Page_229]

studies on microsatellite loci Str^541 and Str^591 in the Vizille hatchery indicated that the introduced strain [SOURCE: Page_229]

belonged to the ATL lineage of brown trout (Launey et al., 2003). Stocking happened every year up until [SOURCE: Page_229]

2004 all along the Borne River with 4-5 cm juveniles usually introduced before summer, when hydrological [SOURCE: Page_229]

stream conditions were stable (Figure 4 – 46). [SOURCE: Page_229]

218 [SOURCE: Page_229]

4.4 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in the Borne River [SOURCE: Page_230]

Figure 4—45: Historical management timeline for the Borne River. Management goals and practices are represented. [SOURCE: Page_230]

Figure 4—46: Spatial representation of stocking practices carried out on the Borne River from 1913 to 2004. The number of fish released throughout the Borne between 1988 and 2004 is indicated (Figure from Caudron et al., 2009). [SOURCE: Page_230]

In 2002, Caudron et al. (2009) started investigating the evolution of demography and genetic diversity – [SOURCE: Page_230]

i.e. introgression rate – as well as the contribution of restocked fish to stage 0+, in both upstream and [SOURCE: Page_230]

219 [SOURCE: Page_230]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_231]

downstream areas of the Borne River. Genetic and demographic monitoring revealed the presence of a [SOURCE: Page_231]

thriving and almost pure native MED population downstream (43-55 fish.100m-2) with a very low [SOURCE: Page_231]

introgression rate (~ 10%), despite the ongoing stocking effort with ATL strains from 1988 to 2004. In the [SOURCE: Page_231]

upstream area, however, introgression rates were very high (ranging from 78 to 100%), where a small [SOURCE: Page_231]

non-native population showed very low densities (1-2 fish.100 m-2). Marked otolith analyses showed that [SOURCE: Page_231]

the majority of fish contributing to stage 0+ upstream were restocked (90-100%), pointing toward a lack [SOURCE: Page_231]

of natural recruitment from stocked ATL brown trout. Altogether, these analyses revealed that after the [SOURCE: Page_231]

flood the native Mediterranean population was restricted to the lower part (Figure 4 – 44, under dam 3) [SOURCE: Page_231]

of the Borne River and recovered naturally downstream without hybridizing with stocked ATL individuals, [SOURCE: Page_231]

while the population upstream most likely originated from and has been maintained by continuous ATL [SOURCE: Page_231]

stocking. [SOURCE: Page_231]

As scientists and managers became more aware of inter and intraspecific diversity, fisheries managers [SOURCE: Page_231]

became interested in the conservation of remaining native brown trout populations to protect well- [SOURCE: Page_231]

adapted local populations. Therefore, in the Mediterranean area management goals changed toward the [SOURCE: Page_231]

conservation of the native MED lineage. In 2005, stocking of ATL brown trout stopped in the Borne River [SOURCE: Page_231]

and a GR strategy was implemented to preserve native alleles. Additionally, in order to restore the native [SOURCE: Page_231]

MED gene pool upstream and establish a viable population, direct translocation of native wild trout had [SOURCE: Page_231]

been implemented for three consecutive years, from 2005 to 2007. In total, 1630 MED brown trout of [SOURCE: Page_231]

various age classes were sampled from the downstream area, where introgression rates were very low, [SOURCE: Page_231]

and directly translocated to two upstream parts of the river (Figure 4 – 47). More trout were translocated [SOURCE: Page_231]

in the lower part of the river stretch – respectively 125, 610 and 370 in sector B compared to 135, 282 and [SOURCE: Page_231]

110 in sector A. Recreational fishing was banned from the translocated zone B (Figure 4 – 47) and its lower [SOURCE: Page_231]

stretch B$_1$ to favour the settlement of the translocated native trout, but not in upper sectors A and A$_1$. [SOURCE: Page_231]

220 [SOURCE: Page_231]

4.4 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in the Borne River [SOURCE: Page_232]

Figure 4—47: Spatial representation of fish translocation practices carried out on the Borne River from 2005 to 2007. [SOURCE: Page_232]

(Caudron, Champigneulle, et al., 2012) monitored the evolution of demography and genetic diversity in [SOURCE: Page_232]

the Borne River following these management practices. Five sectors (Figure 4 – 47), three located [SOURCE: Page_232]

upstream (C, D and E) and two downstream (A and B), were studied to respectively evaluate the efficiency [SOURCE: Page_232]

of the GR strategy and the translocation strategy. Demographic and genetic monitoring were performed [SOURCE: Page_232]

in these sectors in 2004, just before the measures and after, in 2008 and 2009. In 2009, genotypic data [SOURCE: Page_232]

were also studied in two additional sectors (A$_1$ and B$_1$) located downstream of the translocated sectors [SOURCE: Page_232]

(A and B), to investigate the potential migration of introduced trout or their offspring. [SOURCE: Page_232]

221 [SOURCE: Page_232]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_233]

4.4.3.3    Simulation protocol [SOURCE: Page_233]

Simulations.              All simulations – with the same initial parameters – are replicated 15 times. Most [SOURCE: Page_233]

results represent the mean value of all the replications as well as a 95% confidence interval. [SOURCE: Page_233]

Hydrographic network.             In MEDITERRANEA, the Borne hydrographic network is explicitly [SOURCE: Page_233]

represented by a succession of 31 reaches and 25 weirs defined based on the topology of the Borne River. [SOURCE: Page_233]

Five of the 25 weirs represent the Borne impassable barriers (Figure 4 – 48). Reaches and weirs state [SOURCE: Page_233]

variables are informed by the Borne physical characteristics (Appendix D: Table 3 & 4). [SOURCE: Page_233]

Figure 4—48: Representation of the Borne River in MEDITERRANEA. It is made of a succession of reaches and weirs. Unpassable weirs are marked. Reach numbers are represented. [SOURCE: Page_233]

Parameters settings.              Parameters informing and controlling for maximum density are set based [SOURCE: Page_233]

on observational data (Scimabio Interface data). For the Borne River observed density is around 5000 [SOURCE: Page_233]

222 [SOURCE: Page_233]

4.4 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in the Borne River [SOURCE: Page_234]

individuals per hectare (KmaxE = 3.1). The rest of the parameters used in the simulations are set by [SOURCE: Page_234]

default. [SOURCE: Page_234]

Temperature models.             As temperature influences GxE selection and resulting diversity [SOURCE: Page_234]

dynamics, we used two temperature models. One has an overall lower winter temperature (Figure 4 – [SOURCE: Page_234]

49), and hence selective pressure might be potentially higher, while the other has an overall higher winter [SOURCE: Page_234]

temperature (Figure 4 – 50). To implement these models, observed water temperatures in 2003 and 2010 [SOURCE: Page_234]

were used, as yearly water temperature data for the Borne River are scarce. [SOURCE: Page_234]

Figure 4—49: Colder temperature model used for simulations carried out on the Borne River. One water temperature is calculated per reach each month based on altitude. A zoom of winter temperatures shows the temperature from December to March. [SOURCE: Page_234]

223 [SOURCE: Page_234]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_235]

Figure 4—50: Warmer temperature model used for simulations carried out on the Borne River. One water temperature is calculated per reach each month based on altitude. A zoom of winter temperatures shows the temperature from December to March. [SOURCE: Page_235]

Based on its historical management and available data, the Borne River will be used in two separate case [SOURCE: Page_235]

studies. The first one will focus on the period going from 1988 to 2004 when ATL stocking took place to [SOURCE: Page_235]

restore a devastated brown trout population. The second one will represent the management practices [SOURCE: Page_235]

that took place after 2004 to conserve and restore a native MED population all along the Borne River. [SOURCE: Page_235]

224 [SOURCE: Page_235]

## THE BORNE RIVER

From 1988 to 2004: ATL stocking [SOURCE: Page_236]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_237]

4.4.4    Evolution of diversity dynamics and demography in the Borne River during ATL stocking (from 1988 to 2004) [SOURCE: Page_237]

After the devastating flood of the Borne River in 1987, ATL stocking was intensified all along the river to [SOURCE: Page_237]

help restore the brown trout population. The first case study aims at simulating the stocking event that [SOURCE: Page_237]

took place from 1988 to 2004 to predict its impact on diversity dynamics and demography. Therefore, the [SOURCE: Page_237]

simulation will start in September 1987 – time step 0 – right after the flood, and will be run for 17 years. [SOURCE: Page_237]

The initial population will be representative of the post-flood devasted brown trout population, with [SOURCE: Page_237]

barely any individuals located upstream and a very low-density population of MED individuals [SOURCE: Page_237]

downstream. Unlike the other case studies, there will not be any population stabilisation using the model, [SOURCE: Page_237]

as our goal is to start with a devastated population without any nests or juveniles. Management practices [SOURCE: Page_237]

– i.e. intervention – will hence take place directly during year 1 – i.e. 1988. Stocking with ATL fry will [SOURCE: Page_237]

happen every year, in June (time step 9) from 1988 until 2004 included. A given density of fish per metre [SOURCE: Page_237]

square will be added to each reach based on the stocking intensity experienced in that given year (Figure [SOURCE: Page_237]

4 – 51). Individuals are added as part of a group of individuals using the intervention “hatches additions” [SOURCE: Page_237]

(Section 3.4.2). Hatches are added at 6 months old, their mean size is set at 4 cm and their mean [SOURCE: Page_237]

introgression at 1 (Figure 4 – 51, initial hatch characteristics). [SOURCE: Page_237]

In the simulations, stocking practices in the Borne River only led to the admixture of the MED and the ATL [SOURCE: Page_237]

lineages in the downstream section of the Borne River where a small native MED population was present. [SOURCE: Page_237]

Hence, diversity dynamics will only be monitored in this part of the river. [SOURCE: Page_237]

226 [SOURCE: Page_237]

4.4 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in the Borne River 1988 [SOURCE: Page_238]

Figure 4—51: Representation of ATL stocking practices carried out in MEDITERRANEA for the Borne River. The zones that will be studied are highlighted in the downstream (green) and upstream (blue) sections. Stocking intensity used in the simulation is represented as the number of individuals stocked in the entire river. Initial hatches characteristics are informed. [SOURCE: Page_238]

4.4.4.1   Evolution of introgression [SOURCE: Page_238]

A clear and continuous increase in introgression level (Figure 4 – 52) – i.e. the percentage of ATL alleles – [SOURCE: Page_238]

followed the implementation of stocking practices in the three downstream zones (delimited based on [SOURCE: Page_238]

impassable barriers, Figure 4 – 44) and for all eco-evolutionary scenarios. This increase was very steep [SOURCE: Page_238]

during the first three years as stocking was more intensive, and then slowed down. Differences among [SOURCE: Page_238]

eco-evolutionary scenarios could also be observed, with the scenarios considering GxE interactions [SOURCE: Page_238]

leading to a lower and slower increase in introgression rate compared to those that did not. [SOURCE: Page_238]

227 [SOURCE: Page_238]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_239]

Figure 4—52: Evolution of the population introgression rate in each downstream zone of the Borne River from 1988 to 2004, when ATL stocking took place every year. Trajectories are represented for each eco- evolutionary scenario and temperature modality. [SOURCE: Page_239]

228 [SOURCE: Page_239]

4.4 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in the Borne River 1988 [SOURCE: Page_240]

This was generally true; although observed differences could be more or less pronounced based on the [SOURCE: Page_240]

temperature modality and the zone (Figure 4 – 52). In the most downstream zone (reach 1), the [SOURCE: Page_240]

introgression rate went up the highest, to approximately 0.97 versus 0.95 when considering or not GxE [SOURCE: Page_240]

interactions at warmer temperatures, and up to approximately 0.95 versus 0.88 at colder temperatures. [SOURCE: Page_240]

For the two other zones, observed differences among scenarios were more consequent (> 0.1) as the [SOURCE: Page_240]

introgression rate did not go above 0.8 when implementing GxE interactions whereas it was superior to [SOURCE: Page_240]

0.9 otherwise. For these zones, when implementing heterogamy in addition to GxE interactions, one can [SOURCE: Page_240]

notice that it led to a slightly higher rate of introgression, especially at colder temperatures and for the [SOURCE: Page_240]

zone including reaches 2 and 3 (0.69 vs 0.62), where introgression level remained lower compared to the [SOURCE: Page_240]

other zones. However, apart from that, the implementation of heterogamy barely had any impact on [SOURCE: Page_240]

introgression dynamics, as confidence intervals mainly overlapped. [SOURCE: Page_240]

Figure 4—53: Evolution of the population introgression rate in each downstream zone of the Borne River from 1988 to 2004, when ATL stocking took place every year. Trajectories are represented for various strengths of GxE interactions and the colder temperature modality. [SOURCE: Page_240]

229 [SOURCE: Page_240]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_241]

Sensitivity analyses on GxE interactions.                 The strength of GxE interactions also influenced [SOURCE: Page_241]

the evolution of introgression (Figure 4 – 53). Overall the higher the strength of GxE interactions was, the [SOURCE: Page_241]

lower and slower the increase in introgression rate was. For example, in the main downstream zone, the [SOURCE: Page_241]

introgression rate reached approximately 0.9 for low, 0.78 for medium and 0.62 for high strength of GxE [SOURCE: Page_241]

interactions. However, the extent of this effect was mediated by the zone in which the introgression rate [SOURCE: Page_241]

was followed. Introgression dynamics were less influenced by differences in GxE interactions in reach 1 [SOURCE: Page_241]

compared to the other zones. [SOURCE: Page_241]

Overall, temperature modalities, zones and strength of GxE interactions influenced the speed and [SOURCE: Page_241]

intensity of increase in introgression rate, but patterns of introgression dynamics remained similar. [SOURCE: Page_241]

Therefore, the following results will only focus on one of the zone: the downstream area. [SOURCE: Page_241]

4.4.4.2   Genotypic composition of the population [SOURCE: Page_241]

Before the implementation of stocking, the downstream population was mainly composed of a low [SOURCE: Page_241]

number of pure MED individuals, due to the flood (Figure 4 – 54). As population size was initially very low, [SOURCE: Page_241]

the numbers of MED, HYB and ATL individuals increased rapidly within the first three years. However, with [SOURCE: Page_241]

the continuous implementation of stocking, the number of MED individuals then decreased rapidly, until [SOURCE: Page_241]

the extinction of the MED lineage, while more HYB individuals were created. Meanwhile, after a rapid [SOURCE: Page_241]

increase, the ATL lineage also experienced a small decrease in population size as the intensity of stocking [SOURCE: Page_241]

decreased after four years. However, with continuous stocking of ATL individuals, their number then [SOURCE: Page_241]

slowly increased back up, while the number of HYB individuals started decreasing. Only small differences [SOURCE: Page_241]

in the genotypic composition of the population were observed among eco-evolutionary scenarios. The [SOURCE: Page_241]

implementation of GxE interactions led to a lower proportion of ATL individuals and a slower [SOURCE: Page_241]

230 [SOURCE: Page_241]

4.4 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in the Borne River 1988 [SOURCE: Page_242]

disappearance of MED individuals for both temperature modalities. For colder temperatures, it also led [SOURCE: Page_242]

to a slightly lower number of HYB. Barely any differences could be observed when heterogamy was [SOURCE: Page_242]

implemented – both with and without GxE interactions – as confidence intervals mainly overlapped. [SOURCE: Page_242]

Figure 4—54: Evolution of the genotypic composition of the population in each downstream zone of the Borne River from 1988 to 2004, when ATL stocking took place every year. Trajectories are represented for each eco-evolutionary scenario and temperature modality. [SOURCE: Page_242]

231 [SOURCE: Page_242]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_243]

Sensitivity analyses on GxE interactions.    The strength of GxE interactions had a significant impact on [SOURCE: Page_243]

the genotypic composition of the population (Figure 4 – 55). Higher GxE strength led to a slower decrease [SOURCE: Page_243]

in the number of MED individuals and a stronger increase in the number of HYB. For ATL individuals, their [SOURCE: Page_243]

number was initially lower and increased back up more slowly. [SOURCE: Page_243]

Figure 4—55: Evolution of the genotypic composition of the population in each downstream zone of the Borne River from 1988 to 2004, when ATL stocking took place every year. Trajectories are represented for various strengths of GxE interactions and the colder temperature modality. [SOURCE: Page_243]

4.4.4.3   Distribution of individual introgression [SOURCE: Page_243]

The initial distribution of individual introgression indicated that the population was composed of a low [SOURCE: Page_243]

number of pure MED individuals and a few lowly introgressed HYB. No differences were initially observed [SOURCE: Page_243]

among scenarios (Figure 4 – 56). However, after years of stocking, the final distribution of individual [SOURCE: Page_243]

introgression was very different based on the implemented eco-evolutionary scenarios. For neutral and [SOURCE: Page_243]

heterogamous scenarios, the distribution was biased toward pure ATL and highly introgressed individuals, [SOURCE: Page_243]

whereas when GxE interactions were implemented, the distribution was different. While ATL individuals [SOURCE: Page_243]

232 [SOURCE: Page_243]

4.4 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in the Borne River 1988 [SOURCE: Page_244]

remained a majority, they were less numerous (~ 20 000 vs 30 000). In addition, the distribution of [SOURCE: Page_244]

individual introgression for HYB individuals shifted and was centred on a lower value of introgression (0.64 [SOURCE: Page_244]

at colder temperatures and 0.71 at warmer temperatures). [SOURCE: Page_244]

Figure 4—56: Distribution of individual introgression in 1988 when stocking started (initial) and in 2004 it ended (final). Distributions are represented for each eco-evolutionary scenario and temperature modality. [SOURCE: Page_244]

233 [SOURCE: Page_244]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_245]

4.4.4.4   Evolution of demography: total population size [SOURCE: Page_245]

Figure 4—57: Evolution of population size in the main downstream and main upstream parts of the Borne River from 1988 to 2004, when ATL stocking took place every year. Trajectories are represented for each eco-evolutionary scenario and temperature modality. [SOURCE: Page_245]

234 [SOURCE: Page_245]

4.4 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in the Borne River 1988 [SOURCE: Page_246]

The implementation of stocking practices over the years led to a rapid increase in population size, [SOURCE: Page_246]

upstream and downstream of the Borne River (Figure 4 – 57). However, population size at equilibrium [SOURCE: Page_246]

was influenced by the implementation of GxE interactions and to a lesser extent by temperature [SOURCE: Page_246]

modalities. Indeed, in the main upstream zone, scenarios considering GxE interactions reached a [SOURCE: Page_246]

significantly lower population size at equilibrium (~ 40 000 vs 55 000 individuals), with colder [SOURCE: Page_246]

temperatures leading to a slightly higher difference in population size among scenarios. Comparatively, in [SOURCE: Page_246]

the main downstream zone, the implementation of GxE interactions seemed to influence population size [SOURCE: Page_246]

more slowly and progressively. There, population size did not reach an equilibrium. Indeed, while [SOURCE: Page_246]

differences among scenarios were initially low, there was a slight and continuous decrease in population [SOURCE: Page_246]

size over the years for scenarios implementing GxE interactions. This decrease was more pronounced at [SOURCE: Page_246]

colder temperatures. [SOURCE: Page_246]

235 [SOURCE: Page_246]

## THE BORNE RIVER

Starting from 2004: MED translocation [SOURCE: Page_247]

4.4 Exploring various eco-evolutionary scenarios in the Borne River 2004 [SOURCE: Page_248]

4.4.5   Evolution of diversity dynamics and demography in the Borne River following MED translocation (from 2004) [SOURCE: Page_248]

In 2004, after the last stocking event, studies indicated that an almost pure and thriving MED population [SOURCE: Page_248]

had settled downstream of the Borne River, below dam 3, whereas a small non-native population showing [SOURCE: Page_248]

very low densities was located upstream. This second case study aims at simulating the management [SOURCE: Page_248]

practices that took place after 2004, and which aimed at restoring a viable MED population upstream, as [SOURCE: Page_248]

well as conserving the downstream MED population. This case study will therefore start in 2004, just [SOURCE: Page_248]

before the first management practice took place. The initial population is made of pure MED and very low [SOURCE: Page_248]

introgressed HYB downstream and only pure ATL individuals upstream. To stabilise population dynamics [SOURCE: Page_248]

and obtain a realistic initial population – stable population size, age and size distribution, creation of [SOURCE: Page_248]

hatches and juveniles – the model is run for ten years prior to the start of the case study. Hence time 10 [SOURCE: Page_248]

will represent the year 2004 and intervention for management practices will take place just after. From t [SOURCE: Page_248]

= 11 to t = 13, we simulated three consecutive years of translocation. Translocation zones (sampling and [SOURCE: Page_248]

translocated areas) in the model are based on reaches delimitation and match the location of the actual [SOURCE: Page_248]

area. Figure 4 – 58 indicates the corresponding translocation reaches as well as the other monitored areas. [SOURCE: Page_248]

Each year, and for each section, the number of translocated trout was similar to that done in reality. [SOURCE: Page_248]

Therefore, 1630 individuals were also transferred in the model. To match the sample of individuals [SOURCE: Page_248]

transferred, 80% of individuals were pure MED and 20% were allowed to be slightly introgressed – 4 alleles [SOURCE: Page_248]

maximum. To match all the management practices that took place during this period, fishing was also [SOURCE: Page_248]

banned from the translocated zone B and B$_1$ (Figure 4 – 58). [SOURCE: Page_248]

In the simulations, MED translocation only led to the admixture of the MED and the ATL lineages in the [SOURCE: Page_248]

upstream section of the Borne River. Hence, diversity dynamics will only be monitored in this part of the [SOURCE: Page_248]

river. [SOURCE: Page_248]

237 [SOURCE: Page_248]

4.4.5.1   Evolution of introgression [SOURCE: Page_249]

Following translocation, the introgression rate decreased (Figure 4 – 59) in the translocated zones (A and [SOURCE: Page_249]

B) but also in the adjacent zones (A$_1$ and B$_1$), suggesting that movement allowed for the homogenisation [SOURCE: Page_249]

of the population upstream (Upstream zone: entire upstream population). The extent of this decrease [SOURCE: Page_249]

was highly influenced by the implementation of GxE interactions but not by heterogamous mating. For [SOURCE: Page_249]

scenarios without GxE interactions, the introgression rate stabilised rapidly – around 0.95 – after a small [SOURCE: Page_249]

decrease during the translocation period. Temperature modality and zone did not have any influence over [SOURCE: Page_249]

this dynamic. For scenarios considering GxE interactions, a steep decrease in introgression was associated [SOURCE: Page_249]

with the translocation, followed by a slower and continuous decrease. This decrease appeared faster in [SOURCE: Page_249]

zones A and A$_1$, which reached slightly lower rates of introgression than zones B and B$_1$. Colder [SOURCE: Page_249]

temperatures also led to a stronger decrease in all zones. [SOURCE: Page_249]

Sensitivity analyses on GxE interactions.       The strength of GxE interactions also influenced the [SOURCE: Page_251]

higher and stronger the decrease in introgression rate was. In the upstream zone, introgression rates [SOURCE: Page_251]

reached approximately 0.66 for low, 0.42 for medium and 0.28 for high strength of GxE [SOURCE: Page_251]

interactions. However, the influence of the strength of GxE interactions was slightly different based on [SOURCE: Page_252]

the zone. Going from downstream to upstream (i.e. from zone B$_1$ to A), observed differences in final [SOURCE: Page_252]

Overall, the evolution of introgression was quite homogenous in the upstream section. Therefore, the [SOURCE: Page_252]

following descriptions will only focus on the entire upstream population. [SOURCE: Page_252]

4.4.5.2   Genotypic composition of the population [SOURCE: Page_252]

Following MED translocation, the number of HYB individuals increased while that of ATL individuals [SOURCE: Page_252]

decreased (Figure 4 – 61). The number of MED individuals only increased during the translocation, but [SOURCE: Page_252]

rapidly decreased back to zero, for all scenarios. Again, GxE interactions had a strong influence over the [SOURCE: Page_252]

genotypic composition of the population while heterogamous preference did not. When implementing [SOURCE: Page_252]

GxE interactions, the increase in HYB number was higher and faster for both temperature modalities. [SOURCE: Page_252]

While the speed of the decrease in the number of ATL individuals appeared similar, the initial value at [SOURCE: Page_252]

equilibrium was lower, and even more so at colder temperatures. In these scenarios, this decrease [SOURCE: Page_252]

ultimately led to the disappearance of ATL genotypes. [SOURCE: Page_252]

Focusing on the evolution of population size per stage, before translocation, differences in population size [SOURCE: Page_257]

## 4.5 DISCUSSION ON THE ECO-EVOLUTIONARY MECHANISMS

Our simulations based on real case studies demonstrated that management practices could have strong [SOURCE: Page_261]

eco-evolutionary consequences by influencing the evolution of diversity and demography. Specifically, I [SOURCE: Page_261]

showed that the outcome of each management practice was, to some extent, influenced by the various [SOURCE: Page_261]

eco-evolutionary scenarios (Section 4.1.1) implemented. Similarly to the results obtained in the basic case [SOURCE: Page_261]

study, heterogamous preference had very little impact on diversity dynamics. On the opposite, GxE [SOURCE: Page_261]

interactions drove the evolution of diversity, thereby playing a crucial role in determining the outcomes [SOURCE: Page_261]

of management practices. Overall, simulations on real case studies indicate that the EEMs tested - [SOURCE: Page_261]

heterogamous preference and GxE interactions on survival - have the same impact on diversity dynamics [SOURCE: Page_261]

and demography no matter the environmental and ecological context. [SOURCE: Page_261]

4.5.1   Heterogamous preference [SOURCE: Page_261]

Contradictory to our prediction (Section 4.2.2: Discussion), heterogamous preference does not appear to [SOURCE: Page_261]

influence much more diversity dynamics in a more specific context where population structure differs [SOURCE: Page_261]

(Klug & Stone, 2021) – different initial genotypic frequencies. Indeed, biased genotypic frequencies – in [SOURCE: Page_261]

favour of ATL individuals for example in the “Borne^2004” scenarios – did not cause the rare phenotype to [SOURCE: Page_261]

be more successful – frequency-dependent sexual selection hypothesis (O’Donald & Majerus, 1988) – as [SOURCE: Page_261]

trajectories considering random or heterogamous preference did not differ. No matter the mating [SOURCE: Page_261]

preference, the genotype found in higher frequency will reproduce the most. Indeed, given how males [SOURCE: Page_261]

are randomly sampled from the population to undergo mating events during the reproductive season [SOURCE: Page_261]

(Section 3.3.6), the mating system is bound to be related to the frequency of each genotype in a linear [SOURCE: Page_261]

way – i.e. the more of one specific genotype there is in a population, the more it will reproduce. [SOURCE: Page_261]

250 [SOURCE: Page_261]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_262]

This suggests that reproductive interactions might not always drive evolutionary dynamics (Maan & [SOURCE: Page_262]

Seehausen, 2011). However, it is not entirely surprising that heterogamous preference does lead to the [SOURCE: Page_262]

same outcome as random preference, as both have the potential to lead to hybridization. A different [SOURCE: Page_262]

mating system, for example based on homogamy, could have caused mating preference to play a much [SOURCE: Page_262]

more important role in the evolution of diversity by preventing hybridization and enhancing reproductive [SOURCE: Page_262]

isolation between lineages. For example, in other species, models have shown that assortative mating [SOURCE: Page_262]

played a major role in shaping the genetic differentiation between populations experiencing gene flow [SOURCE: Page_262]

(Soularue & Kremer, 2014) and, compared to random mating, could change the genetic variance available [SOURCE: Page_262]

for selection (Godineau et al., 2021). [SOURCE: Page_262]

Therefore, in order to evaluate whether it is any mating preference that does not play a structuring role [SOURCE: Page_262]

in the evolution of diversity, I carried out simple simulations to check the role of homogamous preference [SOURCE: Page_262]

compared to random and heterogamous preference. Results regarding homogamous preference – while [SOURCE: Page_262]

considering (Appendix E) or not (results not presented here) selection mediated by GxE interactions – [SOURCE: Page_262]

were in accordance with another study conducted on salmonids (Nathan et al., 2019). Using a DG-ABM [SOURCE: Page_262]

with a hybrid index similar to our measure of individual introgression, they showed that homogamous [SOURCE: Page_262]

compared to random preference did not influence the outcome of hybridization between bull trout and [SOURCE: Page_262]

Dolly Varden. Despite implementing mating preference in different ways, both our studies indicated that [SOURCE: Page_262]

no matter the mating preference considered, all scenarios ultimately result in a system dominated by [SOURCE: Page_262]

hybrid individuals at similar rates. Put together, these results suggest that mating preference based on [SOURCE: Page_262]

origin does not seem to play an important role in the evolution of diversity dynamics in Salmonids, [SOURCE: Page_262]

potentially because it does not appear to lead to specific patterns of mating system. In our model, this [SOURCE: Page_262]

could be due to the many stochastic processes also influencing mating. Results comparing the relative [SOURCE: Page_262]

effect of heterogamous vs homogamous preference while considering GxE interactions (Appendix E) [SOURCE: Page_262]

251 [SOURCE: Page_262]

4.5 Discussion on the eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_263]

confirmed the antagonistic effect of heterogamous preference over selection and, conversely, pointed to [SOURCE: Page_263]

a synergetic effect of homogamous preference, as it appeared to favour selection. [SOURCE: Page_263]

4.5.2   GxE interactions [SOURCE: Page_263]

As expected, the selection mediated by GxE interactions strongly influences diversity dynamics and [SOURCE: Page_263]

demography in interaction with management practices. As observed in the basic case study, selection [SOURCE: Page_263]

helped decrease the ATL alleles rate while increasing that of MED alleles. Indeed, compared to a neutral [SOURCE: Page_263]

scenario, the implementation of GxE interactions always led to lower rates of introgression following [SOURCE: Page_263]

management practices. In the case of the “Borne 1988”, selection slowed down the increase in [SOURCE: Page_263]

introgression following ATL stocking, by partially preventing a maladaptive gene flow (Garant et al., 2007). [SOURCE: Page_263]

However, as only maternally inherited alleles are under selection, this gene flow is unavoidable, no matter [SOURCE: Page_263]

the strength of the GxE interactions implemented. In the cases of the “Chevenne” and the “Borne 2004”, [SOURCE: Page_263]

selection favoured a rapid decrease in introgression rate by favouring adaptive gene flow (Garant et al., [SOURCE: Page_263]

2007), following the introduction of adaptive genetic variation in the population (through natural [SOURCE: Page_263]

movement and translocation of MED individuals). In these cases, when considering GxE interactions, the [SOURCE: Page_263]

implementation of management practices led to patterns of genetic and evolutionary rescue (Carlson et [SOURCE: Page_263]

al., 2014; Whiteley et al., 2015). Indeed, prior to management, GxE interactions had strong consequences [SOURCE: Page_263]

on demography: population size in the upstream section of the Chevenne Creek and the Borne River were [SOURCE: Page_263]

initially low. Following the implementation of management practices, a progressive increase in population [SOURCE: Page_263]

size was observed, even more so for juveniles, to whom selection directly applied. As management [SOURCE: Page_263]

practices combined with selection led to evolutionary changes, – changes in alleles frequency – the mean [SOURCE: Page_263]

fitness of the population increased, leading to changes in population dynamics – an increase in population [SOURCE: Page_263]

size. Each time, higher strength of GxE interactions led to a higher increase in population size, as the mean [SOURCE: Page_263]

fitness associated with MED alleles increased. In addition, as observed in the simple case study, the [SOURCE: Page_263]

252 [SOURCE: Page_263]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_264]

implementation of colder temperatures, but also higher strength of GxE interactions also led to a stronger [SOURCE: Page_264]

and faster selection against ATL alleles. [SOURCE: Page_264]

These results are in accordance with another study focusing on hybridization between the bull trout and [SOURCE: Page_264]

the Dolly Varden indicating that variations in fitness and temperature influence the outcome of diversity [SOURCE: Page_264]

dynamics. Nathan et al (2019) explored how variation in temperature based-selection and hybrid fitness [SOURCE: Page_264]

relative to either parental species influenced the rate and spatial extent of hybridization. Similar to us, [SOURCE: Page_264]

temperature-based selection – favouring one or the other parental species – would affect the speed of [SOURCE: Page_264]

hybridization depending on the fitness modality implemented. However, unlike us, different fitness [SOURCE: Page_264]

scenarios led to more contrasting results regarding the population genotypic composition. Indeed, hybrid [SOURCE: Page_264]

fitness influenced the proportion of hybrids found at the end of the simulation, whereas our scenarios [SOURCE: Page_264]

always resulted in the extensive creation of hybrid individuals. Compared to Nathan et al (2019), in our [SOURCE: Page_264]

model, hybrid fitness is not directly implemented: only some alleles inherited maternally confers an [SOURCE: Page_264]

advantage. [SOURCE: Page_264]

4.5.3    Importance of hybrid fitness [SOURCE: Page_264]

General results on salmonids indicate the importance of hybrid fitness for determining the fate and [SOURCE: Page_264]

persistence of parental species (Drinan et al., 2015; Hitt et al., 2003; Muhlfeld et al., 2009; Ostberg et al., [SOURCE: Page_264]

2004; E. M. Rubidge & Taylor, 2004; Seehausen, 2004). In our case, empirical studies on the relative fitness [SOURCE: Page_264]

of hybrids are lacking, as we implement only a survival difference based on a linear relationship with [SOURCE: Page_264]

maternal genotype. Indeed, empirical results derived from Folio et al., 2021 indicate a survival difference [SOURCE: Page_264]

between offspring from the MED and the ATL lineages based on a linear relationship with the maternal [SOURCE: Page_264]

genotypic score. However, most crosses involving hybrid parents were missing for various reasons, [SOURCE: Page_264]

253 [SOURCE: Page_264]

4.5 Discussion on the eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_265]

meaning that the implemented linear relationship mainly relies on inference from pure or nearly pure [SOURCE: Page_265]

parental genotypes – as identified by the microsatellites and SNP markers set used for lineage [SOURCE: Page_265]

identification. More studies of hybrid fitness relative to native MED individuals’ fitness across various [SOURCE: Page_265]

environmental gradients – specifically temperature – would improve our understanding and predictions [SOURCE: Page_265]

of the evolution of intraspecific diversity in the MED/ATL complex. [SOURCE: Page_265]

4.5.4   Case studies nuances [SOURCE: Page_265]

Furthermore, the real case studies allowed us to highlight some notable points of interest. The extent of [SOURCE: Page_265]

the impact of GxE interactions over diversity dynamics varies among but also within case studies. It [SOURCE: Page_265]

appears to be modulated by two main factors: management practices and spatial variation (zones). [SOURCE: Page_265]

On the one hand, by interfering with population composition, management practices can influence the [SOURCE: Page_265]

ratio of MED vs ATL alleles found in the population. This could highly influence the speed of selection since [SOURCE: Page_265]

more ATL alleles will take longer to eliminate. For example, in the “Borne 1988”, the stocking of a very [SOURCE: Page_265]

large amount of ATL fry resulted in a large proportion of ATL alleles to select against, hence slow selection [SOURCE: Page_265]

patterns. [SOURCE: Page_265]

On the other hand, results indicate that the strength of selection mediated by GxE interactions can be [SOURCE: Page_265]

variable depending on the zone monitored within the hydrographic network. For example, for the “Borne [SOURCE: Page_265]

1988”, GxE interactions barely slowed down the increase in introgression in the most downstream reach [SOURCE: Page_265]

(reach 1, altitude ~ 460 metres), compared to the other zones (reach 2 & 3: altitude ~ 520 and 580 metres; [SOURCE: Page_265]

“main downstream” zone: altitude ranging from 634 to 1170 metres), where stronger patterns of [SOURCE: Page_265]

selection could be observed. Similarly, in “Borne 2004”, the decrease in introgression rate was slightly [SOURCE: Page_265]

254 [SOURCE: Page_265]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_266]

faster in zone A and A$_1$, located upstream (altitude ~ 850 metres and 900 metres) compared to zone B [SOURCE: Page_266]

and B$_1$, (altitude ~ 960 metres and 1040 metres). In the “Chevenne”, the effect of GxE interactions also [SOURCE: Page_266]

appeared larger in the upstream zone, but only before ATL removal. Overall, the general pattern observed [SOURCE: Page_266]

is the same for all case studies: the selection mediated by GxE interactions appears to be stronger and [SOURCE: Page_266]

faster at higher altitudes. The spatial variations of some important ecological factors, potentially driven [SOURCE: Page_266]

by landscape features – among which altitude – may interplay with GxE interactions to drive different [SOURCE: Page_266]

strengths of selection. [SOURCE: Page_266]

 First, in the model, altitude is the main parameter driving temperature variation in [SOURCE: Page_266]

space. Therefore, zones located upstream experience colder temperatures and hence higher [SOURCE: Page_266]

selective pressure on ATL alleles and vice-versa. Once more, our results highlight the importance [SOURCE: Page_266]

of temperature variations in determining the strength of the selection mediated by GxE [SOURCE: Page_266]

interactions. [SOURCE: Page_266]

 Second, population size and composition could also interplay with selection. Indeed, the ratio of [SOURCE: Page_266]

MED and ATL alleles initially found in the population might highly influence the speed of selection. [SOURCE: Page_266]

For example, in the “Borne 2004”, in addition to temperature differences, population size was [SOURCE: Page_266]

smaller in zones A and A$_1$ compared to B and B$_1$, since population size usually decreases from [SOURCE: Page_266]

downstream to upstream as reaches become smaller due to lower average width. This means that [SOURCE: Page_266]

fewer ATL individuals were initially present in zone A and A$_1$ when translocation occurred causing [SOURCE: Page_266]

variations in the initial ratio of MED vs ATL individuals. [SOURCE: Page_266]

 Finally, connectivity might also influence how these differences play out among zones. Indeed, [SOURCE: Page_266]

the spatial arrangement of habitats can shape individuals’ and alleles’ movement (He et al., 2019). [SOURCE: Page_266]

Comparing the “Borne 1988” to the “Borne 2004”, higher differences in the strength of selection [SOURCE: Page_266]

can be observed for the “Borne 1988” compared to the “Borne 2004”. In the “Borne 2004”, as [SOURCE: Page_266]

255 [SOURCE: Page_266]

4.6 Comparing simulated to empirical data [SOURCE: Page_267]

connectivity is full among zones, there seems to be a strong homogenising effect thanks to gene [SOURCE: Page_267]

flow, despite differences in temperature and population composition. For instance, zone A$_1$ and [SOURCE: Page_267]

B$_1$, which had not been translocated, presented almost a similar decrease in introgression rate [SOURCE: Page_267]

compared to the translocated zones. On the contrary, in the “Borne 1988”, zones are separated [SOURCE: Page_267]

by an unpassable barrier preventing any gene flow. The lack of connectivity, therefore, appears [SOURCE: Page_267]

to accentuate differences in selection caused by other factors. [SOURCE: Page_267]

Nevertheless, the general pattern of selection resulting from the implementation of GxE always remains [SOURCE: Page_267]

the same. This is true independently of the hydrographic network size and structure (one reach, the [SOURCE: Page_267]

Chevenne Creek or the Borne River) or the management practices implemented. Selection will always [SOURCE: Page_267]

interact with management practices to drive the evolution of diversity in a specific direction. This [SOURCE: Page_267]

highlights the robustness of this EEM in structuring diversity dynamics. Contextual factors – management [SOURCE: Page_267]

practices, ecological factors and landscape features – only appear to modulate more or less its effect. For [SOURCE: Page_267]

instance, the force of the resulting selection can be highly driven by temperature variations, with colder [SOURCE: Page_267]

temperatures always leading to stronger patterns of selection. Altogether, our results emphasise the [SOURCE: Page_267]

importance of considering temperature variations for management practices. Indeed, such mechanisms, [SOURCE: Page_267]

as well as variations in selective pressures – here temperature – should never be discarded when [SOURCE: Page_267]

evaluating the potential efficiency of management practices. [SOURCE: Page_267]
