## 4.6 COMPARING SIMULATED TO EMPIRICAL DATA

These more complex case studies are based on real and documented situations, where management [SOURCE: Page_267]

practices had been carried out to restore brown trout populations and/or conserve the native MED [SOURCE: Page_267]

lineage. On the one hand, some punctual genetic and demographic data are available, before and after [SOURCE: Page_267]

256 [SOURCE: Page_267]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_268]

the implementation of management practices. On the other hand, simulations have been replicated, [SOURCE: Page_268]

which provides a confidence interval for results; and empirical data can be either in or out of this [SOURCE: Page_268]

confidence interval. Therefore, I will compare available data to simulation data to determine whether the [SOURCE: Page_268]

inclusion of evolutionary-based mechanisms improves or degrades the ability of the model to predict the [SOURCE: Page_268]

empirical findings. This comparison will be based on the distance between simulation predictions and [SOURCE: Page_268]

empirical data. I will also discuss the main differences observed in each case study and the potential [SOURCE: Page_268]

reasons why. [SOURCE: Page_268]

4.6.1   The Chevenne Creek [SOURCE: Page_268]

Figure 4—68: Boxplot representing the introgression rate measured for each eco-evolutionary scenario at three given time steps (1995: before the GR strategy, 2006: after the GR strategy, 2009: after ATL removal and MED translocation) in the downstream zone. The dotted line (and value) represents the real introgression rate observed in the downstream part of the Chevenne Creek. [SOURCE: Page_268]

257 [SOURCE: Page_268]

4.6 Comparing simulated to empirical data [SOURCE: Page_269]

Introgression in the downstream zone.              In the downstream zone, the scenarios implementing [SOURCE: Page_269]

GxE interactions were closer to the actual introgression rate measured (Figure 4 – 68). Indeed, as observed [SOURCE: Page_269]

in reality (Caudron & Champigneulle, 2011; Scimabio Interface data), these scenarios led to a rate of ATL [SOURCE: Page_269]

alleles inferior to 5% while the other two scenarios resulted in superior values. [SOURCE: Page_269]

Introgression in the intermediate zone.            In the intermediate zone, introgression rates observed in [SOURCE: Page_269]

the model were far from those measured in reality (Caudron & Champigneulle, 2011; Scimabio Interface [SOURCE: Page_269]

data), no matter the scenario considered (Figure 4 – 69). In the model, introgression rates were very low [SOURCE: Page_269]

and constant over time for all scenarios, considering or not GxE interactions. A lower introgression could [SOURCE: Page_269]

be observed owing to the selection mediated by GxE interactions. However, in reality, introgression was [SOURCE: Page_269]

initially higher and increased between 1995 and 2006 during the GR strategy. It then remained almost [SOURCE: Page_269]

stable during ATL removal (done in the upstream zone). These differences are mainly due to the way [SOURCE: Page_269]

individual movement is implemented in the model. First, movement depends on weirs' passability (Section [SOURCE: Page_269]

3.3.4). As a reminder, the higher the value is (scaled on 100) the less likely a fish is to pass. The value for [SOURCE: Page_269]

weir passability in the model between the intermediate and the downstream zone (60/100) is way lower [SOURCE: Page_269]

than between the intermediate and the upstream zone (95/100). Second, in the model parameterisation [SOURCE: Page_269]

used, the probability to choose between upstream and downstream movement is balanced (p = 0.5 for [SOURCE: Page_269]

both, section 3.3.4) during the growth season but biased toward upstream movement during the [SOURCE: Page_269]

reproductive season. Altogether, this suggests that, in the model, there might be a higher gene flow [SOURCE: Page_269]

coming from the downstream zone (MED alleles mainly) compared to the upstream zone (ATL alleles), as [SOURCE: Page_269]

movement from upstream might be more limited. However, in reality, studies have indicated that [SOURCE: Page_269]

admixture has been occurring in the intermediate zone of the Chevenne Creek (Barnetta, 2005) due to [SOURCE: Page_269]

the downstream movement of ATL trout from the upstream zone (Caudron & Champigneulle, 2011). [SOURCE: Page_269]

258 [SOURCE: Page_269]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_270]

Figure 4—69: Boxplot representing the introgression rate measured for each eco-evolutionary scenario at three given time steps (1995: before the GR strategy, 2006: after the GR strategy, 2009: after ATL removal and MED translocation) in the intermediate zone. Dotted lines (and values) represent real introgression rates measured in the intermediate part of the Chevenne Creek in 1995, 2006 and 2009. [SOURCE: Page_270]

These differences indicate that there is some room for improvement in the model. On the one hand, the [SOURCE: Page_270]

transcription of network information might have to be reconsidered. Indeed, the weir passability provided [SOURCE: Page_270]

(by scimabio Interface) might not be adequate given the model parameterisation for movement. To fit [SOURCE: Page_270]

these values properly, one could use the model to test different weir passabilities and evaluate which [SOURCE: Page_270]

value leads to the most adequate patterns of diversity. Replicated on multiple river basins, this could [SOURCE: Page_270]

progressively help improve the definition and scale of weir passability. On the other hand, [SOURCE: Page_270]

parameterisation for movement might have to be tailored case by case when adequate information is [SOURCE: Page_270]

259 [SOURCE: Page_270]

4.6 Comparing simulated to empirical data [SOURCE: Page_271]

available. For instance, here the probability for downstream movement during the growth season should [SOURCE: Page_271]

be set higher than that of upstream movement in order to fit the observed patterns. In addition, [SOURCE: Page_271]

considering downstream-directed water flow, some mechanisms regarding specific downstream [SOURCE: Page_271]

movement might be missing in the model, such as juvenile downstream migration (Arawomo, 1981; Tiffan [SOURCE: Page_271]

et al., 2012). Indeed, the movement of aquatic organisms can be driven by water flow, but also changes [SOURCE: Page_271]

in thermal regimes (Arawomo, 1981; Blanchet et al., 2020; García-Vega et al., 2017), as well as being [SOURCE: Page_271]

impacted by river fragmentations (here weirs). [SOURCE: Page_271]

Figure 4—70: Boxplot representing the introgression rate measured for each eco-evolutionary scenario at three given time steps (1995: before the GR strategy, 2006: after the GR strategy, 2010: after ATL removal and MED translocation) in the upstream zone. Dotted lines (and values) represent real introgression rates measured in the upstream part of the Chevenne Creek in 1995, 2006 and 2010. [SOURCE: Page_271]

260 [SOURCE: Page_271]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_272]

Introgression in the upstream zone.              In the upstream zone, for all simulations, the pattern of [SOURCE: Page_272]

introgression rate simulated matches the one observed in reality before and after the implementation of [SOURCE: Page_272]

the various management practices (Figure 4 – 70; Caudron & Champigneulle, 2011; Scimabio Interface [SOURCE: Page_272]

data). Indeed, a decrease in introgression followed the implementation of the GR strategy. This decrease [SOURCE: Page_272]

was higher for scenarios implementing GxE interactions, which were closer to reality when a medium [SOURCE: Page_272]

strength was implemented. Indeed, stronger GxE interactions led to lower values of introgression rate (~ [SOURCE: Page_272]

0.55) compared to reality (~ 0.75) while the absence of GxE interactions resulted in higher values (~ 0.92). [SOURCE: Page_272]

Following ATL removal and MED translocation, while all scenarios led to a significant decrease in [SOURCE: Page_272]

introgression rate, the scenarios implementing GxE interactions were slightly closer to reality. [SOURCE: Page_272]

Demography in the upstream zone.         In addition, as observed in reality, demography pointed to a self- [SOURCE: Page_272]

sustained population located upstream prior ATL removal for all scenarios. Similarly, following ATL [SOURCE: Page_272]

removal, barely any individuals were left in the population. However, scenarios implementing GxE [SOURCE: Page_272]

interactions showed densities closer to reality. Indeed, without GxE interactions, densities were higher in [SOURCE: Page_272]

the upstream zone prior ATL removal (~ 50 ind.100m-2); whereas it was lower in reality (~ 33 ind.100m-2), [SOURCE: Page_272]

and in scenarios considering GxE interactions (~ 36 ind.100m-2). [SOURCE: Page_272]

4.6.2   The Borne River: from 1988 to 2004 [SOURCE: Page_272]

Introgression in the downstream section.         In the downstream section in 2004, following stocking, [SOURCE: Page_272]

introgression rates observed in the model were far from those measured in reality (Caudron et al., 2009), [SOURCE: Page_272]

no matter the scenario considered (Figure 4 – 71). Indeed, while in reality introgression rate barely [SOURCE: Page_272]

increased in the downstream section despite intensive stocking, it did increase a lot in the model. [SOURCE: Page_272]

However, one can notice that when GxE interactions were implemented, this increase was lessened, even [SOURCE: Page_272]

261 [SOURCE: Page_272]

4.6 Comparing simulated to empirical data [SOURCE: Page_273]

more so in reaches 2 and 3 and in the main downstream zone, where actual introgression rates were also [SOURCE: Page_273]

lower (respectively ~ 0.1 and ~ 0.07) – compared to that of reach 1 (~ 0.18). While simulated data were [SOURCE: Page_273]

getting closer to reality when stronger GxE interactions were implemented, this mechanism alone was [SOURCE: Page_273]

not sufficient to properly predict the evolution of diversity. Some additional knowledge related to ATL fry [SOURCE: Page_273]

contribution to the population and their relative survival is likely to be missing in the model. First, [SOURCE: Page_273]

empirical data indicate that the contribution of ATL fry to stage 0+ in autumn is variable and low (ranging [SOURCE: Page_273]

from 0 to 49%, Caudron et al., 2009). Second, despite a medium contribution in some sectors, it appears [SOURCE: Page_273]

that they do not contribute much further to the population. Indeed, results from Caudron et al., 2009 [SOURCE: Page_273]

indicate that the percentage of stocked ATL trout varied among age classes, and was significantly lower [SOURCE: Page_273]

for 3+ individuals (7% only) compared to 1+ and 2+ (respectively 50% and 47%). In addition, considering [SOURCE: Page_273]

that ATL trout are more susceptible to angling pressure (J. L. García-Marín et al., 1998; Mezzera & [SOURCE: Page_273]

Largiadèr, 2001), the majority of them – which survived to 1+ or 2+ – might have ended up being fished. [SOURCE: Page_273]

Finally, studies indicate that domesticated ATL strains have a survival deficit (Caudron & Champigneulle, [SOURCE: Page_273]

2002). In addition, it has been shown that domesticated trout are more prone to predators (Biro et al., [SOURCE: Page_273]

2004) and anglers (Dwyer, 1990). However, all the underlying mechanisms pertaining to this knowledge [SOURCE: Page_273]

remain unknown and additional empirical data would be required. [SOURCE: Page_273]

262 [SOURCE: Page_273]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_274]

Figure 4—71: Boxplot representing the introgression rate for each eco-evolutionary scenario before and after the implementation of stocking practices for each zone of the downstream part of the Borne River. The dotted line represents the introgression rate sampled in 2004 just before stocking practices stopped. [SOURCE: Page_274]

263 [SOURCE: Page_274]

4.6 Comparing simulated to empirical data [SOURCE: Page_275]

Demography in the downstream section.            Regarding demography, the model overestimated [SOURCE: Page_275]

population size in the downstream zone, and even more so without the implementation of GxE [SOURCE: Page_275]

interactions. Indeed, densities in the main downstream varied between 60 to 75 ind.100m -2, whereas [SOURCE: Page_275]

empirical data indicate densities around 55 ind.100m-2 (Caudron et al., 2009). This is likely to result from [SOURCE: Page_275]

the way density is managed in the model: either through the parameter KMaxE that deals with maximum [SOURCE: Page_275]

carrying capacity in the hydrographic network (Section 3.3.2) or through the values of carrying capacity [SOURCE: Page_275]

given to each reach (Scimabio Interface data, determined based on reaches quality for resources). Any of [SOURCE: Page_275]

these parameters (KMaxE or reach capacity) could have been overestimated. Therefore, more sensitivity [SOURCE: Page_275]

analyses should be performed on each parameter to determine which one may affect the most the final [SOURCE: Page_275]

densities observed. [SOURCE: Page_275]

Demography in the upstream section.              In the upstream section, ATL stocking led to the creation [SOURCE: Page_275]

of a self-sustained ATL population in the model no matter the scenario considered (Section 4.3.4, Figure [SOURCE: Page_275]

4 – 57). However, in reality, only a very small population maintained by stocking was observed (Caudron [SOURCE: Page_275]

et al., 2009). Indeed, densities measured in the upstream zone barely exceeded 14 ind.100m -2 (just [SOURCE: Page_275]

upstream of barrier 3, Figure 4 – 44) and were mainly around 1 or 2 ind.100m-2 (Caudron et al., 2009); [SOURCE: Page_275]

whereas in the model, densities varied between 45 to 65 ind.100m-2 in the upstream sector when [SOURCE: Page_275]

considering or not GxE interactions. Stocking was hypothesised to have failed upstream for three main [SOURCE: Page_275]

reasons (discussed in Caudron et al., 2009). First, poor water quality could have affected the survival of [SOURCE: Page_275]

early developmental stages. Second, high concentrations of ammonium and phosphate were observed, [SOURCE: Page_275]

which can result in egg mortality (Massa et al., 1998; Rubin & Glimsäter, 1996). Finally, a small number of [SOURCE: Page_275]

females was found upstream, potentially due to angling pressure. Beyond these assumptions, the [SOURCE: Page_275]

underlying mechanisms for the observed pattern are still unknown. For example, there are no details of [SOURCE: Page_275]

264 [SOURCE: Page_275]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_276]

the links between pollution and viability available during those years (Caudron et al., 2009). Hence, such [SOURCE: Page_276]

knowledge could not have been implemented in the model. [SOURCE: Page_276]

4.6.3   The Borne River: starting from 2004 [SOURCE: Page_276]

Introgression in the upstream section.           In each zone, following the translocation, the pattern of [SOURCE: Page_276]

introgression rate simulated matched the one observed in reality (Caudron, Champigneulle, et al., 2012) [SOURCE: Page_276]

only for scenarios considering GxE interactions, where a significant decrease in introgression rate could [SOURCE: Page_276]

be observed (Figure 4 – 72). The introgression rate measured in 2004 indicates a significant decrease in [SOURCE: Page_276]

both translocated zones (A and B, respectively down to 0.21 and 0.17) whereas it decreased to a lesser [SOURCE: Page_276]

extent in the zones located just downstream (A$_1$ and B$_1$, respectively down to 0.34 and 0.69). On the [SOURCE: Page_276]

contrary, in the model, the introgression rate decreased similarly among zones, suggesting a [SOURCE: Page_276]

homogenising effect potentially due to movement – with translocated individuals migrating downstream. [SOURCE: Page_276]

This could explain why simulated introgression rates were higher in the translocated zone compared to [SOURCE: Page_276]

reality, despite the implementation of stronger GxE interactions, whereas it was closer to reality (A$_1$) or [SOURCE: Page_276]

even lower (B$_1$) in the zones located downstream. [SOURCE: Page_276]

Genotypic composition in the upstream section.                   Focusing on the genotypic composition [SOURCE: Page_276]

of the population, the simulated results were further away from empirical data (Figure 4 – 73; Caudron, [SOURCE: Page_276]

Champigneulle, et al., 2012). In reality, in the translocated zones (A and B), associated with the decrease [SOURCE: Page_276]

in the proportion of ATL individuals, translocation led to a massive increase in the proportion of pure MED [SOURCE: Page_276]

individuals, whereas only a small proportion of HYB was created. In the simulations, no scenarios allowed [SOURCE: Page_276]

for such an increase in pure MED individuals. Instead, the proportion of HYB individuals increased while [SOURCE: Page_276]

that of ATL decreased, and barely any MED individuals remained. [SOURCE: Page_276]

265 [SOURCE: Page_276]

4.6 Comparing simulated to empirical data [SOURCE: Page_277]

Figure 4—72: Boxplot representing the introgression rate for each eco-evolutionary scenario pre and post MED translocation for each zone monitored in the upstream part of the Borne River. The dotted line represents the introgression rate measured in 2009 after MED translocation. [SOURCE: Page_277]

266 [SOURCE: Page_277]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_278]

Figure 4—73: Genotypic composition of the population in 2009 for each eco-evolutionary scenario and for real data in each zone monitored in the upstream part of the Borne River. [SOURCE: Page_278]

267 [SOURCE: Page_278]

4.6 Comparing simulated to empirical data [SOURCE: Page_279]

Nevertheless, the scenarios implementing GxE interactions were still closer to reality when considering [SOURCE: Page_279]

the extent of the decrease in ATL individuals. Results were more mitigated among the non-translocated [SOURCE: Page_279]

zones. For both, no scenarios could recreate the actual proportion of MED individuals sampled. However, [SOURCE: Page_279]

for zone A$_1$, the model also predicted a correct decrease in the proportion of ATL individuals whereas, for [SOURCE: Page_279]

zone B$_1$, it overestimated the proportion of HYB individuals compared to that of ATL individuals. These [SOURCE: Page_279]

results indicate that when considering GxE interactions, the model always seems to result in full [SOURCE: Page_279]

hybridization, whereas in reality many pure MED individuals are sampled. While one may think that this [SOURCE: Page_279]

is a failure of the model degrading its ability to predict empirical findings, I suggest that this is related to [SOURCE: Page_279]

the number of markers used to discriminate the lineages. Indeed, in reality, a set of only two [SOURCE: Page_279]

microsatellites markers (Str^541 and Str^491, Gharbi et al., 2006) have been used on the Borne River (for [SOURCE: Page_279]

the 1988 case study also: Caudron et al., 2009), whereas in the model we implement seven markers [SOURCE: Page_279]

(Section 3.2.2.4). Therefore, in one case the determination of an individual genotype is based on only four [SOURCE: Page_279]

alleles, whereas in the other it depends on 14 alleles. Consequently, there are three chances out of four [SOURCE: Page_279]

(p = 0.75) to be classified as a hybrid individual in reality versus 13 chances out of 14 (p = 0.93) in the [SOURCE: Page_279]

model. The use of a higher number of markers, in reality, might also have led to a higher proportion of [SOURCE: Page_279]

hybrid individuals. Similarly, the implementation of only two markers in the model could easily change [SOURCE: Page_279]

the genotypic composition of the population. [SOURCE: Page_279]

Demography in the upstream section.                In addition, when GxE interactions were considered, the [SOURCE: Page_279]

main demographic pattern was similar to that observed in reality. Indeed, an increase in population size [SOURCE: Page_279]

was observed following MED translocation. However, the magnitude of this increase was far from that [SOURCE: Page_279]

obtained in reality, mainly because the initial population size was not as low. Indeed, while the selection [SOURCE: Page_279]

mediated by GxE interactions helped in reducing population size, it did not entirely prevent recruitment [SOURCE: Page_279]

in the model, whereas in reality no self-sustained ATL population was found upstream, as indicated in the [SOURCE: Page_279]

268 [SOURCE: Page_279]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_280]

Borne 1988 case study (Caudron et al., 2009). This could not be implemented in the model as underlying [SOURCE: Page_280]

mechanisms were and are still missing. Subsequently, in the model, densities only increased by up to two [SOURCE: Page_280]

folds within three years in the entire upstream area, whereas in reality, densities increased more and [SOURCE: Page_280]

differently between zones. In the translocated zones, densities increased respectively by 6 to 20 folds in [SOURCE: Page_280]

zone A and by 20 to 55 folds in zone B (sometimes up to 55 ind.100m -2, as found downstream). In zones [SOURCE: Page_280]

A$_1$ and B$_1$, densities increased way less, up to respectively 13 and 25 ind.100m-2. In the model, the [SOURCE: Page_280]

presence of a self-sustained population upstream could also account for some of the differences observed [SOURCE: Page_280]

in introgression rate and the genotypic composition of the population, as more ATL individuals would have [SOURCE: Page_280]

been present when translocation happened. [SOURCE: Page_280]

In reality, genetic and demographic effects appeared to be more restricted to the zones where [SOURCE: Page_280]

translocation happened (Caudron, Champigneulle, et al., 2012). As already suggested earlier, individual [SOURCE: Page_280]

movement, which seems to lead to homogenisation among zones, might influence these results. For [SOURCE: Page_280]

instance, in the model, homing movement will make individuals try to go back to their natal reach. As a [SOURCE: Page_280]

result, translocated individuals are more likely to migrate downstream for reproduction. There might [SOURCE: Page_280]

therefore be more movement in the model compared to reality, which could explain why genetic and [SOURCE: Page_280]

demographic effects are more widespread. [SOURCE: Page_280]

4.6.4   Discussion [SOURCE: Page_280]

Altogether, the comparison of empirical data to simulated data indicates that the implementation of GxE [SOURCE: Page_280]

interactions enables the model to reproduce the general patterns of diversity dynamics and demography [SOURCE: Page_280]

observed in reality for each case study (except in the intermediate zone of the Chevenne Creek), much [SOURCE: Page_280]

more than the neutral scenario. In addition, the implementation of stronger GxE interactions and lower [SOURCE: Page_280]

269 [SOURCE: Page_280]

4.6 Comparing simulated to empirical data [SOURCE: Page_281]

selective pressures seems to drive simulated data closer to reality, suggesting that strong patterns of hard [SOURCE: Page_281]

selection may have influenced the evolution of diversity dynamics. However, while simulated data [SOURCE: Page_281]

appears closer to reality when considering EEMs, this does not mean it is the right model, or that these [SOURCE: Page_281]

specific mechanisms only, led to those patterns. Nevertheless, in reality, the consistent change observed [SOURCE: Page_281]

in alleles frequency – from ATL to MED alleles – following management practices across many populations [SOURCE: Page_281]

(Gil et al., 2016) does imply that these alleles are beneficial to individual and population fitness and that [SOURCE: Page_281]

natural selection might have, indeed, played an important role in structuring diversity dynamics. [SOURCE: Page_281]

Therefore, empirical as well as simulated data point toward strong patterns of hard selection in some [SOURCE: Page_281]

environments, while it appears to be less prominent in others. For instance, in the Borne River, the ATL [SOURCE: Page_281]

population located upstream was mainly maintained by continuous stocking and collapsed after the [SOURCE: Page_281]

implementation of the GR, whereas in the Chevenne a self-sufficient ATL population was naturally [SOURCE: Page_281]

maintained. [SOURCE: Page_281]

While for some case studies or zones, the model could reproduce some changes in allele frequency on [SOURCE: Page_281]

similar timescales, the magnitude of these changes was less well represented. In addition, demographic [SOURCE: Page_281]

patterns were further away from reality. However, the differences observed between simulated and [SOURCE: Page_281]

empirical data are not entirely surprising for multiple reasons. [SOURCE: Page_281]

 First, it remains hard to compare empirical and simulated data, given that empirical data are [SOURCE: Page_281]

highly incomplete due to limited sampling and many biases. For instance, depending on the case [SOURCE: Page_281]

study, only one or two years of data were available. Sampling was performed very soon after the [SOURCE: Page_281]

implementation of management practices and no follow-up was available later on. In addition, [SOURCE: Page_281]

data were often collected at some given points of the river stretch and not along the entire section [SOURCE: Page_281]

studied, and may not be fully representative of the population. [SOURCE: Page_281]

270 [SOURCE: Page_281]

Chapter 4. MEDITERRANEA: A tool to measure the role of eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_282]

 Second, while simulation data are much more complete and comprehensive, they rely on a set of [SOURCE: Page_282]

assumptions that were partly verified at most, and that were formulated based on a few empirical [SOURCE: Page_282]

data. Indeed, parameter values used for calibrating some sub-models (such as survival and mating [SOURCE: Page_282]

preference), were mainly based on general observations made from only one or a few [SOURCE: Page_282]

populations, and may not represent the entire MED/ATL hybridization complex. By definition, the [SOURCE: Page_282]

model represents an intentional simplification of the processes observed in reality. It does not, [SOURCE: Page_282]

and could not have included all the eco-evolutionary forces playing in reality, as it will make it way [SOURCE: Page_282]

too complex, but also because much information is still missing on the potential factors driving [SOURCE: Page_282]

hybridization. [SOURCE: Page_282]

 Finally, those differences could arise from differences in temperatures experienced versus [SOURCE: Page_282]

simulated as variations in temperatures modulate the selection mediated by GxE interactions. For [SOURCE: Page_282]

instance, the temperatures used for each case study did not represent actual temperatures [SOURCE: Page_282]

experienced in reality. Indeed, water temperature data were not available to implement an actual [SOURCE: Page_282]

climatic trend for the years over which management practices took place. Consequently, the [SOURCE: Page_282]

temperature model was highly simplified – no climatic trend, i.e. the same every year – and only [SOURCE: Page_282]

based on the very few punctual data available. [SOURCE: Page_282]

Furthermore, these results highlight the importance of movement processes. Indeed, as dispersal has [SOURCE: Page_282]

been acknowledged as central for understanding species distribution among but also within rivers (Tonkin [SOURCE: Page_282]

et al., 2018), factors influencing movement in the model might play an important role in the spatial [SOURCE: Page_282]

structuration of intraspecific diversity. On the one hand, the mechanisms and factors influencing [SOURCE: Page_282]

movement have to be more specific to each case study. A range of values for some parameters, such as [SOURCE: Page_282]

movement probability or weirs passibility, could be tested using the model in order to improve their fit. [SOURCE: Page_282]

271 [SOURCE: Page_282]

4.6 Comparing simulated to empirical data [SOURCE: Page_283]

On the other hand, we may have to consider additional movement processes, such as juvenile downward [SOURCE: Page_283]

migration. [SOURCE: Page_283]

272 [SOURCE: Page_283]

## MANAGEMENT GUIDELINES

The previous chapter investigated the actual role of both empirical eco-evolutionary mechanisms (EEMs) [SOURCE: Page_286]

- heterogamous preference and GxE interactions on survival - in structuring the evolution of diversity. [SOURCE: Page_286]

Specifically, this was investigated in real case studies, where management practices had been [SOURCE: Page_286]

implemented and monitored by managers and scientists. Comparing scenarios considering, or not, these [SOURCE: Page_286]

EEMs, we concluded that the selection mediated by GxE interactions could interfere with management [SOURCE: Page_286]

practices to modify their outcome. Furthermore, we showed that the implementation of GxE interactions [SOURCE: Page_286]

helped improve the predictability of the model, as simulated results were getting closer to reality in most [SOURCE: Page_286]

cases. Therefore, these mechanisms cannot be discarded when implementing management strategies, as [SOURCE: Page_286]

such knowledge could help in building more efficient and evolutionary-inspired management strategies. [SOURCE: Page_286]

However, simulations in the previous chapter were based on punctual and real case studies, wherein data [SOURCE: Page_286]

and knowledge were available. These results alone are not sufficient to infer general knowledge on the [SOURCE: Page_286]

importance of such mechanisms for management. Indeed, variations in some ecological factors, such as [SOURCE: Page_286]

temperatures, landscape features (hydrographic network size, connectivity, structure…), as well as the [SOURCE: Page_286]

ratio and the spatial distribution of genotypes are important to consider. They may modulate the impact [SOURCE: Page_286]

of such EEMs on diversity dynamics and hence influence the outcome of management strategy. Therefore, [SOURCE: Page_286]

selecting the appropriate strategy adapted to each context is not an easy task. [SOURCE: Page_286]

While further research is required to better understand spatial as well as temporal dynamics of [SOURCE: Page_286]

introgression over time, evaluating the effectiveness of management strategies in an adaptive loop [SOURCE: Page_286]

remains essential (B. K. Williams, 2011). This can be achieved through modelling, using DG-ABMs [SOURCE: Page_286]

275 [SOURCE: Page_286]

5.1 Objectives [SOURCE: Page_287]

(Lamarins, Fririon, et al., 2022). Hence, by allowing for the consideration of complex ecological and [SOURCE: Page_287]

evolutionary processes (Ayllón, Railsback, et al., 2019; Castellani et al., 2015; Lamarins, Hugon, et al., [SOURCE: Page_287]

2022; Nathan et al., 2019), as well as management strategies (Ayllón, Nicola, et al., 2019; Santostasi, [SOURCE: Page_287]

prospective-tool to inform decision-making. [SOURCE: Page_287]

## 5.1 OBJECTIVES

As already described before (Section 2.2.3), a few common patterns of diversity have been observed in [SOURCE: Page_287]

the Mediterranean area. Similarly, hydrographic networks can be categorised based on their shape and [SOURCE: Page_287]

their mean winter temperatures. This information can be pooled and translated into many situations in [SOURCE: Page_287]

order to evaluate the impact of various management practices - usually applied in the MED/ATL [SOURCE: Page_287]

hybridization complex (Gil et al., 2016) - in some typical situations. These situations, yet unrealistic, would [SOURCE: Page_287]

be more generic and may help in evaluating the usefulness of evolutionary enlightened management. For [SOURCE: Page_287]

instance, some general dynamics might be observed in certain situations, for example when network [SOURCE: Page_287]

connectivity is higher. [SOURCE: Page_287]

Therefore, the aim of this chapter is to simulate various management practices in some of these generic [SOURCE: Page_287]

situations while accounting for both EEMs, using MEDITERRANEA. On the one hand, I will compare the [SOURCE: Page_287]

evolution of diversity dynamics under various management scenarios (i) to determine whether the [SOURCE: Page_287]

management goal is achievable and (ii) to evaluate the relative efficiency of each management strategy. I [SOURCE: Page_287]

will also consider whether simulated results corroborate what has been observed in reality following the [SOURCE: Page_287]

implementation of these management strategies. On the other hand, I will investigate whether some [SOURCE: Page_287]

differences can be observed based on some initial ecological settings - temperature and network [SOURCE: Page_287]

276 [SOURCE: Page_287]

Chapter 5. MEDITERRANEA: A decision-making tool to provide management guidelines [SOURCE: Page_288]

connectivity. This should allow us to provide some general guidelines to managers based on these [SOURCE: Page_288]

ecological setups. [SOURCE: Page_288]

## 5.2 CASE STUDY AND SIMULATION SCENARIOS

5.2.1      Ecological settings [SOURCE: Page_288]

Initial context.           For these simulations, I used one of the typical diversity distributions observed in [SOURCE: Page_288]

the Mediterranean area (Section 2.2.3, Figure 2 – 7, situation D): an allopatric spatial repartition with an [SOURCE: Page_288]

ATL population located upstream and a MED population downstream. An allopatric distribution was [SOURCE: Page_288]

favoured because conservation strategies are often carried out where nearly pure MED populations are [SOURCE: Page_288]

still found (Gil et al., 2016; Laikre et al., 1999) and where the isolated location of ATL individuals will favour [SOURCE: Page_288]

the implementation of some selective management practices (Caudron & Champigneulle, 2011). An equal [SOURCE: Page_288]

repartition of the population is initially assumed with each population occupying 50% of the geographic [SOURCE: Page_288]

range (Figure 5 – 1). [SOURCE: Page_288]

Hydrographic networks.            Simulations are carried out on two different types of hydrographic [SOURCE: Page_288]

networks: a longitudinal rectilinear network with simple ramification versus an inverted leaf-shaped [SOURCE: Page_288]

dendritic network (Figure 5 – 1) in order to test the impact of connectivity (Labonne et al., 2008) on [SOURCE: Page_288]

management strategies efficiency. Most topological aspects of each hydrographic network are simplified [SOURCE: Page_288]

in order to avoid any confounding effects. Reach characteristics influencing density, such as length, width [SOURCE: Page_288]

or carrying factor are similar among reaches, even though upstream parts of rivers are narrower in reality. [SOURCE: Page_288]

This is to allow the simulation of an admixture event between downstream MED and upstream ATL [SOURCE: Page_288]

populations with the same potential carrying capacity. Both hydrographic networks have the same size [SOURCE: Page_288]

and contain 50 reaches of 10 000 metres square – 2000 metres in length and 5 metres in width, to only [SOURCE: Page_288]

277 [SOURCE: Page_288]

5.2 Case study and simulation scenarios [SOURCE: Page_289]

test for the effect of connectivity. All quality parameters – carrying and reproduction factor – are set at [SOURCE: Page_289]

the maximum and density is set around 30 ind.100m-2. As the temperature model is based on altitude, [SOURCE: Page_289]

altitude is the only parameter to vary between reaches in order to create a temperature contrast from [SOURCE: Page_289]

upstream to downstream. For both hydrographic networks, the difference in altitude, between the lower [SOURCE: Page_289]

and the higher reach of the river, ranges from 400 metres to 1400 metres. An increase in altitude will be [SOURCE: Page_289]

constant from one reach to another within a hydrographic network. [SOURCE: Page_289]

Figure 5—1: Representation of both hydrographic networks under MEDITERRANEA with the shape of the drainage basin and the initial genetic distribution. On each network, a MED population is located downstream and an ATL population upstream each occupying 50% of the geographic range. [SOURCE: Page_289]

278 [SOURCE: Page_289]

Chapter 5. MEDITERRANEA: A decision-making tool to provide management guidelines [SOURCE: Page_290]

Temperature model.        The same temperature models are used for both hydrographic networks. [SOURCE: Page_290]

However, the topology of each network produces temperature differences between reaches as the [SOURCE: Page_290]

dendritic aspect of one network generates more reaches at higher altitudes - the leaf-shaped one. One [SOURCE: Page_290]

temperature model will represent a potentially favourable habitat for MED populations with low winter [SOURCE: Page_290]

temperatures (Figure 5 – 2). Alternatively, the other one will simulate warmer winter temperatures, [SOURCE: Page_290]

where all lineages may fare the same (Figure 5 – 3). [SOURCE: Page_290]

Figure 5—2: Colder temperature model. Water temperature is calculated per reach each month based on altitude. A zoom of winter temperatures shows the temperatures from December to March, which are here always inferior to 5°C. This graph is made using the rectilinear hydrographic network. [SOURCE: Page_290]

279 [SOURCE: Page_290]

5.2 Case study and simulation scenarios [SOURCE: Page_291]

Simulation framework.        Simulations are replicated 20 times per scenario. Each simulation starts in [SOURCE: Page_291]

September and is run over 30 years in order to follow the evolution of diversity dynamics and demography [SOURCE: Page_291]

over a long period after the implementation of management strategies. The first 10 years are used to [SOURCE: Page_291]

stabilise the model and interventions for management strategies are then implemented (t = 11). The [SOURCE: Page_291]

results will only focus on two components of diversity dynamics usually investigated by managers – [SOURCE: Page_291]

introgression rates and genotypic composition of the population (MED, HYB, ATL) – as well as total [SOURCE: Page_291]

population size. [SOURCE: Page_291]

Figure 5—3: Warmer temperature model. Water temperature is calculated per reach each month based on altitude. A zoom of winter temperatures shows the temperatures from December to March, which are often inferior to 5°C. This graph is made using the rectilinear hydrographic network. [SOURCE: Page_291]

280 [SOURCE: Page_291]

Chapter 5. MEDITERRANEA: A decision-making tool to provide management guidelines [SOURCE: Page_292]

5.2.2    Management scenarios [SOURCE: Page_292]

Managers’ goal.          In this context of allopatric repartition, managers often aim at protecting and [SOURCE: Page_292]

restoring a continuous and viable MED population along the river in order to (i) establish a self-sufficient [SOURCE: Page_292]

population upstream when the ATL population does not thrive (Caudron, Champigneulle, et al., 2012) [SOURCE: Page_292]

and/or avoid an ATL gene flow from upstream that may compromise the MED population (Caudron & [SOURCE: Page_292]

Champigneulle, 2011). Over the last 20 years, many management practices have been implemented in [SOURCE: Page_292]

order to conserve and restore the native MED gene pool at the population level (Caudron, Champigneulle, [SOURCE: Page_292]

et al., 2012; Caudron et al., 2006, 2011; Caudron, Vigier, et al., 2012; Caudron & Champigneulle, 2011; Gil [SOURCE: Page_292]

et al., 2016). However, they often lead to the admixture of both lineages. Their impact on the evolution [SOURCE: Page_292]

of diversity dynamics should therefore be monitored in order to avoid counter-productive results (Gil et [SOURCE: Page_292]

al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_292]

Management strategies.          A few of the most common management practices applied by fisheries [SOURCE: Page_292]

managers will be assessed with and without prior removal of non-native individuals by electrofishing [SOURCE: Page_292]

(Caudron et al., 2011; S. E. Moore et al., 1986; Peterson et al., 2004). Management efficiency will be [SOURCE: Page_292]

evaluated for the following strategies (Figure 5 – 4): barrier removal, BR (Hart & Poff, 2002; Roni et al., [SOURCE: Page_292]

2002), stocking with native fry, SNF (Caudron et al., 2006; Cowx, 1994; Crivelli et al., 2000; Leitwein et al., [SOURCE: Page_292]

2016) and direct translocation of wild individuals, DT (Caudron, Champigneulle, et al., 2012; Hilderbrand, [SOURCE: Page_292]

2002; Moritz, 1999; Schmetterling, 2003): genitors exclusively (Caudron et al., 2011) or individuals of [SOURCE: Page_292]

various ages (Caudron, Champigneulle, et al., 2012). Management scenarios are defined as described in [SOURCE: Page_292]

Figure 5 – 5. Management strategies implemented are described below: [SOURCE: Page_292]

 Non-native ATL removal: extra mortality is applied to ATL individuals to ensure that 90% of the [SOURCE: Page_292]

population has been removed with no genitors left (Caudron & Champigneulle, 2011). As the goal [SOURCE: Page_292]

is not to evaluate how long removal might take but only to simulate its outcome, ATL individuals [SOURCE: Page_292]

281 [SOURCE: Page_292]

5.2 Case study and simulation scenarios [SOURCE: Page_293]

are removed over one time step just before the implementation of other management [SOURCE: Page_293]

strategies. Note that, in the model, removal is perfect; i.e. there is no error on genotype [SOURCE: Page_293]

assessment. [SOURCE: Page_293]

 Barrier removal (BR): the impassable barrier between MED and ATL populations is removed to [SOURCE: Page_293]

reinstall full connectivity along the river. [SOURCE: Page_293]

 Stocking with native MED fry (SNF): the ATL zone is stocked with native MED fry for five [SOURCE: Page_293]

consecutive years. 6000 individuals are stocked per hectare and per year. Individuals are stocked [SOURCE: Page_293]

just before summer in May when they are approximately 5 months old with an initial size of 3 [SOURCE: Page_293]

centimetres (Caudron et al., 2006; Gil et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_293]

 Direct Translocation of wild MED Trout (DT): MED individuals from the downstream part of the [SOURCE: Page_293]

river are transferred upstream for five consecutive years. A sufficient number of individuals has [SOURCE: Page_293]

to be translocated to reflect the genetic composition of the source population (Stockwell et al., [SOURCE: Page_293]

1996) and occupy approximately 10% of the carrying capacity (Hilderbrand, 2002) with at least [SOURCE: Page_293]

100 individuals transferred per introduction event to favour their establishment (Fischer & [SOURCE: Page_293]

Lindenmayer, 2000; Minckley, 1995). In addition, it is suggested that fish are introduced along [SOURCE: Page_293]

large areas of the river rather than restricted ones to favour the colonisation of the full area [SOURCE: Page_293]

(Caudron, Champigneulle, et al., 2012). Therefore, 5000 trout are translocated in five reaches, [SOURCE: Page_293]

segregated along the mainstream to cover a larger area (Figure 5 – 4), resulting in the transfer of [SOURCE: Page_293]

200 trout per year per reach. Overall, a density of 200 trout per hectare is transferred (calculated [SOURCE: Page_293]

based on successful results from the Borne River, Caudron, Champigneulle, et al., 2012). For the [SOURCE: Page_293]

translocation of genitors, only individuals above a certain size (~ 20 centimetres) are transferred. [SOURCE: Page_293]

282 [SOURCE: Page_293]

Chapter 5. MEDITERRANEA: A decision-making tool to provide management guidelines [SOURCE: Page_294]

Figure 5—4: Representation of the management strategies assessed. Each practice is tested with and without prior non-native ATL removal. [SOURCE: Page_294]

Management practices efficiency is often measured by evaluating the decrease in population [SOURCE: Page_294]

introgression rate. Based on Laikre et al. (1999), populations with an introgression rate of 25% or less are [SOURCE: Page_294]

considered as nearly pure native MED populations. Hence, management practices leading to such a [SOURCE: Page_294]

decrease will be considered successful. Relative management efficiency will be evaluated based on (i) the [SOURCE: Page_294]

time within which such a decrease happened, (ii) the rate of pure MED versus HYB and ATL individuals [SOURCE: Page_294]

283 [SOURCE: Page_294]

5.3 Evaluating management strategies efficiency [SOURCE: Page_295]

observed in the population and (iii) the consequences on population size. Therefore, results will focus on [SOURCE: Page_295]

diversity dynamics and demography – introgression, genotypic composition and distribution of individual [SOURCE: Page_295]

introgression. [SOURCE: Page_295]

Figure 5—5: Simulations timeline and list of management scenarios (G$_1$ à G$_8$) with implemented strategies. [SOURCE: Page_295]

## 5.3 EVALUATING MANAGEMENT STRATEGIES EFFICIENCY

5.3.1    Rectilinear network under colder temperatures [SOURCE: Page_295]

5.3.1.1   Evolution of introgression [SOURCE: Page_295]

A clear decrease in introgression rate – the percentage of ATL alleles – followed the implementation of all [SOURCE: Page_295]

management strategies (Figure 5 – 6). For most, the decrease was more important when prior ATL removal [SOURCE: Page_295]

was implemented. The SNF strategy led to the highest decrease going from 1 to 0.2 in one year only, and [SOURCE: Page_295]

284 [SOURCE: Page_295]

Chapter 5. MEDITERRANEA: A decision-making tool to provide management guidelines [SOURCE: Page_296]

to nearly 0 after five years of stocking without prior ATL removal. With ATL removal, it went from 1 to [SOURCE: Page_296]

nearly 0 in just one year. For both DT strategies – individuals and genitors only – introgression rates [SOURCE: Page_296]

decreased less rapidly (from 1 to ~ 0.4 in five years), especially without ATL removal (from 1 to less than [SOURCE: Page_296]

0.2 in five years). The decrease was the steepest during translocation and slowed down afterwards. The [SOURCE: Page_296]

BR strategy led to the smallest and most progressive decrease in introgression, which reached respectively [SOURCE: Page_296]

0.35 and 0.7 with and without prior ATL removal, five years after the barrier removal. [SOURCE: Page_296]

Figure 5—6: Evolution of the population introgression rate in the upstream zone of the rectilinear network for twenty years following the first year of management. Trajectories are represented for each management strategy, with and without prior ATL removal under the colder temperature modality. [SOURCE: Page_296]

5.3.1.2    Genotypic composition of the population [SOURCE: Page_296]

After stabilisation (10 years), the upstream area showed an ATL population with a low population size [SOURCE: Page_296]

(Figure 5 – 7, less than 30000 individuals), due to the strong selection exerted by GxE interactions at colder [SOURCE: Page_296]

285 [SOURCE: Page_296]

5.3 Evaluating management strategies efficiency [SOURCE: Page_297]

temperatures. Following the implementation of all management strategies, a decrease in ATL genotypes [SOURCE: Page_297]

was observed. This decrease was faster following the implementation of the SNF strategy and slower for [SOURCE: Page_297]

the barrier removal. Obviously, with the implementation of ATL removal, this decrease was faster and the [SOURCE: Page_297]

same for all management scenarios. [SOURCE: Page_297]

Figure 5—7: Evolution of the genotypic composition of the population in the upstream zone of the rectilinear network for twenty years following the first year of management. Trajectories are represented for each management strategy, with and without prior ATL removal under the colder temperature modality. [SOURCE: Page_297]

286 [SOURCE: Page_297]

Chapter 5. MEDITERRANEA: A decision-making tool to provide management guidelines [SOURCE: Page_298]

For all scenarios, 10 years after the implementation of management practices, ATL genotypes had fully [SOURCE: Page_298]

disappeared from the population (Figure 5 – 7). Therefore, all management strategies seemed quite [SOURCE: Page_298]

efficient in erasing pure ATL genotypes. The SNF strategy was associated with a substantial increase in [SOURCE: Page_298]

MED genotype (up to ~ 70000), even more so with prior ATL removal (up to ~ 90000) since it reduced the [SOURCE: Page_298]

proportion of HYB individuals created (25000 vs less than 5000). Both DT strategies led to the same [SOURCE: Page_298]

outcome, with the creation of a large number of HYB individuals and very few MED individuals remaining [SOURCE: Page_298]

after translocation, especially without prior ATL removal. However, when ATL removal was implemented, [SOURCE: Page_298]

the number of HYB started decreasing at t = 20 years, while there was a progressive increase in the number [SOURCE: Page_298]

of MED individuals. The outcome of the BR strategy was similar but the speed at which HYB individuals [SOURCE: Page_298]

were created was slower. In addition, there were even fewer MED individuals. For all management [SOURCE: Page_298]

strategies, ATL individuals were easily purged from the population. However, the proportion of MED and [SOURCE: Page_298]

HYB individuals varied vastly based on the management strategy implemented. For instance, ATL removal [SOURCE: Page_298]

always allowed for the persistence of more MED individuals. [SOURCE: Page_298]

5.3.1.3   Evolution of demography [SOURCE: Page_298]

A subsequent increase in population size followed the implementation of each management strategy [SOURCE: Page_298]

(Figure 5 – 8, initial population size at t = 10: ~ 20000 individuals) – after a small initial decrease following [SOURCE: Page_298]

ATL removal. This increase was the fastest and the largest for the SNF strategy. During stocking, population [SOURCE: Page_298]

size peaked out as many individuals were stocked (~ 0.6 fry per m^2). After a small decrease, a plateau was [SOURCE: Page_298]

observed with a population size of approximately 90000 individuals. For the DT strategies, the increase in [SOURCE: Page_298]

population size was more progressive, especially without ATL removal. The increase was the steepest [SOURCE: Page_298]

during translocation and then slightly slowed down. A higher population size was reached faster when [SOURCE: Page_298]

ATL removal preceded the implementation of DT (~ 65000 vs 55000 just after translocation and ~ 90000 [SOURCE: Page_298]

vs 80000 at the end of the simulation). The BR strategy led to the slowest and more progressive increase [SOURCE: Page_298]

287 [SOURCE: Page_298]

5.3 Evaluating management strategies efficiency [SOURCE: Page_299]

in population size. For that strategy, the increase was much faster when ATL removal was also [SOURCE: Page_299]

implemented (with ~ 70000 vs without ~ 55000 at the end of the simulation). Overall, different strategies [SOURCE: Page_299]

only differed slightly in maximum population size, but highly in their speed to reach it. [SOURCE: Page_299]

Figure 5—8: Evolution of population size in the upstream zone of the rectilinear network for twenty years following the first year of management. Trajectories are represented for each management strategy, with and without prior ATL removal under the colder temperature modality. [SOURCE: Page_299]

5.3.2   Leaf-shaped network under colder temperatures [SOURCE: Page_299]

5.3.2.1   Evolution of introgression [SOURCE: Page_299]

When implemented in a leaf-shaped network, management strategies also all resulted in a decrease in [SOURCE: Page_299]

introgression (Figure 5 – 9). For the SNF strategy, the decrease was the same compared to the rectilinear [SOURCE: Page_299]

network, probably because this management strategy was carried out on all the reaches, as native fry [SOURCE: Page_299]

288 [SOURCE: Page_299]

Chapter 5. MEDITERRANEA: A decision-making tool to provide management guidelines [SOURCE: Page_300]

were poured all along the river. For the other management strategies – DT and BR – the decrease in [SOURCE: Page_300]

introgression rate was faster and steeper in the leaf-shaped network, especially for the BR strategy. The [SOURCE: Page_300]

effect of prior ATL removal remained the same: a faster decrease. For the DT strategies, after five years [SOURCE: Page_300]

of translocation, introgression reached approximately 0.1 with, and 0.25 without ATL removal. For the BR [SOURCE: Page_300]

strategy, introgression rates valued respectively 0.2 with, and 0.5 without ATL removal five years after the [SOURCE: Page_300]

Trajectories are represented for each management strategy, with and without prior ATL removal under the colder temperature modality. [SOURCE: Page_300]

In the leaf-shaped network, the composition of the population (Figure 5 – 10), as well as its initial size, [SOURCE: Page_300]

slightly varied compared to the rectilinear network. A lower initial number of ATL individuals was found [SOURCE: Page_300]

289 [SOURCE: Page_300]

5.3 Evaluating management strategies efficiency [SOURCE: Page_301]

in the population for the same habitat surface. All management strategies resulted in a slightly higher [SOURCE: Page_301]

proportion of MED individuals, which was associated with a lower proportion of HYB individuals, in some [SOURCE: Page_301]

cases. In the case of the BR strategy, the increase in the proportion of HYB individuals was slower but [SOURCE: Page_301]

Figure 5—10: Evolution of the genotypic composition of the population in the upstream zone of the leaf- shaped network for twenty years following the first year of management. Trajectories are represented for each management strategy, with and without prior ATL removal under the colder temperature modality. [SOURCE: Page_301]

Chapter 5. MEDITERRANEA: A decision-making tool to provide management guidelines [SOURCE: Page_302]

In addition, when ATL removal was considered, the subsequent decrease in HYB proportion (at t = 20 [SOURCE: Page_302]

strategy. For both the SNF and the DT strategies, population size also increased but not as much or as fast [SOURCE: Page_303]

as in the rectilinear network. For example, for both DT strategies, the population size reached 80000 [SOURCE: Page_303]

with ATL removal (~ 75000 individuals here, Figure 5 – 11; versus 70000 in the rectilinear network, Figure [SOURCE: Page_303]

5 – 8). Overall, the leaf-shaped network produced a lower population size compared to the rectilinear [SOURCE: Page_303]

network, except for the BR strategy. [SOURCE: Page_303]

5.3.3   Rectilinear network under warmer temperatures [SOURCE: Page_303]

In this section, the same scenarios are investigated in the rectilinear network but using a different [SOURCE: Page_303]

temperature regime. This temperature regime shows warmer winter temperatures (Figure 5 – 3), around [SOURCE: Page_303]

7°C. Considering GxE interactions, the survival difference between lineages is therefore lower. Hence, the [SOURCE: Page_303]

selective advantage associated with the native lineage might diminish or even disappear, which could [SOURCE: Page_303]

modify the outcomes of management strategies. [SOURCE: Page_303]

5.3.3.1   Evolution of introgression [SOURCE: Page_303]

A warmer temperature modality changed drastically the outcome of some management practices (Figure [SOURCE: Page_303]

5 – 12). For the SNF strategy, the introgression rate still decreased massively during the stocking period [SOURCE: Page_303]

up until values of introgression inferior to 0.2, therefore lower than management goals. However, for the [SOURCE: Page_303]

DT and BR strategies, introgression rates decreased to a much lower extent, and even more so without [SOURCE: Page_303]

prior ATL removal. For the DT strategies, introgression rates reached approximately 0.6 with and 0.8 [SOURCE: Page_303]

without ATL removal after five years of translocation. Both rates then kept decreasing slower up to [SOURCE: Page_303]

292 [SOURCE: Page_303]

Chapter 5. MEDITERRANEA: A decision-making tool to provide management guidelines [SOURCE: Page_304]

respectively 0.45 and 0.65 at the end of the simulation. For the BR strategy, the introgression rate still [SOURCE: Page_304]

decreased progressively but very slowly. Five years after the barrier removal, it had barely decreased [SOURCE: Page_304]

below 0.9 considering ATL removal. [SOURCE: Page_304]

Figure 5—12: Evolution of the population introgression rate in the upstream zone of the rectilinear network for twenty years following the first year of management. Trajectories are represented for each management strategy, with and without prior ATL removal under the warmer temperature modality. [SOURCE: Page_304]

5.3.3.2   Genotypic composition of the population [SOURCE: Page_304]

The ATL population initially located upstream was more than twice bigger when a higher temperature [SOURCE: Page_304]

modality was implemented since GxE mediated selection was less strong (Figure 5 – 13). In this context, [SOURCE: Page_304]

ATL removal highly decreased the proportion of ATL individuals. However, their number remained high [SOURCE: Page_304]

when the other management strategies were implemented. Although all adults were removed, the sub- [SOURCE: Page_304]

adults left were able to mature and reproduce during the next time step, as indicated by the subsequent [SOURCE: Page_304]

293 [SOURCE: Page_304]

5.3 Evaluating management strategies efficiency [SOURCE: Page_305]

increase in the proportion of ATL individuals for the BR and DT strategies. Following the implementation [SOURCE: Page_305]

of each management strategy, the evolution of the genotypic composition of the population differed [SOURCE: Page_305]

compared to scenarios considering colder temperatures, as suggested by the evolution of introgression. [SOURCE: Page_305]

Figure 5—13: Evolution of the genotypic composition of the population in the upstream zone of the rectilinear network for twenty years following the first year of management. Trajectories are represented for each management strategy, with and without prior ATL removal under the warmer temperature modality. [SOURCE: Page_305]

294 [SOURCE: Page_305]

Chapter 5. MEDITERRANEA: A decision-making tool to provide management guidelines [SOURCE: Page_306]

The elimination of ATL individuals took longer as their proportion was initially higher (Figure 5 – 13). It [SOURCE: Page_306]

remained the fastest for the SNF strategy (within the five years of stocking). However, considering higher [SOURCE: Page_306]

temperature, this strategy also led to extensive hybridization, especially without prior ATL removal, where [SOURCE: Page_306]

a higher proportion of HYB individuals was found (~ 80000 HYB vs 15000 MED at the end of the [SOURCE: Page_306]

simulation). Apart from the SNF strategy, here, no other management strategies allowed for the [SOURCE: Page_306]

maintenance of pure MED individuals. The DT strategies both led to an overall decrease in the proportion [SOURCE: Page_306]

of ATL individuals associated with a continuous increase in the proportion of HYB individuals. Ultimately, [SOURCE: Page_306]

the population was exclusively made of HYB individuals (~ 90000 individuals) and this pattern was the [SOURCE: Page_306]

same with and without prior ATL removal. Finally, for the BR strategy, the decrease in ATL individuals was [SOURCE: Page_306]

more continuous and a large proportion of ATL individuals were still found at the end of the simulation, [SOURCE: Page_306]

considering or not ATL removal (respectively 30000 and 35000 individuals). Meanwhile, the proportion of [SOURCE: Page_306]

HYB individuals increased progressively. [SOURCE: Page_306]

Under this temperature regime, the effects of management strategies were much more contrasted. Most [SOURCE: Page_306]

of them (except SNF) were inefficient considering the initial management goal. Indeed, introgression rates [SOURCE: Page_306]

did not decrease as much, and more HYB individuals were created. [SOURCE: Page_306]

5.3.3.3   Evolution of demography [SOURCE: Page_306]

Without prior ATL removal, population size did not increase as much as when colder temperatures were [SOURCE: Page_306]

considered (Figure 5 – 14). The increase remained the largest for the SNF strategy, whereas it was very [SOURCE: Page_306]

slight for the DT strategies and almost null for the BR strategy. This is because the fitness of ATL [SOURCE: Page_306]

populations is not as reduced compared to that of MED populations when winter temperatures are [SOURCE: Page_306]

warmer. Considering ATL removal, population size increased significatively for all scenarios, since removal [SOURCE: Page_306]

had caused a large initial decrease. Similarly, the increase was the largest for the SNF strategy, followed [SOURCE: Page_306]

295 [SOURCE: Page_306]

5.4 Discussion [SOURCE: Page_307]

by the DT strategy and finally the BR strategy. This could be explained by differences in population mean [SOURCE: Page_307]

fitness. Indeed, even if the differences in survival are lower between MED and ATL genotypes in this [SOURCE: Page_307]

context, populations with lower introgression rates – i.e. a lower proportion of ATL alleles – still have a [SOURCE: Page_307]

fitness advantage over populations with more ATL alleles. Overall, under this temperature regime, [SOURCE: Page_307]

population size did not increase as much following management strategies as it was not as low initially. [SOURCE: Page_307]

Figure 5—14: Evolution of population size in the upstream zone of the rectilinear network for twenty years following the first year of management. Trajectories are represented for each management strategy, with and without prior ATL removal under the warmer temperature modality. [SOURCE: Page_307]
