## 5.4 DISCUSSION

The different management strategies implemented in the model all influenced diversity dynamics and [SOURCE: Page_307]

demography. All management strategies led to a decrease in introgression. In most cases, the strategies [SOURCE: Page_307]

296 [SOURCE: Page_307]

Chapter 5. MEDITERRANEA: A decision-making tool to provide management guidelines [SOURCE: Page_308]

allowed the elimination of pure ATL genotypes, while largely increasing the proportion of hybrid [SOURCE: Page_308]

individuals. In favourable temperatures, some management strategies allowed for a small recovery of [SOURCE: Page_308]

pure MED genotypes, especially following ATL removal. These changes were observed in a short period – [SOURCE: Page_308]

within 5 years following the first implementation. When the initial upstream population size was low, [SOURCE: Page_308]

management strategies all led to a significant increase in population size. Around this general pattern, [SOURCE: Page_308]

however, there were some nuances in the efficiency of the different management strategies. [SOURCE: Page_308]

5.4.1   Management strategies efficiency in the model [SOURCE: Page_308]

The general goal of managers was the restoration of a nearly pure MED population upstream. Based on [SOURCE: Page_308]

the threshold used by managers for nearly pure populations, I consider a management strategy to be [SOURCE: Page_308]

successful when the introgression rate is below 25% (Laikre, 1999). If no management strategies proved [SOURCE: Page_308]

successful, the management goal is considered unachievable. The efficiency of a management strategy is [SOURCE: Page_308]

determined by the speed at which the target introgression rate is reached, as well as genotypic [SOURCE: Page_308]

composition. We can therefore also look at the relative efficiencies between management strategies. [SOURCE: Page_308]

Hydrographic networks and temperatures influenced the efficiency of the various management [SOURCE: Page_308]

strategies. For instance, despite a generally good efficiency of all management strategies, it appears that [SOURCE: Page_308]

target introgression was reached faster in the leaf-shaped network. The downside, however, is that it was [SOURCE: Page_308]

accompanied by general lower population sizes. [SOURCE: Page_308]

Furthermore, management strategies' efficiency highly varied based on winter temperatures. For the [SOURCE: Page_308]

warmer winter temperature, most of the management strategies were not successful, as target [SOURCE: Page_308]

introgression was not reached (except for the SNF strategy). For the lower winter temperature modality [SOURCE: Page_308]

297 [SOURCE: Page_308]

5.4 Discussion [SOURCE: Page_309]

– favourable to the MED maternal lineage – most management strategies were successful as introgression [SOURCE: Page_309]

rates ultimately decrease below 25%. Therefore, temperature appears to be the factor influencing most [SOURCE: Page_309]

management practices' efficiency in the simulations. [SOURCE: Page_309]

In cases where most management strategies were successful, the relative efficiency of each management [SOURCE: Page_309]

strategy differed. The SNF strategy was the most efficient one in the model, as it always led to the fastest [SOURCE: Page_309]

and strongest decrease in introgression (nearly 0 within only 5 years). It also allowed for the creation of [SOURCE: Page_309]

more MED genotypes compared to HYB genotypes, and led to the highest population size recovery. [SOURCE: Page_309]

Considering prior ATL removal, this strategy led to the restoration of a nearly pure MED population mainly [SOURCE: Page_309]

composed of pure MED genotypes (barely any HYB remained). Both DT strategies - individuals or genitors [SOURCE: Page_309]

only - then followed with a rapid decrease in introgression and a significant increase in population size. [SOURCE: Page_309]

However, this management strategy was associated with the creation of a majority of HYB individuals. [SOURCE: Page_309]

Finally, the BR strategy was not always successful – i.e. without ATL removal – and was less efficient as [SOURCE: Page_309]

the decrease in introgression and the increase in population size were the slowest. Overall, prior ATL [SOURCE: Page_309]

removal made all management strategies more efficient as it allowed for the removal of a large proportion [SOURCE: Page_309]

of ATL individuals. Specifically for the DT strategies, it resulted in the creation of more MED genotypes. It [SOURCE: Page_309]

also allowed for a stronger increase in population size, as it increased the mean fitness of the population [SOURCE: Page_309]

– through the removal of low fitness genotype. [SOURCE: Page_309]

5.4.2   Evaluation of each strategy [SOURCE: Page_309]

Each of the management strategies tested here has previously been suggested and potentially [SOURCE: Page_309]

implemented in the MED/ATL hybridization complex and/or on other salmonids. Therefore, some [SOURCE: Page_309]

knowledge is available regarding their actual efficiency and feasibility in different cases. This knowledge [SOURCE: Page_309]

298 [SOURCE: Page_309]

Chapter 5. MEDITERRANEA: A decision-making tool to provide management guidelines [SOURCE: Page_310]

combined with our simulated results will help us evaluate each management strategy in a cost-effective [SOURCE: Page_310]

way. [SOURCE: Page_310]

5.4.2.1   Barrier removal [SOURCE: Page_310]

Barrier removal has never been used as a management strategy in the context of MED/ATL hybridization. [SOURCE: Page_310]

Indeed, barriers can stop the spread of non-native individuals, when invasive or domesticated individuals [SOURCE: Page_310]

pose a threat to native diversity (P. E. Jones et al., 2021). For instance, some management practices rely [SOURCE: Page_310]

on, or use, such fragmentation to intentionally prevent the movement of non-native individuals into [SOURCE: Page_310]

native populations (“Isolation management”: (Novinger & Rahel, 2003); “Selective fragmentation”: (Rahel [SOURCE: Page_310]

& McLaughlin, 2018)). This type of strategy may provide short-term benefits by diminishing the risks of [SOURCE: Page_310]

hybridization between divergent gene pools or taxa (Novinger & Rahel, 2003). [SOURCE: Page_310]

However, there might be some long-term consequences to such isolation, including vulnerability to [SOURCE: Page_310]

stochastic processes, diseases, angling pressure, etc., as well as the loss of genetic diversity (Garcia de [SOURCE: Page_310]

Leaniz, 2008; Novinger & Rahel, 2003). Indeed, barriers, such as dams, affect river fragmentation and [SOURCE: Page_310]

connectivity (Nilsson et al., 2005) and can restrict the distribution and abundance of salmonids [SOURCE: Page_310]

populations (Garcia de Leaniz, 2008). In addition to their impact on animal movements and habitat [SOURCE: Page_310]

availability, barriers can also have significant impacts on temperature and hydrological conditions (Hart [SOURCE: Page_310]

et al., 2002). Dam removal represents an important management tool for the restoration of rivers (Hart [SOURCE: Page_310]

& Poff, 2002) and the restitution of connectivity (Roni et al., 2002). [SOURCE: Page_310]

Therefore, managers often wonder whether barrier removal would be a beneficial strategy allowing for [SOURCE: Page_310]

the recolonisation of upstream parts of the river by native individuals. Indeed, increasing the available [SOURCE: Page_310]

surface for growth and reproduction could be one of the best ways to restore a self-sufficient population [SOURCE: Page_310]

299 [SOURCE: Page_310]

5.4 Discussion [SOURCE: Page_311]

all along the river and increase population size. However, they also fear that this strategy would lead to [SOURCE: Page_311]

deleterious impacts on native populations when a non-native population is present upstream. [SOURCE: Page_311]

Our results give some insight into the impact of this management strategy in such contexts. On the one [SOURCE: Page_311]

hand, it suggests that it will likely lead to extensive hybridization upstream when a self-sufficient ATL [SOURCE: Page_311]

population is present. Managers who do not wish to be faced with this outcome should therefore avoid [SOURCE: Page_311]

this management strategy. On the other hand, barrier removal barely had any impact on diversity [SOURCE: Page_311]

dynamics and demography of the downstream population (results not shown). When temperatures were [SOURCE: Page_311]

favourable, selection against ATL alleles appeared to be strong enough to counter the homogenising [SOURCE: Page_311]

effects of gene flow (Garant et al., 2007). Therefore, such management practices could be used by [SOURCE: Page_311]

managers interested in the general resilience of the population, as it will increase stream connectivity and [SOURCE: Page_311]

accessibility (Garcia de Leaniz, 2008), thereby favouring the growth of the population. Nevertheless, for [SOURCE: Page_311]

long hydrographic networks, recolonisation might take a long time. Additional management strategies [SOURCE: Page_311]

could be used to accelerate the settlement of a population. [SOURCE: Page_311]

5.4.2.2   Stocking with native fry [SOURCE: Page_311]

The SNF strategy has already been used multiple times in Haute-Savoie (Caudron et al., 2006) and [SOURCE: Page_311]

elsewhere (Fernández-Cebrián et al., 2014; Leitwein et al., 2016) to restore native MED populations in the [SOURCE: Page_311]

Mediterranean area. While simulations show very promising results, the reality of this management [SOURCE: Page_311]

strategy is more nuanced. In reality, while the introgression rate of populations also decreased [SOURCE: Page_311]

significantly following the SNF strategy (Caudron et al., 2006, 2011; Gil et al., 2016), it did not translate [SOURCE: Page_311]

into a large increase in the proportion of MED genotype, unlike in the model. On the contrary, it led to [SOURCE: Page_311]

the extensive creation of hybrid individuals (Caudron et al., 2006, 2011; Gil et al., 2016). In reality, this [SOURCE: Page_311]

management strategy was therefore not considered as successful regarding managers’ initial goal to [SOURCE: Page_311]

300 [SOURCE: Page_311]

Chapter 5. MEDITERRANEA: A decision-making tool to provide management guidelines [SOURCE: Page_312]

restore a nearly pure MED population with a majority of pure MED genotype (Gil et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_312]

Nevertheless, from another perspective, one could consider this management strategy as efficient given [SOURCE: Page_312]

that it led to the disappearance of the ATL genotype and the establishment of a new lowly introgressed [SOURCE: Page_312]

admixed brown trout population. Other studies on salmonids showed the same results and suggested that [SOURCE: Page_312]

the introduction of pure native fry can lead to the establishment and restoration of new admixed [SOURCE: Page_312]

populations (Hansen et al., 2006; Vincenzi et al., 2008). [SOURCE: Page_312]

However, artificial reproduction in fish farming conditions might still have consequences on individuals’ [SOURCE: Page_312]

fitness, even if genitors have not been domesticated, and hence influence population dynamics. For [SOURCE: Page_312]

instance, crosses are made by human choices and might be biased. In addition, early life stages in artificial [SOURCE: Page_312]

conditions might influence development. Furthermore, genomic studies indicate that heterozygote [SOURCE: Page_312]

deficiency is found in hatchery strains from both, ATL and MED strains (Leitwein et al., 2016). This study, [SOURCE: Page_312]

as well as others (Fernández-Cebrián et al., 2014; Lusardi et al., 2015) suggest that stocking practices – [SOURCE: Page_312]

may it be from native or non-native strains – can result in genetic homogenisation and the actual loss of [SOURCE: Page_312]

native diversity, thereby affecting the genetic integrity of wild populations. [SOURCE: Page_312]

Given the long-term negative consequences associated with the SNF strategy on native diversity [SOURCE: Page_312]

(Fernández-Cebrián et al., 2014; Leitwein et al., 2016; Lusardi et al., 2015), managers should avoid it when [SOURCE: Page_312]

the genetic integrity of MED diversity is at risk. For instance, this could be the case when pure MED [SOURCE: Page_312]

individuals are found in sympatry with ATL and/or HYB individuals, or when gene flow from an ATL [SOURCE: Page_312]

population threatens a MED population. Similarly, if the management goal is to restore nearly pure MED [SOURCE: Page_312]

genotypes this strategy should also be avoided, as the likely outcome will be full hybridization. However, [SOURCE: Page_312]

for managers only aiming at removing and replacing ATL genotypes, this strategy could still be used, but [SOURCE: Page_312]

only if the ATL population is completely isolated. [SOURCE: Page_312]

301 [SOURCE: Page_312]

5.4 Discussion [SOURCE: Page_313]

5.4.2.3    Direct translocation [SOURCE: Page_313]

Direct translocation of MED individuals (Caudron, Champigneulle, et al., 2012; Caudron & Champigneulle, [SOURCE: Page_313]

2011) and MED genitors (Caudron et al., 2011) have been implemented multiple times to manage brown [SOURCE: Page_313]

trout intraspecific diversity. Patterns observed in reality following the implementation of DT strategies are [SOURCE: Page_313]

very similar to those predicted by the model. Indeed, this strategy always leads to a decrease in [SOURCE: Page_313]

introgression; but the genotypic composition of the population varies based on the initial density of ATL [SOURCE: Page_313]

individuals. For instance, this strategy has proved very efficient at restoring nearly pure MED populations [SOURCE: Page_313]

in sectors where ATL density was low (naturally: Caudron, Champigneulle, et al., 2012; Caudron et al., [SOURCE: Page_313]

2011 or following removal: Caudron & Champigneulle, 2011). On the contrary, when ATL density was [SOURCE: Page_313]

higher, it led to admixture between both lineages (Caudron et al., 2011). Comparing the effect of multiple [SOURCE: Page_313]

strategies, Gil et al. (2016) identified this strategy as the most efficient one. This could result from the fact [SOURCE: Page_313]

that this strategy keeps a large part of the natural processes in natura, as translocated individuals have [SOURCE: Page_313]

been subject to natural selection, and will be subject to sexual selection. Managers aiming at restoring [SOURCE: Page_313]

MED populations should therefore favour this strategy when feasible. [SOURCE: Page_313]

Some conditions were proposed to evaluate the feasibility of translocation. First, the receiving [SOURCE: Page_313]

environment should be able to support the introduction of additional individuals in terms of resources [SOURCE: Page_313]

and habitats for reproduction (Dunham et al., 2011). Second, the removal of individuals should not [SOURCE: Page_313]

endanger the donor population (Dunham et al., 2011). Finally, the density of non-native individuals and [SOURCE: Page_313]

their ability to reproduce is crucial to consider, if hybridization is an issue (Gil et al., 2016). When the first [SOURCE: Page_313]

two conditions are validated, managers interested in restoring MED genotypes should apply this strategy [SOURCE: Page_313]

only when initial ATL densities are low. However, managers interested in the resilience of the species can [SOURCE: Page_313]

use this strategy, even when a well-established ATL population is found, as it will lead to large [SOURCE: Page_313]

hybridization, thereby increasing the standing genetic variation of the population. [SOURCE: Page_313]

302 [SOURCE: Page_313]

Chapter 5. MEDITERRANEA: A decision-making tool to provide management guidelines [SOURCE: Page_314]

5.4.2.4   ATL removal [SOURCE: Page_314]

The removal of non-native individuals through electrofishing has already been used as a management [SOURCE: Page_314]

strategy to reduce the density of non-native populations (Caudron & Champigneulle, 2011; Thompson & [SOURCE: Page_314]

Rahel, 1996). While it does lead to a significant decrease in population size, removing the entire non- [SOURCE: Page_314]

native population remains impossible despite years and years of removal (Caudron et al., 2011; S. E. [SOURCE: Page_314]

Moore et al., 1986; Thompson & Rahel, 1996). In the model, even though many ATL individuals remained [SOURCE: Page_314]

in the population, ATL removal was an efficient prior management strategy, as it allowed increasing the [SOURCE: Page_314]

efficiency of other management strategies. It could therefore remain a good strategy prior to the [SOURCE: Page_314]

implementation of restoration strategies such as translocation (Caudron & Champigneulle, 2011). [SOURCE: Page_314]

However, this strategy can be very costly and laborious (Caudron & Champigneulle, 2011; Meyer et al., [SOURCE: Page_314]

2006; S. E. Moore et al., 1986; Peterson et al., 2004; Shepard et al., 2002; Thompson & Rahel, 1996), as [SOURCE: Page_314]

multiple consecutive years of fishing are required. For instance, Caudron & Champigneulle (2011), [SOURCE: Page_314]

estimated a cost of 1 500 € per 100 metres of streams. Considering this aspect, ATL removal might only [SOURCE: Page_314]

be cost-effective in a small hydrographic network, like in the Chevenne Creek (Caudron & Champigneulle, [SOURCE: Page_314]

2011). In a bigger network, it is likely to be impossible to implement. [SOURCE: Page_314]

In addition, the distribution of genotypes might also be important to consider. This strategy might be easy [SOURCE: Page_314]

to implement when native and non-native individuals are in allopatry, as all individuals may just be fished [SOURCE: Page_314]

out. However, when they are distributed in sympatry, distinguishing between fish of different origins [SOURCE: Page_314]

might be complex. [SOURCE: Page_314]

303 [SOURCE: Page_314]

5.4 Discussion [SOURCE: Page_315]

5.4.3   Network shapes and landscape features [SOURCE: Page_315]

Our results indicated that the shape of dendritic networks influences diversity dynamics and demography. [SOURCE: Page_315]

For instance, in the leaf-shaped hydrographic network, introgression rates observed following each [SOURCE: Page_315]

management strategy were slightly lower compared to the rectilinear network. This could be explained [SOURCE: Page_315]

by two main reasons related to differences in landscape features. [SOURCE: Page_315]

 On the one hand, connectivity may vary between both hydrographic networks, as the leaf-shaped [SOURCE: Page_315]

one has more ramifications compared to the rectilinear one. Such variations in the dendritic [SOURCE: Page_315]

aspect of networks might shape animal movement and thereby drive social and sexual [SOURCE: Page_315]

interactions among individuals (He et al., 2019). Focusing on dendritic network shapes, such as [SOURCE: Page_315]

riverine, Labonne et al. (2008) had already shown a strong correlation between population demo- [SOURCE: Page_315]

genetics and connectivity. In our case, stronger connectivity, resulting from higher ramifications, [SOURCE: Page_315]

might favour movement between reaches, and hence gene flow and genetic rescue following the [SOURCE: Page_315]

implementation of management strategies. In fact, using simple simulations, I could observe that [SOURCE: Page_315]

the hybridization dynamics varied based on movement probability. Higher movement threshold [SOURCE: Page_315]

probability led to faster gene flow. The same should be true if the network structure favours [SOURCE: Page_315]

movements through connectivity, and vice-versa. In addition, the potential increase in [SOURCE: Page_315]

connectivity could also explain the slight decrease observed in population size, as also observed [SOURCE: Page_315]

by Labonne et al. (2008). [SOURCE: Page_315]

 On the other hand, as mentioned in section 5.2.1, variations in temperatures among hydrographic [SOURCE: Page_315]

networks are to be expected. Indeed, while both networks have the same temperature model [SOURCE: Page_315]

and the same range of altitude [400 – 1400 metres], differences will be generated due to their [SOURCE: Page_315]

contrasting topology. The leaf-shaped network produces more reaches at higher altitudes. As [SOURCE: Page_315]

304 [SOURCE: Page_315]

Chapter 5. MEDITERRANEA: A decision-making tool to provide management guidelines [SOURCE: Page_316]

higher altitude is associated with lower temperature, this could be a sound explanation for the [SOURCE: Page_316]

differences observed. [SOURCE: Page_316]

In our simulations, the number of landscape feature parameters allowed to vary was highly reduced. [SOURCE: Page_316]

However, in reality, many landscape features of rivers can influence hybridization dynamics (Nathan et [SOURCE: Page_316]

al., 2019) and shapes spatial patterns of diversity (Altermatt, 2013). Consequently, the accumulation of [SOURCE: Page_316]

many differences, including shape, dendritic connectivity, size, habitat quality, etc., is likely to generate [SOURCE: Page_316]

ample differences in hybridization dynamics among rivers. Managers should consider this when planning [SOURCE: Page_316]

their conservation measures, as it can influence the efficiency of management strategies. [SOURCE: Page_316]

5.4.4   The importance of small-scale variations in temperatures [SOURCE: Page_316]

Our results indicated that winter temperatures strongly influence diversity dynamics and demography. [SOURCE: Page_316]

Considering warmer temperatures, most management strategies turned out unsuccessful, as the general [SOURCE: Page_316]

outcome was full hybridization, with medium to high introgression rates (except for SNF). Therefore, [SOURCE: Page_316]

knowledge of temperatures could represent good leverage for managers to guide their decision-making. [SOURCE: Page_316]

For instance, a threshold of mean winter temperature could be defined to decide when to consider [SOURCE: Page_316]

undertaking any measures at all. Indeed, based on the results from Folio et al. (2021), when the water [SOURCE: Page_316]

temperature is above 7.5°C, the maternal MED lineage loses its survival advantage. In such cases, it might [SOURCE: Page_316]

be better to do nothing in order to avoid a waste of time and energy, as management goals might be [SOURCE: Page_316]

impossible to reach. [SOURCE: Page_316]

In mountain hydrosystems, temperature variation is very heterogeneous at different spatial and temporal [SOURCE: Page_316]

scales (Brown & Hannah, 2008; Daigle et al., 2016). For instance, large temperature variations were [SOURCE: Page_316]

305 [SOURCE: Page_316]

5.4 Discussion [SOURCE: Page_317]

observed on the study sites where the empirical study on survival was conducted (Folio et al., 2021). [SOURCE: Page_317]

Indeed, the three rivers used were connected but presented contrasted thermal regimes within a few [SOURCE: Page_317]

kilometres only. This, in conjunction with temperature-based GxE interactions (Folio et al., 2021), could [SOURCE: Page_317]

explain some of the hybridization patterns that are observed in the Haute-Savoie region, and elsewhere. [SOURCE: Page_317]

For instance, some pure MED populations appear to have much greater fitness than some pure ATL [SOURCE: Page_317]

populations in some river stretches. In contrast, in other stretches, they are extremely vulnerable to [SOURCE: Page_317]

introgression and have been extirpated (Gil et al., 2016). Likewise, (Largiadèr & Scholl, 1996) failed to find [SOURCE: Page_317]

frequent hybrids in the Doubs population, and concluded to potent reproductive barriers at work there: [SOURCE: Page_317]

this could be related to cold temperature. [SOURCE: Page_317]

Therefore, management strategies should be implemented at small spatial scales in order to be adapted [SOURCE: Page_317]

the most to local conditions. For this to be possible, managers should consider long-term monitoring of [SOURCE: Page_317]

water temperature in some rivers of interest, in order to have adequate information to inform the model [SOURCE: Page_317]

and their decision-making. Alternatively, when temperature data are unavailable, a better temperature [SOURCE: Page_317]

model could benefit MEDITERRANEA. Indeed, the temperature contrasts observed cannot be summarized [SOURCE: Page_317]

by the altitude gradient only: geothermal influence, land cover as well as the distance to source or [SOURCE: Page_317]

exposure to wind and light, can also strongly condition water temperature dynamics. [SOURCE: Page_317]

Altogether, the combined effect of dendritic network shapes and climatic variations could interplay to [SOURCE: Page_317]

affect the patterns of intraspecific diversity observed in the Mediterranean area, as shown in other species [SOURCE: Page_317]

(e.g. Brauer et al., 2018). Hence, managers should always expect differences in management efficiency [SOURCE: Page_317]

based on local conditions, especially if they are involved in selection. Considering all these potential [SOURCE: Page_317]

factors influencing hybridization dynamics, and consequently management practices, I join others [SOURCE: Page_317]

(Allendorf et al., 2001; Bohling, 2016; Genovart, 2009) in the idea that management strategies should [SOURCE: Page_317]

always be context-specific. [SOURCE: Page_317]

## 6.1 DO ECO-EVOLUTIONARY MECHANISMS REALLY MATTER FOR MANAGEMENT?

6.1.1   Thesis objectives [SOURCE: Page_320]

Empirical studies realised during Jordi Gil’s PhD (Gil, 2015), uncovered some eco-evolutionary mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_320]

(EEMs) – female heterogamous preference (Gil et al., 2015) and GxE interactions on embryonic survival [SOURCE: Page_320]

(Folio et al., 2021) – that could have influenced hybridization dynamics in the Mediterranean area. On the [SOURCE: Page_320]

one hand, female heterogamous preference was hypothesised to have catalysed hybridization between [SOURCE: Page_320]

both lineages following secondary contact (Gil et al., 2015). This could explain why so many stable hybrid [SOURCE: Page_320]

zones are found in this area nowadays (Section 2.2.3, Figure 2 – 6). On the other hand, survival differences [SOURCE: Page_320]

at the embryonic stage resulting from the maternal genotype-by-temperature interaction could have [SOURCE: Page_320]

resulted in post-zygotic reproductive isolation in some environments (Folio et al., 2021). Associated with [SOURCE: Page_320]

small-scale spatial variations in temperature, this could explain the spatial heterogeneity observed in [SOURCE: Page_320]

introgression (Section 2.2.3). [SOURCE: Page_320]

However, there was no actual evidence that these mechanisms were actually influencing the evolution of [SOURCE: Page_320]

diversity in that context. Indeed, knowledge about the existence of non-random preference and GxE [SOURCE: Page_320]

interactions influencing survival of one life stage is insufficient to actually measure the impact of these [SOURCE: Page_320]

EEMs on hybridization and diversity dynamics, over both, short and long time scales. Therefore, the aim [SOURCE: Page_320]

of my PhD was to explore the actual impact and consequences of such EEMs in a realistic spatiotemporal [SOURCE: Page_320]

framework, in order to assess whether EEMs matter for management or not. To do so, I collected and [SOURCE: Page_320]

assembled (i) general knowledge on the brown trout life cycle as well as (ii) the specific knowledge [SOURCE: Page_320]

available on the MED/ATL hybridization complex, both ecological and evolutionary, to contribute to the [SOURCE: Page_320]

309 [SOURCE: Page_320]

6.1 Do eco-evolutionary mechanisms really matter for management? [SOURCE: Page_321]

creation of an agent-based demo-genetic modelling framework. This framework – MEDITERRANEA – [SOURCE: Page_321]

allowed me to consider: (i) the EEMs potentially influencing the evolution of intraspecific diversity, (ii) [SOURCE: Page_321]

their genetic bases, as well as (iii) variations in important environmental parameters. In addition, it [SOURCE: Page_321]

allowed simulating (iv) the effects of various management practices. Using MEDITERRANEA, I investigated [SOURCE: Page_321]

whether each of these mechanisms acted as we expected them to, based on the empirical findings. More [SOURCE: Page_321]

precisely, I aimed at measuring their impact, over short and long time scales, on various case studies to [SOURCE: Page_321]

consider how these mechanisms may interact with other ecological, genetic and demographic drivers, [SOURCE: Page_321]

including management practices. [SOURCE: Page_321]

6.1.2   EEMs do matter for the management of brown trout intraspecific diversity [SOURCE: Page_321]

In the present thesis, I demonstrated that some EEMs do really matter for the management of intraspecific [SOURCE: Page_321]

diversity in the MED/ATL hybridization complex. Indeed, the consideration, or not, of these EEMs in [SOURCE: Page_321]

MEDITERRANEA led to interesting and contrasted results regarding diversity dynamics and demography, [SOURCE: Page_321]

and even more so when considering management practices. [SOURCE: Page_321]

First, the results of Chapter 4 indicated that the consideration of some, but not all EEMs (documented in [SOURCE: Page_321]

the present case) changed the outcomes of diversity dynamics and demography. [SOURCE: Page_321]

 On one side, heterogamous mating preference barely influenced the evolution of diversity [SOURCE: Page_321]

dynamics, no matter the genetic contribution to preference that was implemented, nor the initial [SOURCE: Page_321]

structure of the population or any other variations. These results went against our initial [SOURCE: Page_321]

prediction that heterogamous preference might catalyse hybridization. While the lack of effect [SOURCE: Page_321]

could be explained by the way female preference is implemented in the model (Section 3.3.6), [SOURCE: Page_321]

310 [SOURCE: Page_321]

Chapter 6. General discussion [SOURCE: Page_322]

other studies on hybridization in salmonids have also pointed toward little effects of mating [SOURCE: Page_322]

preference (Nathan et al., 2019). [SOURCE: Page_322]

 On the contrary, GxE interactions on embryonic survival highly influenced diversity dynamics and [SOURCE: Page_322]

demography. The implementation of this EEM led to selection against ATL alleles inherited [SOURCE: Page_322]

maternally. In other words, carriers of MED maternal genes do have a major selective advantage [SOURCE: Page_322]

in cold environments, as suggested by Folio et al. (2021). The strength of the selection mainly [SOURCE: Page_322]

varied based on plausible variations in temperatures. In addition, in ATL populations, it led to a [SOURCE: Page_322]

decrease in population size due to the lack of recruitment resulting from low early life fitness. As [SOURCE: Page_322]

already discussed, similar studies on salmonids highlighted the importance of temperature-based [SOURCE: Page_322]

selection and fitness in driving hybridization dynamics (Nathan et al., 2019). [SOURCE: Page_322]

Second, the comparison of simulated eco-evolutionary scenarios to empirical data (for real case studies, [SOURCE: Page_322]

Section 4.4.7) gave some insight into the mechanisms that may have shaped patterns of diversity in the [SOURCE: Page_322]

Mediterranean area. Overall, results were closer to reality when the effect of GxE interactions over [SOURCE: Page_322]

embryonic survival was implemented. However, more validation – combining simulation and empirical [SOURCE: Page_322]

data – is still required to actually confirm that it has been this uncovered mechanism that actually shaped [SOURCE: Page_322]

these past patterns of diversity. In addition, while this mechanism could explain qualitatively some of the [SOURCE: Page_322]

variations observed; it is not sufficient to predict quantitatively the actual temporal changes in diversity. [SOURCE: Page_322]

Many additional EEMs may come into play and more knowledge will be required to improve our [SOURCE: Page_322]

understanding of hybridization. Nevertheless, one thing is certain, some EEMs must have played a [SOURCE: Page_322]

structuring role in the evolution of diversity in our various case studies, considering that the neutral [SOURCE: Page_322]

scenario is not sufficient to predict the patterns observed in reality. [SOURCE: Page_322]

311 [SOURCE: Page_322]

6.1 Do eco-evolutionary mechanisms really matter for management? [SOURCE: Page_323]

Finally, the outcome of management practices varied based on the eco-evolutionary scenario [SOURCE: Page_323]

implemented, suggesting that both interplay to influence the evolution of diversity. More precisely, it is [SOURCE: Page_323]

the consideration, or not, of GxE interactions that lead to these differences. For instance, patterns of [SOURCE: Page_323]

evolutionary rescue follow the implementation of management practices such as translocation of native [SOURCE: Page_323]

individuals. Furthermore, in Chapter 5, I demonstrated that the efficiency of management practices could [SOURCE: Page_323]

highly vary based on variations in important ecological factors – in our case temperature – when such GxE [SOURCE: Page_323]

interactions are implemented. [SOURCE: Page_323]

Altogether, the results of the present thesis point to the importance of EEMs involved in natural selection, [SOURCE: Page_323]

and the underlying selective pressure, for the management of intraspecific diversity. Indeed, genotype- [SOURCE: Page_323]

by-environment interactions, which lead to various fitness differences in different environments, appear [SOURCE: Page_323]

to be structuring diversity dynamics. It is a good avenue to explain the spatial variation observed in [SOURCE: Page_323]

introgression dynamics in various case studies. It also interacts with management practices to influence [SOURCE: Page_323]

their outcome and efficiency. Therefore, such mechanisms can no longer be discarded in the fields of [SOURCE: Page_323]

wildlife management and conservation science. [SOURCE: Page_323]

6.1.3   General importance of such EEMs for management [SOURCE: Page_323]

Since reproduction and survival of genotypes – parental gene pool or hybrids – drive hybridization [SOURCE: Page_323]

dynamics, the consideration of any EEMs potentially influencing these two aspects of fitness should be [SOURCE: Page_323]

compulsory to the management of intraspecific diversity. On the one hand, reproduction will create the [SOURCE: Page_323]

diversity available for selection to act on. While Nathan et al. (2019), and the present study have [SOURCE: Page_323]

evidenced that mating preference in Salmonids appears to barely influence hybridization dynamics, other [SOURCE: Page_323]

studies in mammals and plants indicate that mating system plays a structuring role in the evolution of [SOURCE: Page_323]

312 [SOURCE: Page_323]

Chapter 6. General discussion [SOURCE: Page_324]

diversity (Fredrickson & Hedrick, 2006; Godineau et al., 2021; Santostasi, Bauduin, et al., 2020; Santostasi, [SOURCE: Page_324]

Ciucci, et al., 2020; Soularue & Kremer, 2014). Therefore, exploring the evolution of traits considering the [SOURCE: Page_324]

dynamic aspects of mating systems, especially when sexual preference and competition over mating [SOURCE: Page_324]

partners occur remains essential in any case. For instance, sexual selection can determine the [SOURCE: Page_324]

reproductive success of immigrants in a population (Labonne & Hendry, 2010). This can influence the [SOURCE: Page_324]

strength and direction of demo-genetic consequences of dispersal, potentially resulting in various [SOURCE: Page_324]

outcomes, from demographic and evolutionary rescue (Carlson et al., 2014; Fitzpatrick et al., 2020) to [SOURCE: Page_324]

gene swamping (Allendorf et al., 2001; Haldane & Ford, 1956; Rutherford et al., 2019); and therefore have [SOURCE: Page_324]

large consequences for management practices. [SOURCE: Page_324]

On the other hand, many studies corroborate our findings and demonstrate the importance of survival [SOURCE: Page_324]

differences in driving hybridization dynamics (Castellani et al., 2015; Nathan et al., 2019; Santostasi, [SOURCE: Page_324]

Bauduin, et al., 2020). Indeed, the survival of each genotype will condition the genotypic and phenotypic [SOURCE: Page_324]

composition of the population, thereby influencing the evolution of diversity. Knowledge of the ecological [SOURCE: Page_324]

factors that may cause such survival differences – or more generally fitness differences – through GxE [SOURCE: Page_324]

interactions, could represent a leverage for managers. Given the importance of such mechanisms, more [SOURCE: Page_324]

research should focus on survival differences between parental and hybrid individuals in various [SOURCE: Page_324]

environments to understand to what extent and in which environmental conditions these mechanisms [SOURCE: Page_324]

are influencing hybridization dynamics. [SOURCE: Page_324]

Indeed, in other taxa, genotype-by-environment interactions may also lead to fitness variations among [SOURCE: Page_324]

individuals and dictate the evolutionary outcome of hybridization. This has been observed at intra and [SOURCE: Page_324]

interspecific levels (Campbell & Waser, 2001; Fraser et al., 2008; Genovart, 2009; Janes & Hamilton, 2017) [SOURCE: Page_324]

with studies pointing toward the effects of environmental factors, such as temperature (Drury & Lirman, [SOURCE: Page_324]

313 [SOURCE: Page_324]

6.2 DG-ABM: A valuable tool in the context of hybridization [SOURCE: Page_325]

2021; Krehenwinkel & Tautz, 2013; Nathan et al., 2019), pH (Fraser et al., 2008), or eutrophication [SOURCE: Page_325]

(Vonlanthen et al., 2012) on the fitness of hybrid complexes. A growing number of studies are highlighting [SOURCE: Page_325]

the need to identify environmental and ecological factors involved in hybridization dynamics (Genovart, [SOURCE: Page_325]

2009; Lindtke et al., 2014; M. K. Schwartz et al., 2004), specifically when a combination of environmental [SOURCE: Page_325]

factors will influence the maintenance of reproductive barriers between and within species (Janes & [SOURCE: Page_325]

Hamilton, 2017). As in our case study, spatial—or even temporal—variations of such factors could be the [SOURCE: Page_325]

key to building future management strategies of intraspecific diversity for admixed gene pools. Indeed, [SOURCE: Page_325]

they will be able to address simultaneously several objectives, such as conserving native diversity in some [SOURCE: Page_325]

areas (Bohling, 2016; Janes & Hamilton, 2017) and maximizing evolutionary potential as a whole in others, [SOURCE: Page_325]

in order to cope with future environmental variation (Nuismer & Gandon, 2008). [SOURCE: Page_325]

Overall, knowledge about the ecological and demographic factors influencing the outcome of [SOURCE: Page_325]

hybridization is essential to develop meaningful management and conservation measures, especially [SOURCE: Page_325]

when hybridization poses a threat to diversity (Bohling, 2016). [SOURCE: Page_325]

## 6.2 DG-ABM: A VALUABLE TOOL IN THE CONTEXT OF HYBRIDIZATION

6.2.1   A framework to understand hybridization dynamics [SOURCE: Page_325]

Our modelling framework, MEDITERRANEA, was a valuable tool to investigate and understand the [SOURCE: Page_325]

evolution of diversity and demography under various eco-evolutionary and management scenarios. Such [SOURCE: Page_325]

DG-ABMs have already been used in similar ways in other studies of hybridization (Castellani et al., 2015; [SOURCE: Page_325]

Nathan et al., 2019; Santostasi, Bauduin, et al., 2020) and have proved to be useful for both scientists and [SOURCE: Page_325]

managers. While the interest of such models is not to give quantitative predictions, their main purpose is [SOURCE: Page_325]

to provide some qualitative insight into the expected outcomes of alternative eco-evolutionary and [SOURCE: Page_325]

314 [SOURCE: Page_325]

Chapter 6. General discussion [SOURCE: Page_326]

management scenarios. Such models can integrate various sources of knowledge, either at the [SOURCE: Page_326]

mechanism level or by using pattern-oriented modelling. Both of these can be provided by various parties [SOURCE: Page_326]

(scientists, managers or users…). [SOURCE: Page_326]

DG-ABMs provide a complete modelling framework to study hybridization dynamics, as they combine [SOURCE: Page_326]

properties of ecological and genetic models. By realistically simulating interactions between individuals, [SOURCE: Page_326]

such models give rise to unique demographic and genetic processes. It allows the implementation of [SOURCE: Page_326]

different sub-models based on the genetic or phenotypic characteristics of individuals. Therefore, many [SOURCE: Page_326]

fitness and behavioural differences between parental gene pools, but also hybrid individuals can be [SOURCE: Page_326]

implemented. Demographic parameters can also be linked to environmental variables, allowing one to [SOURCE: Page_326]

predict hybridization dynamics under fluctuating environmental conditions. Overall, DG-ABM allowed us [SOURCE: Page_326]

to simulate complex ecological and evolutionary processes, which are not easily empirically measured [SOURCE: Page_326]

(Nathan et al., 2019). [SOURCE: Page_326]

6.2.2    A tool to assess management strategies in an adaptive management loop [SOURCE: Page_326]

A key feature of DG-ABMs, described in section 1.6.3, is their capacity to model the impacts of [SOURCE: Page_326]

management practices, or more generally human-induced changes on ecological dynamics – i.e. [SOURCE: Page_326]

individuals, the environment and their interactions. In a growing context of uncertainties, DG-ABMs could [SOURCE: Page_326]

be used to inform decision-making and contribute to management and conservation policies. Indeed, our [SOURCE: Page_326]

results and others (Ayllón, Nicola, et al., 2019; Ayllón, Railsback, et al., 2018; Piou et al., 2015; Santostasi, [SOURCE: Page_326]

Bauduin, et al., 2020; H.-Y. Wang et al., 2017; H.-Y. Wang & Höök, 2009) demonstrate that they are [SOURCE: Page_326]

efficient prospective tools to assess the impact of human induced-changes on ecological and evolutionary [SOURCE: Page_326]

dynamics. [SOURCE: Page_326]

315 [SOURCE: Page_326]

6.2 DG-ABM: A valuable tool in the context of hybridization [SOURCE: Page_327]

However, these types of models rely on a large number of assumptions and parameters, which can be [SOURCE: Page_327]

difficult to estimate and provide. For instance, in our MED/ATL case study, only a few short-term [SOURCE: Page_327]

monitoring, like in the Borne River and the Chevenne Creek are available. Therefore, I suggest that [SOURCE: Page_327]

scientists and managers work together, in an adaptive management loop in order to (i) gather adequate [SOURCE: Page_327]

data to inform the model; (ii) use the model to guide managers' decisions on the “best” management [SOURCE: Page_327]

strategy to implement; (iii) implement that strategy and continue the monitoring; (iv) use these data to [SOURCE: Page_327]

validate (or not) the model (Figure 6 – 1). Going through this adaptive management loop in detail: [SOURCE: Page_327]

 First, scientists and managers should decide on the hydrographic network to implement in [SOURCE: Page_327]

MEDITERRANEA based on the data available – demographic and genetic parameters but also [SOURCE: Page_327]

water temperature. Indeed, the more the model will be calibrated with parameters specific to the [SOURCE: Page_327]

population, the more reliable the management recommendation will be. Regular monitoring [SOURCE: Page_327]

should then be implemented to gather additional data and draw a proper timeline. The same type [SOURCE: Page_327]

of data should always be collected to ease their implementation in the model. [SOURCE: Page_327]

 The second step is to use the model to inform and guide managers’ decisions. To do so the [SOURCE: Page_327]

available data has to be integrated into the model, via the initial data file and the initial [SOURCE: Page_327]

parameters settings (Section 3.5.1 & 3.5.2). Then after defining the management goal and the [SOURCE: Page_327]

various strategies to evaluate using the model, the simulations can be run. The predictions [SOURCE: Page_327]

obtained will then help managers to decide which management strategy is the best one regarding [SOURCE: Page_327]

the predefined management goal. [SOURCE: Page_327]

 The third step is to implement the chosen management strategy and keep on monitoring the [SOURCE: Page_327]

population during and after, in order to gather data that can be used to verify the model [SOURCE: Page_327]

predictions. [SOURCE: Page_327]

316 [SOURCE: Page_327]

Chapter 6. General discussion [SOURCE: Page_328]

 Finally, these data can be used to (i) validate (or not) the model and/or (ii) calibrate some [SOURCE: Page_328]

processes in order to further improve the model. [SOURCE: Page_328]

Figure 6—1: MEDITERRANEA as a decision-making tool for managers: representation of the adaptive management loop to inform management strategies. [SOURCE: Page_328]

317 [SOURCE: Page_328]

6.2 DG-ABM: A valuable tool in the context of hybridization [SOURCE: Page_329]

Since scientific studies are not often considered by resource managers (Hart & Calhoun, 2010; Sutherland [SOURCE: Page_329]

et al., 2004); the development of such collaborative research is important to bring together management [SOURCE: Page_329]

and scientific methods (Caudron, Vigier, et al., 2012). [SOURCE: Page_329]

6.2.3   A tool to guide future empirical studies [SOURCE: Page_329]

DG-ABMs can also be used to identify the EEMs that need to be empirically investigated further to more [SOURCE: Page_329]

accurately predict hybridization dynamics (Nathan et al., 2019), and consequently the outcomes of [SOURCE: Page_329]

management strategies (Santostasi, Bauduin, et al., 2020). Defining and assessing these EEM will further [SOURCE: Page_329]

help to improve the model, as the empirical data may be used to validate and calibrate the model (Figure [SOURCE: Page_329]

6 – 1, step 1), exactly as suggested in the adaptive management loop. [SOURCE: Page_329]

As mentioned in Chapter 1 (Section 2.4.3), other factors have been hypothesised to contribute to the [SOURCE: Page_329]

evolution of diversity in the MED/ATL hybridization complex. MEDITERRANEA could be used to explore [SOURCE: Page_329]

whether these factors actually play a role in diversity dynamics and to what extent. Indeed, as done here [SOURCE: Page_329]

for the two demonstrated mechanisms, we could project how each mechanism will interact with all the [SOURCE: Page_329]

other ecological, genetic, and demographic drivers to influence the evolution of diversity and [SOURCE: Page_329]

demography. [SOURCE: Page_329]

 For instance, the model could be easily improved to test more differences in survival [SOURCE: Page_329]

implemented at various life stages up to adulthood (as indicated in Gil, 2015). Indeed, as done for [SOURCE: Page_329]

embryonic survival, an additional mortality process could be implemented at a given age – for [SOURCE: Page_329]

example, 2 years old when individuals become mature. The user will then be able to test out [SOURCE: Page_329]

different curves of survival based on individual introgression and evaluate whether such [SOURCE: Page_329]

differences in fitness on a given life stage play a role in structuring diversity dynamics. Considering [SOURCE: Page_329]

318 [SOURCE: Page_329]

Chapter 6. General discussion [SOURCE: Page_330]

that survival differences applied at the embryonic stage only had a large impact on diversity [SOURCE: Page_330]

dynamics and demography, we can imagine that such differences throughout life could massively [SOURCE: Page_330]

matter to evolution. [SOURCE: Page_330]

 Regarding the differences in sensitivity to fishing hypothesised, the model allows for the [SOURCE: Page_330]

implementation of selective fishing based on individual introgression (Section 3.4.2). This [SOURCE: Page_330]

intervention could be used to test whether different sensitivity to fishing could affect diversity [SOURCE: Page_330]

dynamics significantly. The user could consider different catchability of individuals based on their [SOURCE: Page_330]

origin - ATL, HYB or MED to evaluate how this may affect diversity dynamics. [SOURCE: Page_330]

 Finally, the model could also be used to evaluate whether differential female choices for [SOURCE: Page_330]

spawning habitat do have an impact on diversity dynamics in case of a flood event. With some [SOURCE: Page_330]

modifications, we could use the viability analyses intervention – which simulates extreme [SOURCE: Page_330]

mortality events (Section 3.4.3) – to simulate the impact of a flood on female nests, considering [SOURCE: Page_330]

that those of MED females might be less impacted due to habitat choice diversification compared [SOURCE: Page_330]

to those of ATL females (Champigneulle et al., 2003). The user could here simply implement [SOURCE: Page_330]

different mortality rates for offspring based on maternal introgression. Trying to test this, in [SOURCE: Page_330]

natura, would be time-consuming and complicated, as flood events are unpredictable. Therefore, [SOURCE: Page_330]

the model could provide valuable information on whether experimental work is really needed [SOURCE: Page_330]

here or not. [SOURCE: Page_330]

## 6.3 POINTING AT THE IMPORTANCE OF MATERNAL EFFECTS

The present results combined with empirical knowledge on the MED/ATL hybridization complex point to [SOURCE: Page_330]

the importance of maternal effects on the evolution of diversity. For instance, the maternal genotype-by- [SOURCE: Page_330]

319 [SOURCE: Page_330]

6.3 Pointing at the importance of maternal effects [SOURCE: Page_331]

temperature interaction on survival plays a structuring role in diversity dynamics. In addition, other [SOURCE: Page_331]

studies on brown trout in the Atlantic zone have also highlighted the importance of maternal effects, [SOURCE: Page_331]

especially during the earlier phases of development (Gauthey et al., 2017; Lelong, 2006; Lelong et al., [SOURCE: Page_331]

2008; Régnier, Bolliet, et al., 2012; Régnier, Labonne, et al., 2012). Therefore, more research on the impact [SOURCE: Page_331]

of maternal lineages should be conducted in the MED/ATL hybridization complex, as it may have the [SOURCE: Page_331]

potential to explain many of the variations in diversity observed in the Mediterranean area. [SOURCE: Page_331]

6.3.1    Maternal ascendance in hybrid zone [SOURCE: Page_331]

The present results combined with empirical findings (Appendix F; Folio et al., 2021) may explain the [SOURCE: Page_331]

predominance of the native MED maternal ascendance in hybrid zones. In the Chevenne creek for instance [SOURCE: Page_331]

– one of the rivers studied here – mitochondrial lineage exclusively points to the MED lineage (Gil et al., [SOURCE: Page_331]

2016). Such a predominance of the native maternal ascendance is also found in other areas (Poteaux, [SOURCE: Page_331]

Bonhomme, et al., 1998) and in other hybrid zones in Salmo trutta for a different lineage (Pujolar, Lucarda, [SOURCE: Page_331]

et al., 2011; Pujolar, Vincenzi, et al., 2011) or in other species (Bonnet et al., 2017; M. K. Schwartz et al., [SOURCE: Page_331]

2004; Taillebois et al., 2020). Combined with the results on GxE interactions (Folio et al., 2021), this [SOURCE: Page_331]

indicates that maternal effects (genetic and non-genetic) may have a preponderant role in hybridization [SOURCE: Page_331]

dynamics, notably because they are especially present during the first stages of life (R. S. Burton et al., [SOURCE: Page_331]

2006; T. Burton et al., 2020; Giesing et al., 2011; Régnier, Bolliet, et al., 2012; Régnier, Labonne, et al., [SOURCE: Page_331]

2012; Shu et al., 2016). In particular, cold adaptation – suggested by Folio et al. (2021) – is partly mediated [SOURCE: Page_331]

through physiological mechanisms, which often involve mitochondrial functions that are hence linked to [SOURCE: Page_331]

maternal ascendancy (White et al., 2012) and can differ between lineages (Kavanagh et al., 2010). [SOURCE: Page_331]

320 [SOURCE: Page_331]

Chapter 6. General discussion [SOURCE: Page_332]

6.3.2   Insight from researches on the ATL trout [SOURCE: Page_332]

Science on ATL trout – performed in the Atlantic zone – has provided considerable knowledge on brown [SOURCE: Page_332]

trout ecology and evidenced the important role of maternal effects. Among other things, it has uncovered [SOURCE: Page_332]

many parts of the life cycle where ample phenotypic and genetic variation occur in relation to maternal [SOURCE: Page_332]

effects. This led us to think that the same sorts of variations could be found for MED trout. In addition, [SOURCE: Page_332]

there is also reason to believe that the mean and variance for each lineage might be different due to their [SOURCE: Page_332]

divergent evolutionary history – allopatric evolution for 0.5 to 2 million years (Bernatchez, 2001). There [SOURCE: Page_332]

are therefore a lot of opportunities to build some new research directions on MED trout to improve our [SOURCE: Page_332]

understanding of hybridization dynamics and fitness differences in hybridized areas. [SOURCE: Page_332]

Emergence timing and competition.                 Research on ATL brown trout has evidenced a large [SOURCE: Page_332]

amount of individual variability in energetic status among emerging fry (Lelong et al., 2008); which might [SOURCE: Page_332]

influence emergence behaviour and timing (Lelong et al., 2008; Régnier, Labonne, et al., 2012). Indeed, a [SOURCE: Page_332]

large variance in emergence dates of ATL trout fry was observed among, but also within female nests [SOURCE: Page_332]

(Régnier, Labonne, et al., 2012). Furthermore, Régnier, Bolliet, et al. (2012) indicate that within clutch [SOURCE: Page_332]

variance of metabolic and yolk consumption rates differ between females, which can lead to [SOURCE: Page_332]

developmental variations. For instance, offspring from larger eggs have lower metabolic costs and higher [SOURCE: Page_332]

yolk conversion efficiencies; and are therefore at an advantage. [SOURCE: Page_332]

It was hypothesised that emergence could result from a trade-off between energy stores and [SOURCE: Page_332]

developmental stage (Lelong et al., 2008) and that within nest variation was related to increased within [SOURCE: Page_332]

clutch variability in metabolic rate (Régnier, Labonne, et al., 2012). In addition, variable investment in egg [SOURCE: Page_332]

size can influence the emergence duration of female nests (Régnier, Labonne, et al., 2012), and therefore [SOURCE: Page_332]

modulate the level of sibling competition. Indeed, in salmonids, the synchronization of emergence timing [SOURCE: Page_332]

can result in intense competition for resources among emerging fry (Ojanguren et al., 1996). [SOURCE: Page_332]

321 [SOURCE: Page_332]

6.3 Pointing at the importance of maternal effects [SOURCE: Page_333]

In the MED/ATL hybridization complex, lineage-related differences in such maternal effects could have [SOURCE: Page_333]

large consequences on diversity dynamics, through offspring competition for territories and resources [SOURCE: Page_333]

during the critical post-emergence period. For instance, if the mean and variance of emergence timing [SOURCE: Page_333]

among but also within clutches vary between lineages – for any of the reasons mentioned above – then [SOURCE: Page_333]

offspring from the lineage that can adjust emergence timing might be at an advantage. Indeed, such [SOURCE: Page_333]

competition will result in soft selection (sensu Wallace, 1975 in Bell et al., 2021) when territories are [SOURCE: Page_333]

limited. Therefore, reduced competition at emergence within one lineage should favour the recruitment [SOURCE: Page_333]

of that genotype to the next generation. [SOURCE: Page_333]

Morphology and dispersal.                Dispersal and larvae morphology can also be influenced by [SOURCE: Page_333]

maternal life history traits (Lelong, 2006). During her PhD, Amandine Lelong showed that populations [SOURCE: Page_333]

living in different environments developed different strategies of emergence, but also differed with regard [SOURCE: Page_333]

to post-emergence dispersal. Some individuals were more likely to perform downstream migration [SOURCE: Page_333]

compared to others, and this strategy was often related to the emergence strategy, in order to reduce [SOURCE: Page_333]

competition and increase survival. In our case study, lineage-related differences in post-emergence [SOURCE: Page_333]

dispersal could play an important role in hybridization given that movement appears to highly influence [SOURCE: Page_333]

diversity dynamics in the model. [SOURCE: Page_333]

Reproductive investment.                 In different ATL populations, differences in reproductive [SOURCE: Page_333]

investment conditional on environmental stochasticity have been observed (Gauthey et al., 2015). For [SOURCE: Page_333]

instance, only individuals from one population – usually experiencing a more stochastic environment – [SOURCE: Page_333]

adapted their reproductive investment based on water flow variations. This study highlighted that [SOURCE: Page_333]

mechanisms to cope with environmental stochasticity might differ between populations of the same [SOURCE: Page_333]

species. In our context, this suggests that such differences might exist between the MED and the ATL [SOURCE: Page_333]

322 [SOURCE: Page_333]

Chapter 6. General discussion [SOURCE: Page_334]

lineages. Indeed, while the MED lineage evolved in highly stochastic environmental conditions, the ATL [SOURCE: Page_334]

lineage came from domesticated strains experiencing buffered conditions. [SOURCE: Page_334]

Egg size differences.            Data from Folio et al. (2021) show different mean egg sizes based on [SOURCE: Page_334]

maternal genotype. In their sample, a majority of MED females produced bigger eggs - but not the biggest, [SOURCE: Page_334]

while ATL females produced either the biggest or the smallest eggs (Appendix G). Such a difference could [SOURCE: Page_334]

modulate lineage contribution to population growth. Indeed, since egg size is positively correlated to [SOURCE: Page_334]

female body size in salmonids, as is fecundity (Lobon-Cervia et al., 1997): Large females lay large and [SOURCE: Page_334]

numerous eggs. However, for the ATL lineage, studies have indicated that “bigger is not always better” [SOURCE: Page_334]

(Régnier et al., 2013) since larger eggs show lower survival (Folio et al., 2021; Régnier et al., 2013). In [SOURCE: Page_334]

addition, given the survival difference between the MED and the ATL lineages at cold temperatures (Folio [SOURCE: Page_334]

et al., 2021), MED females may massively contribute to population growth in some environments [SOURCE: Page_334]

compared to ATL females. [SOURCE: Page_334]

Habitat spawning choice.         Various individual strategies have been evidenced for habitat spawning [SOURCE: Page_334]

choice and nest building in the ATL trout based on female body size (Gauthey et al., 2017). This study also [SOURCE: Page_334]

indicated that these different strategies could have large consequences on scouring risks, which was the [SOURCE: Page_334]

main driver of egg mortality (75%). Nest burial depth and shear stress force above the nest were the main [SOURCE: Page_334]

drivers of scouring. Deeper nests and higher shear force stress reduced scouring risks. Considering the [SOURCE: Page_334]

extremely high mortality rate related to scouring, strategies to reduce scouring risk could be highly [SOURCE: Page_334]

beneficial for females to increase their reproductive success (Gauthey et al., 2017; Montgomery et al., [SOURCE: Page_334]

1996). Indeed, spawning habitats are known to further improve offspring fitness (Armstrong et al., 2003; [SOURCE: Page_334]

DeVries, 1997; Gauthey et al., 2017; Riedl & Peter, 2013). Therefore, Gauthey et al. (2017) suggested that [SOURCE: Page_334]

traits and tactics that reduce nest scouring should be under strong selection. If that is also the case in the [SOURCE: Page_334]

323 [SOURCE: Page_334]

6.4 Native conservation vs hybrids [SOURCE: Page_335]

Mediterranean area, such differences, in addition to the potential impact of spawning habitat [SOURCE: Page_335]

diversification (Section 2.4.3), could result in strong selection in favour of the MED maternal lineage. [SOURCE: Page_335]

If such differences exist between lineages in the MED/ATL hybridization complex, one may expect the [SOURCE: Page_335]

MED maternal lineage to be at a reproductive advantage given that mitochondrial DNA mainly points to [SOURCE: Page_335]

the MED lineage in hybrid zones (Expertise Scimabio Interface). Such an advantage for the MED lineage [SOURCE: Page_335]

may alter the genetic structure of the population by strongly favouring MED maternal ascendancy, as [SOURCE: Page_335]

more offspring from MED females might be recruited; and even more so, in cold environments considering [SOURCE: Page_335]

the GxE interactions uncovered in Folio et al. (2021). [SOURCE: Page_335]

## 6.4 NATIVE CONSERVATION VS HYBRIDS

6.4.1   Native conservation [SOURCE: Page_335]

In the recent past, the terms “hybrid”, “invasive” and “non-native” have mainly been used negatively and [SOURCE: Page_335]

regarded with suspicion by wildlife conservationists and managers. For instance, the process of species [SOURCE: Page_335]

interbreeding with each other, although not new, is often regarded as a problem rather than a normal [SOURCE: Page_335]

evolutionary process. In Europe, hybridization between native and domesticated individuals (often from [SOURCE: Page_335]

different lineages) is considered one of the most serious threats to native diversity conservation in brown [SOURCE: Page_335]

trout (Ferguson, 2007; Laikre et al., 1999). Therefore, in the MED/ATL hybridization complex, managers [SOURCE: Page_335]

have expressed the will to conserve the native MED lineage. Management practices implemented so far [SOURCE: Page_335]

are aimed at the conservation, restoration and introduction of MED populations in areas where an ATL [SOURCE: Page_335]

population threatened a nearby MED population and/or where some hybridization has occurred. There [SOURCE: Page_335]

are multiple reasons to explain this choice: [SOURCE: Page_335]

324 [SOURCE: Page_335]

Chapter 6. General discussion [SOURCE: Page_336]

 First, the native diversity of this species has a considerable socio-economic value (Laikre et al., [SOURCE: Page_336]

1999). For instance, the native MED lineage has a patrimonial value to anglers and fisheries [SOURCE: Page_336]

managers, since MED trout are phenotypically different from ATL and HYB trout. Experienced [SOURCE: Page_336]

anglers can often differentiate between the two lineages and their hybrids. [SOURCE: Page_336]

 Second, the MED lineage appears to show more diversity compared to the ATL lineage, specifically [SOURCE: Page_336]

considering that domesticated individuals were introduced. Indeed, some microsatellites markers [SOURCE: Page_336]

– used on the Borne River for instance – indicate that allelic diversity is higher for the MED lineage [SOURCE: Page_336]

(in total on two markers: 11 alleles for the MED lineage versus 5 alleles for the ATL lineage, [SOURCE: Page_336]

Caudron et al., 2009). Also, more generally, the MED lineage appears to show a higher genetic [SOURCE: Page_336]

diversity compared to the other lineages (Suárez et al., 2001). [SOURCE: Page_336]

 Finally, it has been hypothesised that MED diversity might be associated with local adaptation [SOURCE: Page_336]

since the MED lineage has evolved in the Mediterranean area. In other contexts, there has been [SOURCE: Page_336]

evidence that local adaptation is associated with brown trout intraspecific diversity (Pakkasmaa [SOURCE: Page_336]

& Piironen, 2001; Ryman, 1991). In addition, in the Northern French Alps, many native MED [SOURCE: Page_336]

populations are found in typical high-altitude rivers and streams characterised by harsh [SOURCE: Page_336]

environments for the brown trout species. In brown trout, populations from different [SOURCE: Page_336]

environments – more or less stochastic – show different abilities to adapt to an increase in [SOURCE: Page_336]

stochastic conditions (Gauthey et al., 2015). Therefore, these native populations may have a good [SOURCE: Page_336]

ability to adapt to various conditions and are therefore important to conserve, especially in the [SOURCE: Page_336]

context of climate change. Indeed, these harsh conditions (high stochasticity, frequent floods, [SOURCE: Page_336]

high human disturbances: fragmentation, pollution, exploitation…) might become more and more [SOURCE: Page_336]

frequent with climate change, and not just in high-altitude rivers. [SOURCE: Page_336]

325 [SOURCE: Page_336]

6.4 Native conservation vs hybrids [SOURCE: Page_337]

It seems therefore important to maintain the genetic integrity of MED populations and more generally [SOURCE: Page_337]

that of the various lineages of brown trout (Laikre et al., 1999; Ryman et al., 1995; Taylor et al., 2015; [SOURCE: Page_337]

Utter, 2004) to ensure the long term-evolutionary potential of the species. Indeed, high gene flow may [SOURCE: Page_337]

reduce the genetic diversity between populations and consequently hinder potential adaptive [SOURCE: Page_337]

differentiation, which can result in extinction by hybridization (Rhymer & Simberloff, 1996). However, in [SOURCE: Page_337]

the MED/ATL hybridization complex, there is no evidence of adaptive divergence between the lineages. [SOURCE: Page_337]

6.4.2   Hybrids in the Mediterranean area [SOURCE: Page_337]

While it is certain that the ATL lineage has been (domestication, Caudron & Champigneulle, 2002) and still [SOURCE: Page_337]

is (in cold environments at least, Folio et al., 2021) at a disadvantage compared to the MED lineage. There [SOURCE: Page_337]

is little evidence to assert that HYB individuals found in wild populations fare less well compared to native [SOURCE: Page_337]

MED individuals. On the contrary, data suggest that HYB individuals might have equal or even better [SOURCE: Page_337]

fitness compared to MED individuals. [SOURCE: Page_337]

 First, many populations are fully hybridized in the Mediterranean area (Section 2.2.3, Figure 2 – [SOURCE: Page_337]

6) and no negative impact on population dynamics has been reported. In such contexts, adaptive [SOURCE: Page_337]

introgression may have occurred (De La Torre et al., 2014; Fitzpatrick et al., 2019; Whitney et al., [SOURCE: Page_337]

2015) and adaptive variation may be more resistant to introgression (Fitzpatrick et al., 2020), [SOURCE: Page_337]

leading to highly fit hybrids. Indeed, introgression along the genome has been shown to be [SOURCE: Page_337]

variable but non-random, suggesting that some loci might be more or less sensitive to gene flow [SOURCE: Page_337]

(Ozerov et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_337]

 Second, considering the importance of maternal effects discussed above (Section 6.3.1), and the [SOURCE: Page_337]

maternal genotype-by-temperature interaction uncovered (Folio et al., 2021); HYB individuals [SOURCE: Page_337]

326 [SOURCE: Page_337]

Chapter 6. General discussion [SOURCE: Page_338]

from the MED maternal ascendance might be equally fit compared to pure MED individuals. [SOURCE: Page_338]

Indeed, data points toward the MED mitochondrial lineage in hybridized populations (Scimabio [SOURCE: Page_338]

Interface Expertise). [SOURCE: Page_338]

 Furthermore, one has to keep in mind that most knowledge and lineage-based relationship were [SOURCE: Page_338]

measured on wild populations present in this environment for possibly multiple generations. [SOURCE: Page_338]

Therefore, HYB individuals – but also MED and ATL – do not represent what could have been [SOURCE: Page_338]

observed following the first secondary contact, which happened in some cases more than a [SOURCE: Page_338]

century ago. These individuals have actually undergone decades of selection and are hence the [SOURCE: Page_338]

result of post-invasion evolution (The “after” hypothesis, (Reznick, Losos, et al., 2019)). In invasion [SOURCE: Page_338]

research, the after hypothesis was introduced to oppose the precedent idea that invasion success [SOURCE: Page_338]

was determined by the initial characteristics of organisms (invaders and natives) and/or [SOURCE: Page_338]

environments (the “before” hypothesis, (Reznick, Losos, et al., 2019)). [SOURCE: Page_338]

In the Mediterranean area, hybrid individuals might actually be associated with ample genetic variation [SOURCE: Page_338]

and potentially be adapted to various environmental conditions. Therefore, in addition to the [SOURCE: Page_338]

conservation of the MED lineage in certain environments, managers should also account for this variation [SOURCE: Page_338]

as it could represent a resilience mechanism against environmental change (C. N. Cook & Sgrò, 2018; Jump [SOURCE: Page_338]

et al., 2009; López-Pujol et al., 2012; Violle et al., 2012). Indeed, reliance on the idea that native or local [SOURCE: Page_338]

individuals are more adapted to local environments can be detrimental to management practices, [SOURCE: Page_338]

especially when it reduces genetic diversity (Broadhurst et al., 2008; Kronenberger et al., 2018). This has [SOURCE: Page_338]

already been the case in the Mediterranean area where local genetic diversity was reduced following [SOURCE: Page_338]

stocking with the local strain (Leitwein et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_338]

327 [SOURCE: Page_338]

## 6.5 WHAT IS A HYBRID?

The definition associated with the term “hybrid” remains obscure in our context. For instance, multiple [SOURCE: Page_339]

metrics have been used to differentiate between individuals and categorise them. On the one hand, [SOURCE: Page_339]

hybrids can be defined based on their degree of admixture using some genetic markers. However, genetic [SOURCE: Page_339]

monitoring remains expensive and the number of genetic markers used usually varies from one study to [SOURCE: Page_339]

another. On the other hand, as pigmentation can reflect admixture in salmonids (Aparicio et al., 2005; [SOURCE: Page_339]

Kocabaş et al., 2018; Mezzera et al., 1997), robe criteria can be used as a proxy for admixture. It has been [SOURCE: Page_339]

the case in our context for many management purposes, as anglers often use body patterns to identify [SOURCE: Page_339]

native trout. [SOURCE: Page_339]

6.5.1   Managers’ perception of pure native and hybrid individuals [SOURCE: Page_339]

For management purposes, managers often categorise pure versus hybrid individuals based on trout [SOURCE: Page_339]

phenotype. By doing so, they assume that the robe criteria used are a relevant proxy to infer genome- [SOURCE: Page_339]

wide admixture, which may not always be the case. For instance, in other Mediterranean populations, [SOURCE: Page_339]

Valette et al. (2020) indicated that some pigmentation patterns used by managers were not reliable [SOURCE: Page_339]

proxies of genome-wide admixture between ATL and MED individuals. Therefore, by only relying on robe [SOURCE: Page_339]

criteria, many mistakes are likely to happen in hybridized areas where ATL, HYB and MED individuals are [SOURCE: Page_339]

found in sympatry. For example, considering management practices such as MED translocation, managers [SOURCE: Page_339]

might actually select admixed individuals exhibiting local robe criteria. [SOURCE: Page_339]

328 [SOURCE: Page_339]

Chapter 6. General discussion [SOURCE: Page_340]

6.5.2   Genotype-phenotype relationship [SOURCE: Page_340]

The complex relationship between genotype and phenotype is still unclear in the Mediterranean area. On [SOURCE: Page_340]

the one hand, the correlation between a given set of robe criteria and a given set of genetic markers [SOURCE: Page_340]

appears to vary depending on the study and the sampling site (35%: Aparicio et al., 2005; Gil et al., 2015; [SOURCE: Page_340]

50%: data from Scimabio Interface and 70%: Folio et al., 2021), but also on the statistical analyses used [SOURCE: Page_340]

(not presented in the manuscript). A recent study indicates that such an approach may still miss a large [SOURCE: Page_340]

part of the recent genetic admixture (Saint-Pé et al., 2019). In addition, information is also missing [SOURCE: Page_340]

regarding the links between loci coding for robe criteria and the genetic markers used, and whether these [SOURCE: Page_340]

are actually relevant proxies of genome-wide admixture (Valette et al., 2020). Improving our knowledge [SOURCE: Page_340]

of the links between genotype and phenotype is essential to appropriately support management decisions [SOURCE: Page_340]

if they are to be based on phenotype. [SOURCE: Page_340]

6.5.3   The genetic system used [SOURCE: Page_340]

In the Mediterranean area, many diagnostic markers – including microsatellites and SNPs – can be used [SOURCE: Page_340]

to differentiate between the ATL and the MED lineages. While these markers were initially established on [SOURCE: Page_340]

the two separate lineages, results from Folio et al. (2021) indicate that despite several generations in [SOURCE: Page_340]

sympatry, there is still a link between the markers used and genes under potential selection. These [SOURCE: Page_340]

markers might therefore be good proxies of alleles enhancing local fitness, meaning that management [SOURCE: Page_340]

practices using these markers might help promote locally adapted genetic variation. [SOURCE: Page_340]

In the model, the use of these markers is simplified in the sense that they are directly linked to fitness [SOURCE: Page_340]

following the results found in Gil et al. (2015) and Folio et al. (2021). In reality, we do not know whether [SOURCE: Page_340]

these markers are directly under selection or whether they are linked to functional loci, and especially [SOURCE: Page_340]

329 [SOURCE: Page_340]

6.6 Climate change [SOURCE: Page_341]

which ones of them are. Indeed, the use of markers that are not linked, or even mismatched with alleles [SOURCE: Page_341]

enhancing fitness might put management practices at risk of failure. It will therefore be important to [SOURCE: Page_341]

determine the ecological relevance of the genetic markers used for conservation purposes. [SOURCE: Page_341]

6.5.4   Defining an appropriate individual admixture threshold [SOURCE: Page_341]

As already discussed before (Section 4.6.4), the number of markers used will highly influence the [SOURCE: Page_341]

categorisation of individuals, especially considering that they are all unlinked: an individual is more likely [SOURCE: Page_341]

to be categorised as a hybrid when a higher number of markers are used. Managers should therefore pay [SOURCE: Page_341]

good attention to this aspect, especially when (i) using a very low number of markers or (ii) comparing [SOURCE: Page_341]

data using a different number of markers. Evaluating the relevance of our measure of introgression is [SOURCE: Page_341]

important to define ecologically appropriate admixture thresholds to distinguish between parental and [SOURCE: Page_341]

hybrid individuals. However, the definition of such a threshold is often challenging and context-specific [SOURCE: Page_341]

(Allendorf et al., 2001). Therefore, as a first step to facilitate the comparison of data, we advise managers [SOURCE: Page_341]

to define individual admixture thresholds based on the number of markers used. Indeed, when a high [SOURCE: Page_341]

number of markers is used, very lowly introgressed individuals might be considered pure individuals from [SOURCE: Page_341]

a management perspective – especially if their robe criteria match the local phenotype. [SOURCE: Page_341]
