## 6.6 CLIMATE CHANGE

For the last decades, climate change and global warming have been negatively affecting freshwater fish, [SOURCE: Page_341]

as well as many other taxa (Comte et al., 2013). Trout species have experienced changes in their [SOURCE: Page_341]

distribution and population declines (Almodóvar et al., 2012; Ayllón et al., 2013; Eby et al., 2014). Studies [SOURCE: Page_341]

investigating the impact of climate change on resident trout focused on thermal regimes (Filipe et al., [SOURCE: Page_341]

330 [SOURCE: Page_341]

Chapter 6. General discussion [SOURCE: Page_342]

2013; Kovach et al., 2016) and stream flow (Wade et al., 2016; Wenger et al., 2011). The rise of global [SOURCE: Page_342]

temperature and the unpredictability of river flow are exceptional challenges to freshwater [SOURCE: Page_342]

conservationists. For instance, more extreme events, such as floods and droughts are to be expected with [SOURCE: Page_342]

strong consequences for habitat quality and availability (eventually habitat loss: Wenger et al., 2011). For [SOURCE: Page_342]

salmonids populations, this will likely result in changes in carrying capacity (Ayllón et al., 2012; L. E. Sundt- [SOURCE: Page_342]

Hansen et al., 2018) and hence affect demography. In addition, freshwater fish are faced with more [SOURCE: Page_342]

localised anthropogenic pressures and/or modifications, including management practices (Comte & [SOURCE: Page_342]

Grenouillet, 2013). In particular, the consequences of climate change on hybridization may pose a threat [SOURCE: Page_342]

to native conservation (Muhlfeld et al., 2014, 2017). [SOURCE: Page_342]

6.6.1    Potential consequences of climate change on the MED/ATL hybridization complex [SOURCE: Page_342]

In our context, an increase in water temperature may lead to full hybridization in environments where [SOURCE: Page_342]

cold temperatures were allowing the MED lineage to persist. This poses a problem for managers [SOURCE: Page_342]

interested in the conservation of the native MED lineage. Pressing conservation actions might have to be [SOURCE: Page_342]

undertaken now, while the environment still favours the MED lineage. However, there is no certainty that [SOURCE: Page_342]

the positive effects of certain management strategies – reduction of ATL alleles – will last in the long term, [SOURCE: Page_342]

if temperatures were to increase. Attempting any management strategy in this context might be a waste [SOURCE: Page_342]

of resources. [SOURCE: Page_342]

On the other hand, under such global changes, managers might have to cycle between “managing for [SOURCE: Page_342]

resilience” and “managing for change” (West et al., 2009) to ensure the long-term persistence of wild [SOURCE: Page_342]

populations. In fact, evolutionary change is the norm whereas a static view of biodiversity is a biased [SOURCE: Page_342]

perception of reality. In our context, managers may want to favour diversity given that adaptation to new [SOURCE: Page_342]

331 [SOURCE: Page_342]

6.6 Climate change [SOURCE: Page_343]

environments is mainly incited by standing genetic variation compared to new mutations (Barrett & [SOURCE: Page_343]

Schluter, 2008). For instance, hybridization may help maintain high levels of diversity to help populations [SOURCE: Page_343]

cope with ongoing changes. Managers attempting to only conserve the native lineage take the risk of [SOURCE: Page_343]

decreasing genetic diversity, especially when management strategies result in negative consequences [SOURCE: Page_343]

(Fernández-Cebrián et al., 2014; Leitwein et al., 2016; Lusardi et al., 2015). [SOURCE: Page_343]

6.6.2   DG-ABMs to predict the impact of climate change [SOURCE: Page_343]

MEDITERRANEA could be used to investigate the evolution of diversity considering various scenarios of [SOURCE: Page_343]

climate change combined with different management strategies aiming at either native conservation or [SOURCE: Page_343]

resilience. Indeed, DG-ABMs are becoming essential tools to explore alternative management scenarios [SOURCE: Page_343]

and support decision-making (Ayllón, Nicola, et al., 2019; Schmolke et al., 2010; Stillman et al., 2015). [SOURCE: Page_343]

Particularly, because of their dendritic shape, rivers are likely to experience heterogeneous responses to [SOURCE: Page_343]

global changes. Such a framework will therefore help managers understand the potential consequences [SOURCE: Page_343]

of one or the other avenue based on the local changes experienced. Nevertheless, this work remains [SOURCE: Page_343]

beyond the scope of the present study because it still requires to further develop models to predict water [SOURCE: Page_343]

temperature and hydrologic trends. Indeed, a good quantification of future climatic exposure is required [SOURCE: Page_343]

to understand the role of climate change on demo-genetic processes. In addition, this will require the [SOURCE: Page_343]

improvement of the actual framework to include a hydrological aspect to model stream flow, as is the [SOURCE: Page_343]

case in (Ayllón, Railsback, et al., 2019). For instance, their simulations suggest that hydrological variation [SOURCE: Page_343]

is a critical dimension for the persistence of trout populations. Finally, one will also have to consider the [SOURCE: Page_343]

capacity of individuals to adapt to changing conditions (Wade et al., 2016). Many studies show that species [SOURCE: Page_343]

have already started to adapt to new conditions (Muñoz & Moritz, 2016). [SOURCE: Page_343]

332 [SOURCE: Page_343]

Chapter 6. General discussion [SOURCE: Page_344]

6.6.3   Forecasting temperature and hydrology [SOURCE: Page_344]

It is a challenge to forecast and predict trends for water temperature and stream flow regimes at a small [SOURCE: Page_344]

spatial scale. Very complex models are required to represent realistically the ecological system (Ayllón, [SOURCE: Page_344]

Grimm, et al., 2018; Evans et al., 2013). In the Mediterranean area, no model is yet available to forecast [SOURCE: Page_344]

the state of ecological systems; but scientists are presently working on it. For instance, we had the [SOURCE: Page_344]

opportunity to meet Guillaume Thirel who is currently working on hydrological models in french rivers to [SOURCE: Page_344]

predict trends in water temperatures and stream flow regimes. However, the case of mountain rivers and [SOURCE: Page_344]

streams represents an additional challenge to them. Such models, when available, could then be [SOURCE: Page_344]

implemented in MEDITERRANEA. For example, Ayllón, Railsback, et al. (2019) have already implemented [SOURCE: Page_344]

climatic projections in a DG-ABM to predict the thermal and hydrological effects of climate change on [SOURCE: Page_344]

Mediterranean trout in Spanish rivers. [SOURCE: Page_344]

## 6.7 IMPLICATIONS FOR MANAGEMENT PRACTICES

6.7.1   Advices for the management of MED/ATL hybridization [SOURCE: Page_344]

In the present thesis, I demonstrated that genotype-by-environment interactions on embryonic survival [SOURCE: Page_344]

play a structuring role in the evolution of diversity dynamics in the MED/ATL hybridization complex. The [SOURCE: Page_344]

strength of the resulting selection appeared to be dependent upon various environmental factors, among [SOURCE: Page_344]

which water temperature, a factor that can show contrast at small scales (Brown & Hannah, 2008; Daigle [SOURCE: Page_344]

et al., 2016). In addition, the results of Chapter 5 (Section 5.3.1 vs 5.3.3) demonstrated that temperature [SOURCE: Page_344]

variations can highly influence the efficiency of management practices. Therefore, I suggest that [SOURCE: Page_344]

management practices should always be context-dependent and adapted to the local conditions [SOURCE: Page_344]

333 [SOURCE: Page_344]

6.7 Implications for management practices [SOURCE: Page_345]

considering temperature variations at small spatial scales. I advise allocating management efforts where [SOURCE: Page_345]

they can bring a higher overall benefit. [SOURCE: Page_345]

For managers interested in protecting “native” MED genetic variation, some environments should be [SOURCE: Page_345]

particularly targeted for conservation actions: For instance, cold environments may naturally and very [SOURCE: Page_345]

efficiently select for native MED alleles, as demonstrated in our simulations. While it may not always allow [SOURCE: Page_345]

for the conservation of pure MED genotype, it will at least help to preserve the native MED female lineage [SOURCE: Page_345]

and potential adaptation related to maternal effects. This opens a path for managers to target sanctuaries [SOURCE: Page_345]

based on thermal monitoring. It suggests that baseline information on climatic parameters and spatial [SOURCE: Page_345]

projection related to the local effects of climate change should be performed by managers. Using such [SOURCE: Page_345]

spatial mapping of the factor of interest (here, water temperature) at small scales and considering the [SOURCE: Page_345]

spatial distribution of available genetic variation (Razgour et al., 2019) may considerably help to forecast [SOURCE: Page_345]

the evolution of intraspecific dynamics. Targeted to the right environment, management strategies aimed [SOURCE: Page_345]

at conserving native diversity could become much more efficient. In short, knowledge of such GxE [SOURCE: Page_345]

interactions opens a path for a cost-effective approach to native diversity conservation. [SOURCE: Page_345]

On the other hand, in environments where hybrids already occur, with intermediate-to-high [SOURCE: Page_345]

temperatures, it might be counter-productive to attempt to eradicate them, since they might have there [SOURCE: Page_345]

an equal or even better fitness compared to native MED individuals. Especially considering that [SOURCE: Page_345]

hybridization might be very frequent – as indicated by the model but also managers' data – and does not [SOURCE: Page_345]

necessarily lead to negative impacts on population dynamics. For instance, in Haute-Savoie, many [SOURCE: Page_345]

naturally persistent hybrids populations are found (Section 2.2.3, Figure 2 – 8). In such hybrid zones, ample [SOURCE: Page_345]

genetic variation might be found. Managers interested in the general resilience of the whole species (Chan [SOURCE: Page_345]

et al., 2019; López-Pujol et al., 2012) should also account for such variation. In fact, with specific attention [SOURCE: Page_345]

334 [SOURCE: Page_345]

Chapter 6. General discussion [SOURCE: Page_346]

devoted to connectivity and dispersal rate (Labonne et al., 2008; Razgour et al., 2019), it should be [SOURCE: Page_346]

possible to elaborate eco-evolutionary management plans accounting for small-scale contrasts in [SOURCE: Page_346]

environments. For instance, hybridization could be used as a management tool to perform assisted and [SOURCE: Page_346]

controlled gene flow in order to increase the evolutionary responsiveness of endangered species facing [SOURCE: Page_346]

global changes (Drury & Lirman, 2021; Stelkens et al., 2014). [SOURCE: Page_346]

6.7.2    Management as part of the eco-evolutionary feedback loop [SOURCE: Page_346]

Traditional management has focused on, and used ecological solutions to conservation issues and species [SOURCE: Page_346]

endangerment (Reznick, Losos, et al., 2019). This conception was based on the idea that, as management [SOURCE: Page_346]

practices are happening on short time scales, they will only affect ecological processes. Consequently, [SOURCE: Page_346]

fisheries, as well as wildlife science, have been little influenced by evolutionary biology (Reznick, Losos, et [SOURCE: Page_346]

al., 2019). However, the current assessment is that ecology and evolution can happen on commensurate [SOURCE: Page_346]

timescales (Carroll et al., 2007; N. G. J. Hairston et al., 2005; M. T. Kinnison & Hairston Jr, 2007; Pelletier [SOURCE: Page_346]

et al., 2009; Reznick, Bassar, et al., 2019; Reznick, Losos, et al., 2019). [SOURCE: Page_346]

Consequently, by interfering with population dynamics, management practices might actually have [SOURCE: Page_346]

consequences for evolution. Indeed, by (1) altering the composition of the population (harvesting, [SOURCE: Page_346]

introduction…), modifying the environment (habitat fragmentation, pollution, climate change…) and [SOURCE: Page_346]

potentially modifying or acting as additional selective pressures, (2) they may interfere with eco- [SOURCE: Page_346]

evolutionary processes – direction or intensity – and (3) eventually trigger evolutionary changes (Figure 6 [SOURCE: Page_346]

– 2). For instance, contemporary evolution is actually often triggered by anthropogenic disturbances [SOURCE: Page_346]

(Carroll et al., 2005, 2007; Hendry et al., 2006, 2008, 2017; Strauss et al., 2006) and it has been [SOURCE: Page_346]

demonstrated in many contexts that management practices and more generally human interference can [SOURCE: Page_346]

335 [SOURCE: Page_346]

6.7 Implications for management practices [SOURCE: Page_347]

have large consequences on diversity. A common example is that of selective harvesting – in the case of [SOURCE: Page_347]

exploitation (Allendorf et al., 2008; Heino et al., 2015) or demographic control (Lefèvre et al., 2014) – [SOURCE: Page_347]

which often causes changes in traits distribution and has been acknowledged to cause evolution (Allendorf [SOURCE: Page_347]

& Hard, 2009). In the context of hybridization, the effects of human-induced environmental perturbation [SOURCE: Page_347]

on eco-evolutionary processes often result in the erosion of reproductive isolation between previously [SOURCE: Page_347]

separated lineages (Grabenstein & Taylor, 2018). Elucidating the evolutionary mechanisms controlling this [SOURCE: Page_347]

erosion could help to formulate sound management decisions. [SOURCE: Page_347]

Figure 6—2: Representation of how management practices may influence the entire eco-evolutionary feedback loop through their effects on population dynamics. [SOURCE: Page_347]

Furthermore, (5) evolution might also in turn influence management practices as it can (4) feedback on [SOURCE: Page_347]

population dynamics, by potentially triggering further changes in traits distribution and changing the [SOURCE: Page_347]

336 [SOURCE: Page_347]

Chapter 6. General discussion [SOURCE: Page_348]

demographic structure (Figure 6 – 2). For example, this happened in the context of fish exploitation, where [SOURCE: Page_348]

the overexploitation of large individuals caused the evolution of smaller body size, at the disadvantage of [SOURCE: Page_348]

exploitation practices. In the context of the MED/ATL hybridization, it is initially management practices [SOURCE: Page_348]

that led to the creation of hybrids – changes in the population composition –, which in turn resulted in [SOURCE: Page_348]

changes in management practices – conservation of the MED lineage, having further and further effects [SOURCE: Page_348]

on evolutionary and ecological processes. These constant changes in population dynamics make [SOURCE: Page_348]

conservation more complex (Carroll et al., 2007). [SOURCE: Page_348]

6.7.3    The rise of evolutionary management [SOURCE: Page_348]

This shift in the perception of management and eco-evolutionary dynamics has led to the development [SOURCE: Page_348]

of new ideas, such as genetic or evolutionary rescue, to help management strategies. Indeed, in many [SOURCE: Page_348]

fields of conservation, there is now a growing interest in evolutionary management (Carlson et al., 2014; [SOURCE: Page_348]

Chan et al., 2019; Fitzpatrick et al., 2019; Gauzere et al., 2020; Postolache et al., 2021; Walsworth et al., [SOURCE: Page_348]

2019; Whiteley et al., 2015). The idea is to use evolution to help management strategies. In the context [SOURCE: Page_348]

of climate change, for instance, evolutionary management aims at preserving the evolutionary potential [SOURCE: Page_348]

of populations in order to ease their adaptation to changing environments (Aitken & Whitlock, 2013; [SOURCE: Page_348]

Carroll et al., 2007; Lefèvre et al., 2014; Reznick, Bassar, et al., 2019; Reznick, Losos, et al., 2019). To do [SOURCE: Page_348]

so, scientists promote the conservation of high genetic diversity, as it represents a resilience mechanism [SOURCE: Page_348]

(C. N. Cook & Sgrò, 2018; Jump et al., 2009; López-Pujol et al., 2012; Violle et al., 2012). In addition, they [SOURCE: Page_348]

focus on the environment maximizing the chances of individuals to adapt to new conditions to provide [SOURCE: Page_348]

managers with conservation guidelines (Carlson et al., 2014; Whiteley et al., 2015). Nowadays, with the [SOURCE: Page_348]

increasing impact of climate change on populations, the concept of evolutionary management is growing [SOURCE: Page_348]

337 [SOURCE: Page_348]

6.7 Implications for management practices [SOURCE: Page_349]

fast. Indeed, scientists are becoming more aware that conservation goals may only be met by “harnessing [SOURCE: Page_349]

the power of evolution” (Carroll et al., 2007). [SOURCE: Page_349]

Bernard Werber [SOURCE: Page_350]

## APPENDIX A – STATE VARIABLES ASSOCIATED WITH REACH , WEIR, HATCH AND FISH OBJECTS.

## APPENDIX C – MONTHLY RELATIONSHIP BETWEEN WATER TEMPERATURE AND ALTITUDE .

The relationship between water temperature and altitude was plotted using many rivers in Haute-Savoie, including those studied in this manuscript – the Borne River and the Dranse d’Abondance System with the Chevenne Creek. [SOURCE: Page_396]

Figure 1 illustrates this relationship in spring and summer – from April to September – and shows that water temperature is negatively correlated with altitude. Figure 2 illustrates this relationship in autumn and winter – from October to March – and shows that the negative correlation observed between water temperature and altitude is progressively less pronounced from autumn to winter, as temperatures are getting very low. Usually, observed temperatures in this area rarely exceed 5°C in winter, notably during the embryonic phase – starting from December in the model. [SOURCE: Page_396]

385 [SOURCE: Page_396]

## APPENDICES

23                   28                    0                    100                   100 24                   29                    0                    0                     0 25                   30                    0                    0                     0 [SOURCE: Page_402]

* Father ID corresponds to the weir located just downstream. Length, mean width and altitude are given in metres. Reproduction and capacity are habitat quality variables ranging between [0, 1]. Reproduction determines the reproductive capacity of the reach by influencing female egg number and, possibly, the upstream movement for reproduction. Capacity influences the maximum carrying capacity of each reach. Fishing status determines the fishing regulation in place and CSL represents catch size limits (minimum and maximum value). [SOURCE: Page_402]

** Father ID corresponds to the reach located just downstream. Fish Pass indicates whether an actual fish pass is present at the weir: 0 means “no” and 1 means “yes”. Up and Down Pass inform on the passability of the weir for fish movement. It is measured on a scale [0, 100]. 0 indicates that there is no obstacle and the weir is entirely passable. 1 indicates that a barrier makes the weir fully unpassable. Any intermediate values are for passable weirs (more or less easy to pass depending on the value). [SOURCE: Page_402]

## APPENDIX E – THE ROLE OF HOMOGAMOUS VS HETEROGAMOUS PREFERENCE.

Results from a poster presentation for the EEEF international conference (Berkeley, 2021). [SOURCE: Page_402]

How does intraspecific diversity evolve under different mating preferences (random vs heterogamous vs homogamous), when considering GxE interactions? [SOURCE: Page_402]

The model was used to simulate a first contact between lineages in a simple hydrographic network (similar to section 4.3). The initial population was composed of an equal number of MED and ATL individuals. These simulations aimed at evaluating and comparing the role of different types of female preference on the evolution of diversity dynamics. Three female preference scenarios were defined: homogamous, random and heterogamous preference (Figure 1). For each scenario, the value of GCP was set at 50% to test for an intermediate value. A medium modality of GxE interactions was implemented. [SOURCE: Page_402]

391 [SOURCE: Page_402]

## APPENDICES

Figure 1: Female preference curves implemented in the model for each scenario: homogamous, random and heterogamous preference. [SOURCE: Page_403]

Evolution of introgression.      Due to the selection mediated by GxE interactions, a progressive decrease in introgression rate can be observed for all scenarios (Figure 2). The implementation of different female preferences only appears to either slow down (heterogamous preference) or accelerate (homogamous preference) that decrease compared to random preference. However, the differences observed remain small, as observed in the simulations from the main manuscript. [SOURCE: Page_403]

Figure 2: Evolution of introgression under random (green), heterogamous (orange) and homogamous (blue) preference following first contact and when considering GxE interactions. [SOURCE: Page_403]

392 [SOURCE: Page_403]

- Confidence_Score: High
---
## APPENDICES

Distribution of individual introgression.        The distribution of individual introgression through time shows that no matter the mating preference implemented the main pattern is the same (Figure 3). Following a first secondary contact, there is a large creation of F$_1$ HYB individuals. A bell-shaped distribution is progressively formed throughout time and is biased toward low introgression values, as selection progressively acts to remove ATL alleles. The only slight difference is the value on which the distribution is centred. For heterogamous preference, at t = 40, the distribution is centred around 0.3, while it is lower for random preference and homogamous preference (both around 0.2). [SOURCE: Page_404]

Figure 3: Distribution of individual introgression throughout time, from t = 2 to t = 40 for random (green), heterogamous (orange) and homogamous (blue) preference and when considering GxE interactions. [SOURCE: Page_404]

393 [SOURCE: Page_404]

## APPENDIX F – MED MATERNAL ASCENDANCE IN HYBRID POPULATIONS.

Focusing on hybrid individuals, I followed the proportion of MED versus ATL alleles transmitted maternally and paternally to (i) investigate whether introgression mainly happened through one sex (paternally) and (ii) verify if simulated data also pointed at the MED maternal ascendance in hybrid populations. Results are presented for the eco-evolutionary scenario considering both eco-evolutionary mechanisms: heterogamous female preference and GxE interactions on survival. [SOURCE: Page_405]

Figure 1: Proportion of MED alleles (p) inherited maternally (circle) versus paternally (triangle) throughout time for HYB individuals, in the upstream zone of the Chevenne Creek. For each parent, 1 – p corresponds to the proportion of ATL alleles inherited. [SOURCE: Page_405]

In the Chevenne Creek (Figure 1), following the implementation of the genetic refuge strategy, mean values indicated that a higher proportion of MED alleles was transmitted to HYB individuals maternally compared to paternally. However, following ATL removal and MED translocation, proportions became almost equal (> 90% of MED alleles) as the population was mainly composed of pure MED individuals. [SOURCE: Page_405]

394 [SOURCE: Page_405]

## APPENDICES

Figure 2: Proportion of MED alleles inherited maternally (circle) versus paternally (triangle) throughout time for HYB individuals, in the main downstream section of the Borne River (case study: “Borne 1988”). For each parent, 1 – p corresponds to the proportion of ATL alleles inherited. [SOURCE: Page_406]

In the Borne, following the first years of ATL stocking (Figure 2, up to t = 4), MED alleles represented up to 90% of the alleles transmitted through the maternal way, whereas it was lower through the paternal way. Indeed the proportion of MED alleles transmitted paternally decreased from 80% (t = 0) to 35% (t = 4) within four years of stocking. Throughout time, the proportion of MED alleles transmitted to HYB individuals’, maternally and paternally, decreased as more and more ATL alleles were brought into the population through continuous ATL stocking. Nevertheless, the proportion of MED alleles inherited maternally always remained higher than that inherited paternally. [SOURCE: Page_406]

In the Borne, following MED translocation (Figure 3), HYB individuals inherited a majority of MED alleles maternally (~ 90%) against a majority of ATL alleles paternally (< 10% of MED alleles). Throughout time, the proportion of MED alleles inherited maternally then decreased while that inherited paternally increased until both proportions equalised, as more and more HYB individuals were created. [SOURCE: Page_406]

## APPENDICES

Figure 3: Proportion of MED alleles inherited maternally (circle) versus paternally (triangle) throughout time for HYB individuals, in the upstream zone of the Borne River (case study: “Borne 2004”). For each parent, 1 – p corresponds to the proportion of ATL alleles inherited. [SOURCE: Page_407]

Take home message.      Following allelic transmission (maternal vs paternal) in hybrid individuals, data indicate that more MED alleles are transmitted to hybrids maternally compared to paternally. In addition, a very high initial proportion of MED alleles (vs ATL alleles) is transmitted maternally following the first secondary contact. On the contrary, more ATL alleles (vs MED alleles) are generally transmitted paternally. Therefore, results indicate that introgression initially happens through paternal inheritance and hence point to the MED maternal ascendance in hybridized populations. [SOURCE: Page_407]

396 [SOURCE: Page_407]
